#5002. Etsy Says Gah! The Handmade Marketplace and Playful Fashion Label Team Up on a Home Collection

Since its founding in 2015, Lisa Says Gah has become known for its bowtie-pasta cardigans, checkerboard coats, and swans. Today, the California-based label is releasing its first home collection, which has a similarly playful ethos: “The Lisa Says Gah world really naturally extends into the home,” says founder Lisa Bühler. “Home and clothing ultimately tell the story of who you are.”

A pin board and two eclectic frames from Frame Mavens comprise a third of the collection.

Founder Lisa Bühler co-judged the Etsy Design Awards and selected three winners to co-design Etsy Says Gah!, her brand’s first foray into pieces for the home.

The collection is a collaboration with Etsy, a platform Bühler often uses for her own inspiration (things that make her shout: Gah!). “When you go to Etsy, you’re always going to find something that delights you,” she says.

Lisa Says Gah has stocked independent designers alongside its own line for more than a decade. This year, Bühler co-judged the Etsy Design Awards, and selected three winners to co-design Etsy Says Gah!, a nine-piece collection exclusively available via Etsy. Bühler says she prioritized “unexpected, collected things that you just don’t automatically go to when you think of homeware.”

UK-based Cait Mellor executed Bühler’s mood board through crochet in a floral table runner, a horseshoe decal, and swan coasters.

The home collection’s foundational inspiration comes from Lisa Says Gah’s two fall lines, Pastoral Daze and Swan Dance, which pair horses and swans with paisleys and cabin-inspired knits. Bühler worked with the Etsy designers by sharing mood boards, giving notes when she wanted more of a motif or a different color combination, and trusting each maker with her own medium. “They’re the expert[s],” she says. “The process felt like really trusting and putting it in their hands.”’

Each maker applied the same mood board differently. Jacqueline Sanchez, a Houston-based ceramicist who won this year’s Grand Prize for a vase covered in more than 1,500 hand-applied scales, sculpted the swan in relief on a pale pink vase wound with green vines. She also set a horse’s head in a blue baroque cameo on a cream mug and dotted a wavy platter with tiny fruit as a nod to the brand’s food prints. “[I liked] having the freedom to incorporate elements that feel really true to my work,” says Sanchez. The vase is a piece Bühler keeps coming back to: “You would just be happy to have it on the table without flowers,” she says.

The vase is the piece Bühler keeps coming back to: “You would just be happy to have it on the table without flowers,” she says.

“Because it’s our first home collection, we wanted it to feel like something you collect over time in your house versus a uniform grouping,” says Bühler.

Cait Mellor, a UK-based seller, worked the swan and horse elements into the open grid of filet crochet: swans on a set of coasters, a horseshoe under the word Lucky on a small tapestry, and flowers down a cream table runner. Her goal is to “bring this traditional art form into a more contemporary setting.” Bühler is already picturing the coasters at dinner parties, where “everyone is going to talk about the swans.”

Emma Miller and Kristen Kellar, chosen for their fabric-wrapped frames, focused on color and pattern rather than the animals. They made a burgundy frame with black polka dots on a blue background, a frame featuring blue pixelated flowers that look almost cross-stitched, and a pin board that uses pink fabric crossed with orange ribbon.

The home collection’s foundational inspiration comes from Lisa Says Gah’s two fall lines, Pastoral Daze and Swan Dance, which pair horses and swans with paisleys and cabin-inspired knits.

Bühler didn’t want the nine pieces to look like a matched set. “Because it’s our first home collection, we wanted it to feel like something you collect over time in your house versus a uniform grouping,” she says. Each item costs less than $200, and with the holidays coming—with endless-feeling opportunities for hosting and gifting—now is a great time to start collecting.

#4998. Diana Ross at Home: 10 Photos of the Pop Diva Off-Duty

As the breakout star of the Supremes and later a solo sensation, Diana Ross proved herself a force to be reckoned with. The Detroit native’s booming career allowed the singer-slash-actor to fashion her own residences in her own singular style: A statement purple sofa, a shag-lined conversation pit, beaded curtains, and a mirrored piano were among the features that might’ve stood out elsewhere, but all appeared seamlessly at home with The Boss. As she declared in her 1979 anthem of independence: “There’s my chair / I put it there / Everything you see / Is with love and care / It’s my house and I live here.”

Through 12 number-one singles with The Supremes, six number-one hits as a solo artist, a Lifetime Achievement Award from the Recording Academy, and a Presidential Medal of Freedom, Ross apparently managed to maintain a sense of normalcy in her domestic life. “My mom is such a mom,” Ross’s daughter, actor Tracee Ellis Ross, said on The Kelly Clarkson Show. “I had a really regular upbringing.” Read on for a roundup of the icon in her offstage moments.

Ross poses on a floral sofa in her Miami suite in this photo, dated March 17, 1970. It was a big year for the songstress: She performed her final concert as lead singer of The Supremes that January, and her debut solo album was released in June.

The singer unwinds on the sofa during an interview.

When these portraits were snapped, Ross was in the middle of a nine-day run of shows at Miami’s Eden Roc hotel—her official debut solo show after a preview run in Framingham, Massachusetts. “Welcome to the ‘Let’s see if Diana Ross can do it by herself club,’” the vocalist said during the performance, journalist Shirley Eder wrote in a March 20, 1970, issue of The Philadelphia Inquirer. Eder also reported that “Diana takes a long time to unwind after a performance… she screens movies in her suite almost nightly.”

A pregnant Ross leans against her reflective piano.

Ross, eight months pregnant with Tracee in this 1972 photo, leans against the mirrored piano in her Beverly Hills home during an interview about her first film role: Billie Holiday in Lady Sings the Blues, directed by Sidney J. Furie. Ross’s depiction of the jazz singer garnered her a Golden Globe and an Oscar nomination, among other accolades. “She’s the greatest improviser I’ve ever known,” Furie told The Hollywood Reporter in 2021. “Some of the main scenes were improvised…. She was the consummate pro and not a diva at all.”

The musician at home with her daughter Rhonda.

Ross lounges in her Beverly Hills home alongside her eldest child, Rhonda, in 1972. The “Ain’t No Mountain High Enough” singer’s career was launched by Motown founder Berry Gordy, and the two shared a relationship that was romantic as well as professional. Rhonda was born in 1971 as a result of that liaison, but Ross married music executive Robert Ellis Silberstein two months into the pregnancy, and he raised Rhonda as his own. The couple welcomed two more daughters together: Tracee, who was born not long after this photo was taken, and Chudney, who was born in 1975.

Ross at home taping an appearance for an ABC special.

In the mid-1970s, The Wiz star was once again photographed on the purple sofa in her Beverly Hills living area, which was complete with a white-shag-lined conversation pit and a mirror-bedecked fireplace. According to a 1972 New York Times profile of the artist, her 6,833-square-foot home had 30-foot-tall ceilings and an Andy Warhol tapestry of Marilyn Monroe. The grounds also hosted a swimming pool and a tennis court.

Ross with daughters Tracee (left) and Rhonda.

The Ross family’s Beverly Hills pad boasted five bedrooms and nine bathrooms. According to The Los Angeles Times, the property was built in 1964 for art collector Donald Factor. Because its walls maximized space for paintings, it was reportedly known in the neighborhood as “the house with no windows.” The conversation pit abutted a window adorned with a beaded curtain, as shown in the background of this photo of Ross with Tracee and Rhonda. Both daughters followed in their mother’s footsteps as performers: Tracee is an actor (who recently won her first Daytime Emmy for the program Solo Traveling with Tracee Ellis Ross), while Rhonda was nominated for a Daytime Emmy for her role in the soap opera Another World.

Ross, flanked by her daughters, cradles infant Chudney.

A newborn Chudney also made an appearance during the mid-’70s shoot, as seen in this snapshot. Ross and Silberstein divorced in 1977, and the singer went on to marry Norwegian businessman Arne Naess Jr. in 1986. With Naess, she welcomed two sons, Ross and Evan. The “I’m Coming Out” singer and her second husband split in 2000.

In 1978, a newly single Ross welcomed Barbara Walters to her Beverly Hills home for an interview about her career and life as a single mother in front of a window overlooking the backyard pool. “Her success has brought her every material comfort,” Walters narrated in the television special. “Believe it or not, within 24 hours before we came here she completely refurnished the living room.” The television special offered a glimpse of the conversation pit, now done up with dark maroon carpet and a navy sofa with white cushions.

Ross dressed to impress for her birthday party.

The “Upside Down” singer celebrated her 38th birthday with a party at her Beverly Hills residence in March 1982, as seen in this glamorous snapshot. Later that year, she released Silk Electric, her 13th studio album, and was honored with a star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame.

Ross’s daughters were in attendance, as shown in this photo of her bustling birthday party. The 13-time Grammy nominee sold the Beverly Hills property three years later for $1.75 million. These days, the 82-year-old icon lives at Quarry Farm, a property in Greenwich, Connecticut. In 2021, she released her 25th studio album, Thank You.

#4995. Coinone warns of phishing, identity theft after customer data breach

The South Korean crypto exchange said potentially exposed information includes passwords, bank account numbers, national identification numbers and crypto wallet addresses.

South Korean crypto exchange Coinone warned customers of potential phishing scams and identity theft after an attacker breached its outsourced customer support provider, exposing information shared during customer service interactions.

On Sunday, Coinone said an attacker accessed servers operated by MetaM on Thursday and viewed or extracted customer support records. Coinone said it learned about the breach in the same day.

According to Coinone, the information potentially exposed names, phone numbers, email addresses, dates of birth, bank account numbers, passwords, national identification numbers, home and wallet addresses and transaction IDs.

Coinone said the affected data varies by customer, and people who never contacted its support center were unaffected. It did not disclose how many customers were affected.

The exchange urged customers who shared passwords during support interactions to change them and warned that fraudsters could impersonate Coinone using details from previous conversations.

Coinone said its account, trading and wallet infrastructure operates separately from MetaM’s servers, with no evidence of direct compromise. The exchange said it has reported the incident to South Korean authorities.

The disclosure comes as South Korea moves to strengthen financial-sector cybersecurity. In October, the Financial Services Commission identified security deficiencies at 130 of 555 financial firms reviewed.

On Thursday, the regulator said it would allow crypto exchanges to suspend withdrawals when hacking is suspected, alongside calls for round-the-clock monitoring.

#4994. Andy Baraghani’s New NYC Restaurant is Full of Design Surprises

“I’ve always been obsessed with design, since I was a kid,” says Andy Baraghani. “I love how restaurant interiors can take you to a different place.”

At Ditto—the all-day café he is opening in New York’s Flatiron—the chef, cookbook author, and YouTube star has given that obsession free rein. With each design decision—from the vintage glass-prism Holophane pendants in the front window to the intense blue (tinted to match Benjamin Moore Buckland Blue) of the rear dining area’s Venetian plaster walls—he asked himself: “Would I actually do this in my home?” If the answer was yes, it passed the test.

The interior of Ditto, with Venetian plaster walls in a shade matching Benjamin Moore Buckland Blue, and Model S22 chairs from Galvanitas.

For Andy Baraghani, design is something of a family affair. His husband, Keith Pollock, is a former AD editor and now a West Elm executive. But opening a restaurant was never part of the plan. In fact, when Michael Silber, a restaurant investor behind New York hot spots like Borgo, Penny, and Bistrot Ha, approached him with the idea of a laid-back restaurant open for breakfast, lunch, and dinner, Baraghani admits his first reaction was: “I have no interest.”

It wasn’t for lack of experience. He had started his career in restaurants—from Chez Panisse in Berkeley, where he worked as a teenager, to Estela in New York—before becoming a food editor at Bon Appetit and winning a James Beard Award for his cookbook, The Cook You Want to Be.

But he bonded with Silber over their shared love of greenmarket cooking and decorating deep dives. When it came to Ditto, he says, he wanted the space to convey his personal style. It helped that he and Silber were on the same page. “Andy and I talked about styles ranging from Bauhaus, de Stijl, and classic modernism to 1970s and ’80s aesthetics from places like Italy and France,” Silber says.

They brought in like-minded collaborators: interior designer Eric Wink and architect Alta Indelman. Wink, a close friend of Baraghani who helped with the design of his and Pollock’s home, says the partners told him that New York’s much-missed City Bakery, which was two blocks uptown from Ditto’s 16th Street location, was an inspiration. “They wanted it to feel casual and approachable, with a bit of a New York diner quality,” Wink says.

Holophane pendant lamps hang in the restaurant’s front window.

The space, in a 1905 former factory (its last incarnation was an agnès b. store), is divided into three zones. In the front section, visitors can shop at a market area with hand-picked groceries. A glossy white counter with Bauhaus curves anchors the middle section; this is where customers line up, take a number, and either carry the food out or dine at laminate-topped tables under a trio of oversized custom fabric pendants. The menu, handwritten on a roll of butcher’s paper and updated daily, features dishes like pomegranate spoon salad and what Baraghani calls “the Platonic ideal of granola,” with tahini, maple syrup, and yogurt drizzled with fig-leaf oil.

The third zone, the one painted blue, features an open kitchen and café tables pulled up to a banquette that runs the length of the room. “There’s that good chaos in the middle and the back is a calmer moment,” says Baraghani.

Indelman, a veteran collaborator with New York restaurateurs like Andrew Tarlow and Gabe Stulman, was tasked with giving the space a utilitarian, high-tech ambience. She clad the walls and the open kitchen in patinated stainless steel and preserved industrial elements like structural arches and polished concrete floors.

But it was Baraghani and Silber who took the lead on honing the visual details of the interiors. “We went through 50 possibilities to find the right chair,” Silber says. The one they selected—a 1960s Dutch industrial chair, the Model S22 from Galvanitas—was sourced from Amsterdam Modern in Los Angeles.

Silber also spent hours on eBay bidding on midcentury ceramic bud vases. “They were each under $20 but put them together on a shelf and you have your own little Giorgio Morandi tableau,” he says.

White wall speakers and a French ceramic mask hang above the banquette, tables, and stools upholstered in Maharam’s Stripe It fabric.

The no-nonsense decor also features white wall speakers, created for the space by sound artist Tom Bloxam, and programmed with playlists created by Michael Scanlon of Chandelier Creative, which oversaw Ditto’s branding. But there are quirkier moments too. When it came to choosing fabric for the custom stools, Baraghani insisted on Maharam’s Stripe It, which he felt had a “Baja California” vibe. “We needed that twist,” he says. For his part, Wink specified his favorite stainless-steel toilet for the restrooms—Neo-Metro’s wall-mounted MiniLoo. “I added a custom wood top to soften it a bit,” Wink says.

Meanwhile, a pair of massive midcentury French ceramic faces that hang on a wall opposite the menu of the day are on loan from Baraghani’s own home. “They anchor the space and add a touch of craft,” he says. “I just knew they needed to be here.”

#4989. Gwyneth Paltrow Is Launching a New Podcast—Take the First Look Inside the “Goop Guide” Studio

Viewers tuning in to Gwyneth Paltrow’s new podcast, The Goop Guide, will be forgiven for assuming the show was filmed in a room inside her serene and stylish Montecito home. The setting for her new venture (launching on Netflix and Spotify November 11) upends the paradigm of a podcaster on a sofa next to a potted fern.

The Goop Guide is indeed recorded inside a studio, but Paltrow approached the space with just as much intention as her own home, enlisting interior designer Marie Carson to bring it to life. “I wanted it to feel like a beautiful, elevated nook of somebody’s old stone house somewhere,” Paltrow tells AD of the space.

“If we can’t be recording in my home, I want to give guests the feeling of comfort and coziness,” Paltrow says of her goal for the studio’s design.

The catch? Carson only had mere days to turn the sterile studio into something far cozier and conducive to the wide-ranging conversations Paltrow hoped to conduct during the show, which launches with Zoe Saldana as the first guest. “We started to decorate remotely and I looked at it and thought, This is not the direction,” Paltrow says of the space’s first iteration. “And so I called Marie and said, ‘We’re filming in, like, a week and I need you to come and fix this.’”

It wasn’t the tight timeline that initially gave Carson pause. “At first I said, ‘Oh my gosh, I’ve never done a set! What does that mean?’” recalls the designer. “But when Gwyneth said she wanted it to feel like a beautiful room in her home, I thought, Well, I can do that.” Previously, the two had collaborated on a space at Paltrow’s Brentwood property, which meant there was already a creative shorthand between them—incredibly helpful when they had only days to outfit the space before shooting commenced. “Gwyneth has such a strong direction of what she wants and such excellent taste,” Carson says. “So I was able to take her notes and run with it.”

The focal point for the studio’s design is a late-18th-century Flemish tapestry. “It added life to the space,” says Carson.

The Goop Kitchen founder wanted the focal point of the design to be a late-18th-century Flemish tapestry, whose landscape imagery features a palette of muted greens and taupes. “I thought it would be beautiful because of what they’ve lived through and what they’ve seen,” Paltrow says of tapestries. “I love them because you think about all the life going on in front of these things for hundreds and hundreds of years.” To complement the hanging, Carson added a 19th-century Belgian trestle table from Meg Turner Gallery, antique chairs reupholstered in Loro Piana brushed cashmere, and a handwoven hemp and silk rug sourced from Mansour. “Marie’s execution is flawless,” Paltrow says. “She has a real knack for finding the perfect thing that has the right dimensions and warmth.”

Paltrow commissioned a painting of magnolia from artist Rob Reynolds. The sconces are from Goop’s collection with CB2 and the wicker stools are from Galerie Provenence.

A close-up of one of the podcast chairs: an antique sourced from a shop in Pasadena and reupholstered with Loro Piana cashmere.

In the handful of shows Paltrow’s filmed so far, it appears her guests (which include Rick Rubin, Ke Huy Quan, and more) feel similarly at home. “Everyone who walks in is like, ‘I’ve done so many podcasts and I’ve never seen one like this! It feels like a real room and I feel so at ease.’’’ she says. Considering Paltrow will be filming dozens of episodes inside the space, she’s happy it’s comfortable for her as well. “I would definitely live in this room,” she says. “I think about this room all the time now when I’m not in it because I’m so obsessed with it.”

#4987. JPMorgan’s Bitcoin and Crypto-Linked Holdings Approach $1 Billion

JPMorgan’s Bitcoin and Crypto-Linked Holdings Approach $1 Billion

JPMorgan Chase, whose CEO has spent years deriding crypto, is now sitting on close to $1 billion worth of crypto-linked stocks and funds, a regulatory filing shows, after the bank deepened those positions in the third quarter.

Adding up the disclosures in JPMorgan’s quarterly 13F, the form large institutional managers must file with the Securities and Exchange Commission to report their holdings, the bank held roughly $981 million across spot Bitcoin ETFs, Michael Saylor’s Strategy, and crypto exchange Coinbase as of Sept. 30.

Counting its stakes in Robinhood and Bitcoin miners, the total likely tips past $1 billion.

The largest piece is BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust, or IBIT, where JPMorgan reported 11,408,930 shares worth about $540 million, a 9.6% increase in shares from the prior quarter, when the holding was valued at roughly $356 million. It held smaller positions in Grayscale’s and Bitwise’s Bitcoin ETFs, bringing its total spot Bitcoin ETF exposure to around $549 million.

The bank also leaned further into Strategy, the Bitcoin treasury company, raising its stake 36% to 848,932 shares, up from 624,387 at the end of June. That position was valued at about $131.3 million, up from $57.9 million a quarter earlier, reflecting both the added shares and a rise in Strategy’s stock over the period.

A roughly $301 million stake in Coinbase rounds out the core holdings, alongside positions in Robinhood and miners including Core Scientific and Hut 8. JPMorgan also disclosed sizable options tied to both IBIT and Strategy.

The company’s 13F is a snapshot as of Sept. 30 and doesn’t capture moves since, nor does it necessarily reflect directional bets. Banks often hold such positions to hedge or to facilitate client trades rather than to wager on price, so the near-$1 billion figure is better read as the scale of JPMorgan’s crypto-market activity than as a conviction call.

That nuance matters given CEO Jamie Dimon, who has called Bitcoin worthless and likened crypto to a fraud, even as the bank he runs steadily builds out digital-asset services.

Those services have expanded quickly. JPMorgan recently issued its first price forecast for Bitcoin, rolled out a deposit token called JPM Coin tied to bank dollars on Ethereum, and explored tokenized stocks alongside BlackRock and the DTCC.

The disclosure also lands during a rough stretch for Bitcoin funds. Spot Bitcoin ETFs have bled more than $1.4 billion over a recent five-day span as investors cut risk, a reminder that the institutional appetite captured in JPMorgan’s September snapshot has cooled since.

Bitcoin, which traded near $155 per Strategy share levels at quarter-end, has since slid well off its highs, today trading below $69,000.

#4984. Bitcoin falls to 4-month low near $62K as crypto selloff deepens

The global cryptocurrency market capitalization fell 4% to $2.26 trillion over 24 hours, while Ether and Solana posted double-digit weekly losses.

Bitcoin fell to a four-month low near $62,000 on Sunday, extending a selloff across major digital assets before recovering some of its losses.

On Sunday, Bitcoin (BTC) dropped to a 24-hour low of $62,080.91, according to CoinGecko, its weakest level since July. The asset later rebounded above $65,000 but remained down nearly 15% in the last seven days.

The downturn extended beyond Bitcoin. CoinGecko data showed Ether (ETH) down 19.5% over the past week, while Solana (SOL) lost 20.7%. The total value of the cryptocurrency market fell about 4% over 24 hours to $2.26 trillion, according to CoinGecko.

Market sentiment also deteriorated. The Crypto Fear & Greed Index fell to 10 on Sunday from 13 on Saturday and 20 on Friday, remaining in “extreme fear,” according to Alternative.me.

The move followed Friday’s slide below $70,000, as analysts reported weakening investor sentiment and concerns over institutional outflows and tighter liquidity.

The latest decline came after US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds posted $2.35 billion in net outflows over two consecutive weeks. The funds lost $1.07 billion during the week ended Nov. 13, following $1.28 billion the previous week. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust extended its outflow streak to 11 trading sessions on Friday.

#4983. 15 Best Doormats That Are Durable and Design-Forward (2026)

Displaying a welcoming doormats at your home’s threshold is a crucial opportunity to make a strong first impression on visitors. But it’s not just about wooing your guests upon arrival—they should also have some staying power against the elements and an onslaught of muddy boots this winter.

Thankfully, we found plenty of heavy-duty, high-quality doormats that are both handsome and durable. Many of these options feature natural fibers made from coir, which is mildew-resistant, absorbent, and can survive any kind of weather conditions and foot traffic it endures. Eco-friendly jute is another natural material common among doormat designs—favored for its organic look and its ability to trap dirt while still looking clean. Our top picks include doormats we’ve tested in our own homes as well as a few exciting options from brands we trust, so we don’t suggest (figuratively) dragging your feet on these great designs.

Ruggable’s entire door mat catalog is machine washable, which feels like a non-negotiable for an item literally made to trap dirt. Senior commerce editor Nashia Baker found a kindred match in this pick, telling us that “the balance of whimsical lines and shapes makes walking through the door a harmonious experience.” She also noted that it’s “incredibly durable,” and after months of high-traffic it “can handle any wet or muddy shoes that land on it without getting waterlogged.” Non-slip backing keeps it (and you) from sliding around.

Lean into a modern nautical vibe (think: this contemporary Long Island escape) with Serena & Lily’s chunky mat that looks like it was woven from stolen sailboat rope. The natural/coastal blue colorway offers just a hint of color, while monochromatic options that weave in multiple shades of blue and white make a more vibrant statement.

Sometimes all you need to make a statement is a simple pattern. This honeycomb design is subtle, mildew-resistant, shed-free, and fade-safe. A rubber backing means there won’t be any slipping and it’s designed to hold water—the raised border design helps contain up to 1.5 gallons of water per square yard.

Quilt-like lines feed into a larger geometric pattern in this doormat design that still feels simple but very modern. The grid of black rectangular lines reads as a neutral, and the elongated shape covers more area than some of its peers on this list, so we’d recommend it for an entry hallway or wider doorsteps.

This Heymat doormat is a durable option for covered porches and entryways. Not suitable for true outdoor use (on an uncovered patio or anywhere susceptible to the elements), this Heymat doormat is highly absorbent and soft underfoot. Shopping market editor Kate McGregor’s family owns a few mats from this brand, which they use as an oversized entryway mat in the mudroom for muddy boots and dog paws, as well as a bathmat in the guest bathroom. “This multi-colored mat has held up so well against summer mud and dropped birdseed, despite its pastel grid design,” she says. “Even after five years, the colors have stayed vibrant, and the mat never gets soggy.” Plus, whether sprayed down or shaken, she notes that the cleanup is very easy. “I might protest if my mom ever decides to replace it.”

A departure from the traditional rectangular doormat shape, this Toast alternative offers colorful texture in one compact oval shape that’s fully reversible. This one is crafted from woven and braided jute strands that are ideal for trapping loose dirt and debris. For those looking for an alternative to standard brown jute styles, these alternating patterns are pretty mesmerizing.

If you’ve got more floor space to fill in your greeting room, smaller sizes of The Citizenry’s jute rugs could be exactly what you’ve been looking for. At 24” x 36,” this hand braided mat has a refined look thanks to the contrasting border design, and it’ll be a nice surface to station wet or damp shoes so you’re not damaging the floor.

In our doormat quest, we also found this woven gem from Matilda Goad’s MG&Co. with looped fringing that looks like scalloped edges. The deep burgundy tone will help hide visible mud stains (though the jute weave can also be easily hosed down when it’s time for a clean). It’s also available in a longer, thinner silhouette to help cover sprawling patios.

Floral without feeling overly whimsical (no shame if that’s your vibe, though), Anthropologie’s sweet, embossed doormat is a simple option for anyone with an already-decorated entryway. With an abundance of five-star ratings, lots of reviews praise the design's versatile look and durable material makeup.

The raised, slatted design of Crate & Barrel’s unfinished teakwood doormat keeps moisture, grime, and dirt away sans-bristles. It’s also great for indoor use, perhaps as a minimalist bath mat. Be mindful that teak patinas over time due to weather and regular wear and tear, shifting from a golden wood to a grayish color.

The bold tulip motif on this doormat has a cheerful Scandi-folk style that can brighten your front entry even in the dead of winter. When it starts to look a little worse for wear, the mat easily lifts off its non-slip base for machine washing. In a spot that receives near-constant foot traffic, the ability to refresh it from season to season will be a godsend.

Danish brand HAY is known for their lively color combinations, and their doormats are no exception. With interlocking strands of navy and pink rope, bookended by charming red trim, this pick welcomes guests in with a jolt of maximalist energy. The vegetable fiber strands offer a strong and durable natural alternative to plastic: Shake it out and spray it down with water to clean it and keep the colors fresh.

A stylish coir braid gives this under-$30 doormat a higher-end look. The thick weave creates an almost padded texture, designed to expertly track dirt as your shoe treads catch on the material. With its lattice-like appearance, we envision this doormat at the front door of your Cotswolds-inspired farmhouse, or perhaps a coastal home that could use another natural texture to contrast the white and blue color palette.

Leave it to Sarah Sherman Samuel to create a great doormat. The AD100 designer, arguably most known for Mandy Moore’s Altadena home, offers a twist on the traditional rectangle. Uneven stripes with cutouts give your entryway a modern edge. The coir is dependable, and the black accents help to disguise the inevitable mess.

CB2’s doormat selection is predominantly composed of versatile Chilewich designs—like this monochromatic pattern that feels like a fashionable take on television static. Designed with functionality first, the shag mat uses antimicrobial yarn to keep itself tidy. It also has a no-slip backing to keep it fixed near your doorstep, even when your shoes need a serious wipe down.

What should you look for in the best outdoor doormats?

Although design is important, you also want to make sure that your doormat is low profile enough to fit under your door and also has a non-slip backing to keep your guests from busting it before they make it into your home. (Talk about a first impression!) Materials like PVC with rubber backing are key for the latter, while 100% coir doormats give your entryway a more natural look and are more ideal for trapping dirt and debris.

The answer is a tentative yes. You’re always going to want to see what the manufacturer recommends and it, of course, depends on where you live. Doormats are always made for outdoor use, but various climates will wear down the materials differently. Frequent rain and snow might soften jute while constant sunshine will fade most textiles. An easy solution, if you aren’t sure what your mat can withstand, is to drag your doormat into the mudroom or somewhere with a bit of shelter whenever there’s big weather on its way.

#4980. Enterprise blockchain is going to come back from the dead

Opinion by Paul Brody, founder and chief executive, Nightfall Networks

Do you remember Pets.com? It raised $110 million from Amazon and others to build a business where people ordered pet toys and dog food online.

The company crashed and burned, and everyone learned an important lesson: you can’t profitably ship a 50-pound bag of dog food to a customer’s door.

As online retail entrepreneur Ed Baum asked in a 2001 postmortem: “Did Pets.com’s business plan assume the invention of transporters to magically move bags of pet food without shipping costs?”

Please, whatever you do, don’t share this with the people at Chewy; they might not realize their business model doesn’t work. Chewy reported $12.6 billion in sales and $223 million in net income in its latest fiscal year.

The real lesson of Pets.com wasn’t that shipping dog food was a bad idea. It’s that the idea arrived before the logistics, customer habits and unit economics required to make it work.

As it was with chew toys and dog food, so it may be with enterprise blockchains.

Collectively, the crypto industry probably incinerated just as much money trying that between 2017 and 2023 as Pets.com. Maybe more. But the dozens of companies that went after enterprise blockchain were not delusional; they were just too early.

It’s not hard to understand the appeal of enterprise blockchain solutions, at least superficially. Nearly all enterprise systems today tend to fail at the boundaries between companies. There are two failure modes: mode 1, the OG failure mode, where collaboration information is constantly out of sync.

In this world, enterprise IT systems are islands, communicating asynchronously with each other by digital text messages and emailed spreadsheets. It’s cheap and easy to set up, if you don’t mind always having slightly wrong information.

Failure mode two is the costly, real-time proprietary integration. Powerful industry leaders politely request their key suppliers and partners to join their closed but always-on networks.

Chewy reported $223 million in net income in its latest fiscal year. Source: Chewy.

These are very costly, but they work well and leave everyone with a kind of business version of Stockholm syndrome, where you come to appreciate the thoroughness and efficiency of the digital choke-hold you are in.

Blockchains offered a better path forward: shared information and business logic across an open ecosystem. All the benefits of a proprietary system in terms of real-time visibility and efficiency, without the lock-in.

Sometime around 2017, this started to look like a very obvious good idea. Investment poured in, consortia launched, press releases were drafted and released, and then pretty much everything crashed and burned. TradeLens, We.Trade, Contour, B3i, ASX Chess, Everledger. All came and went in just a few years.

No company wants their competition to see what they’re doing in detail. Mostly, this meant building a private/permissioned chain and then doing lots of non-blockchain-like things to it and in it to try to keep information private. The results were uniformly slow and expensive and failed to gain traction.

It’s hard to understate the bloodbath of companies and startups that tried and failed to make enterprise blockchain work. A quick web and history search turned up 18 different ones, some of them well-known.

What few survivors of this are left have “de-chained” themselves — giving up the blockchain theater and reverting to a classic software-as-a-service (SaaS) model. Many companies that tried and tested these permissioned enterprise systems as users came away from their experience with a clear learning: we tried blockchain, it didn’t work.

That’s unfortunate because everything is different now.

Blockchains have scale. Ethereum in 2020 handled 1 million transactions a day and costs per transaction periodically spiked to $30-50 when demand was high. Today, most transactions get done under a penny and capacity is ramping toward billions of transactions a day. Nobody talks about the “trilemma” anymore. Ethereum is decentralized and it is scaled.

Related: Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade launches on Sepolia testnet

Finally, there’s the money. It’s all moving onchain. Regulators around the globe have blessed stablecoins and the result is a tsunami of liquidity, with transaction volume reaching almost $1.8 trillion in June.

If we combine the operational benefits of blockchains with the financial ones, we get something that enterprises have never really had before: a single ecosystem where you can not only buy, sell, and move assets, but get paid for them as well.

There was just under $1.8 trillion in adjusted stablecoin transaction volume in June. Source: Visa

The return on investment from every single component of this equation is big. Combined, they can yield colossal advantages. McKinsey partners even found that digital supply chain tools could make inventory reductions of 35-75% possible through better planning.

More conservatively, from my experience in the field, just switching away from the existing cascades of spreadsheets and electronic data interchange (EDI) messages can allow firms to run most supply chains with about 20% less inventory at the same service level. That’s worth billions in working capital in most industries.

It gets better from there. Smart contracts reduce the time required to handle many business contract rules from days to minutes and cut the costs of administration in half.

Microsoft began moving its Xbox video game network onto smart contracts for handling royalties and cut the time to access monthly royalty statements from 45 days to four minutes. All that while handling more than 2 million transactions a day.

And once you have inventories, contracts, purchase orders and invoices onchain, you can go after working capital directly as well.

When you can prove your purchase order originates from a top-quality buyer, you can access credit for less. And when you have full visibility into inventory and delivery status, you can factor receivables immediately and efficiently.

The possibilities and the returns are big. The use cases are obvious. It’s time to try again.

Magazine: What Democrats’ US midterm sweep means for the CLARITY Act

Cointelegraph is committed to providing independent, high-quality journalism across the crypto, blockchain, AI, and fintech industries.

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#4979. China’s $3.2 Billion AI Pipeline Still Calls for Nvidia Chips Washington Restricts

China’s $3.2 Billion AI Pipeline Still Calls for Nvidia Chips Washington Restricts

Chinese developers are planning and building billions of dollars worth of AI data centers around Nvidia processors restricted by Washington, according to a Reuters investigation published Friday.

After examining more than 150 procurement notices, investment plans and corporate records, the news agency identified projects worth over 23 billion yuan ($3.2 billion). Many specify Nvidia's H100, H200, or H800 chips, which provide the computing power used to train and run advanced AI models.

But $3.2 billion is the total value of the projects, including buildings, electricity and other infrastructure. The documents do not establish that all the processors were obtained, or that any export laws were broken.

One proposed computing center in Fujian province is valued at 6.2 billion yuan ($873 million). Its official investment listing specifies H100 servers but is still seeking financing partners.

In Shaanxi, a state-owned engineering group won a contract linked to a 3 billion yuan center naming H100 and H200 chips. A 2.26 billion yuan project in Jilin proposes combining Nvidia hardware with Chinese processors.

Inner Mongolia has another proposed development specifying 625 H200 servers. Meanwhile, authorities in Wuxi reported that an H800 computing cluster began operating in March 2025. Operating facilities and proposed investments are not evidence of the same level of chip access.

Nvidia's processors can carry out thousands of calculations at once, making them valuable for AI training. Its widely used software also makes switching to rival systems more complicated and potentially expensive.

The United States began restricting advanced AI chip sales to China in 2022, citing national security concerns. In January 2026, the Commerce Department moved H200 export applications to case-by-case review, although sales still require licenses. H100 shipments remain more tightly restricted.

That leaves Chinese developers with a dilemma: design facilities around familiar Nvidia equipment they may struggle to acquire, or invest in alternative processors that can require software changes and additional engineering.

An IDC report cited by Reuters estimated Nvidia accounted for 34% of China's AI accelerator shipments in the first half of 2026, compared with 28% for Huawei. Those figures cover the broader chip market, not confirmed shipments of restricted models.

Chinese AI developer Z.AI has already released an image-generation model trained entirely on Huawei chips, demonstrating that competitive systems need not depend on American hardware.

Still, the new project records show how deeply Nvidia remains embedded in Chinese AI infrastructure plans. They also add pressure on U.S. officials facing congressional scrutiny over how advanced chip export rules are enforced.

#4977. 8 Best Hotel Mattress Picks for a Five-Star Staycation (2026)

The hotel mattress probably isn't on your vacation itinerary, but it can make or break the trip. A lumpy, unsupportive bed can derail even the most carefully planned getaway. A really good one, though, might become an unexpected highlight—the kind that has you looking forward to bedtime and quietly checking the mattress label before checkout.

Recreating that experience at home doesn't require another plane ticket. Some of our favorite properties, including design-forward waterfront retreats and charming bed-and-breakfasts, put just as much thought into their mattresses as they do their interiors. Many turn to brands like Saatva, Ostermoor, and Casper to outfit their guest rooms; others, like the W Hotels, have signature mattresses you can purchase directly. We've rounded up our favorites, such as options with plush pillow tops, supportive coils, and pressure-relieving foam, to help you bring home the best part of your stay. The room service, unfortunately, is still up to you.

For consistency, all the prices reflect queen-size mattresses.

A stay at the Ritz-Carlton is typically reflected by its refined, elegant bedrooms. The property’s eponymous bed invites the same feeling. At 14 inches, it’s a higher-profile mattress that can help make the bed the centerpiece of the room. One of the standout qualities is its coil system, as it includes 900-plus individual springs for great edge support to help keep the sides from sagging as well as promote motion isolation—a necessary factor for couples who don’t want to sense each other’s every move.

Serta is one of the most well-known mattress manufacturers for high-end hotels, such as the Waldorf-Astoria. While many luxury properties feature custom beds from the namesake brand, it doesn’t mean you can’t shop at popular retailers such as Amazon. You’d never guess it, but this premium bed is priced under $500 (making it the most affordable on our list). While Serta offers memory foam and hybrid versions of its beds, this pillow-top innerspring mattress has been a favorite in our shopping writer Julia Harrison’s space, and she says it lives up to its “Five-Star” name.

It’s filled with three zones of coil support that keep her free of back and neck problems, and the quilted cover gives her something soft to dream about each night. “I’m really not one for pomp and frills in my mattress—I’m a firm believer that a good mattress has very little going on besides a somewhat plush top to keep you from feeling any sort of spring mechanism going on inside,” she says in her mattress review. “I instantly slept well on this mattress I think because it’s quite simply: a great mattress.” Bonus: It’s a mattress-in-a-box, so it can fit easily through narrow doors and hallways once it’s delivered, and inflate to its full size in less than a day’s time after unboxing, according to Harrison. Just in case you’re on the fence, it comes with a 100-day sleep trial so you can really see if it’s right for you before committing.

The Six Bells Countryside Inn opened its doors in the bucolic Hudson Valley in 2025, and it’s already earned two Michelin Keys. In each of the 11 carefully and whimsically designed bedrooms at this retreat, you’ll find handcrafted mattresses and bedding from New York-born brand Ostermoor. Dating back to 1853, Ostermoor’s mattresses have a storied history. The beds have innovated to foam and pocketed coil inner layers and designer covers in recent years, and we can prove they’re worth the splurge.

“From the moment I plopped down on the mattress, I was amazed by how comfortable and supportive it was,” shares contributor Zoë Sessums. “It strikes a perfect balance between spring and cushion—just the right amount of give without ever feeling too soft. I consistently wake up feeling rested and supported.” Firm without feeling rigid, this mattress looks as good as it feels, too. “The tufted top, the blue and white ticking pattern, the hand-stitched details—it feels like a piece of luxury furniture, not just a place to sleep,” Sessums adds, later writing, “I would recommend this to someone who values design and craftsmanship as much as comfort.”

Saatva has found its way into charming bed-and-breakfasts and boutique retreats across the country, including Pasadena's historic Bissell House (an 1887 Victorian filled with antique furnishings) and the Cornell Inn in the Berkshires (known for its colorful, individually designed guest rooms). You can bring that same inviting comfort home with the Saatva Classic, which comes with a plush three-inch Euro pillow top, a responsive dual-coil system, and targeted lumbar support. “It’s firm (which I prefer), regulates temperature well, and is extremely durable,” our tester Erin Kaplan, vice president of communications at Conde Nast, says. “I alternate between sleeping on my side and sleeping on my stomach, and this mattress is great for both positions.”

Choose from three firmness levels—plush soft, luxury firm, or firm—, eight different sizes, and two mattress heights to find your ideal fit. Feeling extra? Saatva also offers two adjustable bases that elevate your head and feet at the touch of a button, including a premium Plus model for an even more indulgent breakfast in bed. A 365-night trial gives you plenty of time to sleep on your decision before committing.

At 1 Hotel Brooklyn Bridge, even the beds have a view. Sweeping views of the Manhattan skyline set the scene, with reclaimed wood floors, rich leather headboards, and locally crafted details like crate-inspired closets and Fresnel glass light fixtures bringing warmth and character to the rooms. With custom Keetsa mattresses in every room, you might find yourself hitting snooze despite the spectacular view.

Back at home, the Keetsa Pillow Plus offers its own take on hotel-worthy comfort. It's something of a Goldilocks mattress: not too soft, not too firm, but just right. A three-inch pillow top combines body-contouring memory foam with responsive comfort foam to help cushion pressure points, and a soft, moisture-wicking cover designed to keep overheating at bay. Individually wrapped coils provide cushioning and support while minimizing motion transfer, so even if your bedmate tosses and turns, you can stay blissfully unaware.

When husband-and-wife duo Ely and Danielle Franko transformed a dated Catskills ski hostel into The Henson, a 16-room boutique hotel, they had a pretty good idea of what makes a great night's sleep. The couple painstakingly restored the property themselves, filling its rooms with vintage furniture, locally sourced finds, and hand-applied wallpaper. But when it came to mattresses, they didn't have to look far for inspiration: They chose the same Casper Dream Hybrid they sleep on at home. In fact, they loved it so much that they insisted on king-size mattresses in every room, even the smallest ones. The hybrid design combines responsive springs with breathable foam and three zones of support that help keep your spine aligned. Its medium feel is meant to offer a little give without leaving you sinking into the mattress, making it a crowd-pleasing choice for your own guest room. After all, the best hosts know a good night's sleep is part of the hospitality.

Hot sleepers, here’s a mattress for you. The Four Season’s signature mattress features a couple of core cooling bed qualities: proprietary cooling gel foam layers with channeled designs that promote airflow throughout the mattress, as well as a patented DualCool top layer that draws heat and moisture away from the body and keeps the bed fresh with its antimicrobial properties. The cooling mattress is designed for sleepers who need extra structure on their bed. This posh number has targeted support for the lower back area and zeroes in on alignment regardless of your sleep style.

The W Hotels are widely popular because of the bold design across the properties worldwide. To mirror this aesthetic, you can cover the brand’s mattress with colorful bedding to let your personal style shine. When it comes to the bed itself, it is defined by a plush top quilted damask design to ease side sleepers into a peaceful slumber. It’s made for people who need extra support too, though, including an individually-wrapped coil system, five-zone support system, and comfort layers for added cushioning and pressure relief.

Which Kind of Mattress Do Five-Star Hotels Use?

Most luxury hotels use hybrid mattresses in their suites to strike the right balance of softness and support, thanks to pocketed coils and layers of foam. These are also typically medium-firm mattresses since a plush mattress can be uncomfortable for some sleep positions like stomach sleepers. Ultra-firm beds are often too stiff for side sleeping and may not align with guests' expectations who are looking for soft mattresses to sink into on their vacations.

If you want to refresh your mattress without the hefty price tag of buying a new one, a mattress topper or featherbed could be a solid transitional pick. The Ritz-Carlton and W Hotels promote one made with white duck feathers, Mandarin Oriental’s exclusive topper comprises white goose feathers, Waldorf Astoria beds feature a down-alternative fill, and Marriott’s selection is polyester.

There’s no one-size-fits-all here, so it really comes down to your preferred loftiness and weight. Duck and goose feathers are heavier than others fills, though duck is slightly less fluffy and won’t trap heat as well. Down-alternatives, like polyester, are more allergen-friendly and “a good option for those who prefer to be vegan in their home,” Danielle Trotter, the former textiles technical designer at West Elm, previously shared.

What Is the Thickness of a Hotel Mattress?

Hotel mattresses usually average 12 inches in thickness. This makes it easier to hop in and out of bed, especially when using a box spring that can add extra height. Plus, it prevents the need for deep pocket sheets.

How Can I Recreate the Hotel Bed Experience at Home?

We’ve explored the entire topic on how to make your bedroom feel like a hotel, and aside from finding a new bed with a medium-firm feel, we’ve learned that percale sheets and down pillows are pretty much a must to complete the setup. When it comes to the former, hotel sheets are usually made with organic cotton percale because the fabric looks and feels crisp, giving that refined aesthetic a boutique stay is known for. Usually a 200 thread count is a good rule of thumb to look out for since this means there’s plenty of airflow between the threads for a breathable feel. Hotel pillows are usually filled with down feathers since they’re so lofty and soft, but a down alternative is just as good in case you’re an allergy-prone sleeper (just look for materials like hypoallergenic gel fibers since they’re known for being squishy without falling flat, just like a feather-filled pillow).

What Is the Difference Between Memory Foam and Latex?

Traditional memory foam has a body-contouring effect that allows your body to sink into your mattress, reducing motion transfer (a must for bedmates!). Meanwhile, latex foam offers more responsiveness (or bounce) and overall support. As Neslihan Ozdemir, founder of Girl on the Mattress, previously told AD, latex also holds up well over time and is temperature-regulating. Latex foam mattresses (and some memory foam options) are certified organic, making them eco-friendly foam picks, too.

Known as the most common mattress type, innerspring mattresses feature a bed of coils. There’s quite a range to these mattresses: Basic versions have a grid of steel coils, while premier options have a pillow top and pocketed coil technology, meaning each coil is individually wrapped to help with motion isolation. Ozdemir added that innerspring is best for people who sleep on the edge of the bed or are heavier in weight. If you prefer a more plush mattress, but still want the support of those coils, consider a hybrid mattress, which is layered with soft foam.

#4971. US sellers drive Bitcoin crash, Jensen plots Nvidia comeback: Hodler’s Digest

Anchorage Digital data suggests the Bitcoin selloff is being driven by US investors. A “war room” has been set up by supporters to reinstate Nvidia boss Jensen Huang.

Bitcoin bottomed after the US midterm elections in 2018 and 2022 — and the great Bitcoin crash of November 2026 looks like history repeating. Bitcoin plunged more than $10,000 this week, and is currently trading at $65,116 for a 12.4% loss.

According to Anchorage Digital researcher David Lawant, the call is coming from inside the house. “BTC sell pressure continues to come primarily from the U.S,” he noted, positing a chart highlighting showing continued heavy net taker sell flow primarily on Coinbase compared with overseas exchanges.

The US spot Bitcoin ETFs have seen heavy outflows, losing $2.35 billion over the past two weeks, and influencer Axel Bitblaze noted that Bitcoin fell in the hour before the US open on all but two of the past fifteen days.

Binance founder Changpeng Zhao rebuffed the idea that Binance is behind the crash. “No, Binance did not sell crypto. I bought crypto in the past few days,” he said. He attributed the crash to money shifting to the Anthropic IPO, the Bitcoin ETF outflows, security fears about crypto in general in the wake of the Binance hack, and hot inflation data raising expectations of interest rate hikes. Markets are now pricing in a quarter point Fed hike in December.

Bitcoin fell to a four month low near $62,000 on Sunday but has since rebounded above $65,000. The Crypto Fear and Greed Index fell to 10, which is Extreme Fear.

History provides some good news in that Bitcoin more then doubled in price within 12 months of the 2018 and 2022 midterm elections.

The Financial Times reports that Jensen Huang is plotting a comeback to the top job at Nvidia with allies, following a determined push in Silicon Valley to pressure the board to reinstate him.

Nvidia shares plunged after Huang resigned as CEO and from the board on Nov. 12, with Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress named as interim CEO. The resignation came shortly after news broke he’d had two other affairs, including one with a subordinate.

Multiple people confirmed Huang has been discussing the idea of returning with confidants, even though an independent probe by the board is examining allegations he was romantically involved with a Chinese spy who helped ferry chips to China.

The FT reported one ally saying that supporters have put together a “war room” to reinstate him. “Everybody that matters in tech outside the frontier labs loves Jensen,” the source said.

A petition to reinstate Huang has garnered more than 100,000 signatures, including 5,600 current Nvidia employees, along with SpaceX boss Elon Musk and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney.

The turmoil at Nvidia has weighed heavily on AI stocks and risk assets like cryptocurrency and the story still has a long way to run, with Congressional moves to investigate the issue and three bills being advanced to tighten AI chip export restrictions to China. According to the New York Time, intelligence officials reportedly briefed President Trump about the allegations Alice Huang was a Chinese spy a year ago, but he did not take any action.

Ethereum research group Ethlabs is withdrawing its Quick Slots proposal from the forthcoming Hegotá upgrade, and has urged core developers to urgently prepare the network for quantum attacks by 2027.

On Wednesday, the group outlined “pqai27,” a proposed roadmap that aims to protect Ethereum against threats from quantum computing and artificial intelligence.

At Devcon, researcher Justin Drake said he hopes to complete the transition in 2028 while the published roadmap still targets December 2029.

Index provider MSCI has confirmed Bitcoin treasury company Strategy will be removed from its global equity indexes, following months of debate over whether crypto treasury firms qualify as operating businesses. Japan’s Metaplanet and crypto treasury company Upexi are also marked for deletion, while Ether treasury firm Sharplink is on a watchlist. The changes take effect after market close on Nov. 30.

“Index inclusion meant ordinary passive investors, like someone holding a global index fund in their pension, were getting leveraged Bitcoin exposure they never chose. MSCI just fixed that,” Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, told Cointelegraph. Strategy fell 6.49% on Thursday and are down by more than one quarter over the past month.

US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) recorded $2.35 billion in net outflows over two consecutive weeks, as redemptions reached more than $1 billion each week.

According to Farside Investors data, spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $1.07 billion between from Monday to Friday, following $1.28 billion in outflows the previous week, the largest weekly redemptions since late June.

Friday marked the seventh consecutive trading session of net withdrawals, with about $1.71 billion redeemed since Nov. 5

At the end of the week, Bitcoin (BTC) is down 12.4% to trade at $65,116, Ethereum (ETH) is down 14.4% to trade at $1,822 and XRP (XRP) is down 13.3% to $0.96. The total market cap is at $2.2 trillion according to CoinMarketCap.

The top three altcoin losers of the week are Zcash (ZEC) which was down 25.4%, Uniswap (UNI) down 23.2% and Aero (AERO) down 23.1%.

Bitcoin Opportunity Fund co-founder Mark Moss says that with banks and ETFs widening access to Bitcoin he expects a new record high in 2027. Wall Street’s embrace of Bitcoin has created the “best buying opportunity ever,” he said.

Microsoft said that the outage stemmed from a problem introduced during an earlier network capacity upgrade that emerged after a recent network change. “This was a technical issue not related to cyber or AI/agents,” the company said.

MEGA, the organization behind Ethereum layer-2 network MegaETH, has reduced its workforce by 20% following months of restructuring as it expands into artificial intelligence products.

A 19.17 billion yen ($119.8 million) loss on the Bitcoin sale and an 8.04 billion yen ($50.3 million) loss from a decline in the value of its Bitcoin holdings outweighed gains in the operating business.

#4970. Should Government Officials Be Banned From Prediction Markets? House Oversight Says Maybe

Should Government Officials Be Banned From Prediction Markets? House Oversight Says Maybe

The chairman of the House Oversight Committee is raising the prospect of barring government officials from trading on prediction markets, citing concerns that insiders are using nonpublic information to profit on platforms like Kalshi and Polymarket.

In a staff memorandum released Friday, the committee detailed an investigation into insider trading by government officials, military personnel and people holding security clearances who have placed bets using information not available to the public.

The probe gathered nearly 1,700 pages of documents and included five bipartisan staff briefings from prediction market platforms about how they detect and report suspicious activity.

Chairman James Comer framed the issue around public trust. “Americans are rightly concerned about public corruption,” he said, arguing Congress “has a duty to ensure our laws adequately prevent insider trading” and prohibit officials “from profiting from nonpublic information.”

He said lawmakers must weigh “additional reforms to prevent public corruption and restore the American people’s trust in government.”

The memo’s central recommendation is that Congress consider new restrictions on federal personnel, ranging from an outright ban on trading prediction markets to narrower limits on specific contracts, such as those tied to government decisions.

Applying such rules across all branches, the committee argued, would reinforce that government employees shouldn’t financially benefit from access to nonpublic information, and would remove even the appearance of it.

The memo stressed that existing insider trading laws already apply to prediction markets. It cited the Commodity Exchange Act, as amended by the STOCK Act, which bars federal employees, members of Congress and others from trading commodity futures, options, or swaps on nonpublic information drawn from their positions.

The committee credited the platforms themselves for policing their markets, noting that most reported insider trading incidents were first detected and flagged to regulators by the platforms. It singled out Polymarket, which recently hired a former law enforcement expert and rolled out surveillance software called “PolyGraph” built with analytics firm Chainalysis, and Kalshi for strengthening their detection protocols.

The scrutiny follows a string of cases involving alleged insider trading by public officials and government employees.

A U.S. soldier was charged in April over alleged Polymarket trades tied to a military operation that resulted in the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolas Maduro. Then, in August, the CFTC fined a former White House teleprompter operator for betting on presidential speeches.

The push also lands amid broader pressure on the sector, with the CFTC moving to assert authority over prediction markets in its fight against several states, while Sen. Elizabeth Warren sets her sights on the industry as the incoming Senate Banking chair.

#4967. Joseph Dirand Joins Forces with Peter Brant and Stephanie Seymour Brant to Reimagine a Historic 1920s Palm Beach Mansion

It began with a chance encounter and a phone call. The French architect Joseph Dirand was walking into a restaurant in New York when he ran into an old friend, the art dealer Tico Mugrabi. Dirand mentioned that he was flying to Palm Beach the next day and his friend lit up. Mugrabi pulled out his phone and made a call. “Peter, my friend Joseph is coming to Palm Beach tomorrow,” Dirand recalls him saying. “You have to meet this guy.” The following day Dirand was sitting across from Peter Brant, the fabled industrialist and publisher, and his wife, Stephanie Seymour Brant, one of the defining supermodels of her generation, in their new home. The Brants had owned the house for only a few days, and it marked the opening of a new chapter for the couple.

The Brants in front of Warhol’s White Disaster (White Car Crash 19 Times), 1963, in the great room. Jean Prouvé coffee table; sofa and chairs by Pierre Jeanneret.

Marc Newson’s aluminum Lockheed Lounge, a Tower Cabinet by Ettore Sottsass, and a Trapeze table by Jean Prouvé all stand in the loggia.

A Végétale mirror by Claude Lalanne reflects Untitled (Richard Prince and Cindy Sherman), 1980, and a 1930s settee by Jean-Michel Frank.

A pair of bespoke Philippe Anthonioz floor lamps Flank Jonathan Horowitz’s Self-portrait in “Mirror #1” (Kevin), 2012.

Brown Jordan chaise longues under umbrellas by Business & Pleasure Co. stand poolside.

There is something quintessentially American in how quickly it all unfolded, and for Dirand, it meant meeting a man he had long admired. As an inquisitive young designer he had pored over photographs of Brant’s earlier homes, and one image had stayed with him: It showed a Jean Prouvé desk beneath a Jean-Michel Basquiat painting, captured at a time when almost no one thought to set French postwar design against contemporary art. “For me, Peter was a kind of collector’s hero,” says Dirand.

Brant’s eye for art and design was formed early. His father loved classical art and considered even Picasso “way too modern,” but on family ski trips to Switzerland the teenage Brant got to know the Zurich dealer Bruno Bischofberger, who subsequently introduced him to Leo Castelli, and the two titans of the art world became the young Brant’s mentors. Brant then bought his first Andy Warhol painting in 1966, and by the time Warhol’s business manager, Fred Hughes, introduced them in November 1968, he already owned about seven of them. Warhol and Brant would go on to become close friends for the remainder of the artist’s life.

In a corner of the Great Room, Roy Lichtenstein’s The Refrigerator, 1962, faces a 1960 copper hanging cabinet by Paul Evans. a 1934 plaster vase by Alberto Giacometti sits on A Joseph Dirand Edition Iseo Gold coffee table. 1950s Hirondelles wall light by Jean Royère; pair of 1958 Diego Giacometti armchairs.

Warhol’s 1975 portrait of Murray Brant (Brant’s father) commands a wall of the paneled study. Direction armchair and Présidence desk by Jean Prouvé; Ettore Sottsass hanging lamp.

Artworks in the entrance hall include, from left, a Massangis stone lion-head fountain feature by Harumi Klossowska de Rola, 2022; Andy Warhol’s Self Portrait (Green), 1964; a Klossowska de Rola wolf sculpture; and Richard Prince’s Nurses’ Dormitory, 2002. The sanded Ceppo di Gré stone modular console is by Dirand.

In the primary bedroom, George Condo’s 2004 portrait of Stephanie Seymour Brant hangs above a 1946 Charlotte Perriand sideboard on which stands an enameled earthenware lamp by Roger Capron. A Jean Prouvé Bridge armchair joins an Ours Polaire chair and sofa by Jean Royère around Prouvé’s Tripod coffee table.

An exterior view of the 1926 Addison Mizner residence

Few people then saw Warhol’s work the way Brant did. When he hung his first Marilyn at home, visitors were shocked: “People would come to my house and think, This is the worst thing I’ve ever seen. In the 1960s, he says, you could count the serious collectors of Pop art “on three hands.”

His rule, he says, is to collect “something that was in style that’s out of style,” though never as speculation. Between their holdings and those of the Brant Foundation, which he established in 1996 to promote the education and appreciation of contemporary art and design, the collection now numbers more than 3,000 works, and he has never bought for a particular wall. What he misses in many newer collections is any sense of the person behind them. “You can see right away that this guy had a great adviser,” he says, “but there’s no madness in the collection. Every collection should have some madness.”

Carlo Mollino’s Tipo B side chair rests beneath Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Per Capita, 1981.

A painting by Glenn Ligon and a stool by Shiro Kuramata in Brant’s bath

Seymour Brant and Brant in the great room

Brant has shared much of his collecting journey with Seymour Brant, whom he married in 1995. She has built a collection of her own: Her passion is clothing rather than paintings, and she has pursued it with the same scholarship and the same sharp eye as her husband. She was a close friend of the legendary couturier Azzedine Alaïa and has one of the most significant holdings of his work, alongside historic couture that Dirand describes as extraordinary. Where Brant is the storyteller, Seymour Brant is the quieter presence, though Dirand is clear that she was integral to every decision. Her collecting has even changed the way Brant sees his own. “It’s not a dress in a closet,” he says. “It’s a piece of art, and it mixes with art.”

Brant has spent some 50 years in the Palm Beach area, first as a polo player. He and Seymour Brant had no need for a new home there—they loved the one they had as their children grew up. But when Collado Hueco, a home by Addison Mizner, the architect who defined 1920s Palm Beach, came on the market in 2020, the collector in him took over. “It was a piece of history,” Dirand says. “For him, there was no choice.” Designed in 1924 for the American businessman Paul Moore, it is one of Mizner’s boldest and most unusual houses.

In a hall, a 1974 Diego Giacometti bench sits in front of Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Untitled (Sugar Ray Robinson), 1982. The curtain rods are by Julian Schnabel.

Warhol’s The Kiss (Bela Lugosi), 1964, and a 1947 Alexandre Noll mahogany armchair in a corner of the primary bedroom. Philippe Anthonioz hanging light; Jean Prouvé tripod coffee table; Jean Royère Ours Polaire sofa.

John Currin’s Constance Towers, 2009, presides over Seymour Brant’s dressing room. Furniture includes an Ambassador chair and Éléphanteau sofa, both by Jean Royère.

In the great room, a plaster bas-relief, Support, and a carved-stone console by Alberto Giacometti are flanked by a pair of Éléphanteau chairs by Jean Royère. In the foreground is a Fondation Maeght chair by Diego Giacometti.

The house presented Dirand with a real puzzle. Asymmetrical, it spreads across multiple wings, varying from one to three levels. The main entertaining rooms had retained their original character, while the entrance and stair hall were dark and enclosed. Dirand set out to let the sunlight that moves across the house reach every part of the interior as much as possible. The collection shaped Dirand’s approach too. With so many treasured works coming into the house, the team wanted to ensure they would be protected against anything the unpredictable Florida weather could bring. What began as a renovation became, in Dirand’s words, “a reconstruction saving the historical.” Rather than demolishing and starting again, the team kept the historic walls standing, reinforced every one of them, and dug beneath to take the foundations deeper. New hurricane-proof windows were set into concrete frames, the roof was removed and rebuilt, and the original tiles and the patina of the plaster fireplace were carefully preserved. The project took about four years. Throughout, Dirand immersed himself in Mizner’s doors, ceilings, and patterns so that the new rooms would be a true homage rather than a copy.

Early on, Brant handed Dirand two thick binders, one of artworks and one of design pieces. “This is what you can use,” he said. A few days later, on holiday with his family, Dirand spread every page across the floors. “I was walking on the paper,” he says. It reversed the usual order in which the architect designs the rooms and the art arrives afterward. Here the collection came first, though never rigidly, because “a collection is also something that is alive.”

A Diego Giacometti bench and A Jean Royère Liane six-branch floor lamp in a hall

A chair and floor lamp by Diego Giacometti stand next to the fireplace in the great room.

Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Per Capita, 1981, commands a wall of the loggia. Ours Polaire chair and Yo-Yo side table, both by Royère.

A family photo and fragrance bottles line up on a windowsill in Seymour Brant’s bath.

Warhol’s Big Campbell’s Soup Can with Can Opener (Vegetable), 1962, hangs in the stairwell, which also features a Jacques Adnet wall light.

At the heart of the home are two main spaces, the great room and the loggia, which are perhaps the spaces that both Dirand and Brant are most proud of. The great room is centered around a monumental fireplace that has the grandeur of a castle. Commanding one wall is a rare carved-stone console by Alberto Giacometti, nearly three meters high and crowned with a bas-relief of a female figure, made for Jean-Michel Frank’s important 1938 commission for the Born family in Argentina. It was one of a pair, and Dirand says no one knows where the other is today. Beside the fireplace hangs Warhol’s 1963 White Disaster (White Car Crash 19 Times), one of Brant’s most recent and important acquisitions; he had wanted a Car Crash painting since the ’60s. Prouvé furniture from Brazzaville, weathered by the Congo climate, sits amid Native American and African objects, and at the center of it all is a mirror polish copper table Dirand designed himself to reflect Giacometti’s bird vase and the fireplace behind it. Brant calls the table a masterpiece.

Art in the main entrance includes, from left, pieces by Jean-Michel Basquiat, Richard Prince and Cindy Sherman, and Brice Marden. Furniture includes an Ours Polaire Chair and sofa and a kidney-shaped coffee table, all by Jean Royère, and a Charlotte Perriand stool. a 1936 Jean-Michel Frank settee stands in the background.

A glimpse into the primary bedroom, where a Jean-Michel Frank table lamp sits atop a bedside table.

A wall niche holds a 2023 ibis sculpture by Harumi Klossowska de Rola

The loggia is lighter and more playful, and its original black, yellow, and turquoise tiled floor and Egyptian-style column capitals offer a glimpse of Mizner at his most adventurous. One of the most prominent works hanging in the room is a monumental 1981 Basquiat painting, Per Capita, which Brant bought from Annina Nosei’s memorial show for the artist. At the center of the room stands an Ettore Sottsass Tower Cabinet, designed in the early 1960s, which Dirand admits he didn’t understand at first, until Brant showed him exactly how to place it. Now he can’t imagine the room without it.

Upstairs, Dirand took down walls so that the primary bedroom runs the full width of the house, capturing light from sunrise over the ocean to sunset over the garden. In Seymour Brant’s bathroom, Estremoz marble walls are carved to evoke curtains, and beyond it lies her dressing room, an intimate boudoir. Brant’s bathroom, by contrast, is a marquetry of white and gray marble built around a pair of Glenn Ligon paintings. Dirand calls the room “minimalist ornamental.”

A 2023 flamingo sculpture by Harumi Klossowska de Rola (one of a pair) stands on the terrace.

In Seymour Brant’s bath, François-Xavier Lalanne’s Singe II, 1999, sits between a stool by Diego Giacometti and a bespoke bench by Philippe Anthonioz.

Every piece in the house comes with a story, and Dirand heard many of them over years of breakfasts, lunches, and dinners with the couple. “Every single piece could be a book,” he says. During those conversations, the collaboration between Brant and Dirand grew into a close friendship. Brant’s other great passion is horse racing, and his formidable stable has included Sottsass, the 2020 winner of the Prix de l’Arc de Triomphe, Europe’s most prestigious race. One day, Brant called Dirand with news: Sottsass had a son, a young thoroughbred already winning races of his own. He had named him Dirand.

#4966. Jensen Huang weighs Nvidia comeback days after shock exit: FT

Silicon Valley executives are reportedly organizing a campaign to bring Huang back and are looking to gain White House support.

Jensen Huang is reportedly considering a return as Nvidia CEO just days after resigning from the company following reports of affairs with a suspected Chinese spy and a subordinate.

Huang has discussed with confidants the possibility of returning to Nvidia, according to the Financial Times, citing multiple people familiar with the matter. One source told the FT they were putting together a “war room” of Silicon Valley executives to lobby for Nvidia’s chief’s return, and are looking to gain support from the White House.

“Everyone that matters in tech outside the frontier labs loves Jensen,” they said.

Meanwhile, a public campaign called “Bring Jensen Back” has attracted more than 161,000 signatures, according to its organizers, who list Elon Musk and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney among its supporters. The campaign website also claims to have over 9,000 signatures from current and former Nvidia employees.

The push for his return goes against calls for new leadership at the AI chipmaker and coincides with an independent board investigation and scrutiny of its chip sales to China.

Related: Former Nvidia CEO Huang had third alleged affair: Reuters

US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent on Sunday called for a “dramatic shake-up” of Nvidia’s leadership, urging the company to hire a CEO from outside the company, while US President Donald Trump suggested Thursday that former Apple CEO Tim Cook take the job.

Nvidia announced Huang’s resignation following reports of alleged extramarital relationships, including one involving a woman whom US intelligence officials reportedly suspected of having ties to Chinese intelligence.

The controversy has also raised questions about Nvidia’s compliance with US restrictions on AI chip sales, particularly involving customers in Asia with potential ties to China.

Nvidia shares fell as much as 14% on Thursday before recovering to close 4.3% lower, underscoring investor uncertainty over the AI chipmaker’s future leadership.

Cointelegraph reached out to Nvidia for comment.
