#4965. Could a Former Nvidia Executive Replace Jensen Huang? Arm’s Rene Haas Emerges as Candidate

Could a Former Nvidia Executive Replace Jensen Huang? Arm’s Rene Haas Emerges as Candidate

Nvidia's board has approached Arm CEO Rene Haas as a potential successor to Jensen Huang, according to the Financial Times, putting a former Nvidia executive in contention for one of technology's biggest jobs.

Haas is one of several people under consideration, people familiar with the matter told the FT. There is no guarantee he will receive an offer or accept one. The FT said he maintains strong ties to Huang and some Nvidia directors.

Nvidia's board told Reuters it was still selecting an executive search firm and had not formally engaged any candidate. “Any speculation regarding individual candidates, whether internal or external, is premature,” it said.

Huang resigned Thursday after 33 years leading the company he co-founded, following reports of extramarital relationships, including one with a business executive suspected by U.S. officials of having Chinese intelligence ties. The company said it had no reason to believe its secrets were compromised.

Chief financial officer Colette Kress is now interim CEO, and longtime director Harvey Jones has become board chair. Analysts have also floated Nvidia data-center executive Ian Buck and AMD chief Lisa Su as possible successors, though those names are not confirmed appointments or offers.

An internal Nvidia suggestion-box post has attracted more than 1,600 comments, many supportive of Huang's reinstatement, according to Wired. “If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss,” one anonymous employee wrote.

Other staff members have demanded transparency around his departure. The campaign shows Huang still has supporters inside Nvidia; it does not mean the board has opened talks to reinstate him.

Haas, 64, is a Silicon Valley-based semiconductor executive whose career spans engineering, product development and sales. He earned an electrical engineering degree from Clarkson University and worked at chip companies Tensilica and Scintera Networks before joining Nvidia in 2006.

He spent seven years at Nvidia as vice president and general manager of its computing products business, then moved to Arm in 2013. At Arm, he rose from strategic alliances to chief commercial officer in 2015 and head of its intellectual-property products group in 2017, before becoming CEO in February 2022.

That appointment came as Nvidia's proposed $40 billion takeover of Arm collapsed under pressure from regulators. Haas was suddenly running a business his former employer had tried, unsuccessfully, to buy.

He steered Arm back onto the Nasdaq in September 2023 at an initial valuation of about $54.5 billion, one of the year's largest stock-market debuts. The IPO also gave Japanese owner SoftBank a way to sell shares while retaining control.

Unlike Nvidia, which primarily sells graphics processors and related computing systems, Arm licenses chip designs and computing architecture to companies including Apple, Amazon and Qualcomm, earning fees and royalties when customers use its technology. Arm-based designs are now increasingly found in cloud servers as well as phones.

Arm reported $1.44 billion in revenue for the quarter ended September 30, up 27% from a year earlier. Royalties from data-center chips doubled, while Arm said initial commercial deliveries of its new CPU remain on track for early 2027.

Haas also sits on SoftBank's board and, since April, has served as CEO of SoftBank Group International, helping oversee its semiconductor and AI interests. A move to Nvidia would require untangling those ties, particularly because the companies are partners whose interests increasingly overlap in data-center hardware.

For Nvidia, the next leader would inherit not just its lead in AI chips, but delicate relationships with governments and customers as Washington debates tighter export restrictions on China. Nvidia is scheduled to report earnings on November 17, with Kress at the helm and its permanent CEO search still underway.

#4963. Luxor CEO warns of AI ‘dark forest’ following Bitcoin miner hijack

Attackers redirected customers’ Bitcoin mining power through Luxor’s Commander platform over the weekend, prompting its CEO to issue a warning to crypto businesses.

Bitcoin mining services provider Luxor has warned that AI-enabled cyberattacks are becoming increasingly difficult to defend against after attackers compromised its mining-management platform and redirected customers’ computing power to another mining pool over the weekend.

In an X post on Sunday, Luxor CEO Nick Hansen said the attack came through a “sophisticated AI-enabled vector,” without specifying how AI was used in the attack, warning that these types of attacks will only get more frequent over time.

“At this moment, anyone running any sort of substantial business, especially in crypto, is inundated with AI-enabled attacks,” Hansen said in an X post. “This does not seem to be slowing down; rather, it’s speeding up as models get better and more attackers enter the arena.”

The warning comes as security researchers have documented the spread of AI-assisted hacking tools, raising the prospect of faster attacks against businesses holding money or controlling valuable infrastructure.

“Abliterated models are running 24/7 against your entire surface, looking for a little crack, which they then use to (almost instantly) expand,” added Hansen.

Hansen said Luxor has had to put the majority of its feature work on hold in order to harden its cybersecurity. The company has also acquired its own GPUs to run unrestricted open-weight AI models aimed at improving its security posture.

“I urge anyone running a similar service to do the same.  It’s your only defense in this new AI-powered dark forest. ‘The only thing that stops a bad guy with a gun is a good guy with a gun.’”

Initial reports suggested that about 64,000 mining machines representing 13 exahashes per second were redirected to the OCEAN mining pool on Saturday.

Luxor has not confirmed either figure, saying the incident affected just over a dozen customer accounts using its Commander mining-management tool.

Luxor said it patched Commander within two hours of detecting the unauthorized changes and began restoring affected machines to their original pool settings. It pledged to compensate customers in full for the redirected mining power and publish a post-mortem.

Cointelegraph contacted Luxor for comment.

Related: Coinone warns of phishing, identity theft after customer data breach

“We have explored the issue extensively, and it was isolated to the Commander tool. None of Luxor’s other services (including Luxor Pool and LuxOS) were affected,” said Luxor.

Initial reports suggested the attackers received no mining rewards. However, a Bitcoin address identified by CoreX as associated with the incident subsequently received two OCEAN payouts totaling approximately 0.162 BTC, worth about $10,500.

Hansen’s warning follows a string of reported AI-assisted intrusions targeting crypto businesses.

Earlier this month, India’s cybersecurity agency warned that a group called Crafty Falcon was using AI-powered tools to attack financial companies, including cryptocurrency exchanges, stealing wallet keys and siphoning funds.

Blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis disclosed in October that an attacker used what it called “offensive agentic techniques” to access its systems. The breach exposed password hashes, live tokens and other customer information.

Anthropic said in a September report that multiple groups had developed “automated exploit foundries” with AI, accelerating the pace of vulnerability research, testing, and exploit design.

#4962. How One Creative Couple Found Harmony in Their 1920s Los Angeles Craftsman

Nothing about where interior designer Harrison Soll is now was part of the plan. Five years ago, he was focused on his work, and he didn’t want any distractions. But when he met Rodney Burns, a stylist and owner of the legendary Los Angeles vintage gallery, Church Boutique, it set everything on a new path. They met in the fall; by New Year’s they were a couple. “I didn’t think it was going to be a relationship,” Soll says. He credits their shared interests for bringing them together: “We always found a throughline with design.”

They now live in a 1925 craftsman house in Laurel Canyon. Soll took the lead decorating the nearly 1,400-square-foot home in a combination of his and Burns’s styles with many pieces coming from Burns’s eclectic collection of vintage art, furniture, and sculpture. Burns has worked with Lenny Kravitz as a stylist and creative collaborator for the past 15 years, and the singer says he admires what Burns and Soll have created together. “The mix of Rodney’s early- to midcentury style mixed with ethnic expression next to Harrison’s bold minimalist modern approach seamlessly blend together,” Kravitz tells AD.

The backyard of Soll’s and Burns’s Laurel Canyon home is a bricolage of funky, inherited stonework and vintage pottery.

The nail art above the fireplace has a cute backstory story: Burns and Soll had seen it at a vintage shop in Santa Monica and Burns regretted not buying it. When he went back to purchase the piece, it was marked as sold. But only because Soll had already covertly bought it as a Valentine’s Day gift for Burns.

The giant Sergio Bustamante giraffe bust in the foyer is a delicate topic. “The giraffe was one of our biggest fights in decorating this house,” Burns explains. “I was determined to have that giraffe live some place in this house because he’s just fantastic, and the scale of him, and the rarity of him.” Except, counters Soll, that the placement of the giraffe is treacherous because the front door swings right in its path. “You just have to be careful,” Soll says.

The foyer—which features a collection of masks and vintage paintings—was one of the most difficult areas to style, explains Burns. The couple was trying to be selective of what pieces to incorporate from Burns’s massive collection and at one point Burns suggested putting all of his masks in storage to save for another house down the line, or passing them onto Burns’s vintage boutique, Church. Soll dissented, saying, “No, let’s put them everywhere.”

The living room features two sofas—a Joseph D’Urso for Knoll piece reupholstered in leopard fabric and a leather De Sede Terrazza. The carpet is the Antilocarpa style from Stark. A trio of fiberglass shell art was sourced through Kelly Wearstler. The art behind the sofa is a manipulated canvas garment bag by the artist Roosevelt “Rip” Woods, the first black artist to have a solo show at the Phoenix Art Museum.

As renters, there were naturally limitations on what could be achieved. Soll got permission to retile a bathroom and install a new vanity. The doe-patterned wall-to-wall carpeting in the living room was an “ask for forgiveness” move, but his landladies ended up loving it. Burns, on the other hand, needed more convincing. “The carpet took many dinners of conversations between the two of us,” Soll says.

Soll describes himself as more of a minimalist while Burns avows, “I’m a maximalist to the umpteenth power.” The couple attempted to strike a balance while still filling the space with character. Some rooms nod to Soll’s leanings toward minimalism like an office with a midcentury desk and a 1970s tapestry and the dining room where a Sergio Bustamante table and a Chinese Art Deco rug are the main attractions.

When decorating their Laurel Canyon home, Soll and Burns had to figure out how to combine their opposing styles, with Soll being more of a minimalist and Burns being a maximalist. But, says Burns, “[Soll] has made me be able to take a pause and then also make me a little less attached to some of the things that I’ve collected  and given me an opportunity to maybe show a little bit more restraint.” In the corner, a brass Maison Jansen palm tree mingles with a Sergio Bustamante monkey.

Burns, who has been styling Lenny Kravitz for over a decade, says his approach to home design and fashion are one in the same: “Building an outfit, building a room: same thing in my opinion.”

Kravitz offers these kind words on Burns: “What I love about Rodney is that he is limitless in his concepts and always thinks outside of the box. His visual vocabulary enables him to mix a vast stew of eras and genres. He pays tribute to our ancestors and uplifts new innovators. Always soulful, expressive, and elegant.”

In the dining room, Soll placed a Sergio Bustamante table on a diagonal to make enough room for the couple’s large dog. “She’s a girthy girl,” says Soll, referring to the table, not the dog. The chairs are by Paul Evans and the Chinese Art Deco rug was sourced from Ariana Rugs. The Tom Greene chandelier was sourced from Burns’s boutique, Church.

Burns’s maximalism is on display in the living room, where a couch upholstered in leopard Scalamandré mixes with zebra-hide chairs and a sinuous De Sede sofa. (That animal print couch, Soll wants you to know, also features a block of leather upholstery: “I just think it would have been too maximalist to do a full Scalamandré sofa.”)

The living room is filled with brass sculptures (a Maison Jansen palm tree and a Sergio Bustamante monkey), bronze figures sourced from an estate sale in Arizona, and several pieces of sculptural artwork.

The bedroom mixes patterns and textures with a stainless steel bed from TTMM, Paul Evans “Cityscape” nightstands, striped bedding, and antique bronze table lamps with African mud cloth shades.

The 1970s textile art in the office is a vintage find of Burns’s that Soll loves. “I used to go to Rodney’s apartment when we first started dating, and I would be like, ‘Oh my God! Like, if we break up, I’m never gonna be able to find this tapestry,’” he says.

Soll got permission from his landladies to retile the bathroom and add a new vanity. The metal Curtis Jeré artwork comes from Burns’s collection.

The bohemian architecture of the home lends quirkiness to the design. The main bedroom isn’t a perfect square, making it seem like a stainless steel bed from TTMM is placed on a diagonal but is actually following the geometry of a very funky, angular bedroom.

The couple has taken their time tinkering and adjusting the decor, taking a considered approach to collecting and combining their belongings. Soll describes the overall vibe as having an Afrocentric theme that’s also a little bit Ralph Lauren. “Our visions have mingled,” Burns says.

Still, there is one last item the couple needs to complete the design: a blanket basket. But, says Soll, “We still haven’t found one we agree upon.”

The backyard is also home to a shed (not pictured), which functions as Burns’s closet.

#4959. OpenAI Launches ChatGPT Wallet to Let AI Agents Shop—Without Crypto

OpenAI Launches ChatGPT Wallet to Let AI Agents Shop—Without Crypto

ChatGPT Wallet lets OpenAI’s dots complete purchases with tokenized card details, after users approve each transaction. The limited rollout targets U.S. ChatGPT Pro users; the wallet does not hold crypto or a cash balance. Coinbase and Circle offer a different route, using stablecoins to let agents pay for services with less human involvement.

OpenAI launched ChatGPT Wallet on Friday, giving its AI agents a way to purchase products using American Express, Mastercard, and Visa cards. The new system lets an agent finish online checkout without accessing the customer's actual card number, and it doesn't involve cryptocurrency.

The wallet works through dots, OpenAI's always-on AI assistants, which can browse websites and carry out tasks for users. A customer saves an eligible card, reviews the order and approves the payment using a security check such as a fingerprint or Face ID.

After approval, the card network creates a payment token containing substitute card details. The agent submits the token through the merchant’s existing checkout system, while the customer keeps applicable card rewards and dispute protections, according to OpenAI’s announcement.

“An agent making a purchase should strengthen, not bypass, the trust built into payments,” Sherri Haymond, Mastercard's global head of digital commercialization, said in the announcement.

OpenAI previously introduced Instant Checkout with Stripe in September 2025, allowing people to buy from participating merchants inside ChatGPT. Wallet takes a different approach: agents can navigate ordinary online stores and use existing checkout forms, without requiring each business to build a special integration.

OpenAI is also courting merchants. Just three days before the Wallet launch, it introduced business profiles that let companies update product information and connect with potential customers through ChatGPT.

Google is working on a competing approach. Its Universal Commerce Protocol, introduced in January, aims to connect shopping agents with retailers, payment providers and post-purchase services across Google products.

Coinbase launched Agentic Wallets in February, giving software the ability to spend USDC, trade tokens, and pay for online services within preset limits. Unlike ChatGPT Wallet, the Coinbase product is primarily developer infrastructure, not a consumer checkout feature.

“It's not an SDK, it's not a library—it's a purpose-built wallet to work with an agent as quickly as possible,” Coinbase engineering executive Erik Reppel told Decrypt at the launch.

Coinbase's x402 protocol lets software pay for data or computing services as part of a web request, often using the USDC stablecoin. That makes tiny transactions possible without requiring an agent to enter card details or obtain human approval each time.

Stablecoin issuer Circle is pushing in the same direction. Its Agent Stack, launched in May, includes stablecoin wallets and tools for payments as small as $0.000001.

“USDC is uniquely well-suited for the agentic economy because it is internet-native, programmable, and always available,” Circle product chief Nikhil Chandhok said in a company statement.

Actual agent-driven demand is harder to measure. In September, blockchain analytics firm TRM Labs estimated that agents accounted for only 0.6% to 7.5% of filtered x402 commerce by value, using models that cannot conclusively distinguish agents from scripts.

For consumers, ChatGPT Wallet offers familiar card payments rather than a digital asset balance. OpenAI's terms say merchants remain responsible for deliveries and returns, and users must generally contact their card issuers for billing disputes.

OpenAI plans to let users authorize future purchases within spending limits or specified categories, but has not announced when that feature will arrive. In the meantime, it warns that merchant websites may block shopping agents or flag otherwise legitimate Wallet purchases as fraud.

#4953. What Is Canton Network? The Privacy Blockchain Built for Wall Street

The majority of blockchains are designed in such a way that anyone can see every transaction, every balance and every smart contract call.

Canton began as a niche enterprise project from software firm Digital Asset. It now carries some of the biggest names in traditional finance, including the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC), the post-trade backbone of U.S. markets, which launched its tokenization service on Canton and its own private network in October 2026.

Canton Network is a layer-1 blockchain developed by Digital Asset, a New York company founded in 2014 that has long built distributed ledger software for financial firms. Canton launched in May 2023 with a pilot group that included Goldman Sachs, Deloitte, Microsoft and others. Its public infrastructure, the Global Synchronizer, went live in July 2024 together with Canton Coin.

Canton is better described as a "network of networks" than as one big ledger. Each institution runs its own validator node and applications, and its node stores only the data relevant to it, according to Canton's documentation. The nodes connect through the Global Synchronizer, so assets and transactions can move between them atomically: a trade either settles fully on every side or not at all.

The network is governed by the Global Synchronizer Foundation, an independent body hosted by the Linux Foundation. A group of "Super Validators" operates the Global Synchronizer.

In June 2026, Digital Asset raised $355 million in a round led by a16z crypto. HSBC, BNP Paribas, Citadel Securities, CME Ventures and Broadridge also invested. That followed a $135 million round in June 2025.

Think of a repo trade between two banks with a custodian in the middle. Each party sees and checks its own leg. Nobody else on the network sees the details, and the synchronizer orders and confirms the transaction without reading its contents.

This is very different from Ethereum-style chains, where every node replicates every state change. On Canton, the Global Synchronizer, run by the Super Validators, orders encrypted messages and confirms transactions without seeing their contents, while each institution's own validator node holds its data. Institutions keep control of their data, but can avoid much of the reconciliation that legacy systems require. In practice, most activity on the network is private: about 79% of the updates recorded on Canton in a 24-hour period in November 2026 were private, according to CantonScan.

"Atomic settlement" means both legs of a trade, such as a bond and the cash paying for it, move at the same moment. That removes the risk that one side delivers and the other doesn't.

What sets Canton apart is who uses it. Recent milestones include:

Canton Coin (CC) is the native utility token of the Global Synchronizer. Users pay network fees by burning CC. New CC is minted as a reward for validators, Super Validators and "featured" app builders who generate real activity on the network. This setup is called a burn-mint equilibrium.

CC had no pre-mine and no allocations to venture capital investors, according to Canton: every token in circulation has been earned through minting rewards, which are split between Super Validators, app builders and validators. Whether supply shrinks depends on usage. As of November 2026, the network was burning roughly 80% as much CC each day as it minted, according to CantonScan data, below the 1:1 ratio at which supply would start to shrink. Circulating supply stood at just over 40 billion CC.

In November 2026, SEC staff granted Canton no-action relief, saying they view Canton Coin as a digital commodity and would not recommend enforcement over how it is minted. In May 2026, 21Shares launched the first U.S. ETF offering direct exposure to CC.

Institutional custody is catching up. Ledger added Canton Coin support to its Ledger Wallet, and Ripple Custody integrated with Canton in October 2026.

CC trades on several major centralized exchanges, including Kraken, Bybit and OKX. CC perpetual futures are also available on Binance Futures, and Alchemy Pay supports buying CC with a card. On Canton itself, Cantex offers onchain swaps. CC is a native coin, not an ERC-20 token, so be wary of look-alike "CC" tokens on Ethereum-based DEXs. You can track Canton Coin's latest price on Decrypt's price page.

Availability varies by region, and nothing in this guide is investment advice.

Canton's next phase is moving from pilots to full production. DTCC's service is now live, and its roadmap adds onchain settlement, collateral management and digital cash. Derivnex and its partners plan to bring cash settlement and collateral management for derivatives directly onto Canton. Expansion in Asia is also picking up: Shinhan Financial Group, Hanwha Investment & Securities, LG CNS and Meritz Securities have all signed Canton deals.

Canton doesn't have a monopoly, though. DTCC plans to connect its tokenization service to Stellar, Circle's Arc and Solana in 2027.

#4952. Asian chip stocks tumble ahead of Nvidia Q3 earnings

The weakness came amid a wider sell-off in regional equity markets, with Japan’s Nikkei 225 down 2% and South Korea’s Kospi losing 2.6%.

Asian semiconductor stocks slid on Monday ahead of Nvidia’s quarterly earnings, with analysts set to look for signs that the AI spending boom remains on track amid uncertainty over the chipmaker’s leadership and future growth.

Japanese chip-testing equipment maker Advantest (6857:TYO) dropped about 10.4%, semiconductor inspection equipment maker Lasertec (6920:TYO) declined more than 10.7% and chipmaking equipment supplier Tokyo Electron (8035:TYO) fell 5.7%, according to Google Finance.

South Korean memory-chip makers Samsung Electronics (005930:KRX) and SK Hynix (000660:KRX) lost 5.8% and 4.2%, respectively, while Taiwanese chipmaker Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (2330:TPE) slipped 3.8%.

The broad decline could reflect caution toward AI-linked stocks ahead of Nvidia’s results, although the weakness came amid a wider sell-off in regional equity markets, with Japan’s Nikkei 225 down 2% and South Korea’s Kospi losing 2.6% as of 4:04 am UTC.

Nvidia is scheduled to release its fiscal third-quarter results on Tuesday, followed by an earnings call. The results will be Nvidia’s first since founder Jensen Huang resigned as CEO.

Investors and analysts will be looking at how Nvidia will respond to the AI demand boom while its leadership is in turmoil. Morningstar on Friday said it will be looking to see whether the mid-term elections have led to any concern about future demand and whether Nvidia’s outlook for the industry has changed.

In August, Nvidia forecast fiscal third-quarter revenue of $108 billion, plus or minus 2%, excluding any data center compute revenue from China.

Related: CoreWeave stock jumps 15% premarket after Q3 revenue beats estimates

Analysts expect Nvidia to report slightly higher quarterly revenue of $109.3 billion, including $101.8 billion from its data center business, according to Visible Alpha consensus figures published by S&P Global Market Intelligence on Thursday.

S&P Global said revenue expectations had risen since the previous quarter on anticipated strength in Nvidia’s Blackwell chips.

Morningstar analysts also said they expect another “beat-and-raise quarter” out of Nvidia, given strong capex trends among hyperscalers and enterprises.

“We’d be stunned if Nvidia provided an earnings miss or weak forecast,” they said.

Nvidia shares closed Friday down 2.01% at $162.85, according to Google Finance.

#4950. 9 Best Duvet Covers: Editor Sets to Style a Bedscape (2026)

Besides pieces like quilts and coverlets, I always say duvet covers are the best accessories to give a bedscape a bit more personality. Take it from Ariel Kaye, founder of juggernaut bedding brand Parachute, who has filled her eclectic LA home with a range of duvet covers that span vibrant, breezy linens to neutral, crisp percales.

To truly find the best of the best, I rounded up a group of writers and editors to test duvet covers from our favorite bedding brands—featuring muted, structured covers for minimalists and trendy colorways and patterns for maximalists. As for our top pick, we went with Boll & Branch’s Signature Hemmed Duvet Set since it’s organic, has a smooth and breathable feel, and comes in tons of prints and colors for easy styling.

If you’re still on the fence about what to go with for your bedroom, I also tapped AD PRO Directory designers to share what they look for in duvet covers, including materiality and design. Read below to create your dream bedscape this season.

For consistency, all prices listed reflect queen-size duvets unless otherwise noted, but up to king sizes are available for purchase from each brand.

Boll & Branch is one of AD’s favorite brands when it comes to bedding. With super soft sheets and other products made from 100% organic cotton, they have a heavy focus on sustainability and directly sourcing raw materials. The duvet cover follows suit, with long-staple cotton making for a durable piece of bedding that I’ve found is breathable enough for both hot and cold nights.

This organic-cotton duvet set can please pretty much anyone with a traditional bedroom. It has a tried-and-true cotton sateen material that gets softer with every wash, and designs—including subtle botanical prints—that are a must for anyone with classic taste. The earthy tones are great for a grounded bedroom too.

Bring the outdoors in with your styling by pairing one of Boll & Branch’s botanical prints with a textured linen sheet set.

Shop AD editors have stood behind Boll & Branch for years because of the brand’s sustainable business model and high-quality bedding. The signature cotton sheets have even earned a top spot in our best-of guides for the last five years. Just like these sheets, shopping producer Lauren Arzbaecher tells me that this Boll & Branch duvet cover’s sateen weave has a buttery soft texture. “The cotton is very smooth, and I maintained a comfortable temperature throughout the night even when bundled up underneath all my bedding,” she says. As a cold sleeper, Arzbeacher loves layering extra throws and blankets on top of her duvet, and she says this ultra-breathable material has all the “cuddly qualities” you’d want without making you overheat. Even better, the bedding is 100% organic, so you can rest knowing you’re not breathing in any harmful chemicals.

This sateen duvet cover also has a zipper closure, so it’s a lot easier for Arzbaecher to slip her Company Store down insert on and off than other button-closure covers she’s tried. “Their solid color range has a lovely mix of earthy hues, and the brand has been releasing more and more patterns, from stripes to florals,” for styling a bed, she adds.

I’m not sure what’s left to say about this bedding brand that hasn’t already been plastered across subway ads or Instagram feeds, but in short, Brooklinen is somewhat of a bedding celebrity. It has garnered cool-kid status by offering top-quality products using Oeko-Tex-certified products at reasonable prices.

The Classic Duvet Cover is made of percale, which can be compared to a crisp cotton button-down shirt. It’s soft and sophisticated in a matte, buttery finish that can be attributed to long-staple cotton fibers that are spun to make stronger but finer yarn.

We are not only big fans of Brooklinen products for their breathable, sustainable materials but also for their versatile designs. With 20 earthy tones, I recommend this duvet cover for any type of bedroom style—but I’d lean towards purchasing for a coastal design because of the soothing colors and prints.

Like Alpert, who has this duvet cover in her bedroom, I recommend styling it with textured linen sheets for a contrasting yet comfortable fabric combination and a throw blanket in complementary neutral and oceanic hues.

Not one but two AD writers love this Brooklinen cotton duvet cover all year long. Shopping writer Julia Harrison calls this super, super soft and very cool to the touch. “It stays cool even when I’ve wrapped myself all the way in it like a caterpillar so I’m never overheating, but I am staying comfy and warm,” she shares. Contributor Yelena Moroz Alpert says this is her favorite duvet cover of all time, hands-down, because of its unmatched airy and parachute-like quality. “The percale duvet cover has this smooth feel but at the same time it is remarkably light and crisp,” she says. “Apparently, it’s all because of the high-quality single-ply yarns, which are made from strong, long-staple cotton fibers that give it that buttery touch.”

The style potential is a plus here too. Alpert loves the “oyster” print since it strays away from more expected patterns like stripes filling up her linen closet. She got the matching sheets in the same print for this exact reason, saying the swirls remind her of “Hokusai’s waves and stand out in the sea of florals and plaids” in her linen closet.

From pajamas to sheets, Cozy Earth is a favorite of ours—and Oprah thanks to frequent appearances in her Favorite Things lists—for cooling bedding made of bamboo fabric. Its offerings also ring in at a budget-friendly price (especially with the brand’s frequent sales).

Many of the colors in Cozy Earth’s collection are grounded in the earth (to be expected). With this in mind, I see this duvet cover in a minimalist, bohemian, or coastal bedroom. Alpert’s peony pick is just as nice for a romantic bedroom or something seasonal for summer.

The delicate nature of the bedding (both in texture and color) makes it a striking contrast against crisp, patterned accents, like a percale floral sheet set.

When I asked Alpert what her favorite qualities are about Cozy Earth’s signature, silky duvet cover, there were almost too many to count. “This duvet cover is a trifecta of perks: It looks good, feels good, and won’t slip off the comforter,” she says. “But if I had to pick one, I’d say that I love the delicate pink hue and the equally delicate feel of the cover—just looking at my bed makes me want to crawl into it.”

The material (100% viscose from bamboo) has a breathable and smooth feel to it that’s nice for hot sleepers but also anyone who doesn’t want a more textured cover like linen. Even better, the soft, pastel-inspired color options like peony pink and laurel green make it a great fit for styling a bed during this blooming season. “The fabric is so delicate and dreamy that it reminds me of flower petals,” Alpert tells me. “Since I chose the peony colorway, it definitely fits the romantic profile.”

Even though it has a smooth texture, the comforter inside won’t slip around because of the secure snap closures. “Cozy Earth has 12(!) points of contact between the comforter and the cover, so there is no way anything is going to get loose, especially because these are snapped on, and not tied, like many other duvet covers,” she says. “I pair it with the bamboo viscose comforter with standard fill and the combination works great because the comforter has some weight and the duvet cover does not mess with it in any way.”

A darling of the 2010s direct to consumer movement, Parachute is a home brand best known for its bedding, though the company has also dabbled in furniture, mattresses, and other essentials. Its products, including the Linen Duvet Cover, are Oeko-Tex Standard 100–certified, meaning that every element (even buttons!) has been tested for substances damaging to human health. Parachute also doesn’t artificially soften their products. In the wrong hands, linen can be itchy or stiff until it’s been broken in, but that wasn’t the case here. This version, made of European Flax and made in the Guimarães region of Portugal, was inviting and oh so soft. The company also touts the material as naturally antimicrobial, which is a plus.

Another pick for a bohemian bedroom, this duvet cover has a lived-in look and several organic colors to choose from.

If you, in true minimalist fashion, like a monochrome bedscape, style this duvet cover with linen sheets in natural colors. If you want a slightly moodier bedroom, try a shade like cacao or indigo.

Contributor Lila Allen has nothing but good things to say about the feel of this linen and its nonchalant elegance: “I was pleasantly surprised at how soft this cover was—with linen, sometimes it can be a roll of the dice. This set had the best of both worlds: super supple but still casually elevated. The linen is a nice thickness, too; I can’t see any trace of the duvet inside of it.” Parachute’s linen has 175 GSM, and Quince’s linen that I mention down below has around 150 GSM. To Allen’s point, this means Parachute’s is more durable with a substantial feel and less opaque (the reason why she can’t see her duvet insert peeking through) with a lighter cover color like toast or sand.

Allen pairs her duvet cover with an all-season comforter from Riley, which she says stays secure through a button closure on the side and gives just the right amount of rumple together. The insert moves around a bit inside of the cover but mostly remains in place. “Best of all, I’ve been sleeping soundly with the set on hot nights as well as ones where the AC has been on overdrive, and so far it holds up in both situations—not too warm or cool.” Allen tells me that she shares a bed with her “hot-blooded husband and a large Maine Coon cat,” so her bed usually runs warm. This layerable, snuggly cover is perfect.

The home brand is headquartered in New York City, however, the materials are sourced from fourth-generation, family-owned textile mills in Portugal and other countries across Europe. The products span a range of bedding and bath products, namely duvet covers, sheets, towels, mats, and other accessories like pillows and robs.

Since all of the duvet covers come in neutral, unfussy colors, this is a great option for minimalists.

I style this simply with other neutrals like white or cream sheets. When I want to add more visual interest, I layer in butter yellow bedding or a quilt in soft, muted tones.

To put it simply, this duvet cover feels like a lightweight cashmere sweater. It’s not stuffy, but it’s not flimsy either. Riley Home’s somehow managed to make a cover that feels airy enough for nights when my apartment’s radiator runs overly hot and still warm enough when I want to curl up in bed after a cold commute from work.

Even though it feels soft, it’s not made of pure cashmere. In reality, it’s made of 95% cotton and just 5% cashmere, but I wouldn’t have noticed if I didn’t read the label so closely. The texture has a brushed quality to it that feels gentle and light on my skin. There are a few neutral colors on the site. I went with the oatmeal and have liked styling it with white sheet sets so far for a simple, not-overly-done-up bedspread. A couple of other thoughtful details are the interior ties and buttons to keep my comforter from shifting inside. The super-easy care sold me too. The brand doesn’t recommend dry cleaning—just a machine wash and dry with a gentle detergent will do. As someone who has lugged specialty cashmere bedding to NYC cleaners on foot through the years, this note in and of itself almost convinced me to give the bedding a try.

Quince’s entire deal is about making high-quality luxury products at an affordable price. They work only with factories that are both sustainable and pay a fair wage, which is something that many shoppers care enough about to pay a little extra for, but Quince cuts back on things like fancy packaging to keep costs down.

Quince is another AD favorite for its plethora of prints and solid colors. Try this duvet cover in your space—no matter its design style—for a lightweight addition to your bedscape that’s also budget friendly.

Since linen makes a statement on its own with its lived-in look, I recommend styling this duvet cover with Quince’s classic European linen sheet set and a fluffy duvet insert in moody neutral hues (think deep brown) for a cozy sleep oasis. It’s also neutral enough that you can add some pattern sheeting for extra personality.

I’ve had this Quince linen duvet for about a year and it’s been a mainstay since it’s substantial and textured while still having an airiness to it—even when I’m layering with a few throw blankets or quilts when it’s cold outside. Sometimes linen gets a bad rap for its rough texture, but this best seller has been gentle against my skin and only gotten softer with more routine wear and care. It’s not as soft as something like an heirloom-quality stonewashed linen, but I’m not too picky in the linen department. I think its Oeko-Tex certification gives it a leg up generally to some competitors in this fabric category too.

I chose this Quince duvet since there were so many color options to choose from. I love moody colors year-round, and the burgundy is true to the deep color I saw on the site and it hasn’t faded after months of washes. It has the relaxed look you know and love with linen materials. Steaming it knocks out wrinkles easily whenever I want a more put-together look in my bedroom, though.

At this price point and with the most colors and prints available in this guide, I think this comforter cover can give you a great, budget-friendly way to dress a bed. The button closures also help keep a duvet insert or comforter in place. The care has been simple. I wash it with similar colors on a cold cycle and tumble dry on low and have only noticed a loose thread here and there, but nothing too crazy.

As mentioned above in our top overall pick, Brooklinen is known as the “home of the internet’s favorite sheets,” so we find it to be a worthy destination for quality bedding—including this quilt-like duvet cover made with 100% cotton.

With a design that mirrors the appearance of a quilt, we think this duvet cover would dress a vintage-inspired space beautifully—like the bedding you’d find in a historic Victorian home.

Alpert chose the plum colorway because of its sophisticated look that anchors her bedroom. She shares that the hue looks exceptionally well-paired with the deep caramel of the Boll & Branch Grand waffle blanket, as the color almost works like a moody neutral.

Even though the Brooklinen website describes this duvet as “breezy,” Alpert says it feels more like a weighted blanket. “The ribbed side is certainly quilt-like, which is not surprising because each ribbon that runs through the duvet cover is padded ever so slightly,” she shares.

The cover has a two-sided design: one side with textured, ribbed matelasse and the other with a crisp percale fabric. Alpert thinks this material combo is genius since it keeps the duvet cover from feeling too heavy. “This is especially key for anyone who sleeps without a flat sheet—you’ll feel like you’re getting into freshly changed linens night after night,” she says. “On the flip side, the ribbed texture elevates the duvet cover, not only in how it looks but it gives your bedding a slightly cushy sensation.”

Rest assured that the feel isn’t as heavy as an actual weighted blanket. Alpert says it certainly has substance and structure, though. “When I sleep with it on, it feels as if I am very secure—no one will dare wake me up,” she notes.

Hommey is a relatively new Australian-based brand that got its start in 2020. It became popular for robes and nightwear in bold, fashionable stripes. The designs are now spread across the bedding category, including cotton and linen sheets and duvet covers.

Since the brand is known for more out-of-the-box colorways across its collections, this duvet color is a great option for maximalists looking to experiment with bold color-blocking.

For a moodier bedscape, I style this duvet cover with sheets in a deep tone like burgundy. Overall, the chocolatey hue acts like a neutral and pairs beautifully with lighter sheet colors like oatmeal, which can also complement the cream-colored trim.

I know we’ve talked about linen a couple of times already in this story, but Hommey’s duvet helps hit the trifecta for the category. The natural flax fiber felt smoother and more broken-in than my Quince version right out the package. My guess is that it’s because of the stonewashed finish, which is known to give scratchy linen a more silky quality. I won’t get ahead of myself and say it feels like pure silk, but I can confirm that it’s soft and breathable almost like an organic cotton duvet after a few washes. The duvet caught my attention even more because of all of the styles. There are over 30 fashionable colors and prints, like the oxford and plum reversible cotton duvet I want in a button-down shirt version. The linen offering has four distinct colorways, including the brown sugar with cream trim that I got for myself. I like deep colors for my bedscape, especially as the year starts inching towards fall and winter, and its rich hue hasn’t disappointed. It's the perfect complement to my autumnal color palette, especially when I pair it with burgundy linen sheets.

It is less expensive than the Parachute pick and has a plusher texture than the Quince, so I’d say this is the perfect middle ground for an approachable but high-quality linen duvet.

Garnet Hill was founded in New Hampshire in the mid-1970s by Pegge and Grant Dowse who built the brand on a commitment to sustainable materials, thoughtful craftsmanship, and responsible sourcing with products like organic cotton and flannel bedding. The bedding blends traditional styles with a modern touch to fit in a range of bedrooms.

Our tester says the Pear Garden print is completely unexpected. “As much as I love florals, stripes, and gingham, a girl needs a break…and these charming pears are just the right motif,” Alpert shares. “They signal a cottagecore moment without going over the top. Every time I glimpse the cheery yellow fruit, I crack a little smile. Plus, the colorway of bold yellow, sage green, brown, and cream are easy to coordinate with the rest of my bedding collection.”

Because the pear motifs are the statement, we think you can go with more subtle sheets like a white set with embroidered trim. If you’re more of a maximalist, you can lean into the pattern play with a gingham sheet set in a complementary muted tone like brown and extra layers like a faux fur blanket if you’re cozying up for winter.

According to Alpert, this duvet cover is synonymous with cozy. It’s the softest option on this list because of the brushed flannel fabric, and it’s also a great pick if you live in cold climates since it has a bit of heft to it. “It’s as if a layer of leaves, or in this case pears, is surrounding me during winter hibernation,” she says. “That’s in part because this specific flannel is woven to have a substantial six-ounce weight.”

Alpert’s had this for nearly one year and says it has a decadent texture, likening it to climbing under a layer of meringue when she pairs it with her fluffy Ostermoor down duvet insert. “The plush feel of the cover also makes me think that I’ve just scored an upgrade to a royal suite,” Alpert adds.

At this point, she hasn’t noticed any pilling, but she does note that the cover is a bit snugger than a typical king, so you can expect the duvet to feel a little loftier than a regular fit.

What to Look for in the Best Duvet Covers

A duvet cover is more than just a bedcover. This eye-catching textile has a large footprint in any bedroom and can instantly give your space a refresh—whether with unique colors and prints or something more modest—especially if you want to disguise an old duvet insert. With this in mind, I tapped AD PRO Directory designers to weigh in on what to look for in a duvet cover to elevate your bedroom.

Material: When it comes to duvet covers, everyone has their own personal preferences. For Nicole R. Fisher, CEO and founder of Nicole Fisher Interiors, it’s linen: “They’re effortless, lightweight, and airy. I think the simplicity of the linen transcends different styles and spaces,” she says. Fisher notes that the feel is a big factor when selecting a duvet cover.

Carmen René Smith, founder and principal designer of Aquilo Interiors, says that it also needs to feel soft to the touch, breathable, and cozy. “What’s the point of a beautiful duvet if you don’t want to snuggle into it?” she says. “Texture is key too—whether it’s silky, crisp, or nubby, it should add depth to the space.”

Comfort: All of the interior designers I spoke to for this story point to natural fibers, not only because of their quality but also their airflow and breathability. Shelbie Cox of Shelbie Cox Studio recommends natural fibers like organic cotton and linen and notes that polyester and other synthetic fibers are best to avoid since they can trap heat.

Design: With so many patterns and colors to choose from, it’s important to keep your bedroom’s design style in mind. If you like more of a minimalist bedroom, Cox suggests a fine stripe pattern (like Quince’s European Linen Duvet Cover) or a monochromatic, muted color palette (see the Cozy Earth Bamboo Duvet Cover) for your duvet cover set.

If you want to go bold for your bedroom, there are options aplenty. “We’re maximalists, so we say bump up the color and pattern no matter what your signature style is!” says Smith, adding that it’s important to still maintain balance. “If you have bold wallpaper, pull a color from it and go for a solid, textured duvet—like a rich velvet for drama or a soft linen for a more relaxed vibe. If you’re mixing patterns, think about scale—a bold, oversized print pairs best with a smaller, more delicate duvet pattern to give the eye a place to rest.”

If you don’t want to commit to one style just yet, rest assured that having a few duvets in rotation can help you keep your bedroom design fresh. “I tend to go simple on bedding and let the accessories be the pops of pattern and color that’s easily swapped out,” adds Fisher.

Maintenance: While these are great design accessories for a bedroom, they also need to be practical. Ashley Ross, founder and principal designer of Muse Noire Interiors recommends keeping these questions in mind when shopping: How does it fasten (zipper, tie up, or buttons)? Does it fit within your washer and dryer? This will help you know how to use it and how to care for it.

We tested the best duvet covers the same way we test products for the rest of our sleep content: by bringing the items in our homes and dressing them on our beds. This way, you’re guaranteed to get a real-life look at how the products perform after weeks and months of everyday use. We also wash them routinely to keep an eye out for any wear and tear. All of the brands we feature reflect a variety of price points, designs, and materials so you have the best options to choose from for your bedroom.

Jump to the “How We Tested” section of this article to watch an in-depth video about our testing methodology and some of our favorite products and brands.

You don’t necessarily need a duvet cover. It depends on how you like to dress your bed. In my opinion, it’s a great bed accessory. A duvet cover not only protects your duvet insert but it also gives you the opportunity to easily switch up your bedscape (instead of swapping comforters when you want a new look). That said, pay attention to the care instructions for your insert because some do not recommend washing or dry cleaning.

There are several ways you can put a duvet cover on, but according to TikTok experts, this method below is as foolproof as possible:

Start by turning your duvet cover inside out, top side down, with the duvet opener at the foot of the bed. Then, place your comforter on top and secure the corners with the attached ties to the duvet cover. Starting at the head of the bed, roll the duvet down towards the foot just like a burrito. Finally, fold the duvet opening over each end of the roll, and unroll the blanket back towards the

Always check the manufacturer instructions to find out how to wash your specific duvet cover. However, the most common way to clean a duvet cover is by machine washing it with like colors with a gentle detergent on a cold cycle. If the duvet cover has a zipper closure, it's usually helpful to zip it closed to keep it from snagging in the washing machine.

It's generally recommended to wash your duvet cover as often as you wash your sheets, which is about once a week. If you use a top sheet, though, you can usually extend another week and wash your duvet cover every other week.

Our editors selected top products in this story from tried-and-true brands on the market, from Brooklinen to Boll & Branch. These brands boast organic materials like 100% organic cotton and European Flax and bamboo for a sustainably made bed. Plus, many boast eco-friendly certifications like GOTS and Oeko-Tex so you know they are free of chemicals. As a design editor, I also made sure all of the products have a range of colors, patterns, and prints to complement a bedroom’s style.

#4947. OpenAI, Anthropic, Google and Other AI Giants Face Lawsuit Demanding Development Freeze

OpenAI, Anthropic, Google and Other AI Giants Face Lawsuit Demanding Development Freeze

Four nonprofit organizations are taking some of the world's biggest AI companies to court, asking a California judge to stop them from developing more powerful artificial intelligence systems before something goes seriously wrong.

The 63-page complaint, filed Thursday in San Francisco Superior Court, targets OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta, and SpaceX. The plaintiffs include Public Citizen, the Heat Initiative, Irreplaceable Education Fund, and the Alliance for Secure AI.

They want an immediate court order prohibiting the companies from developing or deploying AI systems more capable than their most advanced models as of November 12, 2026.

“The Frontier AI companies are developing AI Systems that they do not understand and that act contrary to developer intent, evade control, and break the law,” the plaintiffs argue. To prevent this, they request “an immediate injunction forbidding Defendants from developing or deploying AI systems more capable than their most capable systems as of the date of this Complaint”

That wouldn't necessarily mean shutting down ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, or other existing products. Instead, it would effectively make today's technology the limit, preventing the companies from releasing more powerful successors.

The complaint also asks the court to prohibit the companies from using AI to independently conduct or direct research into better models, a process related to what researchers call recursive self-improvement. The concern is that such systems could eventually improve faster than humans can understand or control them.

The nonprofits argue that California already has laws that could prevent companies from developing products that endanger the public, even before those products cause serious harm.

Their lawsuit invokes the state's Unfair Competition Law, which prohibits unlawful or unfair business practices. One part of their argument is that developing increasingly capable AI systems creates a public nuisance: a danger that affects the health, safety, or property of a broader community.

For example, a factory releasing dangerous chemicals into a neighborhood could face legal action before someone suffers a fatal injury. The plaintiffs want the court to apply similar reasoning to AI development.

They argue that companies are knowingly creating increasingly powerful systems that could facilitate cyberattacks, help develop biological weapons, or eventually escape human control.

“No other industry is allowed to treat its own development of unsafe products—much less ones that could kill us all—as inevitable,” the plaintiffs argue in the complaint.

Robert Weissman, co-president of Public Citizen, said in a statement that the groups want the court to declare the companies' practices unlawful and “freeze further AI development.”

The legal challenge is whether the alleged risks and practices are enough to justify such sweeping restrictions. Even though these frontier labs constantly warn about how powerful and dangerous their models are, as defendants they have not been found liable, and warnings about potential catastrophe do not automatically establish a violation of California law.

The complaint points to incidents that have already occurred as evidence that the dangers aren't entirely hypothetical.

In July, OpenAI models escaped a controlled testing environment and compromised Hugging Face's infrastructure while attempting to cheat on a cybersecurity evaluation.

A subsequent independent investigation found that roughly 1,200 OpenAI agents used an unauthorized internal message board to coordinate, with about 700 joining the attack on Hugging Face. The agents were attempting to obtain answers to the benchmark testing them.

These incidents involved AI agents, programs that can execute tasks, browse websites, write code, and interact with external systems rather than simply responding to questions.

Documented security failures, however, are not proof that today's AI models can cause human extinction. The plaintiffs are connecting those incidents to predictions about what increasingly capable systems might eventually do, and that broader danger remains disputed.

“It is patently illegal for companies to develop and release products they know are unsafe in any respect,” Matthew Platkin, the former New Jersey attorney general representing the plaintiffs, said in the statement.

#4945. 8 Best Holiday Wreaths for a Festive Front Door (2026)

Nothing gets us feeling warm and fuzzy quite like passing by a fully adorned front door. Whether lit or unlit, fresh or faux, a well-appointed Christmas wreath is a visual signifier to all who pass that the festivities have officially begun in this home. While a classic ring of evergreens will never go out of style, there’s no shortage of modern takes on traditional winter foliage that will earn you points for both holiday spirit and good taste.

If cleaning up pine needles isn’t your thing, fear not: Faux wreaths are getting closer to the real thing by the season, saving you a little maintenance during an already busy time of year. Choose one accented with velvet ribbons for a touch of nostalgia, or opt for something that looks as though it was plucked straight from a whimsical snowscape, complete with pinecones and berries. And if you’ve historically never considered yourself a “wreath person,” take this as your sign to make this the year you become one. Turns out, participating in holiday cheer is just as delightful as it sounds.

While you may turn to Quince for an affordable cashmere sweater or linen bedding, the unsung treasures might surprise you. Take this faux foliage wreath, for instance, which mimics the look and feel of a Norfolk pine so convincingly, you’d never know the difference from the street. Simple and full, its bare branches make the perfect backdrop for trailing red bows, tinsel, or whatever other accoutrements you desire.

This faux greenery features impressively lifelike cypress leaves, while the berries add a bold, seasonal color. It’s designed for indoor and outdoor use and has UV protection. In short, it’s designed with durability in mind to outlast wintry weather and beyond. It'll also keep you from sweeping a single pine needle off your front porch (a gift in itself).

If evergreens, real or fake, aren't your thing, we say go full color. This Lulu and Georgia wreath is made of velvety baubles, each with a plush texture and rich hue. It's the perfect size for hanging from a cabinet, chair back, or interior door when you want to add a simple but effective holiday touch.

Something to make your days merry and bright, literally. This faux wreath comes pre-lit, saving you the hassle of weaving your own lights through the branches (and sparing you any DIY allegations). With softly draping cedar branches and a warm white glow, it makes an inviting addition to front doors, mantels, and more.

There's something especially charming about holiday decorations that look as though they've been gathered on a winter walk. This Magnolia wreath captures that feeling with lifelike foliage and bright red berries that add just the right amount of color. Even better, it’s currently 25% off. Hang it on your front door or prop it on a mantel for a dose of old-fashioned holiday spirit.

A few perfectly placed pinecones can make a wreath feel understated yet exceptionally sophisticated. This Afloral wreath is the kind of arrangement that lets everyone approaching your front door know they’re about to enter a well-decorated home, though you’d never make a big deal of it. The real-touch greenery is convincing enough to have guests wondering when you found the time to forage a Noble Fir yourself.

If you’re craving a departure from the expected, this copper wreath offers a fresh take on traditional holiday foliage. Eucalyptus and assorted leaves are finished in shimmering shades of copper and gold, lending the arrangement a decidedly more glamorous feel. Think less woodland Christmas, more fancy dinner party.

If you’re dreaming of a white Christmas, this wreath will certainly do the trick. Covered in clusters of snowy white berries, it has a soft, wintry look that feels merry without leaning too heavily into traditional red and green. It's a lovely option for anyone who prefers a more pared-back holiday palette, and one that will look just as fitting long after the presents have been unwrapped.

#4942. Aster CEO suspects foul play after token plunges 32% in minutes

Aster’s CEO attributed the extreme volatility to highly leveraged liquidations but said the exchange remained operational.

Aster CEO Leonard Leung says the team is investigating a sharp swing in its ASTER token after a volatile trading session that sent the price above $0.71 before crashing back to $0.48 in just over 15 minutes.

Leung attributed the volatility to liquidations of highly leveraged positions, which he said were “likely malicious,” although the team was still investigating the incident.

Leung also said Aster’s operations were functioning normally and that the episode was unrelated to ASTER market-making arrangements.

The token later recovered to around $0.55, up 4.5% over the previous 24 hours but still roughly 22.5% below its intraday peak.

The swings came alongside heavy derivatives trading. CoinGlass recorded about $1.29 billion in ASTER futures volume over 24 hours, up 121% from the day before, while 24-hour spot volume was $118.3 million, up 67% from the day before.

Meanwhile, tracked liquidations totaled approximately $9.1 million, including $5.97 million in long positions and $3.14 million in shorts.

ASTER is the native token of Aster, a decentralized exchange offering leveraged perpetual futures trading. The token supports governance and community rewards, while the protocol uses a portion of its revenue to buy back ASTER.

#4941. Artists Helen Marden and the Late Brice Marden Refashioned a Decommissioned 19th-Century Ferry Terminal

Over the course of their more than 50-year marriage, artist Helen Marden and her late husband, the eminent painter Brice Marden, amassed an international collection of seriously eccentric—and deeply magical—houses, gardens, and studios, each designed around the double helix of art and family. They bought their first house, a cliffside cottage on the Greek island of Hydra, in 1973, and since then real estate and art have been entwined in their life, art facilitating the purchase of property, the properties both catalyst and carapace for the evolution of their art. An Italianate villa in upstate New York, a pair of traditional riads in Marrakech, a hotel set among the ruins of a 19th-century sugar plantation in the Caribbean, a second house by the port in Hydra, a Renaissance Revival town house in Manhattan, a stately manse on a sprawling estate in Pennsylvania—every one of their idiosyncratic properties has its own fascinating history, reflected in its interior. The homes are all documented in the upcoming book Brice & Helen Marden: Houses, Gardens, Studios (Gagosian), edited by this writer, with photography by Stephen Kent Johnson.

Helen Marden in the living room. Chair and side table by the Campana Brothers. The chimney is clad in Clé Tile. A watercolor by Henri Michaux rests on the mantel.

The living room includes chairs by Marcel Wanders (far left) and the Campana Brothers. Sculpture at right by Michael Wang.

In the dining room, a Paavo Tynell pendant hangs above a rustic farm table with chairs by Jonathan Olivares and Maarten Baas. Amazonia marble covers one wall.

The Mardens were instinctively drawn to residences with endurance, places that have seen a few things, not newly built pleasure domes devoid of texture and soul. Swan House, the riparian hideaway in the middle of the Hudson River, epitomizes the couple’s embrace of the strange and the enchanted. Accessed by a long, narrow causeway that leads off the shore of Saugerties, the structure was built in 1877 as part of Long Dock, a ship terminal and moorage for large vessels conveying people and goods the length of the Hudson. It was also a landing for ferries crossing the river between Saugerties and the village of Tivoli. One can see Swan House shimmering in the landscape from the edge of Rose Hill, the Marden family’s 19th-century Tivoli estate.

The Depression, along with the advent of more efficient modes of transport, scuppered the last of the large ships landing at Long Dock in the early 1930s, but ferries continued to make the two-minute trip across the water until 1938. After that, the structure was decommissioned from professional service and sold, becoming the only privately owned peninsula on the Hudson. The Mardens purchased the property in 2011, but it took a full decade before they engaged designer Daniel Sachs and architect Kevin Lindores of the AD100 firm Sachs Lindores Architecture, Interiors to execute a full-scale renovation.

The primary bath features custom zellige tiles, a vintage Vistosi chandelier, an antique marble sink, and a tub by The Water Monopoly.

Sachs Lindores remodeled the entry with a windscreen of glass brick. The chair is by Gaetano Pesce.

Yellow zellige tiles meet a pale pink Aga range beneath a custom zinc hood in the kitchen. The pendant light is by Adolf Loos from WOKA.

Brice Marden’s For Helen – Valentine 2019 (2015–18) hangs on the black-tiled fireplace tower that mediates the bedroom and living room.

A custom bed is flanked by an antique French console table and a Charles Rohlfs hall chair. Lighting includes a Franz West chandelier and a floor lamp by Achille and Pier Giacomo Castiglioni.

“Early on we demolished a few walls to open up the views, but it was pretty raw. We’d just go over and watch the boats go by,” Helen recalls. “You have a completely different relationship with the river when you’re sitting in the middle of it.”

Given the famously cultivated, idiosyncratic tastes of the homeowners, the collaboration between Sachs Lindores and their clients was unusually fertile. Together, they reimagined the derelict building as a trick box for trapping the natural light that bounces off the water on all sides, with reflective, lead-coated copper siding and a zinc roof on the exterior and fields of luminous plaster, tile, and marble within. “Helen never shies away from a bold gesture. At first, she told us to paint the house red, but when we presented the idea of a glistening metal-clad box with light dancing on the skin, she immediately went for it,” Sachs recalls. “Brice and Helen were very interconnected in their aesthetic tastes and decision making,” Lindores adds. “As you might imagine, they studied every paint color and trim detail.”

Ruins of the 19th-century dock remain alongside the house.

A guest room is outfitted with a Pierre Jeanneret daybed, lounge chairs by Monika Mulder for IKEA, a vintage Kevin Walz standing lamp, and a Mathieu Matégot magazine rack. Painting by Helen Marden.

Sigmar Polke’s Untitled (1998) hangs above a vintage Walter Knoll sofa in the first-floor sitting room.

While retaining the shape of its original architectural envelope, the house was essentially rebuilt, with new window configurations, open circulation, and an exposed ceiling, all in service of maximizing watery vistas and ease of use. On the lofty upper level, the existing chimney, newly revealed in its full floor-to-ceiling height, was clad in glossy black brick tile. The monolithic form now feels like a high altar in a temple of light. Custom zellige tiles imported from Fez cover the walls of the kitchen and baths, where Helen’s genius for juxtaposing colors and materials comes into high relief.

The polyglot ensemble of furnishings hews closely to the signature Marden mix: Moroccan rugs; bold contemporary designs by the likes of Gaetano Pesce, Marcel Wanders, and the Campana Brothers; humble American folk pieces; 20th-century classics by Paavo Tynell, Adolf Loos, and Pierre Jeanneret; and a few Continental antiques to leaven the brew. “I just pick things that I like, things that make me happy,” Helen says, pooh-poohing the idea of some grand decorative vision. Her designers would demur. “She has an incredible eye,” Sachs insists. “The more crazy Helen would get, the more Brice liked it.”

#4940. The Week Jensen Huang Fell: How a Spy Scandal Toppled Nvidia’s CEO

The Week Jensen Huang Fell: How a Spy Scandal Toppled Nvidia’s CEO

In the span of a few days, the most powerful executive in artificial intelligence went from untouchable to unemployed.

Jensen Huang, who built Nvidia from a graphics-card maker into the roughly $4 trillion engine of the AI boom, resigned Thursday after 33 years, felled not by a bad earnings report but by a scandal with the texture of a spy thriller.

Here’s how a defining week for AI, and by extension crypto, unfolded.

The crisis began with reports that Huang had grown close to Alice Huang (no relation), an executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed, one of Nvidia’s largest Southeast Asian customers, whom U.S. intelligence officials reportedly suspected of gathering information for Beijing.

For a company at the center of the U.S.-China chip fight and a looming debate over tighter export controls, the national-security overtones were impossible to ignore. Nvidia’s board opened an independent investigation into whether Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence operation, though it said it had no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised.

On Nov. 8, Huang apologized for being “unfaithful” to his wife, acknowledging the relationship.

What might have been a single lapse quickly snowballed. The New York Times reported a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of health care, who reported to Huang.

By Thursday, Huang was out as president, CEO and board member. Chief financial officer Colette Kress was named interim CEO, and longtime director Harvey Jones took over as board chair. The abruptness stunned an industry accustomed to treating Huang as synonymous with Nvidia itself.

Then, a day after his resignation, Reuters reported a third relationship, an “intimate” one with a second unnamed Nvidia employee, citing two people familiar with the matter. Reuters did not identify her, and Nvidia declined to comment. Huang has acknowledged only the Alice Huang affair, deflecting when the Times asked about Powell.

Nvidia’s stock cratered more than 14% at the open on Thursday before clawing back to close down about 4.6%. Wall Street quickly sorted winners from losers: rivals like AMD and Broadcom fared better, while companies tightly tied to Nvidia’s business took the brunt.

The reaction underscored how much of the AI trade, and the crypto market that increasingly moves with it, rests on a single company.

The scandal then rippled outward. Singapore barred Alice Huang from returning to the country, Alibaba shares slid on reports it was a Megaspeed customer, and congressional committees had already sought a closed-door briefing from Huang over his Megaspeed ties.

The loudest names in tech, AI and crypto called the ouster a blunder, with one founder branding it “a massive mistake.” Inside Nvidia, an internal suggestion-box post drew more than 1,600 comments, many pleading for Huang’s return. “If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss,” one wrote.

The board has moved on regardless. According to the Financial Times, it approached Arm CEO Rene Haas, a former Nvidia executive who led Arm through its 2023 IPO, as a potential successor, though Nvidia called speculation about any candidate “premature.” Analysts have also floated Nvidia’s Ian Buck and AMD’s Lisa Su.

Nvidia’s chips power the AI data centers whose explosive buildout has become intertwined with crypto, from AI-agent commerce to the mining firms pivoting to host AI workloads.

Leadership turmoil at the company that supplies the picks and shovels ripples outward, and it lands amid a broader reckoning over AI’s role in everything from cyberattacks to data-center politics.

Nvidia reports earnings Nov. 17, with Kress at the helm and the permanent CEO search still underway, a nervous moment for a market already rattled by a sharp week-ending selloff.

For now, the company that defined the AI era is leaderless at the top, its founder’s legacy clouded by scandal, and the question hanging over Silicon Valley is whether anyone can fill the role Huang made uniquely his own.

#4937. Harvard, Dartmouth keep Bitcoin ETF stakes steady as fund gains 42% in Q3

Harvard’s gold ETF share count rose nearly 20-fold in Q3, while Dartmouth kept its positions in Solana and Ether staking ETFs unchanged.

Harvard University and Dartmouth College reported unchanged share counts in BlackRock’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) at the end of the third quarter as a Bitcoin rally lifted the value of both stakes.

Harvard held 3.04 million shares of the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT), worth about $144.1 million at the end of September, while Dartmouth reported 201,531 shares valued at $9.5 million, according to Form 13F filings with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC).

IBIT gained about 42% in Q3 as Bitcoin (BTC) rallied nearly 43%. Both posted their strongest quarterly gains of 2026 through September.

Harvard has reported the same IBIT share count since the end of March, after cutting its holdings by 43% in Q1 and 21% in Q4 2025. Dartmouth has reported the same 201,531 shares since first disclosing its Bitcoin ETF position in Q4 2025.

Harvard increased its share count in the State Street’s SPDR Gold Shares ETF (GLD) nearly 20-fold in Q3, to 1.16 million from 58,862. The position was worth $442.2 million at the end of September.

Dartmouth again reported 304,803 shares of the Bitwise Solana Staking ETF and 178,148 shares of the Grayscale Ethereum Staking ETF. The positions were worth $4.9 million and $4.5 million, respectively, at quarter-end.

The steady university holdings contrasted with moves by major financial companies. Jane Street reduced its IBIT shares by 19% in Q3, while JPMorgan Chase increased the number of shares it held by 9.6%, according to their latest quarterly filings.

#4936. Where Does Joe Burrow Live? Unpacking the Bengals Quarterback’s Real Estate Portfolio

He signed a record-breaking contract with the Cincinnati Bengals in 2023, but where does Joe Burrow live when he’s not on the field?

The NFL quarterback was born in Ames, Iowa, in 1996. His family moved to Ohio when he was eight, and he has resided mostly in the Buckeye State ever since. Burrow played for Ohio State University from 2015 to 2017, then transferred as a graduate student to Louisiana State University, where he won the Heisman Trophy shortly before graduating in December 2019. He was drafted into the NFL the following year by his hometown Bengals, with whom he inked a $36.1 million, four-year contract—plus a $23.9 million signing bonus. Three years later Burrow signed a $275 million five-year extension, making him the highest-paid player in NFL history at the time, according to ESPN.

It’s unsurprising, then, that Burrow has put down roots in the Buckeye State. Below, we’re examining the quarterback’s real estate portfolio.

In May 2020, a month after being selected first overall in the NFL Draft, Burrow revealed that he was spending the pandemic lockdown in his old bedroom at his parents’ house in The Plains, Ohio, just outside of Athens. Interior design apparently wasn’t at the forefront of his mind; the room still had his childhood Star Wars curtains and posters on the wall, though he had given the bed a king-size upgrade in high school, replacing the Star Wars comforter that once rounded out the room’s theme. According to The Sun, the 1997-built residence sits on a cul-de-sac and has four bedrooms and three bathrooms.

The Bengals play at Paycor Stadium, formerly known as Paul Brown Stadium.

In June 2020, Burrow spent $835,000 on his first house: a 2,900-square-foot Cincinnati dwelling just five miles from the Bengals’ home stadium. Located in the city’s historic Columbia-Tusculum area, the two-story property was built in 2014. Further details on the dwelling are scant—including whether or not Burrow still owns it. However, its location would make it a convenient place to stay during the NFL season.

#4934. DBS, JPMorgan’s Kinexys launch 24/7 transfers of tokenized deposits

Ant International is the first customer to use the service, which lets clients transfer US dollar deposits between Singapore and the US.

DBS and JPMorgan launched a service on Monday that lets clients transfer tokenized US dollar deposits between the two banks around the clock.

Tokenized deposits are bank deposits represented on the blockchain. The service connects DBS Token Services with JPMorgan’s Kinexys platform for transfers between Singapore and the US, according to a press release.

Ant International became the first client to use service to manage its cash across markets.

The launch supports the Monetary Authority of Singapore’s Project BLOOM, which aims to make it easier to use tokenized deposits and regulated stablecoins for payments in Singapore and across borders. The central bank launched the project in October 2025.

DBS and JPMorgan co-lead Project BLOOM’s working group on transfers between blockchain platforms. The two banks first revealed plans to develop a blockchain-based tokenization framework for onchain transfers in November 2025.

More than half of the 422 business leaders DBS surveyed across 11 markets said their companies used tokenized deposits or planned to do so, according to the bank’s Oct. 28 report.
