#4933. Bitcoin ETFs Lose $2.1 Billion in November as Outflow Streak Hits Seven Days

Bitcoin ETFs Lose $2.1 Billion in November as Outflow Streak Hits Seven Days

Bitcoin ETF investors are still heading for the exits, and the price slide is speeding up.

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $185.4 million on Friday, according to Decrypt’s ETF tracker, extending their outflow streak to seven straight trading days.

The funds lost about $829 million this week after shedding nearly $1.3 billion the week before, bringing November’s outflows to roughly $2.1 billion.

The selling so far this month has been relentless. The funds bled $1.4 billion over five sessions earlier in the streak as investors cut risk. Cumulative net inflows since launch have slipped to $55.3 billion, and total net assets fell to $94.9 billion, below the $100 billion mark they held through much of October.

Other crypto funds haven’t escaped the rout. Ethereum ETFs took in a modest $2.3 million on Friday, snapping a six-day outflow streak, but still lost about $336 million on the week and roughly $702 million so far in November. Solana ETFs shed about $28 million this week, though they retain $1.6 billion in cumulative net inflows.

Spot crypto ETFs hold the underlying asset, such as Bitcoin or Ethereum, and trade on stock exchanges like ordinary shares, giving investors exposure through a regular brokerage account. Bitcoin funds have drawn $55.3 billion in net inflows since debuting in January 2024. Because issuers report flows daily and much of the money comes from institutions and financial advisers, the figures have become a closely watched gauge of investor sentiment.

The outflows have tracked a sharp sell-off. Bitcoin traded near $64,500 over the weekend, down 6% over 24 hours and about 15% on the week, according to CoinGecko, and it has lost more than 22% over the past month.

Ethereum fell 8.4% to about $1,790, and Solana dropped 12% to roughly $70. Total crypto market value slid 4.5% to about $2.3 trillion.

Bitcoin hit a 12-week low this week as fears of further Federal Reserve rate hikes erased its summer rally, and concentrated liquidations pushed the price below $69,000 earlier in the week.

Not every institution is retreating. JPMorgan’s holdings of Bitcoin and crypto-linked securities are approaching $1 billion, according to its most recent SEC filing. However, those purchases predate the more recent market downturn.

#4930. BTC price tags 18-week low into Nvidia earnings: Three things to know in Bitcoin this week

Bitcoin traders awaited Nvidia Q3 earnings after BTC price selling pressure saw a dip below $62,000 at the weekend.

Bitcoin (BTC) traded around $64,500 early Monday after a sell-off in thin weekend trading sent it to a four-month low of $61,950.

Data from TradingView showed that BTC/USD fell to $61,950 on Bitstamp, a level last seen on July 13. The pair briefly rebounded to $66,000 before easing to around $64,500 during Monday’s Asian trading session.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The drop pushed Bitcoin below its 200-week simple moving average (SMA) of $68,186. This cemented concerns about Bitcoin’s rebound to $87,000, which began in August, as flipping the 200-week SMA from resistance to support has been a prerequisite for previous bear-market recoveries.

“Deviating below the 200-week SMA has historically been the key to building out a Bear Market bottom formation,” crypto trader and analyst Rekt Capital said Saturday on X.

BTC/USD one-week chart with 200-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

From Friday to 10 am UTC on Monday, Bitcoin open interest fell by around 9.1% to $20.98 billion, according to onchain analytics platform CryptoQuant.

A CryptoQuant contributing analyst drew comparisons to similar open interest drawdowns in February and June, considering whether Bitcoin was forming a local bottom.

“If the flush is complete, a bottoming zone may be near. If shorts keep piling up, even a modest rally could squeeze them. The $59K-$60K summer low remains the next key test,” the analyst wrote on Sunday.

Bitcoin open interest. Source: CryptoQuant

Bitcoin perpetual futures funding rates briefly turned negative near the weekend low, meaning traders with short positions paid those with long positions.

Bitcoin funding rates. Source: CryptoQuant

Nvidia remains in the spotlight this week as the tech giant reports third-quarter results after US markets close Tuesday amid ongoing controversy over former CEO Jensen Huang.

On Tuesday, interim CEO Colette Kress will lead the company’s earnings call. Nvidia previously forecast quarterly revenue of around $108 billion, and analysts expect earnings per share to nearly double year over year.

Huang is reportedly considering a return to Nvidia. He resigned last week after reports that he had an affair with a chip executive whom US authorities suspect of spying for China. Beijing denied the espionage allegation.

The Financial Times reported that a “war room” of tech CEOs and others was lobbying for his reinstatement, while an open letter calling for his return has garnered more than 165,000 signatories, including Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk.

“A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I’m signing it too,” Musk said in an X post on Friday.

The efforts face friction from US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who said that Nvidia should hire its next CEO from outside the company.

“This makes me think that as the board conducts a search for a new leader that ​they need to go outside the ​company, that just [with] what was going on inside ‌the ⁠company, they need a dramatic shakeup,” he told Fox News.

Nvidia shares closed Friday at $162.85, down 12.7% for the week. On Thursday, shares fell to $148.80, their lowest since mid-2025, before recovering some of their losses.

Nvidia stock one-week chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Nvidia headlines rattled tech stocks and crypto markets last week. Investors will be watching the company’s outlook for signs of how demand for AI chips is holding up.

Fed officials will speak throughout the week as traders look for clues about the December rate decision.

Related: Here’s what happened in crypto today

Warsh will deliver opening remarks at the Fed’s Productivity and Jobs Task Force Conference in San Francisco at 9 am PT (5 pm UTC) on Monday. He will also moderate two panels and a fireside chat.

On Wednesday, the Fed will release minutes of its Oct. 27–28 policy meeting. Officials left rates unchanged at 3.75% to 4%, and Warsh cited “resilient” domestic spending in the face of global geopolitical uncertainty.

After the latest economic data, markets fully priced in a quarter-point rate hike from the Fed in December. USD/JPY one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

“With the yen perched precariously at 160 per dollar, a combination of sluggish domestic growth and sticky price pressures, if they were to materialize, would complicate life for both the Bank of Japan and the Ministry of Finance,” economist Mohamed El-Erian wrote over the weekend.

#4929. The Six Bells Madewell Collection 2026

Whimsical design flourishes, cozy interiors, and close proximity to upstate antiquing all make The Six Bells Countryside Inn one of the best weekend escapes in the Empire State. Now, thanks to Madewell, you never have to check out. Launching today, the two brands have joined forces on a 28-piece holiday capsule of heirloom-worthy wardrobe and home essentials inspired by The Six Bells’ vintage aesthetic. Pieces specifically pay homage to the hotel’s interiors through references to archival patterns and wallpaper prints found throughout the Inn’s rooms and communal spaces.

“We fell in love with [The Six Bells’] distinct, whimsical world—the mix of patterns, the nostalgic sentiment, and the personality in every detail,” says Madewell creative director Steven Cateron. During initial planning for the clothing brand’s holiday collection, the hotel in Rosendale, New York became an unexpected source of inspiration for Cateron and his team because of the shared appreciation for heritage and storytelling. “Each piece has intention, whether it’s to wear day-to-day or for a celebration, a gifting moment for a loved one or hostess, or pajama pieces that lean into cozy season,” he says.

Sock slippers from the collaboration and Madewell’s classic denim make a perfect pairing.

Founded by Audrey Gelman in 2025, who designed the hotel in collaboration with interiors firm GrecoDeco, The Six Bells Countryside Inn has quickly risen in popularity—even earning a spot in AD’s list of Great Design Hotels last year. “This project is so rooted in a shared love of the decorative arts and global human history,” designer Adam Greco previously told AD. Design-wise, maximalism dominates the interiors. Picture: rooms wrapped in fretwork wallpaper and deep cinnabar paint, with pistachio window shades and striped seating.

The collection also builds on the fantasy of Barrow’s Green, the fictional English village dreamed up by Gelman that includes its own imaginary landmarks and distinct locals (including a town gossip and poignant editor). “It was so interesting to broaden our imaginations and think about how The Six Bells’ style could be brought to life through clothes,” says Gelman.

A cheeky message makes this classic needlepoint pillow pop.

At the same time, the collaboration was an opportunity to venture into Madwell’s less-explored categories—specifically the home space. The collection’s interior goods are a practice in curated nostalgia: There are holiday ornaments you’ll reach for year after year, scented candles and soaps to integrate into your daily winter routine, and cozy pajamas inspired by vintage sleep silhouettes. “If it’s something we don’t currently offer, we want to work with people that do the category best,” says Cateron. “The Six Bells team were the perfect partners to bring this collection’s home accessories to life.”

Still, versatility isn’t lost in the assortment. In designing the clothing, Cateron made sure to include “pieces to wear upstate, in the city, or for day-to-day celebrations.” Classic denim jackets are neutral staples that you can reach for any day of the week, while Fair Isle wool knits are seasonally appropriate while still feeling adaptable enough to wear after the holidays. “There’s a real point of view behind every detail [at The Six Bells Countryside Inn], from the interiors to collected vintage pieces,” says Cateron. “This collection captures that sensibility in a way that felt authentic to both brands.”

The collection is available to shop on Madewell.com and in Madewell stores starting today, November 12.

“There’s so much character in every detail at The Six Bells, and we wanted to bring that sensibility into each piece,” says Madewell creative director Steven Cateron.

#4928. How Wall Street Is Racing to Claim the Onchain Dollar

How Wall Street Is Racing to Claim the Onchain Dollar

For years, stablecoins were crypto’s business, dominated by Tether and Circle and used mostly to trade digital assets. That era is ending.

Over the past few months, and especially the past few weeks, the biggest names in traditional finance have moved en masse to claim a piece of the onchain dollar. Here’s the map of a land grab that’s accelerating fast.

The clearest signal came this week, when Goldman Sachs, Citigroup, Santander, and Deutsche Bank were reported to be among banks committing roughly $270 million to a joint dollar-stablecoin venture codenamed “Project Faro.”

The capital funds five years of operations for a U.S.-incorporated issuer targeting a 2027 launch, part of a 21-institution coalition that has swelled from an initial group of 10. The goal is explicit: challenge Tether and Circle, which together still control about 85% of a roughly $290 billion market.

While the consortium plans, SoFi has shipped. Earlier this week, its SoFiUSD, billed as the first stablecoin from a U.S. national bank, expanded onto Polygon this month alongside Ethereum and Solana, reaching the bank’s 17 million-plus members.

SoFi also launched SoFi Earn, a tokenized deposit paying 4.40% yield that converts into SoFiUSD, and already settles its entire card program over Mastercard’s network.

Meanwhile, the federally chartered crypto bank Anchorage Digital has positioned itself as a settlement backbone.

Anchorage’s tokens can now settle transactions on Visa’s network, starting with Western Union’s USDPT, and it recently anchored both sides of the U.S.-Mexico stablecoin corridor, custodying tokens across one of the world’s largest remittance routes.

Mastercard has been the most aggressive incumbent among payment giants.

It launched a Global Money Platform with acquired stablecoin firm BVNK, rolled out a Stablecoin Security tool to screen payments for fraud, and unveiled Agent Pay for Machines, letting AI agents transact in stablecoins.

Visa, meanwhile, has expanded its own stablecoin settlement program across multiple blockchains.

The crypto-native incumbents, not to be left out, are also racing to entrench.

Circle struck a deal to bring USDC settlement to banking rails in Latin America, starting with a Colombia pilot, part of a broader expansion into dozens of emerging markets.

The catalyst is regulation. The GENIUS Act, signed in 2025, created the first U.S. framework for dollar-pegged tokens, giving regulated institutions a clear path into a business they had largely watched from the sidelines.

The Donald Trump administration has framed dollar stablecoins as a tool to extend the dollar’s reach and global hegemony, and banks that once dismissed crypto now see a settlement business they can’t afford to cede.

Much of this remains early. Project Faro has no token, no CEO, and no final name. Tether and Circle still dominate, and bank-issued tokens face a steep climb to win share.

But the direction is unmistakable: the onchain dollar, once crypto’s turf, is now contested ground, with Wall Street, the card networks and the stablecoin incumbents all racing to own the rails money moves on.

#4927. Singapore, Germany test cross-border FX settlement with CBDCs

Project Simbolon aims to reduce the cost and time of cross-border settlement and promote common standards for payments and FX

The Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS) and the Deutsche Bundesbank used wholesale central bank digital currencies (CBDCs) to test settlement of euro–Singapore dollar trades across their separate digital asset test environments.

As part of Project Simbolon, the two central banks used payment-versus-payment settlement, meaning the final transfers of each currency depended on the final transfer of the other, MAS said in a Monday press release. Zero-knowledge proofs allowed each system to verify whether settlement conditions were met without directly integrating the central bank ledgers.

The project aims to make cross-border transfers between Singapore and Germany faster and cheaper. It also aims to promote common standards for cross-border payments, foreign exchange and securities transactions tied to tokenized assets.

The test was the first milestone under a November 2025 memorandum of understanding between the two central banks on tokenization and cross-border settlement. It also advances broader efforts to make tokenized financial markets more efficient under Project Guardian, a tokenization initiative MAS launched in May 2022.

“Project Simbolon paves the way for eliminating settlement risk in global foreign exchange transactions,” said Sabine Mauderer, first deputy governor of the Bundesbank.

In September, the Eurosystem launched Pontes, a system that lets financial institutions settle wholesale tokenized asset transactions in central bank money.

#4924. HKMA, Swift explore tokenized deposits for cross-border bank payments

HKMA and Swift will explore linking Project Ensemble with Swift’s ledger for cross-border payments using tokenized deposits in multiple currencies.

The Hong Kong Monetary Authority (HKMA) and Swift will explore connecting their payment infrastructure to support cross-border transfers using tokenized bank deposits, the authority said Monday.

The work will examine links between Swift’s blockchain-based ledger and the settlement infrastructure of Project Ensemble. Participating banks include Bank of China (Hong Kong), Standard Chartered Bank (Hong Kong) and HSBC, according to the announcement.

Tokenized deposits are bank balances represented on a blockchain. They remain claims on the issuing commercial bank, unlike central bank digital currencies, which are claims on a central bank. The proposed Swift connection would explore how separate payment systems could work together across borders in multiple currencies.

The collaboration follows a Nov. 2 upgrade to EnsembleTX, the pilot phase of Project Ensemble, enabling banks to settle tokenized deposit transactions around the clock using tokenized central bank money. The pilot launched in November 2025 and initially relied on Hong Kong’s dollar-denominated real-time gross settlement system.

In July, Swift said its blockchain-based ledger was ready for initial use and that it was preparing to pilot tokenized cross-border payments with 17 major banks, including Citi, DBS and HSBC.

Related: DBS, JPMorgan’s Kinexys launch 24/7 transfers of tokenized deposits

#4923. Nickey Kehoe’s New Collection With The Lacquer Company Takes Its Cues From Beloved Antiques

In 2024, AD heralded the return of lacquer. In our April 2026 issue, cover star Athena Calderone was pictured leaning over an enormous burgundy-lacquered kitchen island, and in May, cover star Mark Grattan showed off his parrot green lacquer walls. Today’s collaboration between AD100 design firm Nickey Kehoe and The Lacquer Company brings the glossy finish down to a slightly more manageable scale, applying it to coffee tables, desks, trays, and tissue boxes. Nickey Kehoe founders Todd Nickey and Amy Kehoe designed the 30-piece collection with The Lacquer Company founder James Lowther, drawing on antique furniture and objects the three have collected over the years.

The Bonne Nuit nightstand in Cassis staged among various Nickey Kehoe textiles and an upholstered headboard.

The Florin Tray in Carreaux & Marine Blue is styled with all small pieces from the Nickey Kehoe collection.

Many of the pieces, as is often true of Nickey Kehoe’s in-house catalog, draw on the founders’ personal vintage collections. The Pastis side table is based on a bamboo folding table that belongs to Nickey. The Hugo desk is a Louis XVI–style writing desk, with tapered, fluted legs, brass details, and three soft-close drawers in a bright red lacquer. The colorways sound like a very civilized, French late lunch: cassis and lettuce green, carreaux and marine blue, khaki and chocolate brown. The Jules catchall tray comes with a matching tissue box cover for the powder room.

Lacquer trays, tissue boxes, side tables, and the collection’s coffee table are staged together here to show the full line.

The Pastis side table references a vintage style with the lacquer and bamboo combination reminiscent of Regency Chinoiserie.

Lowther bought his first piece of lacquer, a burnt-orange betel box, while traveling in Burma in 1999, and it’s still one of his favorite things. He later found the craftspeople he was looking for in Saigon, and every piece from The Lacquer Company is still made there in Sơn Mài lacquer, a Vietnamese art form that goes back centuries. The process is lengthy: Wood is first sealed with a resin called Sinh. It’s then coated repeatedly with natural lacquer mixed with ground mountain rock, alluvial soil, and cashew nut shell oil. Each coat is wet-sanded until it’s smooth before the next coat is applied. “There is no prescribed drying time, no set number of layers,” according to the company. That makes the eight- to 12-week lead time on larger pieces easier to forgive.

Nickey Kehoe’s goal is for its furniture to feel “as if it was acquired, not purchased.” With these pieces so rooted in vintage styles and techniques, they make good on their promise.

The collection is available to shop on NickeyKehoe.com.

#4920. Elon Musk Joins 160,000 Backing Petition to Bring Jensen Huang Back to Nvidia

Elon Musk Joins 160,000 Backing Petition to Bring Jensen Huang Back to Nvidia

Elon Musk has thrown his support behind a campaign to reinstate Jensen Huang as Nvidia's CEO, as the ousted chipmaker co-founder reportedly weighs a comeback just days after resigning.

"A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I'm signing it too," the Tesla and SpaceX CEO tweeted on Friday. Musk had previously said it would be "an honor" to hire Huang.

A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I’m signing it too

— Elon Musk (@elonmusk) November 13, 2026

The "Stand with Jensen" open letter calls on Nvidia's board to bring Huang back while an independent investigation establishes the facts. It has drawn more than 160,000 signatures, including Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney and Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan, CNBC reported, though the site notes that signatories' affiliations are self-reported and unverified. More than 7,500 signatories identified themselves as Nvidia employees, The Guardian reported, a figure it said was equivalent to 18% of the company's workforce.

The campaign follows internal calls from Nvidia staff for Huang's return.

Huang has discussed the possibility of returning with confidants, the Financial Times reported, citing multiple people familiar with the matter. One ally told the newspaper they were assembling a "war room" of Silicon Valley executives to lobby for his reinstatement and would seek support from the White House, adding that the effort had Huang's backing.

Even supporters acknowledged the obstacles. "Having an affair with a subordinate is a fireable offence," one person involved in the effort told the FT.

Two major Nvidia shareholders, Altimeter Capital's Brad Gerstner and Atreides Management's Gavin Baker, urged the board in an open letter on Friday to leave "all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang."

Huang resigned as Nvidia's president, CEO and board member on Thursday, capping a week of reports of affairs with Alice Huang, a former executive at Nvidia customer Megaspeed whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for China, and with an Nvidia executive who reported to him. Chief financial officer Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO.

The campaign faces resistance in Washington. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said on Sunday that Nvidia should look outside the company for its next CEO. "They need a dramatic shake-up," he told Fox News.

Bessent said on Sunday Morning Futures that questions over Nvidia's compliance with U.S. export restrictions were the bigger issue, and that the company should be judged on whether it had "intentionally ignored the warning signs" of shell companies in Singapore. He said he wanted answers "in the next 10 days from the board and from the outside counsel." Huang also faces congressional inquiries into Nvidia's chip exports to China, as well as a proposed shareholder class action.

Nvidia's board has approached Arm CEO Rene Haas as a potential successor, the FT has reported. "We are in the process of selecting a search firm for the NVIDIA CEO role. We have not formally engaged any potential candidates," an Nvidia spokesperson told CNBC.

Analysts are divided on Huang's chances. "We see no chance Jensen comes back to Nvidia as CEO," Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives & Co. said in a note cited by CNBC, while Seaport Research Partners' Jay Goldberg said Huang could stage "a second act" if he gets through his legal troubles. Former White House communications director Anthony Scaramucci was blunter: "The FBI doesn't take petitions," he tweeted.

Nvidia reports quarterly earnings on Tuesday, the first under Kress.

#4919. Binance Pay Later offers credit backed by Bitcoin, Ether and BNB holdings

Eligible users can access up to 1,000 USDC in credit against their Bitcoin, Ether or BNB for purchases via Binance Card or QR Pay.

Binance said Monday it would offer eligible users in Argentina and Mexico up to 1,000 USDC in spending credit through Pay Later, a payment feature backed by their cryptocurrency holdings.

Pay Later allows eligible users to access up to 1,000 USDC (USDC) in spending power against their Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and BNB (BNB) token holdings as collateral, without selling the underlying assets, Binance said in an announcement shared with Cointelegraph. Borrowing is limited to 50% of the eligible collateral’s value, meaning $2,000 in BTC is required to access a maximum $1,000 credit line.

Purchases are interest-free until the repayment deadline on the first of the following month. Binance’s published FAQ lists a one-day grace period and interest of 0.1% per day, compounding daily, on overdue balances.

Borrowing lets users make purchases without first selling their crypto, but falling prices can put those holdings at risk. Binance can liquidate collateral when the outstanding loan reaches 90% of its value, even before repayment is due. It will initially launch for users in Argentina and Mexico, with a gradual rollout planned for additional markets.

“Traditional consumer credit often relies on income documentation and credit history, which leaves people who hold assets but have no formal credit file with fewer options,” said Thomas Gregory, VP of payments and fiat at Binance, adding that asset-backed credit offers an alternative.

#4916. How One Designer Brought a Discerning Eye to Her Deeply Personal Manhattan Apartment

Ask an interior designer about their most difficult client, and there’s a good chance they’ll name themselves. Dani Dennis spent more than a decade at Mark Cunningham Inc. before founding her own New York–based practice, Danielette Dennis Interiors, translating other people’s tastes into rooms. “I’m such a decisive person,” she says of her approach to projects ranging from a traditional Brooklyn Heights townhouse to a sleek Park Avenue pied-à-terre. But when it came to decorating her own Gramercy Park apartment that she shares with her husband and two daughters, Dennis had no client brief to answer to—and no one to make the final call but herself.

When the family moved in three years ago, the apartment had plenty going for it: tall windows, handsome moldings, and an airy living and dining area. Dennis left the architecture alone, putting furniture, textiles, and her growing art collection to work instead. Benjamin Moore’s Steam keeps the walls a warm white, though the rooms are anything but hands-off. “I almost wanted it to feel like a gallery,” she says. “But, obviously, it’s lived in, and I have kids, so nothing is too precious.”

A portrait by Patrick Eugène, sourced via Mariane Ibrahim, anchors one end of the living room. Nearby are an Alfred Grenander chair upholstered in Toyine Sellers fabric, a black mushroom side table by Emma Donnersberg, and Christian Woo’s Noga Cube side table.

A portrait of a young girl by Jerrell Gibbs hangs near a Pierre Augustin Rose Cadillac ottoman upholstered in Rosemary Hallgarten’s alpaca bouclé. Vintage Saint-Louis crystal, sourced at the Paris flea market, sits atop the credenza.

The living room’s opposing sofas encourage conversation. The sofa (right), upholstered in Pierre Frey wool and mohair, is by Marked NYC. The ceiling fixture is a reedition of Pierre Chareau’s Quart de Rond lamp, and the rug is by Scott Group Studio.

Joseph-André Motte chairs surround the dining table, while a 1960s Italian burlwood credenza from Naga Antiques sits beneath an abstract painting by Carmen Neely. The dining rug is by Crosby Street Studios.

A first-generation American of Liberian heritage, Dennis has built a collection of art that includes contemporary Black artists from across the diaspora. A painting of girls playing in a circle takes her back to her school days in Côte d’Ivoire. Others show Black women in moments of rest and repose. “I want my kids and my family to be surrounded by reflections of us,” she says. Her clients’ collections tell their own stories; this one tells hers. Black joy runs through the collection, but Dennis doesn’t shop by theme; she listens to her gut. “I buy what I love,” she says. “I don’t buy for decoration.”

Dennis is equally at ease with an English-country chintz and a graphic geometric. In her own home, she borrows a little from everywhere, favoring clean lines and unexpected combinations. A custom sofa in Sanderson velvet faces a curved Marked NYC design in Pierre Frey wool and mohair. Camel-colored lounge chairs play against the sharp geometry of a Roger Sprunger coffee table; a 1906 Josef Hoffmann side table and an early-20th-century Alfred Grenander chair join the mix. “When the harmony comes from shape and proportion and feeling, I think that’s what makes spaces dynamic,” she says.

An expressive Ferrari Sheppard painting, acquired through Massimo De Carlo in Milan, hangs above a custom sofa upholstered in Sanderson’s indigo Quartz velvet. Camel-colored chairs wear Holland & Sherry fabric. A glass-and-metal coffee table by Roger Sprunger joins a modernist stool by Charles Vandenhove and JM Szymanski’s slender Table No. 3.

Dennis took a moodier approach in the library, where the family gathers. The walls and ceiling are painted the same dark hue, creating a cozy retreat for her two daughters, who prefer sprawling on the soft rug to sitting on the sofa. Artwork includes a floral painting by Alexandria Tarver and Hazel Mphande’s Stone or Lavender (2021). The striped pillows are in Fortuny fabric.

The dining table seats 10. Dinner guests, however, are still forthcoming. “I haven't had people over yet, because in my head it’s not done,” Dennis says. Joseph-André Motte chairs surround the table, while a 1960s Italian burlwood credenza and a Crosby Street Studios rug anchor the dining area. The living room sofas were positioned for entertaining too, although the family’s day-to-day use of the space is less formal. For Dennis’s daughters, 10 and seven, the mohair-and-wool sofa comes with ground rules: “You guys can sit on it, you can perch, but if we want to play, let’s do it elsewhere.”

The library, on the other hand, is fair game. The girls can sprawl out with a book or watch TV, though they rarely bother with the sofa. “They’re always lying on the rug,” Dennis says. She painted the walls a deep olive-green, carrying it across the ceiling for an enveloping effect. The art also takes a moodier turn, with a floral painting by Alexandria Tarver and a black-and-white self-portrait by Hazel Mphande. The primary bedroom, meanwhile, provides a brighter, quieter escape. “I always wanted to be in a cloud,” the designer says of the neutral palette. The upholstered bed, 1970s steel bench in alpaca wool, and leather-bound nightstands supply the texture.

Since the photo shoot, Dennis has switched out accessories and rearranged objects. When she recently swapped a lamp in the living room, even her husband took notice. “Another one?” he asked. At work, Dennis knows when to call a room finished. At home, she’s beginning to wonder why she should.

“I always wanted to be in a cloud,” Dennis says of the primary bedroom, where texture takes precedence over color. A vintage 1970s steel bench with alpaca upholstery sits at the foot of the bed, while leather-clad nightstands add another tactile element. The ceramic lamp is by Christiane Perrochon, and the understated artwork above the bed is by Carmen Neely.

#4914. Vitalik Buterin Now ‘a Total Bull’ on leanPCS as Fast Track to Quantum-Safe Ethereum

Vitalik Buterin Now ‘a Total Bull’ on leanPCS as Fast Track to Quantum-Safe Ethereum

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has thrown his weight behind a proposal he says could make the network quantum-safe much faster than expected.

"Over the past few weeks I've gone from thinking leanPCS is total bull to being a total bull," Buterin tweeted on Monday, in a lengthy explainer on how Ethereum could handle the much larger signatures and proofs that quantum-resistant cryptography requires.

Over the past few weeks I've gone from thinking leanPCS is total bull to being a total bull. So let me explain some of these buzzwords in more detail.

At the core, 8288 is about hyper-scaling data and execution for any computation that needs to get performed on a piece of data…

— vitalik.eth (@VitalikButerin) November 16, 2026

In short, leanPCS is a proposed way for Ethereum to bundle many large, quantum-safe signatures and proofs into a single compact proof, so that the full data never has to be stored onchain. It is built on polynomial commitment schemes, or PCS, the core mathematical building block of STARK proofs.

Quantum computers are expected to eventually break the elliptic curve cryptography that secures Ethereum and Bitcoin accounts today. The quantum-safe replacements are far bulkier: Buterin said signatures would grow from 65 bytes to between 1 and 6 kilobytes, while zero-knowledge proofs would balloon from a few hundred bytes to around 128 kilobytes. "This is unsustainable to put on chain," he wrote.

Buterin's answer is EIP-8288, a draft proposal he co-authored with developer Thomas Coratger in June, which would aggregate those signatures and proofs before they ever reach a block.

Under the design, users publish their signatures or proofs, and network nodes repeatedly bundle everything they have seen into a single STARK, a type of zero-knowledge proof, that attests the underlying data is valid. These aggregate proofs are themselves combined until a single "master proof" verifies everything in a block. Only short claims, rather than the full signatures or proofs, would need to be stored onchain.

He laid out three ways to build it, depending on what users submit. The simplest, leanSPHINCS, would support a single type of quantum-safe, hash-based signature, costing users only a small amount of gas per signature, but it couldn't do anything else.

Buterin's preferred option, leanPCS, offers a third route. It would offer the same generality as leanSTARK but "could potentially be implemented very quickly," he said, because far fewer design choices are needed, and leanSTARK could then be added later.

"So there is a route to getting 'the cryptographic proof singularity' much faster than any of us expected," Buterin wrote, "and that route runs through 8288 with leanPCS."

Buterin linked to SPHINCS-, a family of hash-based signature schemes optimized for Ethereum, which was published on the Ethereum Research forum in June with input from Buterin and Ethereum Foundation cryptographers. The post shows that a standards-aligned quantum-safe signature can already be verified on Ethereum for about 150,000 gas, without any protocol change, compared with about 3,000 gas for an ECDSA signature today, according to an earlier Buterin estimate.

Under protocol-level aggregation, a future "leanSPHINCS" verification would cost about 3,000 gas, according to the post.

#4911. 7 Best Cheese Boards for Entertaining Centerpieces (2026)

The calendar pointing toward the holidays also means that cheese board season is here. The platters function as a key part of any festive gathering, serving as the meeting point for people to nibble and mingle before they take a seat. But it’s much more than a way to display finger foods. Often situated in a central location at a party, a cheese board works as a decorative design piece, and when done well, can even be a conversation starter. In fact, some boards themselves read as a still life, even after half the Brie is gone.

The most unique cheese boards combine material and shape, offering something distinct and memorable to house the bites (or function as a surface for slicing a baguette). Designers are creating beautiful centerpieces by carving wood into soft, stonelike forms or cutting marble into asymmetrical ovals. Ahead, find seven cheese boards to invest in now, each one pretty enough to steal the spotlight at any event.

Sarah Sherman Samuel is known for blending character with clean modern lines in her interiors, and her cheese board trio for Lulu and Georgia brings that same sensibility to the table. The set includes two marble boards and one made of acacia, allowing you to layer textures; situating soft cheeses on cool stone and bread on wood. The three sizes allow you to extend your spread down a long table for a crowd or cuddle everything close on the counter.

These amorphous-shaped boards by Danish brand Ferm Living are inspired by cairns, the stacks of stones that hikers leave as markers on the trail. Each is carved from solid ash that’s certified by the FSC—a third-party organization that confirms responsible sourcing of wood—and is given a carbonized finish that turns the wood a deep espresso shade. When you’re not using the boards to display cheese, you can stack them on a counter where they’ll look like a sculptural piece.

For a more artful approach to your cheese board, check out this tortoiseshell option from CB2. Portuguese artisans hand-finish each piece in a reactive glaze, so no two boards come out identical. The amber and chocolate swirls highlight both white cheeses and green accoutrements, such as green grapes and Granny Smith apples, for an especially striking display. And at roughly 10 by 18 inches, the long board runs neatly down the center of a table.

Serena & Lily has made this wave shape its signature, commonly appearing on its crested mirrors and nightstands, and this board brings that coastal edge to the table. The scalloped surface is the relaxed, beach-house counterpoint to the more architectural boards on this list. At 18 inches, the small size suits a cozy night in for two, while the large, 30-inch board option has room to house a full spread with all the accoutrements.

Red marble has been having a moment, showing up on bathroom vanities and living room floors. This tray is a low-lift way to incorporate the material in your space. Cut from deep red stone into a sleek oval with a thick rim, the color of this CB2 cheese board is inherently festive but will look elevated and at home in your kitchen year-round. Because it’s solid marble, the veining and color will vary from tray to tray.

At 13 by 18.5 inches, the generously sized Romie has room for the full spread, with space for several cheeses, cured meats, a cluster of grapes, and a stack of crackers that won’t slide off the edge. Its classic oval shape is made from acacia that’s stained a deep chocolate, bringing out the grain and hiding knife marks. It’s an Anthropologie exclusive and a beautiful board to reach for when you’re feeding more than a few people.

The artist Neil Cohen designed this 15-inch acacia block for the New Mexico–based brand Nambe, and the piece’s best feature is hidden in plain sight. The wood is carved with nooks that hold the knife and spreader flush with the surface, so the tools stay with the board instead of getting lost in a drawer, which is a pretty great feature. The clean shape is modern, making it an easy hostess gift.

#4910. Strategy buys $33M in Bitcoin, spends $228M on STRC buybacks

Strategy bought 485 Bitcoin for $33.4 million and spent $228.1 million repurchasing STRC preferred shares, largely funded by new common stock sales.

Michael Saylor’s Strategy bought 485 Bitcoin for $33.4 million last week while spending nearly seven times as much repurchasing STRC preferred shares.

The company purchased the Bitcoin (BTC) at an average price of $68,887 between Monday and Sunday, bringing its holdings to 850,849 BTC, according to a Monday filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission.

Strategy said it raised $244.7 million by selling 2.09 million common shares (MSTR), using $33.4 million to buy Bitcoin, $206.3 million to fund STRC repurchases and $5 million to its separate cash balance. It funded the remaining $21.8 million in buybacks with interest earned on its cash and short-term investments.

The company repurchased 2.28 million STRC shares during the week. STRC is Strategy’s variable-rate preferred stock, which pays regular dividends to shareholders.

Strategy reported a $4.75 billion US dollar reserve after using $18.9 million to pay preferred stock dividends. Its separate cash balance stood at $151.2 million as of Sunday.

The company also maintained STRC’s annual dividend rate at 12% for December, saying it intends to keep the rate steady until the shares demonstrate sustained trading near $100.

Strategy spent $188.3 million repurchasing STRC shares and $81.9 million buying Bitcoin the previous week.

Strategy’s MSTR stock fell 8.45% to $102.2 in Monday’s premarket trading, while STRC declined 0.78% to $99.1, according to Yahoo Finance. The losses followed MSCI’s confirmation last week that it would remove Strategy from its global equity indexes after the Nov. 30 market close.

#4909. German Lawmakers Back Customs Powers to Seize Crypto of 'Unclear Origin'

German Lawmakers Back Customs Powers to Seize Crypto of 'Unclear Origin'

Germany's parliament has approved sweeping new powers for the country's customs agency, allowing it to seize assets, including crypto, whose lawful origin is in doubt, even when no criminal investigation is underway.

The Bundestag passed the Customs Financial Justice Act, or Zollfinanzgerechtigkeitsgesetz, on Thursday, November 12, the German government said. Under the law, customs can more easily seize assets where there are "justified doubts" about their lawful origin, an instrument the government describes as a "suspicious wealth order." Customs will also be able to better monitor digital cross-border payment routes and secure crypto assets.

"With this, we are sending a clear signal to criminals: we are getting tougher," German Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil said, according to the government.

The powers apply to "significant" assets, which typically means those worth more than €100,000, according to the government's bill. Because owners of such assets are usually able to explain where they came from, while the state struggles to prove an illegal origin, the government argued that it is reasonable to ask owners to disclose that information. "Almost every law-abiding citizen will be able to explain where these assets came from," the bill's explanatory notes say, as summarized by the Bundestag's news service.

Klingbeil has said customs could hold assets for up to 180 days without a conviction, during which owners would have to show they were obtained legally. "The Porsche and the Rolex are then gone for now," he said, according to the Berliner Zeitung. Any permanent confiscation would require a court ruling. Following an amendment by the governing coalition, that decision will be made by a panel of finance court judges rather than a single judge.

The law also reorganizes Germany's customs administration, strengthens its Financial Intelligence Unit and gives customs a legal basis to use technologies such as artificial intelligence. It forms part of an action plan against organized crime that the government adopted on February 25, 2026.

Germany has seized large amounts of crypto before, and has been quick to sell it. In 2024, authorities in the state of Saxony began selling the nearly 50,000 BTC they had seized from operators of the movie piracy website Movie2K, moving hundreds of millions of dollars' worth at a time as the sell-off continued. Blockchain analytics firm Arkham later said authorities had failed to seize a further 45,000 BTC linked to the site. The new law would give customs a way to seize crypto of unclear origin without first having to tie it to a specific crime.

German authorities have also gone after laundering infrastructure. Last year, they joined Swiss police in shutting down Cryptomixer, a mixing service that had laundered more than $1.4 billion in Bitcoin since 2016. Separately, the finance ministry has drafted a 25% tax on crypto gains from 2027.

The Bundestag's finance committee backed the bill on Wednesday with votes from the governing CDU/CSU and SPD, as well as the far-right AfD. The Left Party voted against, and the Greens abstained.

The Greens said Germany's money laundering problem is estimated at €100 billion, and that the government's expectation of only €308 million in additional revenue showed the gap between its ambitions and the law. The Left Party said the confiscation process was "built for the cash courier, not the banker."

The law still needs to pass the Bundesrat, the upper house of parliament that represents Germany's states.

#4906. Banks are banning AI agents over security... and competition concerns

AI agents are set to automate everyday banking tasks — but banks aren’t ready to give up control of the customer relationship just yet.

Banks in the United Kingdom have drawn a line over how much access artificial intelligence should have to their customers’ money.

Four banks, including HSBC, Barclays, Lloyds, NatWest and Nationwide, have all moved to block or restrict third-party AI agents from accessing customer accounts, especially where they can make transactions without customer involvement.

There’s no evidence yet of AI agents accessing UK bank accounts just yet, but large lenders are worried about what happens if an automated system mismanages customers’ money — and who would be on the hook.

The banks might have other, unspoken concerns too.

Edgars Nemše, chief executive of GenLayer Foundation, an AI-focused blockchain infrastructure project, says routine banking tasks like paying bills, moving money and finding better rates could all be handed to agents.

Unfortunately for banks, this means agents would be able to seamlessly find customers a better deal elsewhere as soon as it’s published on the web.

“For plain accounts, that removes the switching costs and makes the space even more competitive,” Nemše tells Magazine.

That scenario could threaten more than just customer loyalty. In September, Apollo chief economist Torsten Slok warned about a possible “agentic bank run.”

If swarms of agents start moving household cash from low-yield checking accounts to alternatives offering higher returns en masse, that would drain banks of the low-cost funding they rely on to make loans. And the speed and scale “would be a problem for the entire financial system.”

Related: Coinbase CEO touts agentic finance as Base tops 100M AI payments

JPMorgan Chase chief executive Jamie Dimon has also warned against allowing external agents unrestricted access to bank accounts. “You don’t want rogue agents,” he said on an October earnings call.

Is an agentic bank run coming? Source: Apollo

Regardless of short-term moves to hold back the tide, AI agents look more and more likely to revolutionize banking within a few years.

Neeraj Makin, group head of strategy analytics at Emirates NBD, predicts agents will usher in a new era of around-the-clock banking and near-instant payments as normal by 2036.

“Forget about waiting for a payment for 24 hours or inter-country payments taking three days… all of that will be in seconds, atomic,” he says, with AI agents taking over most financial decisions.

Ferdinand Dabitz, chief executive of Augustus, an AI-native banking and payments company, sees a similar shift in how customers interact with financial institutions.

“Every consumer will have 10s and every business will have 100s of agents talking to their bank,” Dabitz says.

A human interface will still remain important for managing those agents and providing a layer of trust, “especially in commercial banking,” he says.

Related: Agents can trade 24/7 — but Yat Siu warns you could lose access at any time

Emirates NBD’s developer experience team argued in July that financial institutions should build their own frameworks for agentic AI, rather than allowing individual teams to develop agents without common controls.

Its Leap framework is designed to govern how agents authenticate with core banking systems, access sensitive data and carry out actions, with audit trails, monitoring and human oversight built in.

Emirates NBD says banks should build their own agentic frameworks. Source: Emirates NBD

However there are still major concerns around the reliability of agents to make major financial decisions on their own.

A recent Bank of England and FCA survey found that while 80% of responding firms had deployed AI agents, just 2% used agents with broad autonomy, identifying data security, third-party dependencies and risks like hallucinations among their biggest concerns.

The AI takeover likely won’t happen overnight; “it will come in steps,” Nemše says.

“Today I use an agent to review my own and my company’s finances every month and queue the payments, and I approve them. The next step is the natural one: once you trust it, you let it spend small amounts without asking.”

Routine bills, subscriptions and the agent’s own running costs would be among the first tasks to become autonomous, he says.

Dabitz says corporate treasury management could be an area delegated to agents, but says banks will need to be involved in developing these early applications.

While consumers may enjoy the upside of automating their finances, who’s on the hook when an agent makes a mistake?

Nemše says banks need to offer “delegated access as a normal feature” to avoid customers sharing logins with agents. Customers could set permissions and spending limits, and agents could make low-value transactions independently while requiring approval for larger amounts.

But existing dispute processes may struggle if agents begin making thousands of transactions on behalf of each customer. “Even a small share going wrong is a lot of disputes,” he says. “The processes for handling them were built for people making a few payments a week. That part still has to be built.”

The US Office of the Comptroller of the Currency issued a supervisory framework for banks’ use of what it calls “super intelligence,” including agentic systems, on Nov. 4.

OCC’s Super Intelligence: Supervisory Framework. Source: OCC

The framework is meant to help bank examiners assess how institutions use the technology, and does not introduce new binding requirements for banks. Scrutiny should reflect the potential consequences of an agent’s actions, its level of autonomy and the degree of human oversight.

So an AI system helping a bank employee draft a report would present a different risk from one authorized to move customer funds without approval, and the banks that get that balance right are likely to come out on top.

#4903. Bella Hadid Goes All-In on Colorado With the Purchase of a $5.5 Million Mountain Home

Looks like Bella Hadid is putting down roots in Colorado. On November 10, the Wall Street Journal revealed that the model had spent $5.5 million on a home in Crested Butte, about 20 miles south of Aspen. The news came just a day after Hadid hard-launched her new boyfriend, construction project manager Nathan Lacy, whose connection to the area goes back five generations. According to a statement Hadid’s listing agent gave to the WSJ, she had been living in the region for “several months” before pulling the trigger on her unique new digs, built from two coal miner sheds that date to the early 20th century.

Situated just a few feet from Coal Creek, the 2,000-square-foot residence is complete with three bedrooms and three bathrooms. The sellers built the home in 2001, using two existing structures composed of wood and corrugated metal that had stood on the property for 100 years. The sheds were reassembled over a new foundation, with each wooden plank returned to its exact spot on its wall, according to the WSJ. Antique timber from an old Montana mine makes up the rest of the exterior, while pine reclaimed from an 18th-century Virginia barn lines the floors.

A reverse floor plan was designed to let more light into the upper level common areas, like the fireplace-warmed great room, when snow piles up around the ground floor. Reclaimed warehouse beams from a similar time period to the original structures line the vaulted ceiling, with original iron bolts still in place.

The primary suite, which is set up in one of the original coal sheds on the ground level, is outfitted with a kiva-style fireplace, skylights, a soaking tub, and French doors opening to a flagstone patio overlooking the creek. A separate “bunkhouse” ADU rounds out the property.

It’s easy to see why Hadid—a horse girl through and through—would be drawn to the rustic, Western-style digs.

#4902. Ledger Brings Clear Signing to Solana, Ending 'Blind' Signing for Jupiter Swaps

Ledger Brings Clear Signing to Solana, Ending 'Blind' Signing for Jupiter Swaps

Ledger has brought clear signing to Solana, allowing users of its hardware wallets to read exactly what they are approving before they sign a transaction.

"Until now, most Solana DeFi on a Ledger meant signing blind," the company tweeted on Monday. Users can now verify a swap on decentralized exchange aggregator Jupiter "in plain words, showing what you send and what you get before you approve," it said.

Would you approve a swap you can’t read?Clear Signing is finally live on Solana. Until now, most Solana DeFi on a Ledger meant signing blind. Now you can verify a Jupiter swap, in plain words, showing what you send and what you get before you approve.

Clear Signing now covers… pic.twitter.com/BuW3m7y5GG

Blind signing, in which users approve transactions displayed as unreadable data, has long been a weak point for hardware wallet users, since it leaves them unable to spot a malicious request. Ian Rogers, Ledger's chief human agency officer, likened it to "signing a blank check" in a presentation at Solana's Breakpoint conference.

A Jupiter swap that previously meant scrolling through 13 hard-to-read screens on a Ledger device now takes three, showing what the user is giving, what they're getting and who they're dealing with, Rogers said. "It's a huge step for every Solana user," he added.

Clear signing now covers nine Solana apps and 18 of the 22 most common actions users take on the network, according to Ledger. It was built with the Solana Foundation and Anza, the developer of Solana's Agave validator client, with Jupiter and lending protocol Kamino as the first supported apps.

Rogers also pitched the company's tools for AI agents. Ledger's approach is that an agent "never holds the keys; you do," he said, comparing it to a teenager who has to ask parents for the family car. The agent requests approval when it needs to move funds, and the user signs off on a Ledger device.

Because that means an agent has to stop and wait, Ledger last month launched Agent Intents, a single queue where approval requests collect so agents can keep working while users approve them when convenient. It works with Pay.sh, a service that lets AI agents pay for APIs on Solana, Rogers said.

He also showed off Ledger Atom, the touchscreen device based on Android's open-source code that Ledger announced last month. When it's time to sign, the device switches its screen to Ledger's secure chip, so secrets stay hidden from Android, Rogers said. Atom will be the first hardware beyond the Solana Seeker phone to carry the Seeker platform, bringing its more than 1,500 apps to the device, he added.

Ledger has previously teamed up with MoonPay on tools to let users control AI agents' crypto spending with its hardware wallets.

The launch comes weeks after Ledger investigated a suspected $87 million drain of user funds, which it later said was tied in all confirmed cases to a reseller, Cryptobilis.

#4899. 9 Best Laundry Hampers That Double As Decor (2026)

Nearly everyone has that one chair in their bedroom that isn’t really used for sitting. Instead, it’s the unofficial holding area for clothes that haven’t quite made it to the laundry room. A beautiful laundry hamper offers a much-needed alternative, rescuing yesterday’s jeans and that growing collection of gym clothes. And while a clothes basket might not be the most exciting purchase on your home decor shopping list, the right one can make a surprisingly big difference in how your space looks and feels.

Fortunately, designs have come a long way from the plastic bins you likely grew up with. Here and now, we’re seeing sculptural woven designs that could pass for decorative storage, sleek steel frames with a more industrial edge, and rolling laundry baskets that make transporting a heavy load of laundry a much easier chore. Some are compact enough to tuck into a narrow closet, while others are spacious enough to handle loads for an entire household. Below, we’ve rounded up our favorites that prove even the most practical household pieces can enhance your decor.

The handles on this trio of Quince baskets make it a breeze to transport this toteable vessel from your bedroom to the laundry room. Reviewers specifically called out that this basket is a great size for an average washing machine load. It might be pricier than your average hamper, but the quality of the durable rattan material more than makes up for it.

Handwoven in Ghana by Baba Tree artisans using durable elephant grass, Goodee’s tall storage basket’s intricate weave and structure add a surprising amount of warmth to the bedroom or bathroom. Its generous size leaves plenty of room for dirty laundry, but it could also double as a storage basket for extra blankets or anything else you’d prefer to keep out of sight. Each large laundry basket is crafted by hand, meaning no two are exactly alike.

The structured wooden frame does a lot of the work when it comes to this silhouette, reading more elegant than just having a softened hamper sadly slumped in your bathroom. Senior shopping editor Nashia Baker especially likes that the removable lid makes it easy to keep clothes out of sight and out of mind until laundry day. “This Zara Home basket is simple, but elevated,” she says. “The wooden frame is sturdy, and the same goes for the cotton basket, which is helpful for someone who stuffs their basket to the brim.” Shopping the hamper online, you might miss that the legs are equipped with brass wheels, but Baker points out it’s one of her favorite features, making it easy to move heavy loads without strain.

Quiet Town’s Canvas Laundry Hamper in pine brings a little more personality to laundry day. iIts rich green hue offers a welcome departure from the usual lineup of beige and gray laundry baskets. Double-sided organic cotton canvas and an interior layer of interfacing help the hamper hold its cylindrical shape, even when it isn’t overflowing with dirty laundry. The brand mentions that it’s easy to clean; a quick wipe with a damp cloth is all it needs to stay looking fresh.

Designed by Dario Antoniali for Italian textile brand Society Limonta, the Isacco Laundry Basket makes sorting your lights and darks dare we say… fun? Its slender iron frame supports a stretchy nylon bag that changes shape as it fills, giving it a sculptural quality that feels more like contemporary furniture than a household necessity. Two separate openings make it easy to sort clothes as you go, while the machine-washable fabric keeps maintenance simple. It’s a clever alternative to traditional divided hampers or having multiple laundry baskets.

Leave it to Yamazaki Home to make even dirty laundry look organized. The Japanese home goods brand’s take on the rolling laundry hamper has a tall, open metal frame silhouette that’s refreshingly minimal. Its built-in handle and wheels make transporting heavy loads far less annoying, and the deep basket comes in handy for anyone whose laundry pile seems to multiply overnight. There’s no removable bag or liner to wash or fuss with either.

This is the best laundry basket in terms of its ability to cameo as something other than a laundry basket. In fact, we’re tempted to use it as an impromptu side table, thanks to the smooth and sturdy wooden lid.

Hay’s line of furniture is an all-time AD favorite, so why not continue your love for the Danish brand by adding this soft-gray laundry bin with geometric lines into your design repertoire. It’s also narrow enough to fit tight spaces.

CB2’s laundry hamper skews more decorative than utilitarian. Its distinctive pebbled finish takes inspiration from shagreen, a richly textured material frequently used by French designers during the Art Deco era, giving the hamper a glamorous edge. An engineered wood frame gives it a clean structured shape, while the hinged lid keeps dirty laundry out of sight. There’s even a polyester-cotton interior laundry bag to keep clothes contained. It’s not designed to hold heavy-duty loads, but for a room where aesthetics reign supreme, this laundry hamper is one you’ll never feel compelled to hide.

#4898. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Harvard and Dartmouth reported unchanged share counts in BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF, mining services provider Luxor warned of more frequent AI-enabled cyberattacks and real-world asset protocol stUSDT began winding down operations nearly three years after reaching $2.3 billion in total value locked.

Harvard University and Dartmouth College reported unchanged share counts in BlackRock’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) at the end of the third quarter as a Bitcoin rally lifted the value of both stakes.

Harvard held 3.04 million shares of the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT), worth about $144.1 million at the end of September, while Dartmouth reported 201,531 shares valued at $9.5 million, according to Form 13F filings with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC).

IBIT gained about 42% in Q3 as Bitcoin rallied nearly 43%. Both posted their strongest quarterly gains of 2026 through September.

Harvard has reported the same IBIT share count since the end of March, after cutting its holdings by 43% in Q1 and 21% in Q4 2025. Dartmouth has reported the same 201,531 shares since first disclosing its Bitcoin ETF position in Q4 2025.

Bitcoin mining services provider Luxor has warned that AI-enabled cyberattacks are becoming increasingly difficult to defend against after attackers compromised its mining-management platform and redirected customers’ computing power to another mining pool over the weekend.

In an X post on Sunday, Luxor CEO Nick Hansen said the attack came through a “sophisticated AI-enabled vector,” without specifying how AI was used in the attack, warning that these types of attacks will only get more frequent over time.

“At this moment, anyone running any sort of substantial business, especially in crypto, is inundated with AI-enabled attacks,” Hansen said in an X post. “This does not seem to be slowing down; rather, it’s speeding up as models get better and more attackers enter the arena.”

The warning comes as security researchers have documented the spread of AI-assisted hacking tools, raising the prospect of faster attacks against businesses holding money or controlling valuable infrastructure.

Real-world asset (RWA) protocol stUSDT has begun winding down operations, nearly three years after reaching a $2.3 billion total value locked (TVL), ending rewards and opening a six-month redemption window for tokenholders.

The team said that interest accrual on stUSDT ended on Saturday, with holders of stUSDT and its wrapped version, wstUSDT, able to redeem through its official portal until May 14, 2027. The team said requests submitted after the deadline will be handled through customer support in monthly batches through August 2027.

The team did not provide a reason for the shutdown but said all underlying assets remain fully backed. Holders with tokens on exchanges or DeFi platforms must transfer them to personal wallets before redeeming.

Cointelegraph reached out to stUSDT for more information but did not receive a response before publication.

Meanwhile, JustLend DAO said it would phase out supply and borrowing functions for its wstUSDT market as part of the transition.

Launched in July 2023 on Tron, stUSDT allowed users to deposit USDT and earn yields from investments in real-world assets, including short-term government bonds. The protocol later expanded to Ethereum.

According to DefiLlama data, stUSDT’s TVL peaked at about $2.3 billion in November 2023 before declining substantially in subsequent years. It stood at about $8.6 million on Sunday, down from roughly $60 million a year earlier.

Despite its name, stUSDT is not affiliated with stablecoin issuer Tether. In September 2023, Tether denied any affiliation with stUSDT, saying the protocol was an independent project.

#4896. Morning Minute: Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

Morning Minute: Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

😱 Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

The Crypto Fear and Greed Index printed 10 on Sunday. A month ago it read 63, which is Greed. A week ago it was 28. It has been sliding every day since, and 10 is about as low as that gauge goes.

This weekend’s price action certainly was extremely fearful. Bitcoin touched roughly $62,000 before stabilizing in the mid-60s, down about a quarter from its early-October high and down something like 9% in three days. ETH fell under $1,850. Solana, XRP and dogecoin are all down more than 25% on the month. Bitcoin is now trading below its 200-week moving average, a level that has historically acted as the floor beneath every cycle, and it lost its 50-week average a week earlier.

It wasn’t just majors. Zcash fell to $800. Hype fell to $62. VVV went sub-$17. Nothing was spared.

The billion-dollar question of course—why is Bitcoin (and crypto) selling off so hard?

CZ addressed the question directly on Sunday, saying “No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation.” But he did give potential reasons.

No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation. Here are some potential reasons.

Money shifting to the Anthropic IPO: https://t.co/jYwVi4huAY

ETF outflows: U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.9 billion in outflows over seven trading… pic.twitter.com/XL2aB1ZL9W

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 15, 2026

The reasons he listed: money rotating into the Anthropic IPO, ETF outflows, a run of security incidents including the Binance hack itself plus drained iPhone-linked wallets, a Chrome exploit and a Microsoft outage, hot US inflation raising the odds of another Fed hike and strengthening the dollar, and political uncertainty after midterm results that point toward stricter crypto policy.

Those are all pretty valid. Some of those overhangs remove soon (Anthropic IPO), some will fade over time (political uncertainty), but the cyber attack threat will remain until Bitcoin and broader crypto can present a real solution. And if we are faced with a hiking cycle in 2027 due to inflation, that will certainly be a continued headwind.

So where does this leave us? Nvidia reports Tuesday with an interim CEO while its former one openly campaigns to return. The Fed meets December 9 with inflation running hot enough to put another hike in play. And Bitcoin is sitting under a technical level that has held for three cycles.

Extreme fear has marked bottoms before. It has also marked the middle of declines. Now we wait for something other than sentiment to turn.

#4893. Bunny Williams Never Set Out to Collect Birdhouses—Now a Whole Village of Them Stands in Her Connecticut Garden

Among the apple trees at the edge of AD100 Hall of Famer Bunny Williams’s storied country garden in Falls Village, Connecticut, stands a church with a sensible tin roof; a thatched cottage; a pagoda; a red house flying a tiny flag that she takes for a general store; and a shuttered confection that suggests a Russian dacha. Each is raised high on its own post, finished on every side, painted and weathered like the structures of a settled town—and none has a door you could fit a hand through. It is a hamlet fashioned for birds, assembled building by building, the way real towns often grow: slowly, and without a master plan.

For many years as Williams and her husband, the antiques dealer John Rosselli, combed fields and fairs for Treillage, the much-loved shop they ran in New York City until 2015, the birdhouses kept finding her. They gathered on shelves in the hayloft of her barn. “When I had two, I didn’t know what to do with them,” she says. “Then all of a sudden there were 10.” She decided they had been in storage long enough.

A farmhouse with a side porch crafted by the late Larry Smith, a North Georgia birdhouse builder known for fashioning charmingly detailed structures with metal roofs.

An A-frame house embellished with gingerbread trim and decorative brackets

Williams, on a tree stump next to the pagoda. The notch in the base of the birdhouse indicates that it was probably designed to be mounted on a roof.

At that point, Williams and Robert Reimer, head gardener on her property, started the process of reclaiming a long-overlooked point of land. To create space and liberate a stand of existing apple trees, they removed invasive species, then planted oat grass, bird-friendly scrub plants, and fruit-bearing pawpaw trees. To create the neighborhood, she had uniform posts made—tall, “so that you look up at them,” each wrapped with a metal band to deter ambitious squirrels from climbing too high and disturbing the tenants she had in mind. Finally, up went the houses, now totaling 17.

The attraction had nothing to do with value or pedigree. “Most of these birdhouses are sort of folk art,” she says. “They were somebody having fun building an unusual structure.” Not Monticello in miniature, not a replica of someone’s own house, just unknown makers, each with “an eye and a kind of humor,” none of them trying to be perfect. The proof is in the details: the gingerbread scallops on one many-holed high-rise (“somebody had a great time with their little saw”), the thatched-roof cottage she prizes because she has never seen another like it, the weather vane crowning the would-be dacha.

A church with a brick chimney, an oversized cupola, and Gothic Revival design elements.

A rare combination house and feeder that evokes a neoclassical temple

Williams calls this one the general store.

A colonial revival house with a columned portico built by Larry Smith

“It has a kind of Russian dacha feeling,” says Williams.

What she didn’t have was history—the dealers who sold them seldom had any to pass along. The record, it turns out, is often recoverable. “The period from 1890 to 1930 is really the birdhouse’s golden age,” says Jacob Torkelson, an architectural historian and researcher at the University of Pennsylvania’s Stuart Weitzman School of Design, and among the very few scholars in the country studying these objects, whose makers and manufacturers he has painstakingly been identifying, where a name survives. Across those decades, government pattern books encouraged farmers to install birdhouses to reduce pesticide use (the birds would eat the insects), while mail-order companies offered them to homeowners as pure whimsy, not to mention a way of promoting biodiversity in their gardens. Shown photographs of Williams’s collection, Torkelson supplied some of what the dealers couldn’t. He traced one house down to the catalog page: the six-room Bungalow Martin House sold by the E.F. Hodgson Company, which listed the model from about 1917 until the firm closed in 1944. “Birdhouses are these wonderful mirrors to the human condition,” Torkelson says. “They tell us the values that we have as a society or as individuals, in our houses, in our gardens.”

A double porch house also built by Larry Smith

This pagoda-inspired birdhouse provides multiple roosting spots.

An unusual thatched-roof cottage prized by Williams, who has never seen another like it.

Williams isn’t finished collecting. Last May at Trade Secrets, the annual Litchfield Hills garden fair she cofounded in 2001 to raise funds for a local nonprofit domestic violence support agency, she stopped at the tent of Jim Sidon, whose company, Sparrow 10:29, crafts avian-size replicas of real buildings. Sidon, based outside Atlanta, came to the work after retiring from the printing industry. His first architectural birdhouse, built in 2017, was a replica of the 1792 Federal-style mansion in Newburyport, Massachusetts, where he and his wife had rented two rooms as newlyweds. It was Sidon who recognized, in three of Williams’s houses, the hand of his late friend Larry Smith, a North Georgia birdhouse builder known for his metal roofs and charmingly rustic details. Williams has asked Sidon to build her something new, though she is still deciding exactly what. Unlike her anonymous makers, Sidon numbers and signs every house. “My hope is that years from now, someone’s searching that out,” he says, “and when they open it up, they’ll see my signature.”

A dovecote-inspired birdhouse offers multiple residences

A picturesque house topped with a prominent weather vane

Drawing the eye like miniature garden follies, the houses rise on poles amid a stand of apple trees from ground planted with oat grass and bird-friendly scrub plants.

In Falls Village, the upkeep routine is minimal: Every couple of years the houses come down, receive a coat of wood preservative, and go back up into the weather. Sidon’s constructions, with cedar exteriors and sealed against the elements, ask even less. His replica of the Newburyport house has stood in his own garden for nearly a decade untouched. “I just hose it off,” he says. None of this troubles Torkelson, a preservationist by training. “These houses were made to be ephemeral,” he says. “They weren’t made to last forever, and they should absolutely be used.”

As for the intended residents? Williams thinks the birds mostly prefer making their homes in the trees, where they carry on, she says, like a symphony orchestra: “I’ve always felt that one of the reasons they don’t live in these houses is the birds think it’s suburbia. But they like the atmosphere.” Come winter, when the woods go bare and the village shows through the branches, Sidon would bet the houses do get used, songbirds crowding in two and three at a time for warmth. Williams can’t say—she has never once climbed up to look. “If they are nesting,” she says, “I don’t want to bother them.”

#4892. 'Nobody Knows Who's in Charge': Boris Johnson Stands by Bitcoin Skepticism at Breakpoint

'Nobody Knows Who's in Charge': Boris Johnson Stands by Bitcoin Skepticism at Breakpoint

Former UK Prime Minister Boris Johnson is sticking to his doubts about Bitcoin, telling a room full of crypto developers that he still can't see who is in charge of the world's largest cryptocurrency.

"As far as I can see, nobody knows who's in charge, apart from Yakimoto Nakasori, and I don't know where to find him," Johnson said at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London, deliberately mangling the name of Bitcoin's pseudonymous creator, Satoshi Nakamoto. "If six billion got stolen from some cryptocurrency, I wouldn't know who to apply to, or how to get the money back. That's my problem."

Johnson was asked about a column he wrote in March for the Daily Mail, in which he called Bitcoin a "giant Ponzi scheme." The column drew pushback from the crypto industry, including Strategy executive chairman Michael Saylor, who tweeted that Bitcoin has no issuer, promoter or guaranteed return.

Bitcoin is not a Ponzi scheme. A Ponzi requires a central operator promising returns and paying early investors with funds from later ones. Bitcoin has no issuer, no promoter, and no guaranteed return—just an open, decentralized monetary network driven by code and market demand.

— Michael Saylor (@saylor) March 13, 2026

On stage, Johnson admitted he is "not a great crypto buff" and holds little, if any, crypto himself. His view was shaped by a man he met in his Oxfordshire village who had lost thousands and kept paying more in the hope of recovering his losses, he said. "If the search for the greater fool had reached the wilds of South Oxfordshire, then probably there was something not quite right about this thing," Johnson said.

He also questioned the name itself, noting that "crypto" comes from the Greek for "hidden." "I don't think it's a good sell to tell people that this thing is concealed," he said.

Johnson drew a sharp line between Bitcoin and the technology behind it. "Please don't think that my Luddite anxiety about crypto in any way means that I wish to slow down the development of blockchain, or the lead that London should rightly have in that sort of technology," he said. When it comes to blockchain and tokenizing assets, the UK has "got to be at the forefront," he added.

Moderator Gareth Jenkinson, head of multimedia at The Block, pushed back when Johnson suggested Bitcoin was valuable to drug dealers, arguing that blockchains are far more traceable than cash. Asked by Johnson who guarantees Bitcoin, Jenkinson replied: "No one has to guarantee it. That's the beauty of the protocol."

Lord Ross Kempsell, a Conservative member of the House of Lords who previously worked for Johnson, said more than 8% of UK adults now hold some form of crypto asset, and that institutional adoption of blockchains is growing in the City of London. That ownership figure has fallen, however: the Financial Conduct Authority's latest consumer research, conducted in August and September 2025, found 8% of UK adults own crypto, down from 12% in 2024, though still double the level in 2021.

The UK is "on the precipice of creating a lot of regulation" for the sector, Kempsell said, pointing to the FCA's work on its regulatory perimeter and the Bank of England's coming stablecoin framework. "I share Boris's anxieties about the risks," he said, but within that perimeter, "there's a lot of scope here for a high growth industry."

In June, the FCA finalized its rulebook for crypto firms ahead of a mandatory regime due to take effect in late 2027, days after the Bank of England eased its stablecoin rules, scrapping planned individual holding caps in favor of a £40 billion issuance limit per coin. The Bank has since been handed a legal duty to foster stablecoin innovation, and it has set out a shared vision for tokenization with the FCA.

Kempsell also argued that being outside the EU's Markets in Crypto-Assets (MiCA) regulation allows the UK to set its own rules for crypto. The EU itself is set to revise MiCA in 2027 to cover foreign stablecoin issuers.
