#4890. Lighter pauses withdrawals after reported $10M account theft

Lighter has paused withdrawals after reporting a leaked trader API key, with Bubblemaps tracking $10.4 million withdrawn.

Decentralized derivatives exchange Lighter paused withdrawals and new pool creation Monday after a security incident it linked to a leaked API key for a trader’s account.

Blockchain analytics firm Bubblemaps said about $10.4 million in USDC had been withdrawn from the platform. It tracked funds being swapped into Ether (ETH) and Wrapped Monero XMR1, a token traded on Hyperliquid.

Lighter said its protocol and smart contracts were unaffected. Lighter’s documentation says API keys can place trades, manage positions and transfer funds between accounts linked to the same blockchain wallet. Transfers to another wallet owner require an additional signature from the account owner’s wallet.

The exchange said it activated Guardian mode as a precaution and that its security council would upgrade the contracts. It aimed to complete the work and restore withdrawals by the end of the day.

The security incident follows a string of high-profile cryptocurrency exploits this year, including the $6.3 billion Binance hack on Oct. 29, the industry’s largest to date.

Lighter and Bubblemaps identified wallet ‘0xb3f’ as the attacker’s address. Blockchain data platform Bitquery separately traced 10,393,057 USDC leaving Lighter’s Ethereum contract at 10:11 am UTC. It said most of those funds were converted into Ether through CoW Swap within minutes, followed by a series of 160 ETH transfers to wallets linked to XMR1 purchases on Hyperliquid.

Lighter’s update did not explain how the API key was exposed, what the contract upgrades would change or whether the affected trader would be reimbursed.

Cointelegraph contacted Lighter for comment but had not received a response by publication.

#4889. The 9 Most Beautiful States in the US

The most beautiful states in the US prove just how shockingly diverse the country is: volcanic beaches, glacier-capped peaks, canyon-carved deserts, and misty rainforests define their landscapes. What people have built in those places is just as varied, from a royal palace in downtown Honolulu to Mary Colter’s stone watchtower on the rim of the Grand Canyon to the cedar-shingled studios of a modernist craft school on the coast of Maine. These nine states stand out on both counts, for the scenery that defines them and the architecture that holds its own against it. Keep in mind that no ranking can settle which state is most beautiful—they’re all worthy of a visit.

Hawaii is far from a monolithic state, with scenery changing from island to island. In Honolulu on Oahu, ʻIolani Palace, completed in 1882 for King Kalākaua, is the only royal palace on US soil—and it had electricity before the White House did.

Near Diamond Head, Doris Duke’s oceanfront estate, Shangri La, pairs 1930s modernism with architectural elements she brought from Morocco, Iran, Syria, and beyond. It’s now a museum of Islamic art, with tours departing from the Honolulu Museum of Art.

As for the natural landscape, Kauai, known as the Garden Isle, is home to the Nā Pali Coast, where green ridges drop sharply toward the Pacific. On Maui, Haleakalā’s broad volcanic summit often rises above a blanket of clouds. And on the Big Island, Hawaii Volcanoes National Park actively showcases the raw power of volcanoes—if you’re lucky, you might even catch an eruption.

Wyoming’s most famous photo moment might be the Teton Range rising abruptly above the sagebrush flats of Jackson Hole, ideally with one of Mormon Row’s weathered homestead barns in the foreground. Nearby, the tiny log Chapel of the Transfiguration, built in 1925, was sited so that the picture window behind its altar frames the peaks where stained glass would normally go.

In Yellowstone National Park, it’s all about color: Grand Prismatic Spring’s vivid rings stand out among the forested landscape, while the Grand Canyon of the Yellowstone exposes ochres and pinks in its steep walls. And steps from its namesake geyser, the Old Faithful Inn—designed by a 29-year-old Robert Reamer and opened in 1904—wraps a soaring lodgepole-pine lobby around a massive stone fireplace. It’s considered the largest log structure in the world.

Florida’s beaches are perhaps the state’s most scenic draw, but the architecture is just as distinctive. In 1979, Miami Beach’s Art Deco District, roughly a square mile of South Beach, became the nation’s first urban 20th-century historic district on the National Register, with more than 800 buildings listed. Across Biscayne Bay, industrialist James Deering’s Vizcaya, a villa completed in 1916, faces the water with formal gardens by Diego Suarez. Addison Mizner gave Palm Beach its Mediterranean Revival look; his Via Mizner, a winding pedestrian lane off Worth Avenue built in 1923, survives today.

St. Augustine, founded by the Spanish in 1565, pairs the coquina-stone Castillo de San Marcos with Henry Flagler’s Hotel Ponce de Leon, a Spanish Renaissance–style palace from 1888 that’s now Flagler College. Architecture buffs should detour to Lakeland, where Frank Lloyd Wright’s campus for Florida Southern College is the largest single-site collection of his architecture in the world. And on the Gulf Coast, Sarasota’s midcentury modernists adapted the International Style to the subtropics, most famously in Paul Rudolph’s 1953 Umbrella House.

On Maine’s coast, Acadia National Park packs a surprising amount of scenery into a small area: granite headlands, spruce forest, offshore islands, and views of the Atlantic. Look closely, and you'll notice the park’s impressive stonework, too. John D. Rockefeller Jr. financed its network of carriage roads, built between 1913 and 1940, along with 16 of the 17 stone-faced bridges that carry them over streams and gorges—no two are alike.

Don’t forget the coastal villages, from ever-popular Bar Harbor and its shingle-style and Queen Anne houses to Stonington, a working fishing town on Deer Isle. Elsewhere on Deer Isle, Edward Larrabee Barnes’s 1961 campus for the Haystack Mountain School of Crafts, an underrated masterpiece of American modernism, steps down a granite slope toward Jericho Bay, its cedar-shingled cabins and studios linked by boardwalks.

California has a little of everything, from the architectural hubs of Los Angeles and San Francisco to breathtaking natural settings. In Yosemite, El Capitan and Half Dome are jaw-dropping from the valley floor, but even more so from the top for those daring enough to make the climb. Back down in the valley, the Ahwahnee hotel, which opened in 1927, appears to be built of granite and redwood—but the “wood” is actually fireproof concrete, poured into molds and stained to look like timber. In the desert, Palm Springs is a showcase of midcentury modernism, with Richard Neutra’s 1946 Kaufmann House as its best-known example.

But the California coast is undeniably the state’s most famous landscape, and the 656 miles of Highway 1 deliver architecture along with the views. Above San Simeon, Hearst Castle, which Julia Morgan designed for William Randolph Hearst over nearly three decades, crowns a hilltop overlooking the Pacific. Continue north past the bluffs of Big Sur and storybook Carmel-by-the-Sea to the Sonoma Coast, where the redwood-clad buildings of the Sea Ranch, a planned community begun in the mid-1960s, are tucked into hedgerows to leave the meadows and bluffs open.

New York State’s famous architecture isn’t limited to the Manhattan skyline—though the city certainly pulls its weight on the architecture front. In the Adirondacks, wooded mountains surround lakes such as Lake Placid and the three Saranac Lakes. The “great camps” that wealthy families began building along their shores in the Gilded Age turned the log cabin into high style. You can still stay in one: The Point, on Upper Saranac Lake, was built in the early 1930s as Camp Wonundra for William Avery Rockefeller II and is now an 11-room Relais & Châteaux hotel, the first in North America.

In the Hudson Valley, painter Frederic Church’s hilltop villa, Olana, features a design style he called “Persian, adapted to the Occident.” The Finger Lakes offer a gentler landscape of long, narrow lakes bordered by vineyards and small towns, plus the gorge at Watkins Glen State Park, where a stone path meanders past tumbling cascades and swirling pools. And in Buffalo, you’ll find Frank Lloyd Wright’s Darwin D. Martin House, one of his finest Prairie-style designs.

The Grand Canyon is a tough act to follow, but on its South Rim, architect Mary Colter spent decades designing buildings meant to hold their own beside it. Her Hopi House, which opened in 1905, is a multistory stone building shaped like a Hopi pueblo; her Desert View Watchtower, completed in 1932, is a 70-foot stone tower modeled on Ancestral Puebloan structures, with a Snake Legend mural inside by Hopi artist Fred Kabotie.

Sedona’s red-rock buttes are spectacular on their own, but the Chapel of the Holy Cross, a modernist concrete chapel built directly into a pair of sandstone buttes and completed in 1956, gives them a focal point. North of Phoenix, Paolo Soleri’s Arcosanti, an experimental desert town of concrete domes and arches begun in 1970, is still a work in progress. And in Scottsdale, in the foothills of the McDowell Mountains, Frank Lloyd Wright’s Taliesin West—his winter home and studio from 1937 until his death in 1959—is now a UNESCO World Heritage Site.

Texas’s landscapes range from the Chihuahuan Desert to the Gulf Coast to the bayous of Caddo Lake. In Big Bend National Park, the Chisos Mountains rise above dry plains, and the Rio Grande cuts through the narrow walls of Santa Elena Canyon. In Marfa, minimalist artist Donald Judd turned a former army post into the Chinati Foundation, a museum built around permanent, large-scale installations, including his 100 mill-aluminum boxes lined up inside two converted artillery sheds.

In Fort Worth, Louis Kahn’s Kimbell Art Museum, completed in 1972, draws architects from around the world to see natural light wash down its concrete cycloid vaults; Tadao Ando’s Modern Art Museum of Fort Worth sits just across the street. Central Texas has rolling hills, and in spring, bluebonnets and Indian paintbrush color the roadsides around Fredericksburg, a town with German heritage.

Mount Rainier is perhaps Washington’s most familiar landmark, its glaciers and snowfields visible above forests and summer wildflower meadows. The North Cascades offer even more peaks, with jagged ridges rising above turquoise Diablo Lake, while the trails in Olympic National Park lead through mossy rainforest and out to driftwood-strewn beaches.

Seattle holds its own against all that scenery. Downtown, the Central Library—designed by Rem Koolhaas’s OMA with local firm LMN and opened in 2004—wraps its stacked floors in a faceted net of glass and steel, while the Space Needle, built for the 1962 World’s Fair, remains the skyline’s signature. Outside Seattle, Port Townsend’s Victorian storefronts overlook the water, and in the Walla Walla Valley, vineyard rows and wheat fields spread across rolling hills, with the Blue Mountains rising beyond them.

#4887. Zelle picks Solana for ZelleUSD stablecoin and international payments

Zelle plans to use Solana for its ZelleUSD stablecoin and international payments, with confidential transfers designed to hide payment amounts.

Zelle, the US peer-to-peer payments network operated by Early Warning Services (EWS), a consortium of banks that includes JPMorgan and Wells Fargo, is partnering with Solana to support its expansion into international payments. The company announced on stage at Breakpoint 2026 that its proprietary dollar-backed stablecoin, ZelleUSD (ZLUSD), will launch on the Solana blockchain to allow for peer-to-peer payments internationally.

The move brings one of the largest domestic payments networks in the US into the blockchain-based payments market. Zelle processed more than $1.2 trillion in US payments in 2025 and has since taken steps to establish an international business. In June, the company named India as its first international remittance corridor, with plans to allow US consumers to send money to friends and family in the country. In the same announcement, the company unveiled ZelleUSD, intended to underpin future international payment capabilities in additional markets. ZelleUSD’s issuer is Anchorage Digital Bank.

“Sending money around the world is a pain; it’s complex, it’s fragmented and it’s often unfamiliar. Over half of the Remittance services that are offered today don’t deliver money as fast as we’d like them to,” said Alexandra Lang, vice president of digital asset partnerships at Zelle. “Solana is a partner we chose because we’re here to deliver payments at scale.”

For Zelle, the decision to select Solana appears to have been driven in large part by the network’s infrastructure. “Zelle has such a massive scale in the United States, we needed a partner that can also deliver at massive scale internationally as well,” said Denise Leonhard, General Manager at Zelle in a video discussing the partnership.

“The number one priority is the responsibility that we have to make sure that payments are safe, secure, and they get to the right place [...] That piece around trust, security, proven infrastructure is the reason why we chose Solana.”

Following several years in which network outages raised questions about Solana’s suitability for financial applications, the chain has maintained 100% uptime since February 2024. This is despite a routing failure at infrastructure provider Teraswitch in August 2026, which took 29% of Solana’s network offline. According to blockchain intelligence platform Metrika, transaction finality slowed, but uptime was sustained.

On stage at Breakpoint 2026, the Solana Foundation announced the migration of Solana from beta into mainnet, bringing with it an improvement in finality speed from 13 seconds to 150 milliseconds.

Related: HKMA, Swift explore tokenized deposits for cross-border bank payments

In the payments wallet, customers will have the option to encrypt their transfer so it is shielded from the public blockchain. Institutions that are part of Zelle’s network will be able to decrypt the transfer to carry out the checks needed to maintain compliance.

“Features like this are paramount to really getting payments to scale,” said Lang.

Related: DBS, JPMorgan’s Kinexys launch 24/7 transfers of tokenized deposits

Although Solana is gaining ground in stablecoin activity, Ethereum and Tron continue to hold substantially larger pools of dollar-pegged assets. According to Token Terminal data pulled on Nov. 14, Ethereum held $150.5 billion in stablecoins while Tron held $93.2 billion, compared with $13.9 billion on Solana.

However, Solana’s stablecoin activity is growing faster in transaction frequency than on either of its larger competitors. The network recorded an 82% increase in monthly stablecoin transfers year-over-year in October, compared with 48% growth on Ethereum, while Tron’s transfer count declined by nearly 8%, according to Token Terminal data.

Zelle’s choice of Solana over both Tron and Ethereum follows other remittance providers that have expanded on-chain. Also at Breakpoint, MoneyGram outlined an integration with Solflare allowing Solflare wallet users to exchange UDSC to cash. The move expands MoneyGram Ramps on Solana, launched by the company in August.

“That’s where MoneyGram brings something different to the ecosystem: the ability to bridge digital dollars and physical cash at scale, making stablecoins more useful in everyday life,” said Luke Tuttle, Chief Product and Technology Officer at MoneyGram in a press release.

While Zelle did not provide a timeline for the launch of international payments on stage, earlier announcements indicated that the India corridor would be live by the end of 2026.

#4886. The 8 Best Slippers for Women for Max Coziness, Tested By a Style Writer

From couch-rotting to errands, these kicks have you covered.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Now that the weather has cooled down and leaves are on the ground, my style writer brain is in peak outfit mode. I’ve already imagined the ways I’ll wear my leather jackets, chunky knits, and bootcut jeans. But before I start styling my way through the fall and winter, there’s one essential I always reach for first: slippers. I test a lot of shoes in the name of journalism, and few spark more joy than a pair of cute slippers that add the perfect cozy feel to any outfit. Whether I’m channeling my inner couch potato on a lazy Sunday or running errands around town, the best slippers in my stash hit the sweet spot of soft, supportive, and actually stylish.

A lot of the same principles that apply to shopping for supportive sneakers also apply to slippers, says Anne Sharkey, DPM, a podiatrist at Solely Podiatry in Austin, TX. After all, a slipper is the ultimate cozy shoe, but it’s also incredibly useful for keeping your arches happy while walking around hardwood floors at home. That’s why Sharkey says cushioning and arch support are key. Your slippers should feel plush underfoot, but not so squishy that you lose stability. Where you plan to wear them also matters: A pair of house slippers needs less traction and durability than a pair for outdoors, which should have a thicker, more grippy bottom.

After over a year of testing, I found slippers that are plush enough to lounge in, supportive enough for a spin around town, and stylish enough that you’ll actually want to wear them.

When it comes to a slipper that checks every box (plush, supportive, and legitimately stylish), the Ugg Cozy Slipper is the one I always come back to. I’ve been wearing this slipper since fall 2024 with everything from jeans and Henleys to matching sweat sets.

The slipper earns its name with extra-cozy materials, from the sheepskin fur lining to the sweater-knit outer and fluffy collar. I love that it keeps my feet warm and toasty, even during cold winter months. Board-certified foot and ankle surgeon Bobby Pourizaee, DPM says this slipper is excellent for comfort and lounging. Podiatrist and board-certified foot and ankle surgeon Bruce Pinker, DPM agrees, adding that the slip-on style makes this slipper easy to wear.

Beneath the fluff, there’s real structure that keeps my feet fatigue-free. The contoured footbed and firm-yet-plush midsole made with 17 mm of sheepskin hug my arches just right. The outsole is sturdy and grippy, made with dense EVA foam and tons of traction. I wear it on long walks with my dog around the Long Island suburbs and never worry about slipping or falling, no matter the weather. However, it’s still lightweight enough to feel like an indoor slipper.

Pinker says that it could be improved if the heel region were closed for more support and stability, but the easy-on, easy-off fit is part of why I love this slipper. If you’re after something warm, cozy, and super stylish for both indoor and outdoor wear, I can’t recommend this slipper enough.

For a cozy, budget-friendly slipper that doesn’t skimp on comfort, the Dearfoams Lacey Memory Foam Slipper is the one I’ve been living in lately. At under $30, this slipper is way more cushy, warm, and supportive than lots of other house slippers I’ve worn.

I padded around my house in this slipper nonstop for about a year, and it held up beautifully through coffee runs to the kitchen, long hours at my desk, and quick trips to grab packages. It’s definitely a house slipper, but in the best way possible—pure comfort, with just enough structure to feel supportive.

The memory foam cushioning is what makes this slipper so comfortable, Sharkey says. Each step in it feels super plush, molding to the shape of my foot for that “walking on pillows” feel. The fleece lining also keeps my feet cozy without overheating, and the soft knit upper gives it a lounge-all-day kind of vibe that pairs perfectly with pajamas and sweatpants. I absolutely love the heel and arch cushioning, especially when my feet ache after a long day commuting to and from Manhattan, but Sharkey says they should only be worn in the house since they lack the structure needed for long periods on your feet.

But for a house slipper, there’s way more support than I expected. The raised heel area keeps the slipper from sliding off my foot, even when I’m walking up or down stairs. It’s a small but thoughtful feature that makes it feel a bit more secure than your average slip-on. And while the outsole isn’t meant for outdoor adventures, it’s durable enough for quick steps onto the porch or grabbing the mail.

I wore this slipper for nearly a year straight while trodding around my house, and absolutely love them. However, I will say that at about the 10-month mark, they needed to be replaced, as they stretched out a bit and lost some of the much-needed cushioning, support, and traction.

Thanks to its incredible level of cushioning, the Vionic Gemma II Mule Slipper keeps my feet pain-free even after walking in it all day. Pourizaee says it's a strong recommendation thanks to the contoured footbed. The midsole is made from a flexible EVA foam that not only feels super plush under my foot but is designed to absorb shock and reduce stress on the joints. I have lingering ankle and foot pain on one side from a previous high ankle sprain, and while some shoes exacerbate those symptoms, this slipper keeps my feet happy. That comes as no surprise, since this slipper earned an APMA Seal of Acceptance.

It also has great support and stability features. Pinker says it would be improved with a closed-heel design, but I find that the deep heel cup helps, and the strap closure keeps my foot firmly planted—even while speed walking on city sidewalks. Pourizaee adds that the contoured footbed helps keep feet aligned and relieves foot strain. While I often wake up with sore feet that hurt as soon as I step out of bed, I find that when I’ve worn this slipper the day before, foot fatigue is next to zero.

If you like a cozy slipper, the Gemma II delivers. I have the cream color, which is made with a wool upper that keeps my feet warm and looks cute with all my cold-weather loungewear. Other styles have a faux-fur or flannel upper, which also looks super warm and cozy. Plus, since the slipper is designed for indoor and outdoor wear, the synthetic outsole is ultra-durable with a patterned tread design. I’ve even gone for post-rain walks with my dog on wet pavement, and never felt like I was going to slip.

Overall, this slipper is warm, cozy, and ultra-cushioned. It keeps my feet happy with the plush feel underfoot, and I love keeping it by the door when I need a cute shoe to slip on fast.

If you need a slipper with serious arch support, the Orthofeet Charlotte Slipper delivers. Plus, it comes with additional insoles and spacers for days when my feet need extra support.

The Ortho-Cushion system (which includes the removable orthotic insole, ergonomic sole, and fitting spacers) helps to customize the fit and level of cushioning and support. I didn’t realize before selecting my normal size that Orthofeet recommends ordering a half-size down for a more snug fit, but placing the smallest blue fitting spacer (1/16 inch) under the insole kept my foot feeling super secure. Even without any customization, I was impressed by the high level of cushioning that hugs my arches and feels really nice under my heels. The ergonomic sole not only has ample cushioning underfoot, but also the subtle “rocker” design, which refers to a little lift under the heel and toe so those two parts of the shoe don’t touch the ground when standing still, similar to a rocking chair. That shape propels you forward with each step and also helps take some pressure off the joints. Plus, the EVA outsole also has lots of traction patterns to keep every step feeling stable and secure.

I also love the faux fur lining and insole that feels super cozy and keeps my feet warm even while walking outdoors on chilly mornings. The suede outer adds to that cozy vibe and looks super cute. I initially wasn’t sure if this slipper would be my style, but I find myself reaching for it all the time when I leave the house for quick errands and coffee dates with friends. I have the taupe color and often pair it with ankle jeans and sweaters for a comfortable fall fit.

While it does run slightly small, the brand offers half sizes, and the slipper comes with customizable features, so I didn’t find it an issue at all. However, if you’re concerned about fit, I recommend ordering a half size down.

The Sunday Mule Slipper from Bombas is perhaps the most stylish casual shoe that I own. The elongated, oval-toe shape looks super chic, and it's wrapped entirely in a fluffy sherpa material for that cozy-yet-put-together feel. Whether I’m couch-rotting on the weekend, grabbing a latte, or taking a casual stroll around town, I can always count on this slipper. It looks great with a wide-leg lounge pant or leggings with slouchy socks, but I’ve also worn it with a cropped pair of jeans.

Sharkey says it's a good cozy and comfortable option, and that Bombas did a great job with the level of cushioning. That’s thanks to the memory foam footbed, which has a nice squishy feel, contours to the shape of the foot, and softens impact with every step. That contouring feature does wonders for making sure the slipper stays on, even while trekking all around town. However, Sharkey says the backless design does tend to slip off the feet if you’re not careful. I haven’t had any issues there, but it’s something to keep in mind if your feet need an ultra-supportive shoe—in which case, the Orthofeet Charlotte Slipper might be a better choice.

The rubber outsole gives this slip-on shoe a major upgrade, since it helps provide traction when I wear it outside. I keep this slipper mainly for wearing around the house or quick errands, but I have worn it during short, casual walks without worry. The fluffy sherpa material, inside and out, makes it a super cozy option for lazy days cuddled up on the couch and keeps my feet warm even when I take it for a spin outdoors.

Plus, there’s plenty of chic color options. I went with the cream, which I find pairs well with almost anything in my closet—but there’s also a black and brown for additional neutrals, and a dusty purple or tomato red option for something more bold. While this slipper from Bombas might not be the most supportive option, I’d recommend it to anyone looking for a plush, super stylish slipper.

Clogs get a bad rap for making your feet sweat, slide around, and (sorry!) being kind of ugly. But this slip-on clog from Rothy’s nails it on the style front, and is also durable, breathable, and super comfortable. The Casual Clog is well-loved by me and executive editor Abigail Cuffey—oh, and Sharkey recommends it, too.

This slipper/clog hybrid is made with the brand’s signature cushioning made from recycled water bottles and arch support that Sharkey says is ultra comfortable. She also says she likes that the heel cup is a bit deeper than your standard slipper, which helps with stability. I find that the deep heel cup helps cover the back of my foot, so it doesn’t slip off when I’m running around the house doing laundry or walking up and down stairs. The outsole is also made from recycled water bottles and has a grooved design that makes it super durable with tons of traction. I usually wear it for casual outings and meeting up with friends, and find it looks great with baggy jeans and sweaters for a casual-yet-put-together look.

While I own the merino wool version, Cuffey owns both the merino wool and hemp styles. She’s been wearing the hemp clog since fall 2024, and loves that the ultra-cushioned footbed molds to her feet. “It feels like a hug for your heels and toes,” she says. She wears it as a lounge shoe and an outdoor shoe, because of the grippy outsole and substantial feel. Cuffey’s thoughts on the merino wool clog are generally the same, but she loves that the wool feels a little cozier while still airy and breathable. “I usually go through slippers so fast because they get so gross, but Rothy’s Casual Clogs are the first ones that have staying power in my closet,” she says.

It does run a little wide, but the fabric is stretchy, so I haven’t had any issues with it feeling too loose on my feet. But if you’re in between sizes, Rothy’s recommends sizing down for a better fit.

The L.L. Bean Wicked Good Moccasin has become my favorite ever since I got it in the mail. A moccasin is a slipper made of soft leather that wraps around the foot with a single gathered seam at the front. This style is the cushiest, coziest, most supportive I’ve ever worn, earning a permanent spot in my fall and winter footwear rotation.

Unlike some moccasins, Pinker says this style feels especially stable thanks to the rubber outsole with added grip and traction. Plus, the suede material is more structured than other moccasins I’ve worn, which makes for a snug, secure feel. I’ve worn this moccasin weekly over the past month, and I can tell it’s extremely durable. The suede has a lot of heft, and combined with the hand-stitched construction, this moccasin stands up to wear and tear. The fur lining is also extremely comfortable and warm, and I love wearing this shoe on colder days to keep my feet toasty. The insole is also made from the same fur material, which delivers a super plush, cushioned feel underfoot.

There’s also plenty of fur lining the outside of the slipper, which I find super stylish, along with the rawhide laces and bow. It looks great with both joggers and wide-leg sweatpants, and I even wore it recently with cuffed jeans and a boxy sweater for a casual brunch. I have the brown pair, but with seven shades to choose from (including blue, red, and purple!), there’s no shortage of styling potential.

One note: this slipper is spot-clean only, unlike others that can go in the washing machine. However, I wear it nearly every morning lately to walk my dog when the ground is still dewy, and I haven’t gotten any stains that took more than a quick pass with a dry washcloth to remove.

I desperately needed a new house slipper a few weeks ago, so I decided to snag this pair from Fulton—and I couldn’t be happier with my decision. While you could totally get away with wearing this slipper outside (it’s super cute and definitely sturdy), I love keeping this pair inside and free from outside germs—IYKYK.

At first, I honestly wasn’t sure this pair would be a winner: the footbed was super uncomfortable the first day that I wore them. It felt like all the raised areas were in the wrong places, which made it a little difficult to walk. Had I known then that the Fulton website explains there’s a 10-hour break-in process, I’d have been way less concerned.

Lo and behold, by the following day the slippers felt much better—and only continued to get more comfortable as the cork footbed molded to the exact shape of my foot. I wouldn’t call this slipper plush by any means (though the wool upper is quite cozy), so if that’s what you’re looking for, the Ugg, Dearfoams, Orthofeet, or L.L. Bean styles might be better options. But this shoe makes my feet feel so good.

I’ve been suffering from some pretty gnarly foot pain from recently developed plantar fasciitis, but since I started wearing this slipper around the house every day, my feet feel brand new. My arches and heels feel way less achy throughout the day, and it no longer hurts to plant my feet on the ground first thing in the morning.

If you prefer a slide over a slipper, this pick from Oofos is perfect. I often bring it to the gym to wear in the locker room, sauna, or on the way to my post-workout smoothie spot, and the cushy foam insole feels great on my achy feet. As our WH 2025 Fitness Awards winner for Best Recovery Shoe, I had to test it myself for this guide.

Pourizaee says this slide is ideal for recovery or all-day wear thanks to its excellent shock absorption and built-in arch support. That’s because it’s made from the brand's signature OOfoam technology—a springy EVA foam that has plenty of cushion and feels nice and squishy underfoot. The insole’s contoured shape creates built-in arch support, with a high mound near the center that fits perfectly under the arches. That shape reduces stress on the knees, ankles, and other joints. My ankle pain (from a previous sprain) and mysterious knee pain definitely feel better when I wear this slide regularly around the house. Plus, the deep heel cup keeps my foot anchored in place, even when I’m doing housework at lightning speed.

WH executive health and fitness director, Jacqueline Andriakos, swears by it for managing her foot pain, too. "My podiatrist told me I had to get a pair for work-from-home days and going to and from barefoot workout classes," she says. "I notice my chronic foot pain is light years better when I’ve been wearing it more regularly."

Another perk? The foam is water-repellent, odor-resistant, and machine washable. The only feature that would make this slide better, Pinker says, is an ankle strap for extra stability and support.

The right cushioning absorbs impact from walking on hard surfaces to help prevent foot pain, says Pourziaee. All of our favorite slippers have at least a medium level of cushioning.

#4885. Jensen Huang’s Houses: Exploring the Former Nvidia CEO’s $38 Million San Francisco Mansion and Other Properties

It’s been a big week for Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang. The billionaire announced he is stepping down as CEO of the AI hardware giant following reports that he had at least two extramarital affairs: one with a suspected Chinese spy, and another with one of his company’s senior executives (other reports allege a third affair with another Nvidia employee). Huang will reportedly testify before Congress later this month to discuss his relationship with the alleged spy. We can only assume that the Taipei City native is weathering the storm at home, but where are Jensen Huang’s houses? Even Huang himself may be wondering about the answer to that question, if a recent New York Times interview is any indication (more on that later).

Unsurprisingly, the tech titan hasn’t strayed too far from Silicon Valley, where Nvidia has been based since it was founded in the mid-’80s, with his real estate purchases. After graduating from Oregon State University, where he met his wife, Lori Huang, Huang began buying properties in California’s Bay Area, and he and Lori still own two of them. So far, the superbillionaire’s only exception to that rule has been an estate on Maui in Hawaii. Time will tell if the scandal spurs any real estate changes for him.

One thing that will presumably change about Huang’s home life in the immediate future: footing the bill for his security team. Nvidia has, until now, covered the cost of its former CEO’s private security. The company spent about $4 million in 2026 on “home security, protection during personal travel, consulting, and drivers” for Huang, per the New York Post. Though that arrangement is likely coming to an end, that shouldn’t be too big a problem for Jensen; the scandal has put Nvidia’s stock—and therefore Jensen’s net worth—in a slight flux, but he is still worth an estimated $144.7 billion at the time of publication.

Read on for a rundown of Jensen Huang’s houses dating back to his arrival in Silicon Valley.

According to The New York Times, Huang married Lori shortly after his 1984 graduation from Oregon State University, where they met as lab partners in an electrical engineering class. The newlywed graduates hightailed it to Silicon Valley to start their careers as microchip designers, and Jensen bought his first house: a 1,500-square-foot three-bedroom home in San Jose, California. It’s unclear exactly when or for how much he bought the property, but Jensen sold it in 1988 for $185,000.

That same year, Huang and Lori moved into a larger San Jose home that they bought for $338,000. With 2,300 square feet of interior living space, it had four bedrooms. Huang lived here when he co-founded Nvidia in 1993; he sold the house in 2002 for $500,000, the Wall Street Journal reports.

From there, the Huangs moved into an even more upgraded residence: a 7,000-square-foot home in Los Altos Hills, a tony area about 15 miles from the Nvidia headquarters. They paid $6.9 million for the newly built six-bedroom dwelling in 2003. According to Realtor.com, the two-story house hosts a resort-worthy swimming pool. The Huangs still own this house.

A year after buying his house in Los Altos Hills, Huang paid $7.5 million for a Hawaiian estate. The oceanfront property is located within Wailea Resort, an exclusive gated community on the south side of Maui, per the Wall Street Journal. The contemporary-style mansion reportedly has seven bedrooms across about 8,000 square feet. The Huangs still maintain this residence too.

In 2017, Huang made his most recent real estate purchase: a newly completed $38 million mansion in the high-end Pacific Heights neighborhood on San Francisco’s Gold Coast. The residence is modern in style, with a curved roofline and a limestone exterior. It boasts 11,400 square feet of interior living space, with seven bedrooms, seven full bathrooms, three half-baths, a wine cellar, an outdoor kitchen, an elevator, a home theater, a gym with a sauna, a library, numerous fireplaces, and a fifth-floor observation deck with views of the Golden Gate Bridge. The house took four years to construct, its developer told the WSJ.

San Francisco’s famous Golden Gate Bridge.

This seems to be Huang’s primary residence—although, according to a September 2026 New York Times interview, he could not tell you where it is. “There are a lot of skills that don’t matter,” Huang told the outlet in response to a question about AI weakening students’ basic math skills. “My first confession, I actually don’t know my address.… One day I had to pump gas—it was a few years ago. They needed my ZIP code, and I panicked. I didn’t know my ZIP code. I don’t know my telephone number. I forget these things. I can live with it.”

#4882. How to Find Out If You Got the Measles Vaccine as a Kid

The US may lose its measles elimination status after multiple outbreaks in the past two years.

THE US is likely to lose its measles elimination status this week when the Pan-American Health Organization, part of the public health arm of the United Nations, meets to review data from the multiple outbreaks that have occurred nationwide in the past two years.As of early November, there are 5,182 confirmed measles cases in 47 states. This is more than double of cases in 2025—the CDC reported 2,289 measles cases in 45 states. And a far cry from when measles were practically eliminated in the US with a record low of 86 cases two decades ago.

Of all the US cases this year, 96 percent come from people who are unvaccinated. Since most of us got the MMR (measles, mumps, and rubella) vaccine as a kid, it wouldn't hurt to double-check that you're protected against one of the most contagious infectious diseases in the world.

Now, there's no central repository of childhood vaccine records in the United States, says Brian Labus, PhD, MPH, assistant professor of epidemiology and biostatistics at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas. So you'd need to find your copy of your vaccination records or get them from your pediatrician's office—which may or may not still be operating. There are a few other places to do that detective work (more below).

In lieu of that, some people are getting blood tests to check if they have antibodies for some of the diseases children are vaccinated for, but there are pros and cons with those. Here's what to know about making sure you're protected against measles and whether you got other vaccinations on the childhood immunization schedule.

IF YOU WERE born before 1957, it’s assumed that you were exposed to measles as a child and don’t need to be vaccinated, Labus says. If you’re around 45 or older, you were likely exposed to chicken pox and don’t need that vaccine, adds Thomas Russo, MD, chief of the division of infectious diseases at the University at Buffalo. There aren’t specific age cutoffs for other diseases, though.

But there is a blood test, called an antibody serology test, that can tell you if you have antibodies against certain diseases, including measles, mumps, chicken pox, hepatitis, tetanus, HIV, mononucleosis, and others.

These blood tests can suggest whether you’ve been previously infected with these diseases or vaccinated against them, Dr. Russo says. “The presence of antibodies indicates that your body mounted an immune response against a pathogen either due to a natural exposure or to a vaccination,” Labus explains. “It can also give you an idea about the level of protection you have in your body.”

While that generally means that you’re immune, it’s also not a guarantee, says Georges Benjamin, MD, executive director of the American Public Health Association. Also, antibodies decline over time.

Starting with an antibody test to determine your vaccination status might make sense for some people, but Labus says there are some practicalities to consider.

For one, the tests can be expensive, costing around $250, Dr. Benjamin says. Your insurance may or may not cover them.

It can also take several days to get the results. Labus says if you’re using the test to determine if you need a vaccine quickly, you’re potentially not protected during that wait. “While this doesn't matter for routine situations, it could be the difference between getting measles or avoiding it if you live in a community experiencing an outbreak,” he says.

If you're not sure you were vaccinated, Labus and the other doctors we spoke to suggest just getting the MMR, polio, DTap (diphtheria, tetanus, and acellular pertussis), and other shots just to be safe (more about that below).

But if you're not sure you want to get jabbed, your pediatrician retired long ago, and you have a moment to invest in detective work on your records, here's where to look:

• Old records you or your parents have at home, like a baby book.

• Any schools you attended. Many states require certain vaccines before you start kindergarten, including MMR, polio, DTap, and varicella, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. If you were in the military, you would have been required to get certain vaccines.

• The health department where you grew up. Some state health departments keep immunization records, Dr. Benjamin says. But whether yours are there may depend on your age. “State immunization registries are a relatively new development, and those systems were not likely in place when you were young if you are more than 25 or 30 years old,” Labus says.

TALK TO YOUR doctor about your concerns and uncertainty about your childhood vaccination status and level of protection against diseases, and they can suggest whether just getting the vaccine for the first time or a booster is appropriate, Labus says. “Most vaccines can be given to adults without any problems.”

Getting the shot is usually cheaper and faster than an antibody test, he adds. Dr. Benjamin points out that boosters are a good idea to consider especially if you have chronic diseases, immune system dysfunction, or work in the healthcare industry. Also consider it if you plan to travel to parts of the world with higher instances of diseases like measles, such as countries in the Middle East, Dr. Russo adds.

And by the way, even if you did get all your vaccines as a child, you still need to get a DTap or Tdap booster every 10 years as an adult for protection against diphtheria and tetanus, Dr. Russo says.

For the other childhood vaccines, “receiving an extra dose is going to boost your antibody levels even if you have previously been vaccinated, which is a good thing,” Labus explains. Dr. Russo agrees: “The path of least resistance is when in doubt, there’s no harm in getting a booster shot.”

#4880. This 377-Square-Foot Island Getaway in Denmark Is a Picture-Perfect Pint-Sized Retreat

A mini vacation home in Zealand, Denmark, is a particularly appealing retreat, no matter the season. With its rugged coastlines, forests, and small villages, the western side of Zealand offers an ideal setting for a break from everyday life. Nestled among trees is a tiny house that was designed to be as unobtrusive as possible. Its compact shape, natural color palette, and large windows allow it to blend almost seamlessly into the forest. Here, nature isn’t just right outside the door—it’s always present inside as well.

Surrounded by trees, this small wooden house in Denmark blends seamlessly with nature.

Named the EcoNeo House, the home was designed by the Danish architectural firm SAGA Space Architects, both as a family home and as a prototype for tiny house-style vacation homes. Although originally conceived as a summer house, the cottage has been insulated and heated, allowing it to be used even during colder weather. At only 377 square feet, it combines the essential features of a vacation home, sleeping up to six people. The design prioritizes a thoughtful use of the available space and forward-thinking architecture that blends into the landscape.

The spacious terrace is perfect for sunbathing and dining outdoors, especially in the summer.

Inside, the living and dining areas flow seamlessly into one another. Thanks to the large windows that offer views of the surrounding trees, there’s a connection between indoors and outdoors. The kitchen is set slightly apart, but opens up into the dining area, blending smoothly into the floor plan. Designed with functionality in mind, it’s still fully equipped with all the necessary appliances.

The dining area flows seamlessly into the kitchen. Additional storage space was created below the stairs on the left, which lead to the sleeping lofts.

Instead of breaking up the floor plan with additional walls, the architects made use of the home’s height: Stairs next to the kitchen lead to two sleeping lofts that offer a private retreat for up to four people. Storage was also a priority in the design. Items not needed on a daily basis can be stowed under the stairs, a clever solution that again makes the most of the small space.

Large windows let in plenty of natural light and create a connection between indoors and outdoors. Despite its compact size, the kitchen has all the necessary appliances.

There’s even a sauna, which can be converted into a sleeping space for two additional guests if needed. The generous window front offers a view of the surrounding landscape, while a cold plunge pool on the terrace is waiting for a refreshing dip afterward.

Through the sauna’s large window, you can enjoy the surrounding nature even while relaxing. It’s an appealing retreat, especially on chilly days. There’s also a cold plunge pool on the terrace.

The choice of materials complements the understated architecture. The floor’s wide Douglas fir planks introduce a warm color and vibrant grain. The use of wood is consistently carried through to other areas of the house, creating a visual connection between the living space and the sauna. Natural materials are also used in the bathroom, including Kolmården marble, a Swedish stone distinguished by its characteristic green hue.

Wood defines the interior and lends the rooms a natural warmth. Large windows bring the surrounding nature into the home, while the dining area is spacious even within the compact layout.

Together with the stones and the greenery outside, the result is a design that deliberately avoids contrasts and excess. “When designing the summer house, every detail was intended to reflect the harmony between sustainable innovation and timeless craftsmanship,” explains Niklas Munk-Andersen, architect and partner at SAGA Space Architects.

Beanbag chairs provide extra seating and blend harmoniously into the interior with their colors.

The Douglas fir floorboards also give the clean design a natural, tactile quality. The material is more than a surface design choice, instead it shapes the way that the rooms feel.

The bathroom features natural materials and Swedish marble finishes.

The sauna can also be converted into a sleeping area for two additional guests.

The compact design also has a positive impact on the home’s carbon footprint. According to calculations by SAGA Space Architects, the EcoNeo House produces approximately 386 pounds of CO₂ annually, whereas an average vacation home for six people is estimated to emit about 2,282 pounds. This means emissions are approximately 83% lower. In addition to the small living area, bio-based materials are the primary factor contributing to this difference.

The boundary between indoors and outdoors blurs, bringing nature into focus.

The architects also designed the construction process to be as efficient as possible by digitally planning the house, precisely prefabricating its individual components, and then assembling them on-site in a modular fashion. This approach not only simplified assembly but also minimized the impact on the site.

Thanks to its small footprint, this vacation home’s emissions are 83% lower than those of the average vacation home that can accommodate six people. In addition, the modular construction minimizes the impact on the natural environment.

The EcoNeo House demonstrates that sustainable construction isn’t just a matter of the materials used. The size of a building and how the available space is utilized also play a crucial role. Though it may sound small, 377 square feet can be perfectly sufficient for a getaway home—provided the floor plan, storage space, and amenities are all carefully designed.

Although primarily intended as a summer home, this small vacation home can also be enjoyed in colder months.

A skylight lets light into the upper sleeping lofts.

The small vacation home has two separate sleeping lofts accommodating four people.

#4878. bp pulse makes 850+ EV fast chargers easier to use in the US

EV drivers have another way to charge at bp pulse without adding its app to their phones. The network has joined ChargeHub’s Passport Hub, connecting more than 850 charging points at over 95 US locations to ChargeHub and participating charging services and automakers.

Drivers can find, access, and pay for bp pulse charging through ChargeHub or participating services connected to Passport Hub.

Passport Hub handles the connections between charging networks and the services drivers use, including exchanging session information and settling payments. That lets a charging operator connect to multiple services through a single integration.

In practice, ChargeHub users can pull up a bp pulse location, select a port, and start charging from their existing account. The app displays the charging cost and wallet balance, and drivers need funds in their ChargeHub wallet to begin charging.

ChargeHub also shows real-time charger availability and lets drivers filter by connector type. Its bp pulse guide says most of the network’s chargers use CCS, with a growing share offering NACS.

Meanwhile, bp pulse has been adding physical charging sites, too. Recent openings include 12 charging bays at a TravelCenters of America location in Grand Bay, Alabama, and 22 bays near Dallas-Fort Worth International Airport.

Before starting a session, check the price shown in the app. ChargeHub’s guide notes that its Plus subscription waives activation fees, so using an existing account doesn’t necessarily mean paying the same total as another payment method.

#4875. Yamaha unveils new NMAX E, bringing electric power to its popular motorbike lineup

Yamaha is expanding its electric two-wheeler lineup with a much more capable machine, unveiling the new NMAX E as an electric version of its popular NMAX sport scooter.

The 2027 Yamaha NMAX E is being positioned as a 125cc-equivalent electric scooter, putting it firmly above the low-speed urban electric scooters that have made up much of Yamaha’s electric lineup thus far.

At its heart is Yamaha’s new compact eAxle drive unit, which sends power to the rear wheel through a belt drive. The electric motor produces 6.7 kW (9 hp) of nominal power and peaks at 7.5 kW (10 hp), along with 33.3 Nm (24.6 lb-ft) of torque.

Yamaha hasn’t published a top speed in the specifications, but the NMAX E’s A1 classification puts it in the same general category as 125cc combustion scooters rather than urban mopeds.

Power comes from a centrally mounted 3.5 kWh lithium-ion battery, which Yamaha says provides up to 104 km (65 miles) of range on the WMTC Class 1 testing cycle. The L-shaped battery is mounted low and centrally in the chassis to help maintain a low center of gravity.

Unlike many electric scooters that rely on removable batteries, the NMAX E uses a fixed pack. Yamaha has compensated for that with an onboard 800W charger, allowing riders to plug the scooter directly into a household outlet or an AC Type 2 public charging station. A full 0-100% charge takes around 5.5 hours.

Riders can choose between three riding modes. Sport (S) mode offers the quickest acceleration, Touring (T) provides smoother power delivery for everyday riding and carrying a passenger, and Eco (E) reduces acceleration and top speed while maximizing range. Regenerative braking is also included.

The rest of the scooter is fairly well equipped, including traction control, ABS, 245mm front and rear disc brakes, 13-inch wheels, a 33mm fork, dual rear shocks, and even a reverse mode to make low-speed maneuvering easier.

Nice, the Nmax has always been the premium scoot we try to get when traveling in southeast asia. Always felt like it would've been even better electrified. No removable battery seems a bit of a miss. That might constrain a big chunk of demand.

A five-inch TFT display includes smartphone connectivity through Yamaha’s Motor On app, providing turn-by-turn navigation as well as battery status, ride information, maintenance reminders, and other connected features. There’s also keyless ignition, USB-C charging, LED lighting, and illuminated underseat storage large enough for a full-face helmet, depending on its size.

Perhaps most importantly, the NMAX E looks like a proper Yamaha scooter rather than an electric experiment. Its dual headlights and distinctive side panels closely follow the styling language of Yamaha’s existing MAX family.

That could prove important as established motorcycle manufacturers increasingly attempt to bring electric models into their existing lineups rather than treating them as separate curiosities.

Pricing and exact market availability haven’t yet been announced, but Yamaha is positioning the NMAX E as a 2027 model for the European market.

#4874. 007 Off Duty: 10 Photos of Former James Bond Actors at Home

For a secret agent, James Bond generates a remarkable amount of public discussion. Barely a day goes by without fresh speculation about who will be the next actor to take on the role of 007. Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor are among the names linked to the role, with recent reports suggesting the search has reached final screen tests.

Whoever takes over will join the small group who have played the part since Sean Connery’s debut in Dr. No in 1962. Away from the screen, the homes of these former James Bond actors reveal more about the men in the dinner jackets. These archival photographs find them settling into family life and indulging interests that occasionally followed them onto set. Below, find a little domestic distraction while we wait for the next Bond to introduce himself.

Sean Connery relaxing in his ground-floor basement flat in NW5.

Photographed at home in England in 1955, Connery is still around seven years away from his first appearance as James Bond in this shapshot. His surroundings are modest, with little suggestion of the polished interiors his screen alter ego was about to frequent. Connery established the role of Bond across six films in during the 1960s and early 1970s.

Landing the role of James Bond required a little wardrobe planning on George Lazenby’s part. According to the official Bond website, the former model bought an unused suit made for Connery, then visited his predecessor’s barber at the Dorchester to request the same haircut. Producer Cubby Broccoli happened to be in the neighboring chair.

George Lazenby pictured at his home in Kensington, London, 1973.

His only appearance as 007 was in the 1969 film On Her Majesty's Secret Service. It gave Bond one of his most personal storylines, with Diana Rigg playing his ill-fated bride, Tracy. By the time of the Kensington photograph shown above in February 1973, Lazenby had already left the role. Seated with papers in hand, he is surrounded by books, records and a guitar propped beside the sofa. The glass-topped coffee table is crowded with objects, including a decorative horse’s head, while a substantial wooden cabinet provides storage behind him. Later that year, Roger Moore made his Bond debut in Live and Let Die.

Roger Moore reading the dictionary at his home in Los Angeles, circa 1955.

Roger Moore had yet to order his first on-screen Bond martini when he was photographed at home reading a dictionary, while working as a television actor.

Roger Moore kissing his wife, Welsh singer Dorothy Squires, outside their home in Bexley Heath, London, in 1957.

He married Welsh singer Dorothy Squires in 1953, and they lived primarily at The Mount—a mansion on Wansunt Road in Old Bexley, Kent.

The couple divorced in 1968, and a year later, Moore married Luisa Mattioli. Sherwood House in Denham, located close to Pinewood Studios, served as the couple's base during Moore's first three Bond films. In his autobiography, he described the property as “near London and perfect for a growing family.”

Its appeal extended to 11 acres of grounds, which included a tennis court, heated swimming pool and greenhouses when the house was advertised for £5 million in 2015.

When he finally took on Bond's on-screen persona, Timothy Dalton was well-prepared for the role.

Producers considered Timothy Dalton for Bond early in his career, but he felt too young for the role. When he eventually signed on for The Living Daylights in 1986, he reread Fleming’s novels to prepare for a more serious interpretation of the character.

Pierce Brosnan at home in Los Angeles in 1983.

Pierce Brosnan was making his name in Remington Steele when he posed at his Los Angeles home in June 1983, but his introduction to the world of Bond had come through his first wife, actress Cassandra Harris. She appeared in For Your Eyes Only, and visiting her on location in Corfu brought him into the producers’ orbit. Brosnan was offered the role in 1986, but was prevented from taking it due to his television commitments at the time. His debut in GoldenEye came almost a decade later, in 1995.

Pierce Brosnan playing pool with his son, Sean, at their Los Angeles home in 1992.

This 1992 photographs show Brosnan playing guitar on the sofa, shooting pool with his eight-year-old son Sean, and entertaining two dogs in the garden. Cream sofas and floral upholstery adorn the living room, while arched windows and white shutters open the space to the surrounding greenery. Taken the year after Harris died of ovarian cancer, the photographs capture Brosnan bringing up their family before Bond transformed his career.

Pierce Brosnan with his dogs outside of their Los Angeles home in 1992.

Painting had become a creative outlet for the star during Harris’s illness. Having trained as a commercial artist before acting, Brosnan returned to it in the late 1980s, recalling on his website: “I started painting again and out came every colour.” By the time he became Bond, painting was so sufficiently embedded in his life that his dressing room at Leavesden doubled as a studio during GoldenEye.

Daniel Craig attends the Bond 25 film launch at Ian Fleming's Home, GoldenEye, in Montego Bay, Jamaica, 2019.

For this final entry, the front door stays closed. Of all the former James Bond actors, Daniel Craig represents a more guarded approach to sharing domestic life. His wife, actress Rachel Weisz, explained the couple’s position in a 2012 conversation with British Vogue: “I think it’s pretty ordinary to think that marriage is private and sacred.”

Speculation over who will inherit Craig's martini glass keeps his name in the headlines even after his departure.

#4872. Maximo milestone: friendly robot has completed 300,000 tasks

After completing the first half of Amazon’s Bellefield complex last summer, Maximo engineers have put their helpful robot friend into high gear, with the latest version 3.0 robot-equipped construction crews installing as many as 24 solar panel modules per hour, per person. And now, the company is reporting that Maximo has completed its 300,000th construction task. (!)

If that 24 modules per hour, per person rate sounds fast, that’s because it is. At full tilt, the latest Maximo robot-equipped crews have nearly doubled the output of traditional installation methods at similar solar locations throughout Southern California. Now, with 300,000 tasks under its belt, the robot is proving to be both fast and durable.

“The future of solar construction depends on bringing together the experience of our people and new technology,” explains Matt Tetrault, President of Primoris’ Renewables business.

Like just about everyone these days, Maximo is playing up its machines’ NVDIVIA hardware, re-branding its robots’ repetitive automation as “physical AI.” That’s not to say the robots don’t work, however – as their deployment at Amazon’s Bellefield solar field is proving.

(By) leveraging NVIDIA AI infrastructure together with NVIDIA Omniverse libraries and NVIDIA Isaac Sim open robotics simulation framework, the Maximo team was able to develop, test and refine robotic capabilities through physics-based simulation and AI driven modeling before deploying updates in the field. The combination of AI, vision, robotics and simulation driven engineering reduced development and validation timelines and increased confidence in field performance as the robotic fleet scaled.

Amazon Web Services (AWS) powered the development, deployment, and operation of Maximo’s AI-driven field systems. AWS provides scalable computing, automated software delivery, and advanced data analytics, including real-time construction intelligence, enabling Maximo to collect operational robotics data and continuously improve performance.

The rapid deployment of solar panels at utility scale projects like this one, which is set to eventually reach over a GW of solar generating capacity, will help companies like Amazon reduce their carbon footprint, of course – but also save them big money as the price of fossil fuels continues to climb.

So Primoris’ Renewables is the name of a business, Maximo is the name of a solar robotics company incubated by The AES Corporation. Maximo makes a robot (also called Maximo I think) using NVDIVIA hardware and software, and also AWS cloud software, and Bellefield is a solar field owned by Amazon. Or so I gather so far.

The Maximo team says it has plans to extend its robots potential use cases beyond solar and into construction tasks that require similar pick and place and feeding and attaching movements, site logistics, and site cleanup. And like just about every other business that demands a high degree of physical labor, the construction industry is facing huge labor shortages, making machines like Maximo that provide real efficiency gains welcome additions to the job site.

#4870. Former CAKE team is back in the kitchen with a new electric motorcycle

If the new ReBake Abler electric motorcycle looks like it might share a bit of DNA with Sweden’s dearly departed CAKE, there’s a good reason for that.

ReBake Motors has officially unveiled its first electric two-wheeler at EICMA 2026 in Milan, introducing the Abler as a highly modular, utility-focused electric moped and light motorcycle.

Behind the new Swedish company is founder and CEO Karl Ytterborn, who spent eight years at CAKE from its founding in 2016 through 2024. CAKE famously built some of the most interesting minimalist electric motorcycles and utility bikes on the market before ultimately collapsing after failing to secure additional funding. Ytterborn says ReBake is intended to apply many of the lessons learned during that experience, while eliminating unnecessary cost and complexity.

Its chunky 42mm aluminum tube frame isn’t just a styling exercise. ReBake designed it as a mounting system, allowing cargo accessories to attach directly to the frame at multiple locations. That means riders can configure the bike for everything from carrying groceries and passengers to more serious utility duties.

There are two versions. The Abler45 is an EU L1e-B moped with a 45 km/h (28 mph) top speed, 4 kW nominal motor and 5.12 kW peak output. It starts at €3,990 including VAT.

The €4,990 Abler80 steps up to the A1 light motorcycle category, increasing nominal power to 6.14 kW and peak power to 7.68 kW while pushing the top speed beyond 80 km/h (50 mph). Both versions use a direct-drive rear hub motor.

Energy comes from removable 2 kWh lithium iron phosphate (LFP) batteries weighing 17 kg (37.5 lb) apiece. The Abler45 comes standard with one battery but can carry two, while the Abler80 includes two for 4 kWh of total capacity. With both batteries, ReBake claims up to 160 km (99 miles) of low-speed urban range on the Abler45 or 114 km (71 miles) on the Abler80. Each battery can be charged either on or off the bike from a normal 230V outlet, reaching a full charge in four hours.

The rest of the hardware includes 14-inch cast wheels, 140mm of front suspension travel, dual rear shocks, front and rear disc brakes with combined braking, regenerative braking, and full LED lighting.

Perhaps more interesting than any individual specification, though, is simply seeing another Swedish electric motorcycle startup emerge from CAKE’s ashes.

CAKE demonstrated that there was plenty of appetite for unconventional, lightweight electric motorcycles. Unfortunately, it also demonstrated just how difficult it is to turn that enthusiasm into a sustainable business.

ReBake appears to be getting another crack at that equation – this time with eight years’ worth of lessons already baked in.

#4869. 19 Best Christmas Ornament Picks, Vetted by AD (2026)

There’s no universally correct way to decorate a Christmas tree, though everyone seems to have an opinion about it. Some people swear by coordinated color palettes and perfectly spaced ornaments. Others treasure the funky glass baubles that have graced their family trees for decades, a hand-carved wooden figure picked up at a German Christmas market, or the lopsided ornament made in elementary school. The sentimental ones usually come with better stories. A Christmas tree, after all, is as much a collection of memories as it is a decorating opportunity.

And yet, there’s always room for one more. Maybe it’s a handblown glass ornament, a miniature version of your favorite food, or a decoration that’s so uniquely you it somehow feels essential. Your tree doesn’t need a theme, and your ornaments certainly don’t need to match. #4866. Tesla wins trade zone status for Texas Megapacks, but China tariffs stay

Tesla’s Megapack factory in Brookshire, Texas, is now officially a foreign-trade zone, a US customs program that lets manufacturers defer duties on imported parts. The FTZ Board’s approval, published in the Federal Register on Monday, runs for 14 months.

It’s easy to read this as Tesla getting a pass on tariffs for the Chinese LFP cells inside its Megapacks. Tesla’s own application says otherwise: parts hit by Section 301, 232, or 122 tariffs still pay them.

Tesla filed the request on May 20 for Subzone 84AM, part of FTZ 84 in the Houston area. That’s the factory where Tesla started Megapack 3 production in August, with a planned capacity of 50 GWh per year.

The application lists roughly 80 imported components Tesla wants to bring in under zone procedures, from coolant pumps and transformers to busbars, wiring harnesses, circuit boards and battery cells. Duty rates on those parts go up to 6.5%, while finished Megapacks and Bento inverters range from duty-free to 3.4%.

That gap is the usual reason companies set up an FTZ. A manufacturer in a zone can normally elect to pay the finished product’s lower rate instead of the rate on each imported part.

But the application spells out the limit. Components subject to duties under Section 122 of the Trade Act, Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act, or Section 301 “require subject merchandise to be admitted to FTZs in privileged foreign status,” according to the notice.

Privileged foreign status locks in a part’s own classification and tariff treatment when it enters the zone. So a Chinese LFP cell that ends up in a Megapack sold in the US still pays the Section 301 tariff on Chinese non-EV lithium-ion batteries, which jumped from 7.5% to 25% on January 1, 2026. Tesla just pays it later, when the finished Megapack leaves the zone.

Here’s what Tesla actually gets out of it:

The approval came with the same wording the FTZ Board has used all year. Production was “authorized on a limited basis,” and “further subject to a fourteen-month time period.”

Tesla got an identical decision for Megapack and inverter production at Fremont, Livermore and Lathrop in California this summer, with a one-year limit instead. Eastman Chemical, battery pack maker Rose Batteries, and chipmaker Qorvo all received similar time-limited, “limited basis” approvals in 2026.

The 14-month clock puts the expiry around the end of 2027. That’s roughly when LG Energy Solution is due to start supplying Tesla with LFP cells from Michigan under a $4.3 billion contract for Megapack 3, with mass production expected in the second half of 2027.

Tesla hasn’t hidden the issue. When tariffs escalated in April 2025, CFO Vaibhav Taneja said the impact on energy storage would be “outsized since we source lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery cells from China.” We wrote at the time that Tesla’s only growing business was kneecapped by Trump’s tariffs.

Tesla has since brought up its own LFP cell factory in Nevada, but Taneja said it would only cover a fraction of Tesla’s installed capacity. And Brookshire alone is designed for 50 GWh a year, on top of the 40 GWh Lathrop factory in California.

Tesla’s Megapacks are bringing big batteries to the grid, but you can get the same benefits at home by pairing solar with storage. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #4865. Hormone Therapy May Help Improve Menopause Brain Fog, Mood Changes

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Menopause hormone therapy (MHT), also known as hormone replacement therapy (HRT), helps manage various menopause symptoms.

Currently, MHT is approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to help relieve hot flashes, night sweats, vaginal dryness, and dyspareunia (pain during sex).

However, recent research has found that MHT may also help improve mental symptoms like “brain fog” and mood changes.

Other research also found that MHT does not affect dementia risk. This research followed the FDA’s removal of the black box warning on these products, which suggested they may increase the risk.

Together, these studies show that MHT may help people experiencing menopause in ways not previously realized and that it is generally safe to use.

Research published in Scientific Reports on September 1 suggests that an estrogen hormone treatment that combines estriol and progesterone may help ease the symptoms of brain fog during menopause.

Brain fog may involve memory lapses, difficulty concentrating, and slower thinking.

Just about two-thirds of women experience cognitive difficulties, like brain fog, during the menopausal transition. However, there is currently no Food and Drug Administration (FDA)- approved treatment for these symptoms.

This study was an observational case series that followed 20 menopausal women with an average age of 53. Each participant was given a baseline assessment for cognitive symptoms prior to treatment.

They were then treated for 12 months with a customized regimen of estriol and progesterone.

Estriol is a natural form of estrogen that occurs during pregnancy and has been used in Europe and Asia to treat other symptoms of menopause.

After 12 months, the participants reported significant reductions in:

“Estrogen plays a well-documented role in protecting the brain, yet there are still no approved type and dose of treatment that specifically targets the cognitive symptoms so many women experience during menopause,” Rhonda Voskuhl, MD, neurologist, member of the Comprehensive Menopause Center at UCLA Health, and senior study author, said in a press release.

“Women are often told to simply live with brain fog, when in fact there is a neuroscience basis for it, and potentially a way to address it. That gap in care is what motivated us to look more closely at estriol,” Voskuhl said.

This was a small, preliminary case series without a placebo group or randomization. More research is needed to confirm the benefits for cognitive symptoms.

A study published September 9 in Menopause, the journal of the Menopause Society, showed that hormone therapy may help improve mood changes in women in midlife.

The researchers noted that mood disturbances, such as depression, irritability, anxiety, and mental exhaustion, affect up to 68% of women during menopause.

“The erratic changes and loss of estrogen associated with this hormonal transition can lower serotonin and dopamine levels, which [are] associated with depression and cognitive changes, including poor concentration, brain fog, and memory loss,” said Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

The study included 260 individuals with an average age of 52 years. Just over half of the participants had a history of anxiety or depression, and 24% were using an antidepressant medication at the start of the study.

Each participant completed the Menopause Rating Scale (MRS) both before and after initiating hormone therapy for FDA-approved indications, such as hot flashes and night sweats.

The average follow-up during the study was 4.5 months. Between the baseline and the follow-up, there was a significant reduction in the MRS psychological scores. The percentage of participants with severe mood symptoms decreased from 62.3% to 24.6%.

Treatment response didn’t appear to differ by age, menopausal stage, psychiatric history, or antidepressant use. These findings suggest that hormone therapy may help improve mood symptoms in midlife, especially for those with higher severity of symptoms before treatment.

“This study supports the evidence that starting hormone therapy for the typical disruptive symptoms of menopause may bring collateral benefits, including improvement in mood,” Ross said. “Communicating with your healthcare provider is always best to manage your symptoms and prepare yourself properly for this next stage of life.”

For decades, women have been warned about the serious potential risks associated with menopause hormone therapy (MHT). This included an FDA “black box” warning citing complications like cardiovascular disease, cancer, and dementia.

However, a recent meta-analysis commissioned by the World Health Organization (WHO) that included data from more than a million women, and was published in The Lancet Healthy Longevity, found that MHT neither increased nor decreased dementia risk.

“The World Health Organisation currently provides no guidance on MHT and dementia risk, leaving a critical gap for women, clinicians, and policymakers. Our work will help inform upcoming WHO guidelines on reducing the risk of cognitive decline and dementia,” Melissa Melville, a PhD candidate and aging researcher in the Department of Clinical Health Psychology at the University College London, and first author of the study, told Healthline in a previous interview.

In the same interview, Melville stressed that the importance of this work is twofold: for clinicians, there is an urgent need for higher-quality research, and for patients, decisions about MHT should be guided by symptoms and well-established benefits and risks.

Higher-priced trims, including the Light Long Range, Wind, Land, and GT-Line, use a larger 99.8 kWh battery that delivers up to 305 miles of range in RWD variants.
