#4862. 6 Best Bamboo Sheets That Mirror Silk, Tested by AD (2026)

If you love the slight sheen and smooth feel of silk bedding like me, my guess is that bamboo sheets are probably up your alley too. The natural material can make your bedroom look elevated, and you won’t actually have to pay for pure silk or satin.

What makes bamboo sheets so popular is that they’re also just as good for hot sleepers: They have moisture-wicking properties, are hypoallergenic by nature, and feel cool to the touch (no disrespect to linen lovers). The lightweight weave also has a lovely drape to it. For a dramatic look, especially in a dark and moody color palette like this Upper East Side family home, these sumptuous sheets can’t be beat.

I’ve put on Sleep Week for the last couple of years, where AD editors share the essentials for styling a bedroom. Needless to say, I’ve tried a lot of sheets from our roster of beloved bedding brands to match my ever-changing space. I arguably have too many sets to count, some new and some old, to give my space personality—and my colleagues can say the same. After all this research, I can confidently share this concise list of our all-time favorites in the bamboo category.

For consistency, all of the prices reflect queen sizes. However, many brands reach up to a king size.

Cozy Earth is a personal favorite of mine for bamboo sheets—the brand’s bread and butter. They’re so breathable—even when I layer with a thick down comforter on top and throw blanket at the foot of the bed when it’s cold outside or when my AC is blasting—and have deep pockets to fit my notoriously tall mattress and topper combo. My taste just so happens to overlap with Oprah’s, if you’ve peeked at her Favorite Things list recently, which feels like a good omen, too.

I’m not the only fan, this set has earned the top overall pick in this story because pretty much every Shop AD writer, editor, and contributor who’s tried the Cozy Earth bamboo sheet set stands behind it for a few reasons: It is temperature-regulating, long-lasting (even after several cycles in the washing machine), and some of the softest bedding out there. Contributor Yelena Moroz Alpert calls her relationship to Cozy Earth a “borderline obsession” because of the natural fibers and neutral colorways that give the bedding an “earthy sophistication.” Shopping editor Audrey Lee calls the sheets “super light and airy, so it’s mostly a summer staple.”

Contributor Megan Wahn adds that the set is a favorite of hers because it feels much more expensive than it actually is: “I honestly thought these sheets were silk when I first slept on them, that’s how smooth and buttery soft they feel.” I love the walnut shade since my bedroom leans more moody, but if you’re into a clean, neutral shade like 2026’s color of the year, Cloud Dancer, I recommend the white or gray color for a calm, minimal bedroom. Their shore set is another style for anyone who likes a more coastal look. But Alpert says it best: “Not sure how Cozy Earth does it but the sheets feel like velvet without being heavy.”

Quince is the luxury-on-a-budget brand I’ve become pretty familiar with over the last two years of trying their duvets, sheet sets, and even their bath towels—and the bamboo sheets don’t fall short. Breathable bedding is a must for my sleep setup, and I’ve been a little surprised by how well I sleep through the night with this set. It feels smooth and cool to the touch, but still cozy enough to bundle up with during the winter months. One of my best friends is a hot sleeper, so whenever he visits, I use these (and the matching bamboo duvet cover) to make his bed. He says these truly keep him at a comfortable temperature throughout the night, so he’s never throwing the covers off mid-REM sleep.

This is the brand I’d recommend if you want to style your bed to a tee. I have the bamboo sheets in a warm terracotta hue to give my bedroom a serene, almost desert Airbnb-like aesthetic in the summertime. There are almost a dozen neutral and vibrant earth tones to match your bedroom’s design, though. These points, on top of the sub-$150 price tag, earned them a top spot in this list. However, if you’re like me and wear oily sheet masks and use thick body butters at night, you might notice some light staining on the sheets.

Because of their glossy sheen, these Luxome sheets probably look the closest to silk out of all of the picks in this list. I have the bedding in a truffle color, but what makes this option so unique are the jewel tones available, like sapphire, that are hard to find with other brands. The generous size of these sheets (they have a 17-inch pocket depth) gives them an even more elegant drape for a glam bedroom.

The neutral tones are compelling, too. For Sleep Week this March, the Shop AD team and I explored how to design a bedroom with a narrative, so we designed rooms inspired by classic novels. One of those storybook sanctuaries took inspiration from Dune, and I think Luxome’s sheets (which happen to have the same name) have a cool, chrome-like finish for fans of futuristic Sci-Fi novels like this one. All in all, these are also soft and breathable, a welcome relief for me when I’m combating hot NYC days.

Yes, we like heritage brands that have been acclaimed for ages at AD, but we’re always on the hunt for new and budget-friendly brands that are worthwhile, too. Sijo launched an Amazon-exclusive line a few years ago that offers a luxe feel and sustainable material sourcing at a lower price point. This basic Doz by Sijo set of bamboo sheets is another staple for my sleep setup. My skin has gotten pretty sensitive over time, and I love how soothed I feel when I sink into these sheets. They have a little more of a polished texture than other picks in this list, so I glide into bed. It’s hard to tell the difference between these and other high-end picks on the market by virtue of their material; it feels like a softer, less slippery satin. The pockets aren’t as deep as Cozy Earth’s, and the colors aren’t as striking as Quince’s burgundy and terracotta options, but for the price, I say it’s still a nice and simple set to shop.

Even though contributor Blake Bakkila prefers more textured bedding like linen, she says Cariloha’s “Aloha” bedding has been a saving grace to keep her cool. “These were the perfect sheets to layer on our bed during a heat wave that rocked Southern California, and they only got softer with each wash,” she shares. After Bakkila and her husband adjusted to the supple texture, she says the “temperature-regulating bamboo” sheets have been a great choice to keep in rotation since they’re “exceptionally smooth and lightweight” with a “cooling effect.” Keep in mind that these are truly thin sheets and almost have a transparent appearance. So if you’re looking for bedding that’s a bit weightier, the Quince or Cozy Earth in this list might be a better way to go.

These sheets are made of 100% bamboo infused with what Bearaby calls “smart botanicals,” including extracts like peppermint and marine proteins. They don’t feel sticky like some silky sheets can when it’s hot outside—they’re airy and refreshing to sleep on and the minty essence only enhances the feeling of freshness. These botanicals do more than smell good—they eliminate up to 99.9% of bacteria directly within the fabric. As your heat rises, the botanicals activate to cool you while keeping the material clean. And—to answer the million-dollar question—the sheets do live up to their “second skin” name—the fabric is so soft and fluid, it feels like it's melting on my body.

Here at Shop AD, we don’t test sheets in a lab—we test them in our very own homes. Why? Because that’s where you’ll be using them too. Each of the sets in our Best Of roundups has been washed, dried, and slept on for countless nights by one of our editors to ensure that they’ve put them through the ringer. The sets are selected to reflect a variety of price points, materials, and styles, and we pride ourselves in giving each set firsthand, real-world use.

Whether you’re a bamboo bedding faithful or it’s your first time exploring the category, there are a few factors I recommend keeping in mind below.

When it comes to the world of linens, bamboo fibers are made from the bamboo plant, which requires far less water than cotton and regrows quickly, so bamboo sheets are often considered one of the more eco-friendly options available. There are few popular bamboo materials: bamboo viscose, rayon, and lyocell. All are antimicrobial, soft to the touch, and absorbent. Depending on if and what they are blended with, they can take on more linen- or cotton-like properties. Here’s a breakdown of each:

The fabric’s weave will change the texture, softness, and general propensity for wrinkling. Below, here are more details on the two most popular weaves: twill, sateen, and percale.

To keep laundry day simple, the bedding in our list simply requires a machine wash and tumble dry. We generally recommend washing on cold and drying on low, as these gentler settings can help preserve the bedding’s quality. However, be sure to check the labels on the sheets for any specific care instructions.

While all bamboo sheets do a superb job of temperature regulating, your best bet (for overall comfort plus silkiness!) is to find a 100% bamboo viscose or lyocell set—like the Cozy Earth pick—with a thread count between 250 and 350, made with a sateen or percale weave. This typically indicates a durable weave that won’t wear easily over time.

As enthusiasts for all things interiors, we recommend looking for bamboo sheet sets that come in colors, prints, and patterns that can match your bedroom’s design. So, if you lean more minimalist, try neutrals like ivory. If you love maximalist style, go big with bold designs and hues—from embroidered details to splashy colors like terracotta or butter yellow.

You can usually find a set of this bedding type between the $50 to $400 range. To get the most out of your purchase, we recommend looking for eco-friendly certifications to help make sure your sheet set has the best quality. For example, the Oeko-Tex Standard 100 applies to bedding that is free of harmful chemicals.

In essence, yes—but how much so really depends on the quality of your set. Bamboo bedding is known for being cool to the touch overall, but the weave itself will ultimately determine the breathability of the sheets. Keep an eye out for bamboo sheets with a 300 thread count weave or above, as our editors mention that these typically have the most airflow for a good night’s rest.

Is Bamboo Viscose Better Than Egyptian Cotton?

Bamboo sheets, because of the strength of their fibers, actually have better longevity than cotton sheets, and offer superior moisture-wicking and temperature regulation to cotton sheets. Bamboo, especially bamboo viscose, also brags a smoother, more soothing feel against the skin compared to cotton, which can require more washes to get the the super soft or silky feel you desire.

Yes! The nature of bamboo is hypoallergenic, making bamboo bed sheets perfect for those with sensitive skin, eczema, or anyone prone to allergies. Bamboo’s super-fine fibers inherently resist things like dust mites, mold, and mildew. Even at the molecular level, it’s a moisture-wicking material, helping you stay sweat-free while also creating an environment inhospitable for dust mites (that need moisture). The fibers are naturally smooth, too, making for a soft sleeping experience.

How Do Bamboo Sheets Compare to Linen in Terms of Softness and Durability?

In our experience, both materials are durable when the sheets are made of 100% natural fibers instead of blends, but bamboo sheets are much softer. Unless it’s stonewashed, linen is usually known to feel a bit scratchy at first and then soften with wear and care. Bamboo sheets are softer down to their microscopic makeup, as their very fibers are smoother than linen.

#4860. Heart Failure Is Becoming More Common in Adults Under 65, Report Finds

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

An estimated 56 million people worldwide were living with heart failure in 2023, more than double the number in 1990, according to a new Global Burden of Disease (GBD) analysis published September 30 in JAMA Cardiology.

Heart failure is a chronic condition in which the heart cannot pump enough blood to meet the body’s needs. Despite its name, heart failure doesn’t mean the heart has stopped beating.

A separate 2026 report from the Heart Failure Society of America (HFSA) found that heart failure contributed to more than 450,000 deaths in the United States in 2023, with particularly sharp increases in mortality among adults under 65.

Approximately 7.7 million U.S. adults (3%) are living with heart failure, according to the HFSA report. That number is projected to rise to 8.7 million by 2030, 10.3 million by 2040, and 11.4 million by 2050.

The GBD researchers said the findings highlight the growing burden of heart failure associated with population growth and aging, as well as the need for prevention and treatment strategies targeting common causes such as ischemic heart disease and hypertension (high blood pressure).

Between 2010 and 2022, heart failure hospitalization rates increased among adults under 65 while declining among those 65 and older. Between 2001 and 2020, estimated heart failure mortality rates more than doubled among adults ages 25 to 44 and nearly doubled among those ages 45 to 64.

“I think we’re seeing the impact of cardiovascular risk factors developing earlier in life. Conditions such as obesity, diabetes, hypertension, and sleep apnea are affecting younger adults at higher rates than in previous generations,” Kevin Shah, MD, Program Director of Heart Failure Outreach at MemorialCare Heart & Vascular Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in Long Beach, CA, who wasn’t involved in the analysis, told Healthline.

“When those risk factors are present for decades, they can eventually lead to heart failure at a younger age,” he said.

Amit Blumfield, MD, a cardiologist at Manhattan Cardiology in New York City who was also not involved in the analysis, noted that improvements in early detection may also play a role.

“Our tools to detect early heart failure are also improving with time, and as a result, patients may receive an earlier diagnosis and can hopefully start medical therapy to prevent progression or possibly even reverse the process entirely.”

Heart failure contributed to 452,573 deaths in the United States in 2023, accounting for approximately 14.6% of all deaths that year, according to the HFSA report.

Age-adjusted heart failure mortality rates increased by approximately 37% between 2010 and 2023, with preliminary 2024 data suggesting the upward trend is continuing.

This increase appears to have reversed more than a decade of previous progress in reducing heart failure mortality.

Ischemic heart disease and hypertension were among the most common causes of heart failure in older adults, according to the GBD report. However, the underlying causes varied considerably by age group and region.

Among children and young adults, congenital heart abnormalities and rheumatic heart disease (RHD) were common causes of heart failure. Heart failure associated with alcohol use was most prevalent during middle adulthood.

The analysis also identified regional differences in the underlying causes of heart failure:

The global burden of heart failure is expected to grow as populations age.

While not all cases of heart failure can be prevented, managing certain risk factors can help reduce your risk. These include:

According to Shah, other important steps to help prevent heart failure include maintaining a healthy weight, staying physically active, and managing conditions such as diabetes and high cholesterol.

He added that regular medical care is important because many of these risk factors can be identified and treated long before heart failure develops.

“Heart failure is often treatable and may even be reversible with early diagnosis and treatment. It is important for patients to seek help early if they have concerning symptoms, such as exertional shortness of breath, chest pain, leg swelling, or anything else they’re concerned about. Talk with your doctors early and often,” said Blumfield.

#4859. This electric microcar just got a 50 mph upgrade, plus a secret Turbo mode

The adorable little Eli ZERO electric microcar is growing up. At this year’s EICMA motorcycle show in Milan, Eli Electric Vehicles is unveiling a faster, more powerful version of its pint-sized electric car, along with a sporty new limited-edition model that even includes a hidden Turbo mode.

First teased last week, the new Eli ZERO MX is the company’s first L7e-class electric vehicle, allowing it to reach speeds of up to 80 km/h (50 mph). That’s a significant jump from the 45 km/h (28 mph) top speed of the original L6e version that we originally reviewed, opening the door to a much wider range of driving scenarios.

And perhaps most importantly, the company managed to squeeze that extra performance into the same tiny package.

The Eli ZERO MX retains the original’s ultra-compact 2.25-meter (7.4-foot) length, making it considerably smaller than even the smallest conventional passenger cars.

But under that tiny exterior, the MX now packs up to 20 kW (27 hp) of peak power, compared to the 12 kW (16 hp) of the original Eli ZERO.

The upgraded model also features a 14.4 kWh lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery, providing a claimed range of up to 200 km (124 miles) per charge.

That’s a pretty respectable figure for a vehicle designed primarily for short urban and suburban trips.

The rear-wheel-drive microcar features selectable ECO and SPORT driving modes, a 30% climbing capability, and a tight 7.2-meter turning circle. Charging is handled by a 3.3 kW onboard charger, allowing owners to plug in at home or use public AC charging stations.

Despite its diminutive size, the Eli ZERO MX also includes a surprisingly long list of features normally associated with much larger cars.

Those include air conditioning and heating, a panoramic moonroof, soft-close doors, keyless entry and start, electric power steering, ABS, front and rear disc brakes, and an electronic parking brake.

There’s also an 8.8-inch digital instrument cluster, a reversing camera with rear radar, and an optional 10.1-inch infotainment display with Apple CarPlay.

The MX is available in four colors: Pearl White, Arctic Silver, Graphite, and Champagne.

The biggest difference, though, is the vehicle’s classification. While the original Eli ZERO falls under Europe’s L6e light quadricycle category, the new MX qualifies as an L7e heavy quadricycle. That means it generally requires a higher-level driving license, but also allows for considerably more performance.

For drivers who love the idea of a tiny electric runabout but need something capable of keeping up with faster suburban traffic, the MX could be a much more compelling option.

Eli isn’t stopping at a faster version of its microcar.

The company is also introducing the Eli ZERO Race Edition, a limited-production model available in both the original L6e and the new L7e configurations.

Finished in an exclusive Turbo Red paint job, the Race Edition features racing stripes across the hood, roof, and body panels, along with a rear spoiler and special wheel badging.

Inside, the sporty treatment continues with a two-tone Race Red microsuede and light gray interior, including special upholstery, dashboard accents, and embroidered seats.

But the most interesting feature isn’t immediately visible.

Eli has included a secret Turbo mode activated by pressing and holding the hazard light button. After four quick chimes, the digital instrument cluster switches to Turbo mode and the car’s acceleration response becomes sharper.

Importantly, the feature doesn’t increase the vehicle’s top speed or exceed its regulatory performance limits. Instead, it changes the way the car responds to accelerator inputs, giving it a sportier driving feel.

It’s a fun little Easter egg in a vehicle that already has plenty of personality.

Eli is also offering a new Carbon Aero Kit, consisting of a carbon fiber front splitter, side skirts, and rear wing. The kit is compatible with both the Race Edition and other Eli ZERO models.

Eli has spent years developing its vision of a smaller, lighter, and more efficient alternative to conventional cars, particularly for the short trips that make up much of our daily driving.

The company has already launched its second-generation Eli ZERO, which shares the same 96V electrical architecture and aluminum chassis as the new MX.

By expanding into the L7e category, Eli is addressing one of the biggest limitations of its original design: speed.

A 28 mph (45 km/h) microcar makes plenty of sense in dense urban centers, but its usefulness quickly drops off when drivers need to venture onto faster roads. A 50 mph (80 km/h) version, on the other hand, could make the same tiny vehicle practical for considerably more people.

In the US, this could go pretty much anywhere that didn't require highway driving. Perfect one-and-a-halfth car.

The Eli ZERO family is currently available through authorized dealers in several European markets, including Italy, Norway, and the Netherlands, with prices starting at €10,500 (approximately US $12,000) for the base model. Pricing for the new MX and Race Edition hasn’t yet been announced.

Eli also has a pilot program underway in the United States, though the new models are being introduced primarily for the European market.

Both the Eli ZERO MX and Race Edition will be on display at EICMA 2026 in Milan from November 3–8.

For a vehicle that takes up less than half the space of a conventional car, the Eli ZERO is certainly finding ways to make a bigger impression!

#4856. 7 Best Moroccan Rugs (2026)

The Moroccan rug is one of the easiest ways to add texture and intrigue to any room. Originally made to protect floors against the harsh cold of the Atlas Mountains, we see these lush, woolly carpets as a staple in the modern design world because of the range of color and style options available. The rug type has become synonymous with traditional undyed cream colors, vibrant jewel tones, and geometric patterns. It’s no wonder they pop up across tons of our home tours—like this artful Brooklyn studio and floral Victorian house—and have become a favorite among artists like Claudio Bravo and celebs like Patrick Dempsey.

Boucherouite, the hand-weaving technique employed to make these rugs, was created by semi-nomadic Amazigh tribes, and the style can be found in abundance in the busy souks of Marrakech. Now, with many direct-to-consumer and vintage Beni Ourain-inspired styles on the market, the perfect Moroccan rug is just one click away. With all the options at your disposal, it may be intimidating to know where to start. Fear not, we’ve rounded up a few top picks below along with advice on how we’d style them.

Give your guests the coziest welcome with this Azilal rug from Revival Rugs. Its long, narrow design makes it ideal for tight entryways. And while the oblong diamond pattern speaks to geometric patterns you’ll see across traditional Moroccan rugs, the pink tassel is a niche, colorful detail that won’t overpower surrounding decor. This is an authentic hand-knotted Moroccan rug, with wool locally sourced from the Atlas Mountains. Because of its small size, you may also consider hanging it up to add a textural element to your wall decor.

Break up the monotony of home office decor by placing an eclectic rug under your desk setup. While the soft grey and subtle design still create a distraction-free workspace, the cheeky oxblood hues add a touch of personality to make you smile between emails and Zoom meetings.

You don’t have to get a custom carpet straight from Marrakesh to have a high-end rug. The Saira rug from Quince is a handwoven option at an affordable price. The geometric motif is heavily inspired by Moroccan design and the medium-pile, hand-spun wool is designed to feel plush under your feet.

Calling all maximalists and color drenchers. The rich, vibrant colors from this rug come from a family of third-generation dyers, and the irregular shapes of the boxes infuse a quirky texture to whatever room they are placed in. Either have this rug as the standout piece in a room that is otherwise neutral, or pair it with other green pieces like this couch from Ellison Studios to engulf yourself in a verdant indoor forest of your own making.

Even though this muted rug may look understated at first glance, it brings visual interest thanks to the grid design along the border. The motif takes inspiration from Mediterranean tile patterns, in case you were wondering. We can see this as a soft, elegant layer underneath your dining table. Water-resistant and machine washable, this rug can survive stains and spills without making you bat an eye.

Why should the adults have all the fun? Bring whimsical design to your child’s bedroom or playroom with this Moroccan area rug from Benisouk. We have a feeling that the high pile will feel comfy for the kids to run around on and durable enough to withstand a little wear and tear from playtime. The dreamy creamsicle hues of this rug can match youthful decor, but the abstract minimalist design is elevated enough to be a piece that grows with them through the years.

If you find yourself longing for sleek, beige interiors—or are in the midst of remodeling your home to mirror an Aman resort—look no further. This handmade sheep’s wool rug is a minimalist dream. The neutral cream brings a layer of calm, while the organic shapes offer a wabi-sabi element that may be necessary in a pristine white room.

And with a targeted price of less than US $8,000, it could be one of Zero’s most accessible street motorcycles yet.

The bike features a new electric powertrain producing over 20 kW (27 hp) of peak power, delivering a sustained top speed of 65 mph (105 km/h) and a momentary maximum speed of 81 mph (130 km/h).

That’s a particularly interesting distinction, as it positions the bike as being able to briefly reach highway-capable speeds with the occasional burst of extra performance, but perhaps not for longer sustained highway stretches.

Importantly, we don’t yet know the battery capacity, though Zero is giving us a hint by publishing a range estimate. The company is quoting an estimated real-world range of 93 miles (150 km), but without a real battery capacity figure to parse, it’s hard to take that range estimate at face value.

We do know that the bike will feature onboard Level 2 charging, allowing riders to plug into public AC charging stations without carrying around a separate external charger – something more common on the smallest of electric motorcycles that attempt to cut down on weight and cost by using an off-board charger.

Other features include standard ABS, Bluetooth connectivity, integration with Zero’s upcoming smartphone app, and a chassis designed to accommodate a passenger.

The riding position is upright, and the bike’s supermoto-inspired geometry is intended to deliver agile handling and a playful riding experience.

Perhaps most interestingly, Zero isn’t hiding the fact that it sees the explosion of e-bikes and smaller electric motos as a potential gateway to motorcycle ownership.

“When we set out to build Project Ventura, we had three guiding principles: first, build a bike so good-looking you’d want its poster above your bed. Second, make it a proper electric motorcycle that feels like a natural next step for the millions of riders who’ve spent the last four or five years on e-bikes and e-motos. And third, make it ridiculously fun to ride,” explained Jim Callahan, Zero’s Chief Product and Partnerships Officer.

That’s a notable strategy for a company that has historically focused heavily on higher-performance electric motorcycles. However, the launch of Zero’s smaller Sur Ron-style electric dirt bikes last summer showed that the motorcycle maker did see the writing on the wall in terms of the success of the e-moto market compared to larger flagship bikes.

Instead of trying to convince existing gasoline motorcycle riders to make the electric switch, Zero is increasingly looking toward younger riders who may have already fallen in love with electric two-wheelers and are ready for something faster and more capable.

But for those interested in the new Ventura, they’ll have to wait just a bit longer. The motorcycle is expected to become available in summer 2027.

Another major announcement is a new street-legal version of Zero’s XE electric dirt bike.

The original XE was introduced as part of Zero’s more affordable X Line of lightweight electric off-road motorcycles, offering a more accessible entry point to the brand than its traditional street and dual-sport models. While the model was available in street-legal format in Europe, the US versions were purely off-road affairs.

Now Zero is taking that same general concept and making it legal for public roads, opening up significantly more riding possibilities.

The new XE is fully homologated and features the necessary street equipment, including a headlight, taillight, and centrally mounted dashboard. A small integrated flyscreen and black bodywork give the motorcycle a more refined appearance, while wide dual-sport tires help it transition between pavement and dirt.

Zero has also revised the gearing, using a rear sprocket designed to balance off-road performance with the higher speeds required for street riding.

The result is a claimed top speed of 60 mph (97 km/h), making the XE capable of considerably more than simply putting around the neighborhood, though with a slight torque sacrifice.

For riders who live near off-road riding areas, the appeal is obvious. Instead of loading a dirt bike into a truck or trailer to reach the trails, owners could potentially ride directly from home to the trailhead, provided local regulations allow it.

That’s a particularly useful capability for a lightweight electric dirt bike, where much of the appeal comes from its simplicity, low maintenance, and ability to explore places that larger motorcycles might struggle to reach.

The new street-legal XE is expected to launch in spring 2027, with pricing starting at US $6,295.

Zero is also expanding its electric scooter offerings with the European debut of the LS3, a faster and more capable scooter designed for urban transportation and longer commutes.

While Zero has traditionally been associated with electric motorcycles, its growing interest in scooters represents another effort to reach riders who prioritize everyday transportation over outright performance.

The LS3 offers a top speed of 75 mph (121 km/h), which puts it well beyond the typical low-speed urban electric scooter category.

Zero also quotes a range of up to 93 miles (150 km), powered by a battery system consisting of three onboard batteries with a combined capacity of 5.6 kWh.

Interestingly, one of those batteries is removable and located beneath the seat. Riders can remove it to free up additional storage space, offering some flexibility between maximum battery capacity and cargo-carrying convenience.

That could prove useful for commuters who don’t always need the scooter’s full range but would appreciate extra room for everyday items.

The LS3 also comes equipped with heated grips and a heated seat, features that should be particularly welcome to European riders commuting through colder months.

Connected technology rounds out the package, including compatibility with Zero’s upcoming smartphone app.

The LS3 is expected to become available in Europe in spring 2027.

Beyond the new vehicles, Zero is also working on a redesigned smartphone application that promises to give riders considerably more control over their motorcycles.

The new app is being developed for the LS3, 2027 Street and Dual Sport motorcycles, and Project Ventura.

One of the more interesting features is the ability to customize riding modes. In addition to preset modes that adjust power delivery and regenerative braking, riders will be able to create a custom mode and individually tune parameters including speed, power, torque, and regeneration.

The app will also include a charging-station finder, allowing riders to search for nearby chargers and filter them by charging speed, network, and availability. Apple Maps integration will provide route guidance to selected charging locations.

An optional ride-history feature can automatically record trips, including their distance, duration, and route previews.

Riders will also be able to customize their motorcycle’s dashboard layout, choosing which information appears in each quadrant of the display.

Battery management is getting some attention, too. The app will provide controls for scheduled charging, target charge levels, charging limits, and long-term battery storage settings.

Zero is also adding integrated feedback and support tools that allow owners to report problems or suggest improvements directly through the app, including photos and diagnostic logs.

The redesigned app is expected to arrive in 2027.

Perhaps the most consequential technology announcement, at least for riders who want to take their electric motorcycles beyond short commutes and weekend rides, is Zero’s continued development of DC fast charging.

Zero says it is targeting a 2027 launch for its new fast-charging system, which will support compatible Street and Dual Sport motorcycles.

The system is being designed to charge compatible batteries from 20% to 80% in less than 30 minutes.

That’s a major improvement in convenience compared to the longer AC charging stops that have traditionally been associated with Zero motorcycles.

Importantly, the company is developing the system for both North America and Europe, with different charging connectors for each market.

European motorcycles will use the CCS standard, while North American models will feature NACS connectors.

The latter is particularly interesting given the growing adoption of NACS charging infrastructure across North America, potentially making it easier for electric motorcycle riders to access a wider range of charging locations.

Zero is also developing a retrofit option for eligible existing motorcycles, meaning some current owners may eventually be able to upgrade their bikes rather than purchasing entirely new models to take advantage of the technology.

The company hasn’t yet announced pricing or a complete list of compatible motorcycles, and the retrofit option remains under development.

Still, the prospect of adding significantly faster charging to existing electric motorcycles could be an important development for the brand.

There are several exciting announcements here, but I think the biggest takeaway is that Zero seems to be increasingly focused on making electric motorcycling more accessible through smaller and more affordable options.

For much of its 20-year history, Zero has been a pioneer in the electric motorcycle industry, often offering impressive performance and technology, but at prices that made its motorcycles difficult for many riders to justify. The bikes have long won acclaim, but $20k was a big ask.

The company’s more affordable X Line bikes have already demonstrated that Zero is interested in attracting riders who might never have considered one of its larger electric motorcycles. The street-legal XE takes that concept a step further, and Project Ventura could be an even more important addition.

I’m particularly intrigued by Zero’s explicit acknowledgment that millions of people have spent the last several years riding e-bikes and smaller electric motos, and that many of those riders may be ready to graduate to something bigger.

That’s an audience I’ve been watching grow for years. These are riders who are already comfortable with electric drivetrains, who appreciate the simplicity of twist-and-go riding, and who don’t necessarily have any attachment to gasoline engines or traditional motorcycle culture.

For those riders, a lightweight, fun electric motorcycle capable of 81 mph and priced below $8,000 could be an awfully compelling next step.

The new street-legal XE is similarly exciting, especially for riders who want to combine urban transportation with off-road adventures without needing a truck to haul their bike around.

During the late 60’s to the early 70’s the in thing in motorcycles was the street legal dirt bike. I had a 1971 model Kawasaki 125 street legal dirtbike that I bought for $525 and it was great for getting around as a kid. When this street legal type of EV bike goes below $5000 it could be the next big thing.

And while the new motorcycles are likely to get most of the attention, I wouldn’t overlook the DC fast-charging announcement. Charging speed has long been one of the biggest limitations facing electric motorcycles, particularly for riders interested in longer trips. A sub-30-minute charging stop isn’t quite gasoline-level convenience, but it’s getting much closer to something that can work for real motorcycle touring.

Of course, several important details remain unknown, and we’ll have to wait until 2027 to see how these products and technologies perform in the real world.

But taken together, these announcements suggest Zero is making a serious effort to expand beyond its traditional audience and build electric motorcycles for a much broader range of riders.

And after two decades of helping pioneer the electric motorcycle industry, that’s exactly the kind of evolution I’d like to see.

#4854. Viola Davis Says Shingles Pain Was a ‘9 Out of 10’: How It Changed Her Life

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Emmy, Grammy, Oscar, and Tony Award-winning actress and producer Viola Davis was 50 years old and at the height of her career when she developed shingles.

“All of a sudden, I got a rash in the middle of traveling and juggling [back-to-back] movies that started in the middle of my stomach [and] wrapped around my back,” she told Healthline. “And the pain was definitely at a 9 out of 10.”

Focused on work, Davis didn’t see a doctor right away. Her symptoms eventually subsided but later returned on her upper back.

“That is when I was like, ‘Oh, my goodness, I just got bitten by a poisonous spider,” said Davis. “It felt like really bad nerve pain…then someone mentioned the word shingles.”

After seeing a doctor, Davis learned she had shingles, which is caused by the same virus responsible for chickenpox. She also learned that the risk increases with age as the immune system naturally weakens.

“It can also lead to herpetic neuralgia, lifelong pain that can result from a shingles outbreak,” Robyn Liu, MD, member of the board of directors at the American Academy of Family Physicians, told Healthline.

Shingles can affect daily life, including work and sleep.

“Many people don’t realize that shingles can be associated with nerve pain, and for some, that pain can last for months or even years after the rash has resolved due to potential long-term complications,” Jennifer Caudle, DO, family medicine physician and associate professor at Rowan University School of Osteopathic Medicine, told Healthline.

Nearly one in three people in the U.S. develops shingles in their lifetime.

“Anyone who has had chickenpox can get it, but the risk goes up as we age,” said Liu.

After learning about shingles, Davis said she realized that she could no longer ignore her body.

“You just cannot. I mean, I know that people say it at nauseam, but, for me, it meant slowing down,” she said. “But then the shingles slowed me down…even though I was getting up, I was working out, [the] pain and the sluggishness was awful.”

Now 61, Davis has teamed up with GSK for its “Shingles, actually” campaign to raise awareness about shingles.

“[I’m] sending the message out there for women to really honor themselves and honor their bodies,” she said. “Their bodies literally speak. They’re the other character in their lives.”

After her experience with shingles, Davis began scheduling regular annual checkups.

“Before that time, I did absolutely go to my doctor, but only when it was absolutely necessary, you know, to get my colonoscopy, get my mammogram, but I never went if something felt odd,” said Davis.

Now, whenever she feels off, she doesn’t hesitate to see her doctor. She makes it a point to be honest with them about what’s going on with her body, and she is not shy about asking questions.

“I feel that it’s my job. It’s what I owe myself, and not only [do] I owe myself, it’s what I owe my daughter, it’s what I owe my husband, it’s what I owe my elderly mom, who is in advanced dementia [and] I take care of her,” Davis said.

Caudle stressed the importance of bringing questions to your doctor.

“That’s what we’re here for. Sometimes patients ​tell me they don’t realize they can ask questions or introduce a topic that their healthcare provider didn’t bring up first,” she said. “But a simple question like, ‘Am I at risk for shingles?’ can be a great place to begin.”

Davis has made stress reduction a priority. When she developed shingles, she was juggling an intense workload and adjusting to life as a new mom after adopting her daughter.

“I was someone who just ignored stress,” said Davis. “[When] we meet each other, it becomes a contest: Who can work through their stress the best? I felt like I had to strike while the iron was hot.”

Today, Davis makes stress reduction a priority through activities such as spraying her pillows with lavender, taking Epsom salt baths, meditating, going to therapy, exercising, spending time outdoors, using vagus nerve stimulation, and connecting with family and friends.

“It’s like I overdo it now because I feel like I have to overdo it in order to compete with my schedule. But that’s what I owe myself,” Davis said.

Still, her profession can be intense and time consuming due to shooting and promotional schedules. She still makes time to de-stress and focuses on projects that bring her joy.

“Your career is only as important as how much you enjoy it; how much it adds to your life,” said Davis. “[For] me, it used to be something that I felt defined my worth. I don’t feel like it defines my worth now…I feel that I’m already worthy… I was born worthy. That’s how I see it.”

Vaccination is the best way to protect against shingles, Liu said. The shingles vaccine is more than 90% effective at preventing the disease.

“For children, that protection begins with the chickenpox vaccine, which can be given along with other routine childhood immunizations. Preventing chickenpox also lowers the risk of developing shingles later in life,” she said.

Liu recommends two doses of the shingles vaccine for adults 50 and older, as well as adults 19 and older with weakened immune systems.

“Some patients may have more questions because side effects can be more noticeable than with other vaccines, so it’s important to understand the tradeoff: short-term discomfort from the vaccine versus the risk of lasting complications from shingles,” said Liu.

Side effects may include headache or redness, swelling, itching, and soreness where the shot was given.

“I tell my patients that these reactions are common and usually clear up within a day or two. They’re a normal sign that your body is building protection,” said Liu.

#4852. Urologists Have Some Hot Takes on Whether Men Should Sit or Stand to Pee

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

AROUND THE WORLD—and on Reddit—there’s an eternal debate raging about whether men should sit or stand to pee. In the US, the answer seems like a no-brainer: standing. One YouGov poll found that only 10 percent of American men regularly sit down to pee, while 31 percent said they never do this. (There’s even that viral video years ago of a pastor arguing “a man is someone who pisses against the wall.”)

But that same YouGov poll found that where you wee varies. In Germany, 40 percent of guys actually sit down every time to urinate. About 22 percent of Swedish men, for example, sit down every time, but 28 percent sit down most of the time. Entire threads online are devoted to this question, including a hybrid tactic as a compromise in which a guy pees sitting down at home but stands up at a urinal in public.

Looking beyond preference, is there a right answer to take a leak? One study published in the journal Neurourology and Urodynamics tried to give a definitive answer. Depending on your camp, the answer might validate what you know—or piss you off.

Researchers had no issue finding 52 guys to sign up for this peeing experiment. These dudes had no prior health issues and were between the ages of 18 and 39. The guys were randomly divided into two groups that sat to urinate. Then they would stand to pee. The other group did the same but in reverse order. Between each change in position was a "washout period" of 24 hours where guys did their business as usual.

Researchers tracked how much urine came out and how quickly. So who reigned supreme on the porcelain throne? Well, neither.

Sitting or standing to urinate didn’t change how much pee they got out in each session. There was a slight difference in how fast they got to max flow, though. Men were able to reach their peak speed slightly sooner when they peed sitting down, but researchers say this finding was “unlikely to be clinically meaningful and was partly dependent on a single outlier.” Meaning, one guy was pissing really hard while sitting, while everyone else peed at a similar rate and volume no matter what position they did it.

The findings are not surprising to S. Adam Ramin, MD, urologist and medical director of Urology Cancer Specialists. “If you're a normal, young, healthy male, it will make no difference whether you sit or stand to urinate,” he says. “You will have a good stream either way.”

THE FINDINGS WOULD likely be different for older men, especially those with an enlarged prostate. “Sometimes in older men with an obstructing prostate, sitting to urinate may help them to empty their bladders more effectively as they are able to generate more abdominal force to squeeze the urine out,” says Boback Berookhim, MD, urologist at Northwell’s Lenox Hill Hospital.

HEALTHWISE, NOT REALLY. But if you have a certain preference for one over the other, then there’s no harm in sticking with one position. There's some convenience with standing to pee: You pull it out, do your business, and move on. Peeing this way is “likely to save time,” says Dr. Berookhim.

Sitters, on the other hand, might prefer the position because it’s slightly more hygienic. Dr. Ramin says guys are more likely to splash, sprinkle on the toilet seat, or even miss entirely when they’re standing versus sitting. Plus, sitters might be more likely to wash their hands after every use. Men are 1.5 times less likely to wash their hands than women after peeing—probably where they could use those saved seconds.

Beyond that necessary bathroom etiquette, there’s really no other reason to switch up your tried-and-true urinary habits. Dr. Berookhim says he certainly wouldn’t tell a young, healthy guy to change the way they are using the bathroom if they are already doing fine.

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#4851. A Brooklyn Townhouse Celebrates a Forgotten Design Pioneer

In the early 1920s, the Pittsburgh-based manufacturer Armstrong Cork Company (now Armstrong World Industries) made the prescient move of tapping a young arts educator named Hazel Dell Brown to act as its in-house decorator. Hers would be an under-recognized yet wildly influential career. Over the course of more than three decades, she designed countless rooms for advertisements, booklets, catalogs, and more that showcased the brand’s products, chief among them linoleum flooring. With their bold colors, fearless mix of patterns, and daring pastiche, her interiors shaped an entire generation of homes in the Rust Belt and beyond.

For two contemporary artists setting out to renovate a Brooklyn town house for their young family, Brown’s little-known work recently served not only as inspiration but also as a litmus test. When interviewing architects to take on the project, the couple first presented examples of vintage Armstrong schemes. “A lot of people would take one look and then show me something different,” reflects the wife, who learned about Brown while working on a project in Pittsburgh. Not so in the case of Tal Schori and Rustam Mehta of GRT Architects. The AD100 duo instantly responded to Brown’s surface-heavy approach and inward-facing embrace of small spaces, or as Schori puts it, “rooms within rooms.” As for their interior designer, Penelope August, a friend of the wife’s since preschool—she implicitly passed with flying colors.

A circa 1980 Gio Ponti chandelier crowns the living room, which is furnished with a vintage Vladimir Kagan sectional sofa and a Jens Risom chair upholstered in a custom fabric based on a Macgregor Harp painting; artwork by Rose Marcus and wallpaper by Antoinette Poisson.

The kitchen features art-glass cabinet fronts by Paul Wissmach Glass Company and a custom terrazzo floor inspired by ancient roman mosaics of unswept debris.

Hembus wallpaper in a custom blue distinguishes the primary bedroom; painting (above bed) by Michael Wang.

The project evolved into a dynamic collaboration as the couple and their team gutted the town house, set in Carroll Gardens and previously renovated in the 1990s. Together they took care to preserve historic details like the original doors and staircase, with its original mahogany newel posts and handrails. Wherever appropriate, they embraced imperfections, filling in the gaps between old floorboards with contrasting tinted epoxy and leaving levels ever so askew. “This house shows signs of age and that’s not a bad thing,” says Schori. Adds Mehta, “As artists, they understood what is and isn’t precious so that the home does not feel overly restored.”

The backyard was updated by Brook Landscape, with a shed and swing set by GRT Architects.

In the son’s room, shaker-style cabinetry reveals a reading nook.

A refurbished tub from The Demolition Depot anchors the primary bath, which is lined in tiles by Balineum (on walls) and Artistic Tile (on floor).

Brown was nothing if not a gleeful problem solver, presenting flexible solutions to the needs of everyday life, often through inventive secondary spaces. (“Your rumpus room can be anything you like” proclaimed one Armstong campaign.) True to that strategy, GRT devised a floor plan full of versatile surprises. Adjoining the dining room is a cave-like lounge for watching television or just relaxing, with a pass-through to the kitchen for the easy arrival of snacks. Opposite the cookspace—more on that showstopper later—is a cozy breakfast area, with a load-bearing skylight that offers glimpses of the roof terrace (and sometimes curious children) above. And on the second level, a secret passage leads from the primary suite into the library, one column of shelving concealing a door. “All these appendages to the main spaces allow multiple things to happen at once,” says Schori.

In the daughter’s room, Shaker-style trim is painted in Farrow & Ball’s Blue Ground.

The kids’ bath mixes terrazzo walls with custom floor tiles by Haand.

Oak millwork incorporates vintage Piero Fornasetti panels in the dining room, where Guillerme et Chambron chairs surround an antique farm table; painting by Jo Nigoghossian, and custom fireplace fabricated by Daniel Armitano.

Surfaces and furnishings tend toward the eclectic and one-of-a-kind, with a mix of wallpapers and fabrics, some deadstock, setting the stage for vintage treasures. In the living room, a Vladimir Kagan sectional sofa found at auction doubles as a jungle gym of sorts. The primary bath is anchored by a salvaged earthenware tub, since glazed a light blue to complement the retro checkerboard tiles and scalloped vanity. And the dining room’s custom millwork incorporates a series of midcentury Fornasetti panels. Doorknobs, lighting, motifs, art by their circle of creative friends—it’s all a mishmash. “We liked things independently of how they worked together,” says the wife. Adds August: “It was so fun to stitch all these elements into an old house—I love a space where you come upon something unexpected.”

A detail of the kitchen’s terrazzo floor shows stone and metal shapes (some abstract, some legible) inspired by detritus.

A custom sofa frames the lounge off the dining room, which has a pass-through to the kitchen; carpet by Stark.

In the living room, a Gretta Johnson painting hangs above the fireplace, which is flanked by artworks by Cletus Johnson (left) and Ted Mineo (near right) and Miles Huston (Far right).

The husband’s office/music room is cocooned in cork; painting (at left, bottom) by MacGregor Harp.

The stairwell’s painted checkerboard floor hints at classic Americana.

Floor to floor, the mood shifts. The son’s and daughter’s rooms each riff on classic Shaker woodwork, with rails and built-in cabinetry that respond to the exposed ceiling joists. Enlivened by a splatter-painted floor and celestial configuration of ceiling lights, the plywood-lined attic operates as a kids’ club, craft room, and family theater in one. (How’s that for a rumpus room, Hazel?) Thanks to some clever excavation and engineering on the part of GRT, the combined garden level and cellar is now a double-height studio for the wife, complete with a gantry crane for hoisting heavy sculptures. Outside, meanwhile, Brook Landscape transformed the backyard into a romantic swath of greenery.

Vintage globe lights from a one-time optometry office add a surreal touch to the powder room.

Loose arrangements of greenery frame the steps to the backyard.

GRT Architects combined the garden level and cellar into a double-height art studio with a register of exposed brick.

A vintage Vilhelm Lauritzen ceiling light punctuates the primary bedroom; painting by Margaux Ogden.

As for that kitchen, here the team took inspiration not only from Brown but from two additional touchstones. Cabinet fronts nod to art glass at the Upper East Side’s late great Glaser’s Bake Shop, the salvaged interiors of which August and the wife chanced upon at The Demolition Depot. The floor, meanwhile, riffs on ancient Roman mosaics of unswept detritus, an example of which can be found at the Vatican Museums. “We wanted a kitchen that wouldn’t look bad if it got messy,” says the wife. Working closely with GRT and August, she and her husband evolved that historic precedent into a contemporary series of shapes, some abstract and some legible (a knife, a carrot, a wine glass). Rendered in scraps of stone or metal, their forms now appear to float in poured terrazzo.

“The way artists think about objects and architecture is as a constant work in progress,” says Mehta. “These are visual people with an incredible mental library of references.” Would Brown approve? Without a doubt. As the designer once wrote: “Whatever you do, use your own ideas. Don’t be afraid of them.”

#4850. BYD’s 3,000-hp EV crashes at about 280 mph — Watch the driver escape unharmed

BYD’s electric supercar delivers an insane nearly 3,000 hp from four electric motors. During a high-speed test, the Yangwang U9 Extreme crashed at about 280 mph, but the driver walked away unharmed.

The Yangwang U9 Xtreme is a “dream car,” as BYD calls it, a souped-up, track-focused version of its electric hypercar.

After it became the world’s fastest production car last year, reaching a top speed of 496.22 km/h, or about 308 mph, BYD is attempting to beat it by breaking the 500 km/h (310 mph) mark.

The Yangwang U9 Xtreme features the world’s first mass-produced 1,200V ultra-high-voltage platform and BYD’s e4 platform.

The quad-motor setup is the first in the world rated at 30,000 rpm, BYD said, delivering nearly 3,000 hp (3,000 PS) combined.

While BYD is preparing to attempt a run this month, the company shared a video of the Yangwang U9 Xtreme crashing in a violent high-speed test.

During the first run, it clocked over 350 km/h (217 mph), no problem. On the second run, it hit over 420 km/h (261 mph).

However, during the third run, BYD was attempting to reach over 450 km/h (280 mph) when the vehicle violently crashed into the guardrail and continued spinning out of control. You can see some of the footage from inside the vehicle.

BYD said that after a comprehensive medical exam, the hospital found the driver in good health with no serious injuries.

When I was still in law enforcement, we did some pursuit driving training and you learn a lot about the dynamics of a vehicle. It's amazing just how quickly and badly something can go wrong at just 100 mph let alone nearly triple that speed. The fact is that the average person can't drive to the max limits of a basic car like a Corolla let alone super cars.

Is it time to reign in the HP and speed wars? Do we need vehicles that can do 0-60 in less than 3 seconds? Unless these companies begin to require 40+ hour specialized training courses in order to buy/operate these high HP vehicles today, then maybe it's time to stop the silly push for more.

And BTW. No matter what the performance levels are of any cars today, I still feel that it would be smart to push legal driving age to at least 17 or 18 instead of 16 and make getting a DL harder to do with way more emphasis on driver's education training.

The vehicle’s high-strength carbon-fiber cabin also held up impressively despite the front and rear damage.

It didn’t take long for BYD to try again, reaching a top speed of 457.89 km/h (284.5 mph) on the next run. Jiang Gong, one of Yangwang’s riders, said he sees some leeway and believes it can break 500 km/h (310 mph).

At the end of the video, BYD said (translated into English), “The ultimate pursuit has no end; the summit will eventually be reached.”

#4849. Tesla cuts FSD’s speed margin from 50% to 10% over the limit in Europe

Tesla’s driver-assistance system in Europe can no longer be set to drive 50% over the speed limit. The Netherlands’ RDW, which approved the system in April, confirmed today that it signed off on an update capping the speed margin at 10% and officially renaming “FSD Supervised” to Tesla Assisted Driving.

The changes land weeks before EU member states could vote on approving the system across the bloc, and they match the conditions Germany set last month.

The RDW’s notice is the clearest official confirmation of how the system was configured when the Dutch regulator approved FSD Supervised on April 10. The “speed margin,” which lets the system deviate from the detected limit for “safety and traffic flow,” could go up to +50%. On a 100 km/h highway, that’s 150 km/h. In a 30 km/h city zone, it’s 45 km/h.

Now the margin tops out at +10%. It also resets to 0% every time the car starts, and the RDW says other speed-related functions are now disabled by default. It didn’t say which ones.

The new name shows up on the in-car display, in the owner’s manual, and in other driver information on HW4 vehicles. Tesla pushes all of it over the air.

The RDW describes the update as “several minor changes” that Tesla asked it to assess. The regulator says it checked the implementation, found no adverse or unintended effects, and folded the changes into the existing type approval without amending or extending it. It also stresses that the rename doesn’t change what the system does. It’s still driver assistance, and the driver is still responsible.

The RDW notified the nine member states that recognize its approval: Belgium, Croatia, Czechia, Denmark, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Slovakia, and Slovenia. That’s 10 countries counting the Netherlands, up from eight at the start of October.

The EU-wide decision still sits with the Technical Committee on Motor Vehicles (TCMV). The RDW says those talks are confidential and won’t comment on them. As we reported, the EU pushed the vote to December at the earliest, and passing it takes a qualified majority: 15 of 27 member states representing at least 65% of the EU population.

Speed has been the biggest problem for Tesla’s file in Brussels. In June, Sweden asked the EU to reject FSD unless Tesla removed the ability to set speeds above the posted limit. In September, a Brussels pedestrian safety group found the system speeding in 55% of the 30 km/h segments it tested, in a country that had already approved it. France has cited urban speeding as one of its reasons for refusing to recognize the Dutch approval.

Then, on October 6, Germany’s Transport Ministry said it would back EU approval after Tesla agreed to drop the “Full Self-Driving” name, which the ministry called misleading, and to cap speed tolerance at 10%. Today’s RDW update delivers both.

But 10% isn’t what Sweden asked for. Sweden wanted no setting above the limit at all. Several European countries are known for enforcing speed limits strictly.

Good, but that’s only part of the problem. Tesla’s biggest issue with FSD right now is that it doesn’t properly detect the speed limits.

Doing 10% over the limit in North America is almost expected. However, in Europe you can get a speeding ticket for 5 kms over the limit by an automated camera/radar system. When I rent a car over there I drive the posted limit.

Being locked at 10% over the limit doesn’t mean much if you don’t know the actual limit.

The name change is needed globally and it is past due. “Tesla Assisted Driving” describes what the system does. Meanwhile, Tesla still sells the exact same kind of software in the US as “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)” for $99 a month. If it’s misleading in Berlin, it’s misleading in California.

Practically, this update gets Tesla Germany’s vote. The question for December is whether a 10% cap is enough for France, Italy, and Spain, or whether Sweden’s zero-tolerance line wins out.

#4847. The Peptide Revolution Has Hit Your Grooming Routine

Potent compounds in topical products promise to strengthen hair, hydrate skin, and even buff out wrinkles. It’s time you harness their powers.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

YOU’VE HEARD ALL about the kinda-sorta-illegal new world of supposedly health-transforming injectable peptides. But what’s wild is that topical peptides are totally legal and have long been hanging out in the grooming aisle—and some are actually backed by science. Yes, the right peptide-powered products can soften wrinkles, fade dark spots, and moisturize well beyond the traditional stuff. Are they worth your time and money? Possibly. Is there a lot to unpack before you slather them on? Definitely.

THEY’RE PROTEINS. OR, more specifically, “chains of amino acids that vary in length,” says Carl D’Ruiz, a senior manager of business development at global bioscience company DSM-Firmenich. “Peptides can be classified by the length of their chain—tripeptides feature three amino acids, pentapeptides have five, and so on—or their biological function.” To create peptides for grooming products, technicians either construct them in a cosmetics lab or pull them from natural materials such as yeast, E. coli, or animal cells.

LOTS. BUT HERE’S the thing: Because many brands use proprietary, patented peptides or peptide blends, independent research on specific products is scarce. Yet in general, there is some data to support the use of certain peptides for skin health. Three types of peptides are the most well studied:

“THE DATA IS less robust than what we have for skin, but certain peptides show promise for hair health and can improve density with consistent use,” says Dr. Nakhla. A recent research review found that short-chain peptides, particularly GHK-Cu, improved hormone-related pattern hair loss. The theory is that, again, by improving cellular communication, peptides tell follicles to retain hair longer or grow thicker. Peptides aren’t as effective as proven hair-strengthening ingredients like minoxidil or finasteride. But they seem to carry fewer side effects. (Topical minoxidil can cause itchy-as-all-hell contact dermatitis in some patients; compounded topical finasteride products, which are not FDA approved, may cause insomnia, erectile dysfunction, and anxiety.)

The high concentration of peptide-packed plasma is designed to shrink pores, erase wrinkles, and clear up dark spots.

#4846. PMOS, Formerly PCOS, May Be Associated with Alzheimer's Disease Risk

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome (PMOS), formerly known as polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS), has been linked to changes in brain function and cognition that may overlap with those seen in Alzheimer’s disease.

However, a review published October 13 in the Journal of the Endocrine Society suggests that metabolic conditions commonly associated with PMOS, rather than PMOS itself, may help explain a potential link to Alzheimer’s disease and dementia risk.

“Researchers are exploring whether some metabolic conditions commonly seen in women with PMOS, such as insulin resistance, obesity, type 2 diabetes, [and] elevated cholesterol, could play a role in dementia, since these are already known risk factors for cognitive decline,” Yesika Garcia, MD, board certified endocrinologist with Medical Offices of Manhattan, who was not involved in the study, told Healthline.

“In other words, any potential connection may be related to these underlying metabolic issues rather than PMOS itself. I think it’s important to clarify that an association does not mean causation,” she added.

The researchers searched PubMed for studies published through 2025 examining brain structure and function, cognitive performance, biomarkers associated with neurodegenerative diseases, and dementia risk in people with PMOS.

Because PMOS is a newer term, the researchers searched for studies using both PMOS and PCOS.

The review identified studies suggesting that people with PMOS may experience mild differences in certain cognitive abilities, including:

Some brain imaging studies using MRI and PET scans identified differences in brain structure and function among people with PMOS. Researchers also observed hormonal changes associated with differences in gray matter.

Some studies identified elevated biomarkers associated with Alzheimer’s disease in people with PMOS. These findings appeared to overlap with metabolic changes seen in type 2 diabetes, particularly those associated with obesity and insulin resistance.

The researchers also found that neurological differences were less pronounced when people with PMOS were compared with metabolically healthy control groups, suggesting that associated metabolic conditions may play an important role in these findings.

Overall, the review suggests that metabolic conditions associated with PMOS may contribute to changes in brain health, although more research is needed to determine whether PMOS independently affects Alzheimer’s disease or dementia risk.

“I think we need to be careful about drawing conclusions too early. The research raises some good questions, but it doesn’t prove that PCOS [PMOS] causes Alzheimer’s disease,” said Dung Trinh, MD, internist, of MemorialCare Medical Group and Chief Medical Officer of Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“What it does reinforce is how important metabolic health may be when it comes to protecting our brains as we get older,” he added.

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who also wasn’t involved in the study, agreed. She also added that more attention and research need to be given to PMOS, brain changes, and future risks of dementia.

There are various ways that PMOS and dementia may share pathways and overlap, according to the researchers. Some of these shared pathways include:

Trinh explained that insulin resistance may be one of the most important connections between PMOS and dementia.

Although insulin is commonly associated with diabetes and blood sugar regulation, it also plays an important role in brain function. When insulin signaling doesn’t work properly, it may interfere with how brain cells use energy, potentially contributing to problems with memory and thinking over time.

Chronic inflammation, which can occur in people with PMOS, may also play a role in Alzheimer’s disease, Trinh told Healthline.

“Researchers are looking at how these metabolic changes may affect amyloid and tau, two proteins associated with Alzheimer’s. Some brain imaging studies have even found differences in brain structure and function among women with PCOS [PMOS],” he said.

Ross added that elevated androgen levels, which are common in people with PMOS, may also play a role in dementia risk.

“An important distinction is that these risk factors are not present in every woman with PMOS, and having PMOS does not automatically mean someone will experience cognitive decline,” cautioned Garcia.

Garcia noted that many of the dementia risk factors associated with PMOS are modifiable.

“Regular exercise, including both cardiovascular activity and strength training, can improve insulin sensitivity, help with weight management, and support cardiovascular and brain health,” she added.

“A Mediterranean-style diet, with plenty of vegetables, fruits, whole grains, healthy fats, and lean sources of protein, is a reasonable place to start,” he said.

Managing blood sugar, cholesterol, and blood pressure is also important, Trinh and Garcia noted.

“Medication, like metformin, can also improve insulin resistance, which may further reduce the risk of dementia,” Ross added.

Getting adequate sleep, avoiding smoking, and staying mentally and socially active are additional ways to support long-term brain health, Garcia told Healthline.

“For someone with PCOS [PMOS], I would encourage regular visits with their healthcare provider to monitor metabolic health and address problems early,” said Trinh.

“While we cannot say that these interventions specifically prevent dementia in women with PMOS, we know they benefit overall metabolic and cardiovascular health, which are closely connected to brain health,” explained Garcia.

#4845. Volkswagen’s affordable ID. Polo wins German Car of the Year 2027 as EVs dominate

Volkswagen’s new ID. Polo took the overall title of “German Car of the Year 2027,” starting at just €24,995 ($28,000).

After winning in its class among entry-level models (up to €25,000), the VW ID. Polo beat out strong international competition to take the top spot as German Car of the Year for 2027 among all vehicles.

The Volkswagen Group has won two years in a row with a compact EV. Last year, it was the Skoda Elroq.

Volkswagen opened orders for the ID. Polo with the 52 kWh battery at the end of April. In July, it added the entry-level model with the 37 kWh battery, starting at just €24,995 ($28,000) with a WLTP range of up to 207 miles (334 km).

The Life and Style variants are also available with the 37 kWh battery, starting from €29,195 ($32,500).

With DC fast charging speeds of up to 90 kW, the ID. Polo with the 37 kWh battery can recharge from 10% to 80% in about 23 minutes.

The prestigious award “confirms what the ID. Polo stands for,” VW brand CEO Thomas Schäfer said. He added the entry-level EV “brings electric mobility to where it can make the greatest impact: right into people’s everyday lives.”

Since orders opened at the end of April, Schäfer said “well over 50,000 people have ordered their ID. Polo.”

As the first of what the company calls “a true Volkswagen again,” the ID. Polo is the first to bear an established name and the brand’s new Pure Positive design language.

The jury includes nearly 50 German and international auto journalists, and the award is considered one of the most prestigious in the industry.

The reason this class of vehicles is winning awards is that Europe buys a lot of B-segment vehicles (small hatchbacks) that aren't intended as city-only transport. Many families use them as their only vehicle.

And, up till now, there haven't been many BEVs available in that category.

It is compact, reasonably powerful, reasonably fast charging, acceptable charging rate, has good luggage capacity and feels nice to drive.

In other segments, electric cars swept the awards, with the Kia PV5 van winning in the compact class (€25,000 and €40,000), and Mercedes-Benz GLC Electric taking the top spot in the premium segment (€40,000 to €70,000). Hyundai’s electric sports car, the IONIQ 6 N, won German Performance Car 2027.

Martin Sander, the Group’s sales boss, said earlier this month the company is “seeing a significantly accelerated trend towards all-electric vehicles” in its home market.

VW Group’s Electric Urban Car Family has received nearly 150,000 orders after series production began in June. The lineup currently includes the VW ID. Polo and ID. Cross, plus the Cupra Raval and Skoda Epiq, all based on the MEB+ platform.

While those in the US won’t see the entry-level ID. Polo, Volkswagen is set to launch the ID. Tiguan in early 2027 as a replacement for the ID.4.

#4844. The Potential Benefits of Using an Anglo First Name

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Many Psychology Today readers are familiar with the dispiriting findings of studies conducted by social scientists: When they apply for jobs or housing, persons with ethnic minority names (like Lakeisha and Abdullah) are generally treated less well than persons with majority group names (like Daniel and Jennifer).

Will foreigners in the United States and Americans with foreign-sounding names be treated better if they adopt an Anglicized name? Would the immensely successful Nvidia executive Jensen Huang have been less successful in business if he had used his birth name, Jen-Hsun?

Two University of Kansas researchers, Xian Zhao and Monica Biernat, have conducted three studies that investigate the potential benefits that may accrue to foreigners who choose to use an Anglo name.

In the first study, 102 Americans—almost all of them White—looked at four photos of Chinese students who were attending college in the United States. Some participants saw photos that were labeled with an Anglo name like Andrew. Others saw photos that showed a Chinese name like Xiang. The participants used a 7-point scale to rate the similarity of each target “to Americans.”

On average, the pictured individuals with Anglo names were rated as more similar to Americans, and the ones with Chinese names were rated as less similar. The difference wasn’t large—about a half-point on the 7-point scale—but it was statistically significant.

In the second study, more than 400 college professors around the U.S. received an e-mail from “a student from China” who asked to meet and talk about the possibility of assisting with the professor’s research. Some professors received an e-mail in which the student introduced himself as Xian Zhao. Others received an e-mail in which the student introduced himself as “Xian Zhao, but you can call me by my English name Alex.” In every other way, the e-mail requests were identical.

The different names affected the likelihood of getting a response. The “Alex” version received responses from 62 percent of the professors, while the “Xian” version received a response from just 53 percent.

In the third study, 184 undergraduates—almost all of them White—listened to a four-minute mini-lecture about affective forecasting. The lecturer introduced himself as a student from China who was pursuing a doctorate in social psychology at the university. In one version of the recording, the lecturer said, “Hi everyone. My name is Jian.” In another version, he said, “Hi everyone. My name is John” (an Anglicized version of Jian). In every other way, the recorded lectures were identical.

The participants were told to listen carefully to the lecture and then candidly evaluate the speaker because the department was “screening and selecting Ph.D. students to be instructors.” The participants also indicated the degree to which they support components of multiculturalism (wanting to learn from other cultural groups, for example) and the degree to which they believe that people who come to the United States should change their behavior to be more like Americans.

The participants evaluated the lecturer more favorably when he used an Anglo name and less favorably when he used his original Chinese name, but only among participants who rejected multiculturalism and believed foreigners should assimilate to local norms.

The opposite pattern was observed among participants who endorsed multiculturalism and did not endorse assimilationism. Those participants actually evaluated the lecturer more favorably when he used his original Chinese name.

The differences observed in these studies were not large, but the bottom line seems clear: Chinese students in the United States—and perhaps other international students—can potentially benefit by choosing to use an Anglo name, especially in more parochial parts of the country.

This strategy, however, may also have a downside. In an earlier post, I reported the results of a 2018 study conducted by the same two Kansas researchers. In that study, Chinese students in the U.S. who adopted an Anglicized name reported slightly lower levels of self-esteem.

It seems that adopting an Anglo name can be a double-edged sword for foreigners. Some employers may evaluate Jian Li slightly lower than John Lee, but Jian may feel somewhat better about himself than John does.

Zhao, X., & Biernat, M. (2017). Welcome to the U.S. but change your name? Adopting Anglo names and discrimination. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology, 70, 59-68.
