#4841. 10 Best Sex Positions for Deep Penetration, According to Sex Therapists

Plus, what most guys get wrong about "going deep."

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

"Go deep!" may be your favorite thing to shout when playing football (or watching it from the comfort of your couch), but if you hear your partner say it during sex, do you know what to do? There's a big misconception that deep penetration means jackhammering or pounding away, says Ian Kerner, PhD, LMFT, a sex therapist and author of She Comes First. Instead, "it's more about slow thrusts that create a really nice sense of fullness," he explains.

Not only can deep, fast thrusts feel uncomfortable for your partner, but they may actually hurt. In fact, 30 percent of women reported pain during vaginal intercourse, according to a 2015 study. Here's the thing: “Deep penetration can be extremely pleasurable, but it can also be extremely painful, depending on the person and depending on the circumstances,” says Carol Queen, PhD, sex educator, staff sexologist at Good Vibes, and author of The Sex & Pleasure Book. For example, the receiving partner (a.k.a. the person being penetrated) might experience pain with penetration due to a pelvic issue, such as endometriosis, she adds.

So, if your sexual partner hasn't specifically asked you to "go deep," you need to discuss trying a deep penetration sex position with them to ensure you have clear consent, says Kerner. Try something simple like, "I think this would be so hot" or "This looks like it would feel amazing." It won't be weird, I swear. At Men’s Health, we aim to describe sexual experiences as inclusively as possible. While many of the positions on this list have traditionally been associated with penis-in-vagina sex, they can be adapted by couples of all genders and sexual orientations and identities. We encourage readers to modify any position to suit their bodies, preferences, and needs. To avoid making assumptions about gender or anatomy, we use non-gendered language throughout this article. The “giving partner” or “giver” refers to the partner doing the penetrating, whether with a penis, a strap-on, or even fingers. The “receiving partner” or “receiver” refers to the partner being penetrated vaginally or anally. That said, we also have an article dedicated to the best anal sex positions, including deep penetration options.

Because the receiving partner is lying flat on their stomach in this position, he recommends they stimulate their clit with their hands or a small bullet vibrator.

#4839. RJ Mitte's Death Highlights Mental Health Challenges for People With Cerebral Palsy

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

“Breaking Bad” actor and disability advocate RJ Mitte has died at age 34.

Mitte reportedly died by suicide, his representative told People on October 12. However, local authorities have said the official cause and manner of his death remain under investigation.

Mitte, who was best-known for playing Walter White Jr. on the Emmy Award-winning television series “Breaking Bad,” lived with cerebral palsy and advocated for disability inclusion.

There is currently no evidence to suggest that cerebral palsy caused the actor’s death. Still, Mitte’s untimely passing highlights the mental health challenges that people with cerebral palsy may face.

Research has demonstrated elevated risks of anxiety and depression among those with the condition, alongside barriers to diagnosis, social participation, and employment.

“Living with cerebral palsy can affect mental health through several interacting pathways,” said Shannel Elhelou, neuropsychologist and founder of Brain Centric Neuropsychology in Westlake Village, CA.

“Chronic pain, fatigue, disrupted sleep, and the effort required to navigate daily activities can take an emotional toll. Experiences of bullying, discrimination, social exclusion, and difficulty accessing care can add to that burden. Also, the early brain injury underlying cerebral palsy may contribute to difficulties with emotional regulation, although each person’s experience is different,” Elhelou told Healthline.

If you or someone you know is in crisis and considering suicide or self-harm, please seek support:

If you’re calling on behalf of someone else, stay with them until help arrives. You may remove weapons or substances that can cause harm if you can do so safely.

If you’re not in the same household, stay on the phone with them until help arrives.

Mitte played a key role in challenging stereotypes by portraying a teenager with cerebral palsy on “Breaking Bad.”

Outside of acting, Mitte advocated for people with disabilities through organizations including United Cerebral Palsy (UCP) and Shriners Children’s.

He also led the Mitte Foundation, a non-profit corporation founded in 1994 by his grandparents, Roy F. and Joann Cole Mitte, that improves the quality of life for people in Central Texas.

Mitte’s advocacy emphasized representation, inclusion, and opportunities for people with disabilities.

The legacy he leaves behind underscores the need to recognize mental health as an essential part of care for individuals with cerebral palsy and other disabilities.

Cerebral palsy (CP) is a group of conditions affecting movement, posture, and coordination.

Most people with cerebral palsy have typical intelligence, although some also have intellectual disabilities or other comorbid physical and mental health conditions.

A 2018 study published in JAMA Neurology found that adults with cerebral palsy had a 28% higher risk of developing depression and a 40% higher risk of anxiety than adults without the condition.

A 2024 study in JAMA Network Open found higher recorded rates of anxiety and attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) among children and young adults with cerebral palsy.

The researchers also identified comparatively low rates of depression and suicidal ideation, highlighting concerns that these conditions may be underdiagnosed.

“Research shows that adults with cerebral palsy have an increased risk of depression and anxiety,” Elhelou said.

“However, these conditions should never be viewed as an inevitable part of having a disability. One concern is ‘diagnostic overshadowing,’ when symptoms of depression, anxiety, or other mental health conditions are attributed to cerebral palsy itself, leading those conditions to go unrecognized and untreated. Someone’s fatigue, withdrawal, or changes in behavior deserve careful assessment rather than the assumption that ‘this is just their cerebral palsy,'” she continued.

“While these findings help us understand mental health risks among people with cerebral palsy, they cannot tell us what any one person was experiencing. Many interacting factors may contribute to someone dying by suicide, and it would be inappropriate to attribute an individual’s death to their disability.”

A 2019 study published in Developmental Medicine & Child Neurology found that children with cerebral palsy had higher odds of anxiety disorders and behavioral challenges than children without the condition.

Difficulties with friendships and bullying were associated with some of these differences.

Factors that may affect well-being include:

The researchers also found that supportive friendships may help reduce some of these disparities, reinforcing the importance of inclusive schools and communities.

A 2021 study published in the American Journal of Preventive Medicine found that U.S. adults with disabilities faced increased risks of suicidal thoughts, planning, and attempts compared with adults without disabilities.

The 988 Lifeline received more than 8 million contacts through calls, texts, chats, and American Sign Language videophone in 2025, according to the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration.

Federal funding for the Lifeline’s dedicated LGBTQ+ youth service ended in July 2025, removing the option to press 3 for specialized support.

After pressure from Congress, the service was recently restored at the end of September, according to the Kaiser Family Foundation (KFF).

Employment and financial independence can influence well-being for anyone, particularly those with cerebral palsy.

However, a 2024 study found that just 30.4% of young adults with cerebral palsy who participated in vocational rehabilitation programs were employed when they exited those programs.

Workplace accommodations, accessible hiring, vocational rehabilitation, and inclusive social opportunities can help address these barriers.

“Depression and anxiety are treatable, and they should never be dismissed as an inevitable part of living with a disability,” Elhelou said.

“If you notice persistent sadness, anxiety, hopelessness, or withdrawal, reach out to someone you trust and a mental health professional. You deserve a clinician who takes your concerns seriously and adapts treatment to your communication, mobility, or other access needs. Care should also address pain, fatigue, and sleep difficulties, which can affect emotional well-being.”

Organizations such as United Cerebral Palsy offer information and support for people with cerebral palsy and their families.

Healthcare professionals can also improve outcomes by screening for mental health concerns, using appropriate assessment tools, and connecting patients with accessible treatment.

Improved access to care, inclusive communities, and meaningful opportunities can help people with cerebral palsy and other disabilities thrive.

“Asking for help does not diminish your resilience or your contributions. If you are experiencing thoughts of suicide or feel overwhelmed, you do not have to wait until things feel unbearable to reach out,” Elhelou said.

“Supportive relationships, peer groups, and disability communities can offer connection and understanding. Staying engaged in activities that bring enjoyment and meaning can also help.”

#4838. Hyundai’s electric SUV finds some footing, with IONIQ 5 sales topping 33,000 in 2026

The Hyundai IONIQ 5 remains one of America’s most popular EVs outside Tesla, with October sales up 21%.

While the Tesla Model Y and Model 3 still dominate the US electric vehicle market, accounting for about 50% of all sales through the third quarter, it was the Hyundai IONIQ 5 that ranked third.

The market has slowed since the $7,500 federal tax credit was removed last September, with several automakers reporting fewer EV sales than in 2025 when buyers rushed to claim the credit.

Hyundai responded by cutting prices on the 2026 IONIQ 5 by up to nearly $10,000 on some trims, with a new starting MSRP of around $35,000.

As the market shows signs of recovery, IONIQ 5 sales bounced back in October, rising 21% to 1,981 units.

Through the first 10 months of 2026, Hyundai sold 33,093 IONIQ 5s in the US, still down 23% from the more than 42,700 it sold during the same period last year, when credit was still available.

Hyundai said the IONIQ 5 helped drive growth as the company posted its best October in the US ever with 73,452 vehicles sold, a 5% increase YOY.

While Hyundai sold only 169 IONIQ 9s last month (-47%), sales are still up 49% through October, with nearly 6,700 units sold.

Hyundai’s hybrids set a new October sales record, rising 20% from last year and accounting for 29% of total sales in October. Through October, Hyundai’s total US sales are up 3% YOY at 770,916 units.

Since not all automakers report monthly sales figures, it’s hard to determine where the IONIQ 5 stands through October.

Earlier today, Hyundai’s sister brand Kia reported that sales of its three-row electric SUV, the EV9, were up 11% year over year in October. EV9 sales are now up 5% year-to-date, with 13,835 units sold through October.

The cheapest 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE Standard Range starts at $35,250 and offers up to 245 miles of range.

If you want more range, upgrading to the extended-range SE RWD model costs an extra $2,500, but you get up to 318 miles of range on a single charge.

#4837. How to Increase Psychological Safety Amid Chronic Illness

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

How safe do you feel in this moment? Pause what you are doing, close your eyes, take a couple of deep breaths, and tune in to your body. Start at your feet and work up, scanning your body with your mind’s eye. Are there places where your muscles feel tense? Is your breathing shallow? Is it hard to tune in to your body because your mind will not settle? Don’t try to change anything right now. Just notice the sensations you are having in this moment.

Psychological safety is defined as “a person’s capacity to experience the self, the body, and the interpersonal environment as sufficiently safe to sustain openness, affect regulation, and adaptive engagement" (Poli & Miccoli, 2026). The body “is one of the main channels through which safety, threat, and dysregulation are experienced" (Poli & Miccoli, 2026).

Safety is a continuum. We feel safe (and unsafe) by degrees. Typically, we have conscious awareness of when we are at either end of this continuum. We know when we feel safe, for example, when our muscles are relaxed, and our thoughts are pleasant. At those moments, we enjoy the positive sensations in our body that let us know, “I am happy. I am safe.” Conversely, we know when we feel very unsafe. Our heart rate goes up; we may feel nauseated or light-headed; breathing may feel labored. We interpret these sensations as a lack of safety.

The middle of the safety continuum is where we spend the vast majority of our time. Without the extremes of body sensations that occur at either end of the continuum, we often tune out the body, putting it outside of our consciousness. Paying attention to body sensations in the middle of the safety continuum can lead to a deeper relationship with the body and greater psychological safety.

Many people living with illness describe their relationship with their body as challenging. Lack of trust in the body, anger at the body, and alienation from the body are all sequelae of chronic illness (Taylor, Morton, & Cogan, 2026). Questions like “How safe do you feel in this moment?” are complicated by the bodily mistrust that chronic illness perpetuates. Illness “disrupts...the felt sense of psychological safety" (Taylor, Morton, & Cogan, 2026).

In order to grow psychological safety, we must have an intentional relationship with our body. Take a few moments and ascertain what that relationship currently looks like. Write a few sentences responding to each of the following statements. How true or false does each statement feel to you?

Now that you have a sense of your current relationship with your body, write a few sentences about how you would like this relationship to be different. Some ideas might be: “I’d like to feel less hostile toward my body” or “I’d like to appreciate my body for the things it can do, rather than be solely focused on the ways it disappoints me.”

If you were seeking to improve a relationship with another person, you’d likely (a) spend intentional time with them, and (b) have open communication with them. Similarly, in improving your relationship with your body, you will want to carve out intentional time together and increase honest communication (both listening and feeling heard).

In order to relate harmoniously with our body, we have to get to know it. In order to know it, we have to spend intentional time with it. What you do during this time is less important than your non-distracted presence. You could take a shower or bath, go for a walk, do a yoga session, or simply sit quietly and follow your breath. You want to be present during your time with your body. Notice without trying to change the sensations that come up.

You’re also going to want to communicate with your body during these “being with” sessions. You can speak aloud or in your mind, or journal. Just put words to the sensations you have, being as honest as possible. You might say, “This is really stupid. I feel fidgety and anxious. It’s frustrating to be with you, body.” Perhaps you have a different experience and say, “I feel vulnerable and shy. I’m not used to showing up in my body like this.” It may be that you feel a lessening of tension, such that you say, “Wow, this feels nice to be with my body, undistracted by other concerns.”

Open your mind, too, to the ways your body is communicating with you during these sessions. Be curious. Maybe your body is whispering, “I’m tired. I do the best I can, and I don’t feel very appreciated.” Perhaps your body is screaming, “I hurt! I’m angry and agitated, and I need some relief!” It’s possible that your body is saying, “I feel you tuning in to me, and I have been waiting a long time for your presence.”

Make it a habit to communicate with your body in the ways described above. Your body is constantly changing, and your experiences of tuning in to it will be variable. Stay open and curious; let yourself be surprised.

Psychological safety depends upon a connection between body and psyche. Working to deepen that connection is an important tool in managing chronic illness.

Poli, A., & Miccoli, M. (2026). The Role of Self-Compassion and Body Perception in Predicting Psychological Safety. Journal of Clinical Medicine, 15(13), 5177.

Taylor, P., Morton, L., & Cogan, N. (2026). Feeling unsafe in one’s own body: The impact of illness on psychological safety and social engagement. International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health, 23(2), 148.

#4834. Pennsylvania is way ahead on NEVI charging – now comes another $37M

Pennsylvania is putting $37 million in federal NEVI funding toward what PennDOT says will be 515 new public EV charging ports across the Philadelphia region, including curbside chargers.

The awards cover Bucks, Chester, Delaware, Montgomery, and Philadelphia counties. PennDOT says the projects will expand public charging availability by 27% across the region and 53% in Philadelphia.

For drivers without a driveway, curbside charging gives them somewhere to plug in while parked near home.

It’s Electric PA received $2.93 million for 157 on-street Level 2 ports across 13 Philadelphia neighborhoods. Voltpost received funding for another 62 curbside ports across the four suburban counties.

Fast charging is getting funding, too. PositivEnergy received $7.2 million for 54 DC fast-charging ports at 11 Philadelphia Parking Authority lots. Tesla received two $392,000 awards for eight ports each at Royal Farms in Broomall and Wawa in Plymouth Meeting.

The money comes from the federal National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure (NEVI) program, created by the Biden administration’s Bipartisan Infrastructure Law. Pennsylvania’s five-year allocation is $171.5 million.

NEVI initially focused on fast charging along designated highway corridors. States can spend the money more broadly after the Federal Highway Administration certifies that their corridor charging requirements have been met. Pennsylvania received that certification on September 22, 2025.

In fact, Pennsylvania has built 54 EV charging stations using NEVI funds so far – more than double the number of any other state.

PennDOT’s community charging program supports both Level 2 and DC fast charging. Its published program minimum requirements include at least four ports per proposed station, at least 6 kW per port, two ADA-compliant parking spaces, and J1772 or CCS connectors on every port. Equipment must also meet Buy America requirements.

PennDOT lists 71 awards in this southeastern round as part of its roughly $100 million statewide community charging effort.

Applications for the western region close November 20. Central and eastern Pennsylvania applications close March 12, 2027.

thanks for posting. PA continues to lead the country in turning this program into actual chargers. The statewide coverage is night and day from just 2-3 years ago.

For the southeastern projects, PennDOT’s estimated schedule puts environmental reviews in November 2026 through January 2027, followed by agreement execution in January and February. Drivers will still have to wait for construction and commissioning before these awards become places to plug in.

#4833. The 8 Best Nose Hair Trimmers for Men in 2026, Tested by Grooming Editors

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

This process doesn't need to be painful with the right tool.

IT STARTS WITH a quick glance—a double-take in the mirror under harsh bathroom lighting. Then there it is: a single, wiry hair brazenly peeking out of your nostril like it’s trying to escape. Soon, you’re spotting strands in places they never used to grow, like your ears, and this only multiplies with time. While a beard trimmer, electric razor, or pair of tweezers can help, the best way to keep them in check is to arm yourself with a nose hair trimmer—preferably one that won’t feel like a medieval torture device.

Nose hair trimmers are specifically built to tackle those tricky, hard-to-reach spots. Many models come loaded with extras—think built-in lights, interchangeable heads, even mini vacuums to keep your sink clean. Technically, you could use scissors, but it’s a risk. Snipping near sensitive nasal tissue can lead to nicks and cuts, and as for waxing—well, that’s best left to professionals, not your bathroom mirror. Unless you enjoy excruciating pain and opening your nostrils to infection and disease.

It might be unpleasant to look at when overgrown, but nose hair exists for a reason. It acts as a natural filter, trapping dust, debris, and other particles before they make their way into your body and potentially cause infection or illness. That’s why fully removing it, like with waxing, isn’t a great idea. But those long, visible strands sticking out? They’re not doing the job any better than the hidden ones. The key is balance: trim regularly, but don’t overdo it. Just tidy up when the hairs start to show.

Most nose hair trimmers are reasonably affordable, and shelling out a ton of money isn’t necessarily going to get you the best one. Think about how often you’ll use it, and if you’re mostly concerned with nose hairs or want to use it for other things, too. Many come with multiple attachments to help you tackle errant eyebrow hairs and sometimes even detail your beard. Plus, there are quality nose hair trimmers available at every budget point, so get ready to say goodbye to those long hairs for good.

This is the Rolls-Royce of nose hair trimmers—pricey, yes, but absolutely premium in performance. Its comfortable, well-balanced design lets me use it not just on my nose, but also for my ears and eyebrows. Still, where it really stands out is clearing nasal hair quickly and comfortably. The built-in vacuum keeps things sanitary by reducing leftover clippings and minimizing bacterial buildup.

I also appreciate the wet/dry functionality—it’s easy to clean whether I’m using it in the shower or at the sink. During testing, I found the dual-edge blade trimmed hair much faster than cheaper options, like the Zorami trimmer, which often needed extra time to do the same job. And while the vacuum system is a major plus for cleanup, I did have to empty it about every four months or so with regular use.

Testing Notes: This nose hair trimmer is bulky, per se, because nose hair trimmers are, by default, small appliances, but it is hefty. It sits well in your hand and is easy to hold and grip, which is a surprisingly needed and under-represented feature in the nose hair trimmer world. It’s truly the Rolls-Royce of trimmers, powering through thick and long nose hairs with ease, and leaving your nostrils feeling shorn and clean.

The vacuum is also a very welcome feature that I didn’t know I needed until I tried it. After all, who really wants to clean up nose hair clippings from their sink? The one thing I will say is that while you can technically use this in the shower thanks to the wet/dry feature, cutting hair of all kinds is easier when it’s dry, even nose hairs. I’d recommend sticking to using it outside of the shower.

Long-Term Results: After six months of using Panasonic's Ear and Nose Hair Trimmer, I found that it trims my errant nostril hairs as well as it did right out of the box. The battery life did decrease slightly after six months of using it, but not enough of a decline to make a huge difference.

Battery-powered nose trimmers work fine if you’re only trimming once in a while. But if you stay on top of your nostril grooming (and you should—nose hair grows faster than most people think), a rechargeable model makes way more sense. That’s why I went with this one from Manscaped.

It's not surprising that the brand that reinvented the body groomer delivered the same technology above your neck with the first iteration of The Weed Whacker, but the 2.0 takes it to a whole other level.

It packs a 7,000 rpm motor—the only more powerful option is the Panasonic trimmer, our best overall—into a compact design, and the hypoallergenic stainless steel cone protects sensitive skin while trimming. That means you get a smooth cut without the tugging or irritation.

Testing Notes: During my first time using it, I noticed how seamlessly it cut nasal fur. Even more impressive: it was the quietest trimmer I’ve tried, which made for an even more pleasant shaving experience. Plus, the tip is angled to allow you to reach even the hardest-to-reach crevices in your schnoz.

Unlike most of the other nose hair trimmers on this list, it’s rechargeable via USB, so no need to switch out batteries. Each charge also gets you 45 minutes of trim time, which is the longest on this list. For comparison, Panasonic’s best in-case option gets 40 minutes per charge.

Long-Term Results: After using it for two months, Manscape's marquee nose trimmer cut just as close as it did when I first tested it. Not to mention, its 45-minute battery life still held up after multiple charges.

Read More: Should You Trim Your Nose Hair?

I get it, building a solid grooming arsenal can get expensive quickly. So if you're looking to trim costs (pun intended) on your grooming gadgets, but still want something that gets the job done, then look to Zorami's trimmer that's less than $14. Given that it's a top-rated rig on Amazon, I decided to try the Zorami ourselves.

At this price point, I was impressed by its waterproof capabilities and a 6,500 RPM motor that slices hair pretty cleanly. While it didn't cut as closely as the Manscaped or Philips Norelco trimmers, it still did a commendable job cleaning our nostrils, only adding a few extra minutes to our shaving time compared to our top-rated trimmers.

You can easily pop off the top and rinse under water to clean. Then, after every use, you can snap on the protective top case, a useful feature for keeping your gadget clean while traveling. The only qualms about this trimmer might be overall durability and lifespan, but for those who don't have a lot of nose hair to battle, this is an ideal starter trimmer to go with.

Testing Notes: When you’re using this trimmer, compared to the others on this list, you can definitely tell it’s an inexpensive version. That’s not necessarily a bad thing; it just means that it doesn’t have a lot of the bells and whistles that some of the more expensive options have. That said, it delivers where it matters—namely, cutting ability.

It’s not as fast as some of the others on this list and, in some cases, doesn’t cut quite as close (those with thicker nose hairs will need to use it a bit more frequently), but for the average guy with average nose foliage, it does the job and does it well. The pop-off cap cleaning option is also a nice touch, which makes cleaning it easy to remember.

Long-Term Results: After testing Zorami's trimmer for a month, I noticed a slight decline in cutting ability, and some minor pulling. But the difference wasn't too drastic, and I’d imagine most guys wouldn’t notice.

If you’re looking for an all-in-one trimming option, this Philips Norelco model is pretty hard to beat. Yeah, it doesn't have Panasonic's vacuum feature, but it can still cut nose hair just as effectively, thanks to a dual-sided steel blade that chops down strands without any tugging.

The blades have protective tube technology designed to protect the nostrils from cuts and irritation, making it great for sensitive skin, especially considering the nostrils are one of the most sensitive areas on the face. The nose, ear, and eyebrow attachments (with multiple-length combs) all work well, and the trimmer even comes with a nifty attachment for your sideburns.

Testing Notes: As a nose hair trimmer, this multifunctional tool delivers on everything you want a nose hair trimmer to be. It cuts with ease; it doesn’t pull or snag your hair, and it doesn’t irritate or nick the inside of your nose. The blades are sharp and fast, and the tube housing makes it easy to use.

If you’re into things like eyebrow detailing and other small tweaks to your facial hair, the additional attachments may come in handy, but I found that they are a little trickier to use and not as efficient as the nose hair attachment.

Good thing there is a pouch included, as well, because these attachments are tiny and definitely easy to lose at the bottom of your Dopp kit or bathroom drawer. I usually prefer a rechargeable tool, but the replaceable lithium battery is fine and convenient for travel.

Long-Term Results: After using it for four months, I can safely say that Philips' Norelco trimmer still trimmed just as efficiently as it did when I first received it. While it comes with several attachments for shaving the rest of your body, I found that they didn't cut as closely as the original blade, so I’d recommend buying a separate body groomer.

Most nose hair trimmers use either a rotary blade or a dual-blade setup. What really sets the Conair model apart, though, is its distinctive mushroom-shaped cutting head. The lip at the tip lets me guide it precisely along the inner edge of my nostrils, reaching spots that other trimmers couldn't quite get to. Of all the options I’ve tried, this one gave me the closest trim.

While a lot of people—including many of my fellow editors—really appreciate this unique design, some are perfectly content with a standard rotary trimmer. Honestly, it comes down to personal preference, the way your nose hair grows, and how often you find yourself trimming.

Beyond the cutting performance, I also appreciated the Conair’s ergonomic build. Its textured grip gave me better control, and the slim body fit easily into my travel kit—ideal for keeping things tidy on the go. It also includes three attachments for beard and body grooming, but I found those didn’t shave nearly as close as the nose hair trimmer itself.

Testing Notes: Let’s be honest—the extra attachments are fine if you need them, but they don’t compare to the main attraction: the nose hair trimmer. What really impressed me was its mushroom-shaped head. It’s a smart design tweak that lets me reach spots inside my nostrils that traditional trimmers usually miss.

Long-Term Results: After using it for over five months, I still dig this trimmer’s super close cut. However, Conair's motor has gotten slightly louder over time.

If you’re primarily interested in portability, Wahl’s Micro Groomsman is your best bet. This little gadget packs serious power and efficiency while barely being larger than a Sharpie. Perfect to fit in any travel bag or Dopp kit, the Micro Groomsman will ensure there are no lingering nose hairs poking out, whether you’re departing for the beach or the boardroom.

Its sleek rotary head can smoothly manage nose hairs (not to mention ear whiskers), while its detailer blade adjustment can help shape and neaten facial hair. This add-on also comes in handy for cleaning up necklines and other last-minute touchups when traveling.

Thanks to its streamlined design, operating the device was quick and simple—another boon for busy travelers. While the Micro Groomsman is not waterproof, cleaning it only requires removing the stainless steel rotary head to run it under water.

Testing Notes: I primarily use the Micro Groomsman when traveling or for last-minute touchups in a jam, but it certainly has the power to be an everyday nose hair trimmer, especially for those with less thick hair.

Long-Term Results: The integrity and effectiveness of the Micro Groomsman’s design hold up over time; however, the battery life may leave some wanting more. The batteries don’t last as long as other trimmers on this list, which could prove taxing to last-minute travelers. I initially got about two months of regular use with one fresh battery, but it’s closer to six weeks now, so I’d recommend bringing along a backup battery for any traveling.

Sure, electric nose hair trimmers are probably best for daily grooming, but let’s be real—sometimes these trusty devices just can't get rid of that one stubborn hair. That's when a good pair of nose hair scissors comes in handy to take care of those last few strays.

Of all the scissors I've tried and snipped with, the Shears 3.0 were by far the most effective—and durable. Doubling as nail clippers (though you probably shouldn't use the same pair for both), these shears are sharp enough to eliminate any lingering hairs and tiny enough to fit into the smallest of nostrils. Plus, it comes in a nifty travel kit that includes a fingernail clipper, toenail clipper, nail file, and slant-tip tweezers—all of which are stainless steel and waterproof. It’s a mainstay in my Dopp kit.

Testing Notes: In theory, you could use these scissors to trim all your nose hairs, especially if your nose isn’t too fuzzy. But, honestly, they're best suited for those precise touch-ups—like snipping away a few stray hairs when needed. But either way, take it slow. As these were designed to also trim fingernails, they’re quite sharp and can certainly cause a cut if you're not careful. To limit risk, I recommend you get up close to the mirror and be deliberate, so you're only trimming the hairs you want to get rid of.

Long-Term Results: I don’t use these shears nearly as often as my electric trimmer, since they're best for touch-ups or travel, but they still cut as cleanly as when I first tested them. Thanks to their stainless steel design, there’s been minimal wear and tear over the six months I've been testing them, not to mention zero rusting.

Of course, one of the few complaints about electric nose hair trimmers—battery-powered or otherwise—is often their need to be recharged or refueled with new batteries. This Royal nose hair trimmer combats that common gripe by eschewing electricity entirely.

It works by simply squeezing the handles together, which manually rotates the blades to trim hair. The indented handles also help to ensure you never lose your grip and the tool stays properly placed in your nostril.

The Royal Nose Hair Trimmer is specifically designed to avoid irritation by enclosing blades so that they won’t come in contact with skin, limiting the risk of cuts and nicks.

Sure, the process is slightly more laborious, and the cuts aren’t as precise as other nose hair trimmers on this list, but it’s barely enough to notice. That tradeoff may be well worth it for those who are sensitive to the noisier nose hair trimmer.

Testing Notes: Given its manual design, there were times when this trimmer pulled hair rather than cut it. This can occasionally feel like plucking your nose hairs instead of cutting them, but when operated properly with some patience, it usually holds up without much pulling.

That said, cleaning was a bit more involved than the above options, as you have to pop the top off to do so. The device is water-resistant, however, so you can simply run it under the faucet in a jam.

Long-Term Results: As an everyday nose hair trimmer, this one might not be ideal, but it’s a great option when traveling. I found it can also come in handy during those ever-stressful times when batteries or a charge abruptly die.

When it comes to nose hair trimming, or any manscaping, really, the means by which blades will be brushed against your skin is crucial. For nose trimmers, this typically comes down to one of two options: either a rotary or slide cutter. Slide cutters resemble more traditional clippers, while rotary heads are the ones that look closer to a pen cap. While rotary options tend to give a closer shave, slide cutters offer more control and precision since they only cut in a straight, back-and-forth motion.

Given that these devices are made to be inserted into the nostril, most are primarily equipped with a rotary head, but some offer the option of a slide for folks who prefer the old-school feel. Of course, there are also a few with adjustments for both to accommodate the more indecisive among us.

With most products, you might look solely for a long battery life to ensure you can spend less time charging or replacing batteries, and more time using the device. However, with nose hair trimmers, there are a few other caveats to consider, which can impact the overall grooming experience—namely, the source of its power.

Battery-operated nose hair trimmers take up less storage space, as they don’t need a charging mount or cable, but will likely cost more long-term due to battery replacements. On the other hand, nose hair trimmers with wall chargers can be refueled at any time as long as there’s an outlet nearby. Plus, there are few things more annoying than a trimmer sputtering out with dying batteries, which chargers make easier to avoid. While nose hair trimming takes substantially less time than beard or body hair, you’ll still want one that has at least 30 minutes of runtime to avoid frequently recharging.

At Men’s Health, we’ve been testing nose hair trimmers for years now, focusing on key performance factors like trimming precision, comfort during use, ease of handling, value for the price, and long-term durability. There isn’t a nose trimmer out there that we haven’t gotten our hands on at some point, while also paying close attention to common pain points, like pulling, irritation, and inconsistent results.

While updating this roundup with the latest and greatest nose trimmers, I considered past favorites and emerging tools, ultimately landing on over 20 to try or give another look. After testing each for at least three weeks, I refined our findings to a final shortlist: the eight best nose hair trimmers that truly deliver on performance and reliability.

I’ll admit, the idea of sticking a trimmer up your nose can feel a little intimidating at first, but trust me, the best nose hair trimmers are built to be safe and painless. Most use rotary blades, which are tucked inside a cylindrical guard to protect your skin. That design helps prevent snags and, thankfully, those awful little nicks that hurt way more than they should.

When I trim, I gently place the device just inside one nostril, switch it on, and slowly rotate it to catch as many hairs as possible. There’s no need to push it too far—keeping it shallow is safer and more effective. I usually listen for the sound of hair being cut, and once it goes quiet, I move on to the other nostril.

Some trimmers also come with a slide cutter, which I’ve found more useful for shaping sideburns or cleaning up my neckline. That said, if you’ve got extra-long hairs in your nose or ears, you can carefully trim them down with the slide cutter before switching back to the rotary head for a closer finish.

Also, rest assured that this process isn’t painful with the right tool. Even if you have really long or thick nostril hairs, using a nose hair trimmer won’t hurt. The blades aren’t shaving the hairs down to the skin—they’re just cutting them to a length so they won’t stick out of your nose. Think of it the same as getting a haircut.

Despite their relatively low price, it can still be hard to justify a tool for such a specific issue. Luckily, you can use a nose hair trimmer in some other places. Even the simplest ones are good for ear and eyebrow hairs.

Some other trimmers also come with interchangeable attachments to help shape facial hair and trim mustaches, beards, and sometimes body hair. But keep in mind that if you use an attachment for your nose, you shouldn’t use it anywhere else to avoid cross-contamination. Even if you wash it off thoroughly, it’s a risk.

Still, others are full-on clippers with a nose hair trimmer attachment. Choose one depending on where you want to use it, but don’t try to go rogue. Stick to what the trimmer and attachments are designed for.

#4832. Pope Leo XIV Diagnosed With Lung Cancer After Surgery to Remove Part of Lung

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Pope Leo XIV has been diagnosed with lung cancer following a successful surgery, the Vatican said.

The Pope, 71, underwent surgery at Agostino Gemelli University Policlinic in Rome on October 13 to remove a nodule from his right lung. This was determined to be pulmonary adenocarcinoma, the most common type of lung cancer.

The surgeons removed the lower lobe of the Pope’s lung to “ensure a radical procedure; the lower lobe of the right lung was removed using a video-assisted technique,” said Dr. Luigi Carbone, the Director of Vatican Healthcare Services, and Professor Stephano Margaritora, the lead surgeon, in a press release.

“An intraoperative histological examination confirmed the diagnosis of lung adenocarcinoma, as suspected from the preoperative examinations,” they said.

Pope Leo was in the operating room for 3 hours, while the operation itself lasted around 90 minutes.

The doctors noted that it was a successful operation with no complications.

According to Matteo Bruni, the Director of the Holy See Press Office, the Pope asked for dinner after the surgery and spent the evening with his secretaries and other Vatican staff.

In a press release on Wednesday, the Vatican said that the Pope’s postoperative recovery is progressing well.

According to Vatican News, Pope Leo had a calm first night following his surgery.

In the coming days, exams will be conducted on the removed tissue. There has currently been no change in the initially announced week of hospitalization.

On October 14, the Pope underwent blood tests and chest X-rays that were scheduled following his surgery. The results of these tests confirm that his postoperative recovery is progressing as expected, and the values are within a normal range for the type of operation performed.

Pope Leo has also undergone some respiratory physiotherapy and has begun walking around his room, the Vatican said.

It has not yet been announced whether he will require any further treatment.

Adenocarcinoma is the most common subtype of lung cancer globally. It accounts for around 40% of all lung cancer diagnoses.

Adenocarcinoma is a type of non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), which accounts for about 80–85% of all lung cancers.

Lung adenocarcinoma begins in the epithelial cells that produce mucus. It is the most common type of NSCLC.

Adenocarcinoma can be classified further by the genetic changes driving the tumor. For example, EGFR-positive lung cancer is found more often in people who have never smoked and can often be treated with targeted therapies, which is why biomarker testing of tumor tissue helps guide treatment decisions.

Treatment for NSCLC is generally determined by the stage. However, it typically involves one or a combination of:

Surgery is generally the first step in treatment for this type of lung cancer. The right lung has three lobes, or sections, and the left has two.

A doctor will often remove the entire lobe that has the tumor in order to remove the tumor and improve the outlook.

The body eventually adapts in order to compensate for the loss of function.

According to the American Cancer Society (ACS), for NSCLC that hasn’t spread outside of the lung, the 5-year relative survival rate is 67%.

#4830. BYD’s flagship luxury EV with 1,000+ km of range hits 4,000 sales in just 19 days

BYD wasted no time, with sales of its new flagship electric sedan reaching 4,000 in just 19 days since its launch.

With a record of more than 300,000 electric vehicles sold last month, BYD sold more EVs than the US did in the entire third quarter, combined.

A big part of the higher demand is thanks to BYD’s latest breakthrough technology, including its second-generation Blade Batteries and Flash Charging technology.

BYD began equipping vehicles with the new battery and charging tech in March, and over the past six months it has posted higher year-over-year sales growth. Through October, BYD sold 1,953,700 vehicles, up 6% compared to the same period in 2025.

On October 13, BYD launched the Great Han, or “Da Han” in China, the first fully electric D-segment flagship sedan in its Dynasty lineup.

Just 19 days after its launch, BYD said Great Han EV sales reached 4,000 units. At that rate, sales could reach 10,000 or more in its first full month.

With “class-beating high value,” BYD said, “it has firmly established itself in the mid-to-high-end sedan segment above 250,000 yuan,” which is about $37,500.

The Great Han is available in three variants, priced from 235,900 yuan, or about $35,000. Buyers can choose from single- or dual-motor powertrains. The standard rear-wheel-drive (RWD) Premium trim offers up to 1,008 km (626 miles) of CLTC range, while the all-wheel-drive (AWD) Flagship variant offers up to 880 km (546 miles).

All models use a 102.326 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery pack. The RWD models have a single 370 kW (496 hp) motor, while the AWD variant adds a 200 kW (268 hp) front motor for a combined 570 kW (764 hp).

Like most of its new vehicles, the Great Han has a roof-mounted LiDAR and BYD’s God’s Eye B, a more advanced version of its intelligent driving system.

The ADAS system offers safety and convenience features, including Navigate on Autopilot on highways and in cities, plus remote autonomous parking.

All variants also include premium features, such as BYD’s DiSus-A dual-chamber air suspension system, rear-wheel steering, and zero-gravity front seats.

Measuring 5,256 mm in length, 1,999 mm in width, and 1,510 mm in height, with a 3,130 mm wheelbase, the Great Han is slightly bigger than the Tesla Model S and Lucid Air. It’s closer in size to the long-wheelbase Mercedes-Benz S-Class.

Thanks to BYD’s second-gen Blade Batteries and Flash Charging technology, the vehicle’s battery can charge from 10% to 70% in just 5 minutes. A full recharge (10% to 97%) takes just 9 minutes.

The Great Han joins the Great Tang SUV as the flagship sedan under BYD’s Dynasty lineup. BYD announced that Great Tang sales reached 14,000 units in October, marking four months of sales above 10,000.

#4829. How Does AI Make Us Stupid?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Current versions of artificial intelligence have created so much well-deserved excitement. However, along with all that progress has come a wave of apprehension about the destructive potential of using AI. Fears that AI could be harming us. Fears that it might be making us stupid.

I wrote about this possibility in 2019, in a post I titled "The Second Singularity," and I am not happy to see my predictions coming true.

However, this post asks a different question. Now I am looking back to 2003 and the original publication of my book The Power of Intuition. This was before large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Claude and the generative AI technologies. My book examined different facets of intuition/expertise. Chapter 16 was titled “Smart Technology Can Make Us Stupid.” The smart technology I was writing about was simply the 2003-style information technology, not current LLMs, but the mechanisms I identified seem quite relevant today.

In that chapter, I wrote, “Information technologies are intended to make us more productive, but their use carries a penalty. The computer programs, the decision support systems, the search engines, databases, and shareware we use can diminish our intuition and expertise. They can make us stupid.” I worried that the 2003-era technologies could disable our expertise, or at least slow down our acquisition of expertise, or, more ominously, teach dysfunctional skills that interfered with expertise.

But how would that work? In 2003, I identified several possibilities.

Recognizing patterns. That’s something we are good at—spotting patterns in data, seeing trends, seeing anomalies, spotting nonevents. But information technology “helps” us by doing the analyses for us, since the high data flows make it impossible for us to examine all or even most of the data. So we are removed from direct access to the data and removed from our ability to spot patterns.

For example, in one project we conducted on weather forecasters, a technologist at a weather forecasting conference explained how his system helpfully smoothed the data in order to highlight trends. Most of the audience appreciated this assistance, but the experienced forecasters in the room winced—they wanted to see the noise because that was where turbulence was going to affect the weather picture. The smoothing was removing a critical cue.

Searching for data. That’s another important human ability—to adaptively search through data. However, information technologies often organize the search space in advance and define the basic level of detail. But in complex settings there is no basic level of detail, and the appropriate level of detail depends on what you are searching for and what you are finding. The relevant data depend on how you are interpreting the situation, a phenomenon called the relevance realization process (Vervaeke et al., 2012)—skimming through the large amounts of data to identify what matters. Information technologies can reduce their users to passive recipients of data rather than active sensemakers.

Mental models. As we gain expertise, we are acquiring richer mental models about the phenomena we are interacting with. But information technology can weaken our mental models by keeping us tethered to the original categories we started our searches and analyses with. Decision-makers may have to set up categories in advance, and subsequent events may tie them into these categories rather than allowing and supporting the evolution of categories based on new experiences.

The story behind the data. As we gain expertise, we learn more about how the data were collected, and we become sensitive to limitations in the data collection process. However, information technology often makes it difficult to trace the story of the data and to appreciate how the data were collected and synthesized—the pedigree of the data. Borders et al. (2024) presented a mental model matrix that highlighted the importance of understanding the limitations and boundary conditions of a system in order to work effectively with it. However, if we don’t know the pedigree of the data, we will struggle to identify boundary conditions.

Adaptability. This is a critical function of expertise—being able to pivot based on discoveries about what is really going on in a situation. Unfortunately, many information technologies can go off the rails if we don’t follow the prescribed procedures for interacting with them or if we don’t follow the steps that have been laid out. As a result, it can become more difficult for us to improvise when new and unexpected conditions arise. When we stray from the prescribed path, we are likely to be bombarded with error messages, discouraging us from adapting.

Active stance. With expertise comes an active stance of taking charge and making things happen. Yet information technologies can shift us into a passive stance, unthinkingly accepting the recommendations of the system. Here the link to LLMs is particularly strong, as users relying on LLMs contentedly accept their products, often without doing any kind of due diligence.

These are the attributes of expertise that information technologies seemed to jeopardize, in my 2003 analysis. Fast-forward two decades. Most of these attributes also seem threatened today by the spread of generative AI. I leave it to readers to gauge how closely the generative AI dysfunctions map onto the earlier information technology problems. At the very least, I hope this post will be helpful in unpacking what may be contributing to today’s generative AI dysfunctions. The challenge is to better understand the basis of expertise in order to find ways to safeguard and enhance it.

Borders, J., Klein, G., & Besuijen, R. (2024). Mental model matrix: Implications for system design and training. Journal of Cognitive Engineering and Decision Making, 18(2), 75-98.

Klein, G. (2004). The power of intuition. New York, NY: A Currency Book/Doubleday. Hardcover edition titled Intuition at Work (2003).

Vervaeke, J., Lillicrap, T.P., & Richards, B.A. (2012). Relevance realization and the emerging framework in cognitive science. Journal of Logic and Computation, 22(1), 79–99. https://doi.org/10.1093/logcom/exp067

#4827. What does a blue Texas sweep mean for Elon Musk, Tesla & SpaceX?

After a historic election defeat in one of the most expensive elections in US election history, the state of Texas has turned blue. Along with this come potential implications for Elon Musk and his companies.

The US midterm election was yesterday, and ended up in a stunning result, with Democrats taking control of both houses of Congress by wide margins, and flipping seats that had been considered safe around the nation.

Perhaps most notably, many of these seats were in Texas – a republican stronghold which saw a clean sweep, with the Democrats winning every statewide contest. Republicans maintained some state legislative power and won the State Senate, but as of this writing Democrats are currently ahead in the count for the State House.

The highest profile contest was for Texas Senate, where James Talarico won by a 55-43 margin. This race set the record for the most expensive Senate race in the nation’s history, with over half a billion dollars spent on this one seat alone.

About half of the total money spent on the race came courtesy of republican PACs, a method by which companies and the ultra-wealthy can launder money into bribes to buy elections. And most of the PAC money in the race came from one man, Elon Musk, who spent $212 million to support the enormously unpopular and scandal-ridden outgoing Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton

That’s nearly half of the $474 million dollars that Musk put into this election cycle, suggesting that he saw this race of particular importance. And yet, his candidate lost miserably.

But the rout did not stop with Paxton. Paxton’s old job as AG is now going to be filled by Democrat Nathan Johnson. And Greg Abbott, the outgoing Governor, was defeated by Gina Hinojosa. Even the less high-profile races for court seats were swept by Democrats – three seats on the 15th Court of Appeals and four seats on the Texas Supreme Court.

And those results, even beyond the big-spending Senate race, could all matter quite a lot to Elon Musk.

In 2021, Musk announced that Tesla would move its headquarters to Texas, after Musk got in a spat with California government because he didn’t want to follow COVID restrictions for worker safety.

But the move wasn’t only about COVID restrictions – it was also about Musk’s vision of having more control over the business environment. And, by making allies with Ken Paxton and Greg Abbott, he has had quite a bit of control in Texas.

The two have rolled out the red carpet for Musk, giving him carte blanche in his adopted state.

Autonomous vehicle regulations have been changed, allowing driverless Robotaxis to operate where other states with more advanced regulations and transparency requirements would not. And many other laws benefitting Tesla and SpaceX have been passed. Paxton even sued Sam Altman, Musk’s chief AI rival, just weeks ago.

Perhaps more notably for Musk personally, Texas has promised to allow Musk to get away with more financial shenanigans than states with more established business regulations might.

With Tesla both headquartered and incorporated in Texas, it can now use special “business courts” instituted by Abbott at Musk’s own urging. If cases from these courts are appealed, they go to the 15th Court of Appeals, a court created by Abbott and filled with 3 hand-picked judges, intended to reduce the chance that a Democratic district court (say, one in Austin, whereTesla is headquartered) gets final say on any issue.

But, the 15th Court of Appeals just had elections last night – and its three seats were flipped to Democratic control.

The 15th Court of Appeals also, just last week, stopped an inquiry into thousands of pages of emails between Abbott and Musk. The emails had been released, but are heavily redacted, with Abbott referring to the redacted portions as having “intimate” information in them.

And who made that request for inquiry into the emails? Gina Hinojosa, a name you might recognize, as she is the next Governor of Texas, as of last night.

As of yet, those emails have not all been released, so we don’t actually know exactly how much influence Musk was able to exercise over the state’s outgoing Governor. But given the changes we know about, the money Musk spent in Texas, and the thousands of pages of emails that have been redacted, it seems likely that there is something more to be found in them.

So what does this all mean for Tesla, our main area of coverage here at Electrek?

On a Texas state level, probably not all that much. There’s no reason that Democrats in the state would interfere with Tesla’s production of electric vehicles – in fact, they’d probably encourage it more than republicans do. Talarico has stated that he supports Tesla – it is a large employer in the state, after all.

And certainly, the national wins by Democrats could be a boon for EVs, and for climate action, given how hostile republicans have been to them (and Musk has been the single largest supporter of that hostility towards EVs, which has cost Tesla billions).

The same likely goes for SpaceX – perhaps a blue Texas will be more restrictive of the company’s improper usage of protected habitats, or building of data centers, or whatever the hell is going on on twitter. But the core businesses of space launches and Starlink don’t seem likely to go anywhere.

For Elon Musk, though, things could be different. Given his “intimate” relationship with Abbott, the extreme amounts of money he spent on Paxton, and the highly personal nature of his truly ridiculous attacks against Talarico, it seems unlikely that he will have a similar level of allyship with Texas government actors as he did in prior years. Talarico’s main anti-corruption message was seemingly aimed straight at Musk’s attempts to buy elections, after all.

And given his high levels of spending on a party that is outright hostile electric vehicles, it seems clear that his personal fortunes are far more important to him than the well-being of his companies.

as a texan, I had zero faith that talrico would win, especially with the amount of money musk dumped in the race at the last minute. I’m so heartened to see that money cannot buy everything and that we, as a country, are finally pushing back against the trump/musk insanity. maybe we can start upholding the constitution and ensuring the law applies to all citizens regardless of wealth.

This is all important to note, because Musk has been working towards a scheme to merge SpaceX and Tesla, a deal which would likely be a detriment to both companies and all of their shareholders. Musk wants the deal so he can exercise more control over, and extract more personal wealth from, the combined entity.

But that scheme could rely on a favorable legal environment in Texas courts, an environment that just got less favorable.

And, given Musk’s penchant for childish lashings-out anytime he deems people insufficiently obsequious to his imagined greatness, could we see another situation where Musk encounters the tiniest amount of pushback from state government and then picks up his toys and leaves, as he already did in both California and Delaware? Time will have to tell. Keep your popcorn buckets primed and ready.

#4825. Ultra-Processed Food Intake May Not Be Linked to Colorectal Cancer

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Ultra-processed foods as a group do not increase the risk of colorectal cancer, according to a new study.

Researchers from the National Cancer Institute (NCI) report that they found no association between overall intake of ultra-processed foods and colorectal cancer incidence among the more than 400,000 adults they studied.

They said their findings are important because ultra-processed foods now account for more than half of adults’ energy intake in the United States. They published their research on October 15 in PLOS Medicine.

The study comes amid recent research that has connected ultra-processed foods to an increased risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes, and obesity.

However, the link between colorectal cancer and ultra-processed foods has been less clear.

A 2025 study reported that ultra-processed foods may increase the risk of colorectal cancer in females under 50.

A 2022 study found that males who consumed high rates of ultra-processed foods had a higher risk of developing colorectal cancer.

NCI scientists noted in their new study that ultra-processed products represent a wide range of foods.

They pointed out that foods such as whole grains and dairy products were associated with a lower risk of colorectal cancer among study participants, while foods such as processed meats were associated with a higher risk.

“We did not observe an association between total ultra-processed food (UPF) intake and colorectal cancer incidence,” said Erikka Loftfield, PhD, a former investigator with the NCI, in a news release. “However, when we looked at food groups, like whole grains, dairy, and meat, by UPF or non-UPF source, we found that associations were in line with current dietary guidance for colorectal cancer prevention.”

Experts not involved in the study told Healthline that the NCI scientists appear to be on the right track.

“While the study did not find that overall ultra-processed food intake was associated with colorectal cancer risk, the findings suggest that simply classifying foods by their level of processing may not tell the whole story,” said Marissa Shams-White, PhD, a senior principal scientist in population science at the American Cancer Society.

“One of the challenges with studying ultra-processed foods is that the category includes a very wide range of foods and beverages, and we don’t yet know whether they all relate to colorectal cancer risk in the same way,” she added.

Ketan Thanki, MD, a colorectal surgeon specializing in benign and malignant diseases of the colon, rectum, and anus at the MemorialCare Todd Cancer Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California, agrees.

“Just because it is ultra-processed doesn’t mean it is inherently bad or good for you,” he said. “The source of the food and the additives like preservatives, flavorings, sugars, and pigments that are added to increase palatability probably play a much larger role in increasing cancer risk.”

“A whole grain cereal and a hot dog are both UPF, but they push risk in opposite directions,” Thanki noted.

In their study, the National Cancer Institute researchers analyzed data from 461,682 adults aged 50 to 71 years who enrolled in the NIH-AARP Diet and Health Study between 1995 and 1996.

The participants recorded their eating habits using a food frequency questionnaire. Researchers then used that data to estimate each person’s intake of ultra-processed foods. The median consumption of ultra-processed foods among participants was 43% of daily median intake.

The participants were then followed until 2018, for a median of nearly 19 years. The scientists reported that 10,075 cases of colorectal adenocarcinoma were diagnosed during that period.

The researchers reported no association between overall intake of ultra-processed foods and the incidence of colorectal cancer.

However, they said their findings varied by food type. Higher intake of whole grains, fiber, dairy products, and calcium derived from UPF sources was associated with lower colorectal cancer incidence, while meat from UPF sources was associated with higher incidence.

Researchers also found that replacing 10% of gram weight from UPF intake with minimally processed foods was associated with a 2% lower incidence of CRC.

The researchers acknowledged limitations to their study.

They noted that the participants were primarily older, white Americans. In addition, UPF intake was only measured once at the beginning of the study period, so the findings cannot account for changes in eating habits or the increasing availability of ultra-processed foods.

Nonetheless, Ashkan Farhadi, MD, a gastroenterologist at MemorialCare Orange Coast Medical Center in California, who wasn’t involved in the study, said he found the results “very interesting.”

“The definition of ultra-processed foods is so broad,” he told Healthline. “It’s important to designate what type of ultra-processed foods you’re talking about.”

“You shouldn’t just go by whether a food is designated as ultra-processed,” Farhadi added. “You should go by what’s on the [ingredient] label.”

Shams-White shared a similar approach. “When it comes to diet, it is important to follow an overall healthy dietary pattern,” she told Healthline. “Emphasize a variety of fruits and vegetables and whole grains and limit red and processed meats, sugar-sweetened beverages, ultra-processed foods, and refined grain products.”

“Eating a diet rich in fiber and whole grains, dairy and calcium, and limiting processed and red meat and alcohol is what really reduces your risk of developing cancer,” added Thanki.

The American Cancer Society estimates there will be about 108,000 new cases of colon cancer and nearly 50,000 new cases of rectal cancer in the United States in 2026.

The incidence rate is slightly higher in men than in females.

The organization reports that the rate of colorectal cancer cases in the United States has declined since the mid-1980s, but they note that most of that decrease has occurred in older adults.

The incidence rate among people under 50 increased by nearly 3% from 2013 to 2022. Colorectal cancer is now the leading cause of death from cancer for U.S. adults under the age of 50.

Colorectal cancer occurs when cells in the large intestine grow out of control. About 70% of cases begin in the colon.

There are a number of symptoms of colorectal cancer. Some of the common ones include:

Diet is considered one of the risk factors for colorectal cancer. However, there are other considerations.

Farhadi said genetics, obesity, smoking, alcohol consumption, and a sedentary lifestyle can also increase a person’s risk of developing colorectal cancer.

Shams-White noted that the American Cancer Society Guideline for Diet and Physical Activity for Cancer Prevention recommended achieving and maintaining a healthy weight, being physically active, following a healthy eating pattern, and avoiding alcohol and tobacco.

Thanki points out that there are risk factors for colorectal cancer that can be modified and others that can’t.

“Among non-modifiable factors, the biggest are age, a personal or family history of colorectal cancer or advanced polyps, inherited syndromes such as Lynch syndrome, and longstanding inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis or Crohn’s colitis),” he told Healthline.

“On the modifiable side, the evidence is strongest for obesity, physical inactivity, smoking, alcohol, and diets high in red and processed meat and low in fiber, whole grains, and calcium,” he added.

Colorectal cancer treatment outcomes improve if the disease is caught at an early stage. The 5-year relative survival for localized colorectal cancer is 91.3%. The 5-year relative survival rate for colorectal cancer caught at various stages, including cancer that has spread, is 65.4%.

Shams-White points out that the American Cancer Society Colorectal Cancer Guidelines recommend that people at average risk of colorectal cancer start getting screened at age 45.

“Screening can help prevent cancer by finding abnormal cells and polyps so they can be removed before they can become colorectal cancer, as well as detect cancer at an earlier stage,” she said.

Thanki had similar advice. “Get screened starting at 45, or earlier if you have a family history or other risk factors, because colonoscopies can prevent cancer by removing polyps,” he said. “And never ignore symptoms like rectal bleeding or a change in bowel habits at any age.”

#4824. Tesla accused of selling FSD to Korean owners who can’t legally use it

A South Korean consumer group is asking regulators to stop Tesla from marketing “Full Self-Driving” to customers whose cars can’t legally run it. By Tesla’s own numbers, that covers 99% of the Teslas it sold in Korea last year.

Citizens United for Consumer Sovereignty plans to file the complaint with the Korea Fair Trade Commission (KFTC) on Thursday, according to Reuters, which reviewed it. It comes just a week after a Seoul court threw out a refund lawsuit from Korean owners who paid for FSD.

It all comes down to where the car was built. The Model S, Model X, and Cybertruck come from US factories and enter Korea under the KORUS free trade agreement, which recognizes US safety standards. Those are the only Teslas that got “FSD (Supervised)” when Tesla switched it on in Korea late last year.

The problem is that those don’t represent the vast majority of Tesla vehicles sold in Korea.

The Model 3 and Model Y sold in Korea come from Giga Shanghai. There’s no equivalent deal with China, so those cars need Korean approval that FSD doesn’t have.

And that’s most of the fleet. Tesla Korea country manager Seo Young-deuk told lawmakers last month that FSD works on about 20% of Teslas registered in the country. Tesla has sold more than 170,000 Shanghai-built vehicles in Korea since 2023, per data it handed to the National Assembly, and sales of those China-made cars grew more than fivefold between 2023 and 2025.

Seo’s figure also clears up the conflicting eligibility numbers we flagged when Korean police booked 80 Tesla drivers for using illegal FSD unlock devices last month. A lawmaker’s data from May put it at 2.4%, while a data firm counted 20.7% in August. Tesla’s own testimony matches the higher one.

For years, Tesla charged Korean buyers a one-time 9.043 million won (about $6,726) for FSD. It moved to a 150,000 won ($112) monthly subscription in August.

The complaint points to Tesla’s “coming soon” promotion last year and a demo video shot on Korean roads. Neither made it clear that FSD would only work on a small slice of the cars Tesla was selling. The group says Tesla knew that and kept pitching the package to every buyer anyway.

“Tesla generated substantial revenue by selling an expensive driver-assistance system to mass-market buyers who cannot use it for even a single day,” said Choi Young-suk, who heads automotive issues at the group.

It wants the KFTC to temporarily order Tesla to stop marketing FSD for ineligible cars. It’s also asking for fines and for the case to be sent to state investigators.

The KFTC is already looking at this. An official told Reuters the agency opened a probe into Tesla’s FSD advertising earlier this year and has received written responses from the company. Tesla didn’t respond to Reuters’ requests for comment.

Tesla has collected FSD money from buyers of China-made cars in Korea for more than three years. But at an October 8 parliamentary hearing, Seo testified that Tesla had never formally asked the transport ministry to test FSD. Tesla filed for certification about two weeks later.

Approval won’t be a rubber stamp either. The ministry told a lawmaker that FSD doesn’t comply with local safety rules, including the requirement that drivers keep their hands on the wheel. Current FSD versions let drivers go hands-free as long as the cabin camera sees them watching the road.

None of this slowed sales. Tesla passed BMW this year to become Korea’s top imported car brand.

So far, owners haven’t had much luck in court. Last month, a Seoul court dismissed a lawsuit from Tesla owners seeking FSD refunds, with the judge pointing to Korean regulations, not Tesla, as the reason more features never launched.

#4823. To Grow Up, First Grow Down

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Our culture likes easy measurements of maturity, such as chronological age, number of achievements, financial stability, and the ability to adapt to conventional norms for having a job and a place to live. However, a person can meet these criteria for growing up and still have little or no psychological maturity. The word maturity comes from the Latin word matures, meaning “ripen.” We can understand ripen as meaning “moving beyond early stages of development.” But before we can ripen, we may need to grow down.

When we grow down, we confront early attitudes and beliefs that strengthen neither our relationship with ourselves nor our relationship with life. Here are a number of these attitudes and beliefs.

Growing down is a process of letting go or dying, where denial and certain attachments are challenged and gradually let go. It often takes multiple acts of letting go before an attachment is released. Let’s look at how the growing down process can translate into ripening or growing up.

Maturity can so often be such a vague concept that we don’t know what it is or whether we can actually influence it. Everyone seems to want to “grow up” into a flourishing adulthood, yet it can help to take “growing down” seriously because it shows us what we need to let go of and get ready for. It's not much different from clearing land of rocks and debris in order to prepare for planting and growing. The goal is to take some of the mystique out of becoming an adult. We owe it to ourselves and to others to attend first to some of our more primitive attitudes before we find ourselves merely pretending we are adults.
