#4820. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: What Breast Cancer Patients Need When Treatment Ends

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Supporting your health & well-being at every stage of your breast cancer journey

Resources to support your emotional health and well-being

Tips for nourishing your body and mind while living with breast cancer

Navigating your next steps after diagnosis

A guide to medications, treatments, and procedures for breast cancer

Guide to symptoms, tests, and screenings for early detection

Resources to support you during remission or recurrence

Breast cancer awareness has never been higher. Every October, the world turns pink and the key messages—that early diagnosis saves lives and that knowing the risk factors reduces the risk—are hard to miss.

Breast cancer remains the leading cause of cancer death amongst women in most countries, with an estimated 694,000 deaths annually and around 8.2 million women globally living with a breast cancer diagnosis, according to 2024 data from the World Health Organization (WHO)’s Global Health Observatory.

But when October ends, and the wall-to-wall campaigns move on, what has awareness actually changed for those impacted by breast cancer?

For Marisa Weiss, MD, the answer is that there is always more to be done, and she’s uniquely placed to hold that view.

Dr. Weiss has treated breast cancer patients for more than 30 years. In 2000, she founded Breastcancer.org, a not-for-profit that provides expert-reviewed medical and lifestyle information on breast health and breast cancer to millions of people globally.

Sixteen years ago, she was diagnosed with stage 1 breast cancer, becoming, in her own words, a “dual citizen” in that moment, with lived experience on both sides of the doctor’s desk.

I had the pleasure of sitting down with Dr. Weiss as part of Healthline’s Breast Cancer Awareness Month coverage to talk about how awareness has changed care for the better for millions of people around the world, but why critical gaps remain, and how access to trusted information and community can help close those gaps.

“It’s good that awareness is high, but knowledge remains low,” she said. The misconception she sees most is the belief that breast cancer is something that happens to other families.

“Most people think they’re off the hook because it doesn’t run in their family,” Dr. Weiss explained. In fact, 85%-90% of people who get breast cancer have no known family history and no known gene in the family, “so we’re all at risk,” she warns.

For most, a diagnosis arrives as a shock. “They’re completely surprised, flattened by it. Their life is upside down, and they scramble to find what they need to know,” Dr. Weiss noted.

That aftershock caused by a diagnosis is at the core of why Breastcancer.org exists. Because the person whose life is at stake is increasingly expected to direct their own care and advocate for their needs, access to accurate and empowering information is critical in that process. “Decisions aren’t being made around the patient and for the patient in a bubble,” Dr. Weiss said. “Decision-making is supposed to be shared.”

With appointments hard to get and clinicians stretched, she said patients have to do more of the work.

In practice, Dr. Weiss says that means preparing by writing down your family history on both sides and making sure your care team knows who you are. “What’s important to you? Who’s in your life? Do you want more children? Are you planning a vacation? All those decisions can have an impact on the care you receive.”

Where Dr. Weiss sees the widest gap is after treatment ends. Speaking from personal experience, she said: “I had the best care possible, and there were still gaps. You can find yourself alone with lots of questions and fears.”

Understandably, a great deal of focus and attention is centered on diagnosis and treatment, she said. Then it stops. “When your life is supposed to go back to normal, it never does. You have to find a new normal. That quiet phase can be really lonely and difficult.”

For Dr. Weiss, the questions that fill that silence are often as practical as they are emotional: “How do I know my treatment is working? How do I tell leftover side effects from new ones? How do I go back to work, and who do I tell? How do I talk to my kids? What about that new treatment I heard about on TV last night?”

That post-treatment period can also be the first time anyone has really had a chance to stop, take stock, and start asking “what next?”

“You might think, what happened to me? Who do I want to be moving forward? How do I have a long life full of wonderful things, and remain as independent as possible?”

Some of what patients do post-care never reaches their doctor at all, with Dr. Weiss pointing to a growing amount of personal care happening outside the hospital: GLP-1s, peptides, hormonal therapies, supplements, medical cannabis, to name a few. Breastcancer.org’s annual Pulse survey shows that people reported being interested in all of those things and more, and that they tend to learn about them online rather than risk the stigma of bringing them up with their doctors.

“They don’t really trust that their doctor knows much about these things,” she said, “and they don’t want a conversation that makes them feel judged. Some patients worry about what ends up in their medical record and then everyone’s going to know.”

Dr. Weiss says the stakes are real, because some of those choices can interact with cancer medications with potential for adverse or unexpected side effects. “That is why people turn to Breastcancer.org with questions that are personal, private, and important, including “what do I need to share with my doctor, and who’s on a need-to-know basis?”

Peer communities fill part of that gap. “Breastcancer.org’s members share medical information, tips, and practical advice with people who have just had the call, or are about to start treatment and don’t know what to expect,” Dr. Weiss shared, and said that while she values that kind of support system hugely, it does come with a caveat:

The same community can also be intrusive. Dr. Weiss describes standing at a party, drink in hand, when someone asks what kind of cancer you had, what you did, and whether it came back. Her advice to patients, which she took herself: “Put up your hand and say, ‘thank you so much for caring, but I cannot hear another story right now.’”

On the topic of AI and its impact on treatment and care, Dr. Weiss is cautiously optimistic. On the treatment front, she points to tools that help radiologists read mammograms more thoroughly and assess future risk as game changers in the fight against breast cancer.

Breastcancer.org also uses AI for tasks such as finding citations and interpreting pathology reports. But like many, she is wary of misinformation. “When you’re fearful, you look for reassurance, and you don’t want to be reassured by misinformation. When it comes to health information, every word matters.”

Her final advice is for the appointment itself, which she says she has even struggled with. “When they start speaking quickly in complex medical language, you can get lost really fast. The risk is you kind of fake it. That must not happen.”

“Do your research in advance. Write things down. Bring someone you trust as a second set of ears. Ask direct questions. Don’t worry if it sounds funny or silly. It’s always a smart question when you are dealing with your health.” Just be prepared for the questions to multiply from there. “Once you get a certain set of questions answered, that raises more questions. So just write it all down.”

If breast cancer has touched your life, know that you are not alone. Healthline’s Bezzy Breast Cancer Community is a space for peer support, feel-good stories, and advice in a welcoming, stigma-free environment when your lived experience is seen, heard and valued.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#4819. I Was Making an Important Point, but Now It’s Gone

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Have you ever been in a situation where you knew exactly what you wanted to talk about, but when the moment arrived, you couldn’t think of what you had planned to say?

That’s what happened to Jen* when she made her first big presentation at a new job. She practiced the talk repeatedly, to the point at which she felt confident that she could make it without a mistake. Yet when a senior colleague interrupted her to ask her to clarify what she had just said, her brain went blank.

“I felt so stupid,” she said. “It was a simple question. I knew the answer. But my mind left the room. My brain froze completely.”

Many factors can contribute to this kind of “brain freeze,” which fits into a category of cognitive problems called “word retrieval difficulties.” There are a number of possible causes, both physiological and psychological, but there is also the odd and amazing role of unconscious dynamics that can make our brains stop working just when we need them to work at top capacity.

The brain is a complex organ that controls thought, memory, emotion, touch, motor skills, vision, breathing, temperature, hunger, and every process that regulates our body. When any of these factors is in play, the complex system of neurons through which the brain stores and recovers words may not be firing as quickly as you would like.

Anxiety—already a complex emotion—can be a lot more complicated when unconscious thoughts and feelings play a role. These are thoughts and feelings that go on outside of our awareness. They are often behind behaviors that we can’t explain to ourselves.

For instance, Jen’s brain freeze during her presentation could certainly have been caused by her anxiety about proving herself to her new colleagues and bosses. But contributing to her anxiety may have been some thoughts and feelings that she kept hidden away, even from herself. It’s very hard for us to uncover these hidden emotions on our own, because we push them out of our minds and into what Freud called the “unconscious” mind.

Today we think of these ideas as “split off” or “dissociated” from awareness.

The psychoanalyst Christopher Bollas gave the name “unthought known” to the thoughts and feelings, memories, and ideas about ourselves that we don’t let ourselves know about.

Sometimes we relegate information, without realizing it, to the unknown part of ourselves, to protect ourselves from old, painful memories. But we can also use this powerful tool to step away from a side of ourselves that we don’t like. This emotional process almost always happens outside of our awareness.

The presence of unconscious thoughts and feelings does not necessarily rule out physiological causes for memory loss. If it happens to you on a regular basis, it may still not be a cause for concern, but it’s important to have a medical professional check to make sure it’s not a symptom of a medical problem.

If this type of memory loss happens only on occasion, even if you aren’t in psychotherapy, you can start to look for some patterns that reveal unconscious reasons for behaviors and feelings that you can’t explain. And once you understand what’s going on behind the scenes in your psyche, it’s much easier to do something about it.

If you consistently go blank at work, for example, you can start to track the specific moments when it happens. When do you forget? Is it with a particular person or group of people? Does it happen in certain situations?

As Jen began to sort through some of her unacknowledged thoughts and feelings about the presentation, she realized that she was not only trying to prove herself to her new colleagues, but also secretly trying to prove to her family how well she was doing.

She wanted to impress them, particularly her father and her older sister. “I imagined telling them how well I had done,” she said. “I love the expression on my dad’s face when he tells me he’s proud of me. But that’s something I know and think about all the time. Maybe I’m too much of a daddy’s girl.”

After a few minutes of talking and thinking, Jen said, “I think maybe part of the problem is that a secret part of me also wanted to make my sister feel bad. She always does well at everything, and I’m always feeling like I can’t quite match up to her. I wanted her to feel the same way for once.”

As Jen talked about this dynamic with her sister, something she knew but did not like to think about, she had to face her competitiveness head-on. “It’s something I don’t like about myself,” she said. “I mean, it’s OK to be competitive. But it’s not OK to want to hurt the person you’re competing with. It makes me feel so bad about myself.”

We suspected that Jen had shut down just as she was beginning to show off. Of course, we can’t know, but it seemed likely that her internal dialogue connected showing off at work with a wish to hurt her sister by rubbing her success in. Because Jen was so critical of this part of herself, she may have shut it down, stopping her brain in the process.

Once we had talked out the meaning of Jen’s brain fog, we began to explore Jen’s competition with her sister. It had many different components, and it took a while, but eventually Jen realized that she could succeed without hurting her sister. The next time she made a presentation, she did not fog up.

It’s not always easy to know what your unconscious mind is thinking. But if you decide to do some internal exploration, start with the idea that the "unthought known" is something you don’t like about yourself. Once you’ve located the difficult thoughts, it’s easier to change your behavior. And the “unthought known” is much less likely to sabotage you.

#4816. Kia EV7 revealed as an upcoming midsize electric SUV

Kia is crushing it with a full lineup of electric vehicles ranging from entry-level SUVs to the three-row EV9 and PV5 van. A leaked document revealed that Kia plans to add the EV7, a new midsize electric SUV, to fill the gap between the EV6 and the three-row EV9.

With 291,219 vehicles sold last month, up 10.3% from October 2025, Kia posted its eighth consecutive month of year-over-year sales growth.

Kia said it plans to build on the momentum by focusing on electrified vehicles in key markets, including hybrids in the US and fully electric vehicles in Europe.

After launching the EV2 earlier this year, Kia now offers one of the most complete EV lineups, including the EV2, EV3, EV4, EV5, EV6, EV9, and PV5 van.

According to a recently leaked document, a new midsize electric SUV will join the lineup. KoreanCarBlog uncovered an internal document from Kia’s Employment Stability Committee that reveals the company’s upcoming production plans.

The report claims “production of mid-range electric SUV ‘EV7’ to proceed” at Kia’s Gwangju factory in South Korea.

Although no other details were revealed, the EV7 will be a midsize electric SUV, slotted between the EV6 and three-row EV9.

During its 2026 CEO Investor Day in April, Kia announced plans to expand its EV lineup to 14 models by 2030.

In Europe, Kia plans for EVs to account for 66% of sales by 2030, with a full lineup that includes the EV2, EV3, EV4, EV5, EV6, EV9, three PBVs, a new C-segment electric SUV, and its first “SDV B-segment hatchback.”

So what can we expect from the upcoming EV7? Kia said earlier this year that it’s developing a new EV platform “to significantly enhance both product performance and cost competitiveness.”

Although it didn’t offer specifics, Kia claimed the new EV platform delivers up to a 40% increase in battery capacity and a 9% improvement in motor output.

It will use Kia’s fifth-generation batteries, providing up to 15% higher energy density than its current tech.

Kia’s upcoming software-defined vehicles (SDVs) are expected to integrate Hyundai’s SDV architecture (CODA), Pleos Connect infotainment system, and Gleo AI, an in-vehicle Agenic AI.They will also have Level 2++ autonomous driving capabilities on highways.

The EV5, Kia’s current midsize electric SUV, is 4,615 mm long, 1,875 mm wide, and 1,675 mm tall, with a 2,750 mm wheelbase, which is about the size of the Sportage, so you can expect the EV7 to be slightly bigger. It will likely be closer in size to the Sorento.

Powered by an 81.4 kWh battery, the Kia’s current EV5 offers a WLTP range of up to 329 miles (530 km).

Not a full lineup, the EV van is still missing. Why offer so many different SUVs between Kia and Hyundai but no van/minivan?

However, unlike the EV6 and EV9, the EV5 uses a 400V electrical architecture rather than an 800V. As a result, charging the battery from 10% to 80% takes about 30 minutes, rather than 18 to 25 minutes. The upcoming EV7 will likely use the 800V system for faster charging.

The document also revealed Kia will begin production of “completely redesigned models” at its Gwangmyeong Factory, including the 2028 Carnival Hybrid and a hydrogen-powered Carnival by 2029.

At its Gyeonggi Hwaseong Factory, Kia will reportedly also produce redesigned models, including the Sorento Hybrid in 2028.

#4814. Women With Autoimmune Diseases More Likely to Be Misdiagnosed Than Men

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A new international study found that women with autoimmune diseases often experience longer delays in getting a correct diagnosis compared to men.

The research, published in eClinicalMedicine on October 12, also shows that females report higher rates of being misdiagnosed, especially with mental health or psychosomatic conditions.

These findings highlight challenges in diagnosing autoimmune diseases and suggest that sex differences affect patients’ experiences in healthcare.

This is significant given that autoimmune diseases, conditions in which the body’s immune system attacks its own healthy cells, are estimated to affect around 4.6% of the U.S. population. Additionally, despite the observed disparities in diagnoses, women are almost two times as likely as men to be diagnosed with an autoimmune disease.

Researchers conducted an online survey between December 7, 2024, and March 7, 2025, inviting adults ages 18 and older who had a confirmed autoimmune or rheumatic disease.

The survey was carefully designed and tested with input from patients, charities, and medical experts to ensure it captured important information about patients’ diagnostic journeys.

Participants were asked about the time it took from when they first noticed symptoms to when they received their autoimmune diagnosis.

They also reported how many healthcare visits they made before diagnosis and whether they felt their symptoms were ever misdiagnosed as mental health issues, psychosomatic problems (physical symptoms caused or worsened by psychological factors), or another physical disease.

The survey was widely shared on social media, patient support groups, and community organizations to reach a broad audience.

Special efforts were made to include underrepresented groups such as males, younger individuals, and people from minority ethnic backgrounds.

The survey was available in five languages, and participants could complete it with help from researchers if needed.

To analyze the data, the team used statistical methods, adjusting for factors like age, type of autoimmune disease, continent of residence, and financial situation.

These methods helped compare the experiences of males and females while accounting for differences in disease types and other factors

The study included 6,607 participants, with 91.2% identifying as female and most living in the United Kingdom. On average, women were younger than men in the study.

Autoimmune thyroid disease was the most common condition reported. Non-autoimmune conditions like anxiety were more commonly reported by women, while men reported higher rates of hypertension.

One of the main findings was that women reported longer delays before getting a diagnosis. Nearly 32% of females experienced delays of four years or more from symptom onset to diagnosis, compared to about 26% of males.

On the other hand, men were more likely to be diagnosed within a year of symptom onset (nearly 50% of males versus 40% of females).

Women also made more visits to healthcare providers before diagnosis, with 26.2% having 11 or more visits compared to 18.4% of men.

When it came to misdiagnoses, women were significantly more likely to report that their symptoms had been mistaken for mental health issues (43.3% of females vs. 32.7% of males) or psychosomatic causes (33.9% of females vs. 21.5% of males).

However, there was no significant difference between men and women in being misdiagnosed with another physical disease.

The type of autoimmune disease also influenced misdiagnosis rates. Diseases with clear physical symptoms or definitive lab tests, like type 1 diabetes or rheumatoid arthritis, had lower rates of mental health and psychosomatic misdiagnoses.

In contrast, conditions such as autoimmune encephalitis or undifferentiated connective tissue disease had higher rates of these misdiagnoses (over 50%).

After adjusting for disease type and other factors, women still had higher odds of reporting mental health or psychosomatic misdiagnoses compared to men.

Other factors affected misdiagnosis rates, too. People living in continents outside the United Kingdom, those with lower household income, and certain disease groups were more likely to report psychiatric or psychosomatic misdiagnoses. Age, however, was not linked to the risk of being misdiagnosed.

Muhammad Qasim, MD, a pulmonologist and medical writer with MySleepApneaTeam, told Healthline that autoimmune diseases are often difficult to diagnose. Qasim wasn’t involved in the study.

“Early symptoms can be vague, fluctuate over time, and affect several parts of the body,” he said. “Fatigue, pain, sleep problems, brain fog, and mood changes do not immediately point to one diagnosis.”

Qasim echoed the study’s findings, explaining that similar symptoms may be interpreted differently in women versus men, with complaints being attributed to anxiety, stress, or psychosomatic causes.

However, there are probably multiple factors contributing to this phenomenon, he said.

“Differences in disease presentation, gaps in research, fragmented care, and unconscious bias can all contribute,” said Qasim. “Once a psychiatric explanation enters the medical record, there is also a risk that later clinicians view new symptoms through that same lens.”

Regarding unconscious bias, the study’s authors noted that women have historically been treated by doctors as “less credible” and more “emotional” in reporting their symptoms, which can influence how doctors interpret them.

These disparities in diagnosis are important, however, because longer waits and erroneous diagnoses can delay proper treatment and increase women’s frustration, leading them to stop asking for help.

The researchers suggest that healthcare providers need to be aware of these concerns and consider autoimmune diseases carefully when patients, especially females, present with more complicated symptoms.

Adam Carewe, MD, a physician in Boulder, CO, and a clinical informaticist with General Medicine who wasn’t involved in the study, offered guidance to those experiencing symptoms on how to obtain a proper diagnosis.

Healthgrades is owned by RVO Health. By clicking on this link, we may receive a commission. Learn more.

“Patients can keep an accurate symptom timeline, bring in family history, ask what else could be explaining these symptoms, ask for a plan for next steps if initial testing/treatment, do not change anything, and lastly get a second opinion,” he advised.

Carewe emphasized the value of not giving up on finding answers. “The most important thing for anyone with persistent symptoms of any kind is to continue to work with your primary care clinician and continue to advocate for yourself,” he said.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

31-0: That was the headline-grabbing regular season record of the 2025-26 Miami RedHawks NCAA Division I basketball team. We’re talking about the first Miami University, located in Oxford, Ohio, and founded in 1809. Not Miami of Florida, a college launched in 1925

A large chunk of credit for the RedHawks’ remarkable run belongs to Head Coach Travis Steele and how he optimizes performance with the mental approach he trains. Steele was the much-deserved recipient of the 2026 John McLendon National Coach-of-the-Year Award, honoring the top head coach across all levels of collegiate basketball.

“I find this stuff (the mental game) fascinating," Steele shared in a recent interview conducted for this post. “If you can get guys to play at their peak level, man, you’re going to be really good. I’ve seen talented guys that don’t believe in themselves, and they stink. I’ve seen average players that have a high level of belief in themselves, and they’re really good.”

The difference stems from their mental approach to practice, performance, and competition.

“We [coaches] all talk about the X’s and O’s, and I could talk basketball all day long,” Steele continued. “But then you go back to the ‘why?’ piece. ‘Why can this guy have the belief, and the other guy doesn’t?’ Maybe they don’t embrace the process. Maybe they don’t handle adversity. And then how can we help them in an area, like the mental toughness piece, to overcome adversity?

“That, to me, is coaching,” Steele summarized.

Bo Schembechler, Ara Parseghian, Woody Hayes, Paul Brown, Sean McVay, Weeb Ewbank, Carmen Cozza, John Harbaugh, Walter Alston, John Pont, and Paul Dietzel: Legendary, Hall-of-Fame sports coaches, all with the common denominator of Miami University, regarded in the sports world as the “Cradle of Coaches.”

Deriving from those coaching greats is the Miami Mindset, a set of four core values, fine-tuned by Steele, that defines the RedHawk basketball mental approach. Here's a list of those Miami Mindset core values:

“We try to basically make our core values come to life rather than just being words on a wall,” Steele explained. “If we embody our four core values, that’s what we call the Miami Mindset.”

#4812. Puerto Rico tapped Sunrun’s home batteries for 18 straight days

Sunrun has doubled participation in its Puerto Rico virtual power plant to more than 60,000 customers, and the grid is putting those home batteries to work: It tapped into them for 18 straight days in October.

A virtual power plant (VPP) connects batteries across thousands of properties and coordinates them to supply power together.

Sunrun says its VPP network in Puerto Rico now includes nearly 70,000 batteries, frequently delivering 45 megawatts (MW) for four hours at a time. It has dispatched them more than 60 times since April, including daily deployments beginning October 13 during unusually hot weather.

For Puerto Ricans, who pay some of the highest electricity prices in the US, getting reliable power for all that money remains a problem. Residential electricity averaged 26.36 cents per kilowatt-hour in August, compared with a US average of 18.16 cents, according to the US Energy Information Administration (EIA).

That makes the batteries already installed at homes pretty useful. The grid can pay to tap their stored energy when supplies get tight, putting existing equipment to work without waiting for a new power plant to be built.

Sunrun’s network grew from around 4,000 customers in 2024 to more than 30,000 in 2025. It says it’s the largest aggregator in Puerto Rico’s Customer Battery Energy Sharing (CBES) program.

LUMA, the island’s grid operator, calls on CBES when it expects electricity demand or reserve needs to exceed available generation. Companies such as Sunrun then manage the battery response automatically.

Homeowners get paid for sharing energy and choose how much to keep for backup. Sunrun says most share a small portion of their total battery capacity; the company earns recurring revenue for operating the network.

Across the wider CBES program, LUMA reported in July that more than 90,000 participating customers had helped provide an average of 75 MW during generation shortfalls.

Puerto Rico’s reliability problem goes well beyond hurricanes.

Customers averaged 36 hours without power in 2025, even excluding major events, according to the US EIA. That’s up 19% from 2024. Mainland US customers generally experience about two hours a year under the same measure. Major events added another 23 hours of outages on the island.

More batteries can help, but the size of the shortfall still matters. In an April regulatory filing, participating aggregators said the program couldn’t prevent every rolling blackout in 2025 because some supply gaps exceeded its roughly 50 MW of four-hour capacity. They’re looking to enroll more batteries so the program can cover larger shortfalls.

#4809. Xiaomi’s humanoid robots work a full 8-hour shift in its EV factory

Xiaomi has released footage of its humanoid robots working a full 8-hour shift at its EV factory, sorting center console side covers for its cars. The company says the robots did it on their own, catching and fixing their own mistakes along the way.

If that’s fully autonomous, it’s truly impressive. But we’ve been disappointed by humanoid robot demos before, and Xiaomi left out the numbers that would let anyone check.

Xiaomi Tech posted the video on X on November 3, as the company picked up awards at the World Internet Conference in Wuzhen. It shows the robots pulling side covers from bins, carrying them to storage racks, and placing them into slots, with a running clock of cumulative working time and an “Autonomous, 100x Speed” label in the corner.

8 hours on the production line. 🤖Xiaomi’s full-size humanoid robot takes on an 8-hour task at the Center Console Side-Cover Sorting Workstation at Xiaomi Auto Factory.Beyond sustained operation, it can autonomously detect and correct anomalies while coordinating with the… pic.twitter.com/brpO3S5bCn

According to Xiaomi, the robot covered the full day of production needs at that workstation. The company says it detected and corrected anomalies on its own and coordinated with the production line’s control system, calling it “another step toward real-world industrial deployment.”

This is the third milestone in a program Xiaomi has been running in public all year. In March, it put a humanoid in its Beijing EV factory as an “intern” installing self-tapping nuts. Xiaomi reported a 90.2% success rate over three hours, fast enough to meet the station’s 76-second cycle time. By July, it said that success rate was up to 98%, and it added two harder stations, including the side-cover sorting job from this week’s video.

Last December, Xiaomi’s CEO, Lei Jun, said the company plans to deploy humanoid robots in large numbers across its factories within five years.

Xiaomi also announced that its open-source Xiaomi-Robotics-1 AI model, pre-trained on 100,000 hours of real-world robot data, has started trial runs in Xiaomi staff apartments. In a 30-minute run, it completed more than 50 task chains across three rooms.

Xiaomi didn’t say how many parts the robots sorted, what the success rate was over eight hours, or how many times a human had to step in. It also didn’t explain how the robots stayed powered for a full shift: swapped batteries, a tether, or charging breaks.

And that matters, because “autonomous” in a robot video can mean anything. Tesla’s Optimus robots at the 2024 “We, Robot” event were remotely operated by humans, which Tesla didn’t disclose at the time. Last December, an Optimus took a suspicious tumble in a Miami demo, raising its hands to its head like a teleoperator pulling off a VR headset as it fell.

To be fair to Xiaomi, it has published success rates and cycle times for its earlier factory tests. That’s more than Tesla has ever shared about Optimus doing useful work, and Tesla now says it won’t deliver Optimus to outside customers until late 2027.

The most interesting moment in the video comes around the 1:37 mark. In a segment Xiaomi labels “Dropped Material: Multi-Robot Collaboration,” a humanoid upper body mounted on a wheeled base rolls in to help the two legged robots.

It’s not the only thing on wheels in that scene, either. The bins of parts are shuttled around by autonomous mobile robots, the same kind of wheeled platforms that have been moving materials in car factories for years.

They could have got a lot more done if they'd moved the parts bins closer so the bots didn't have to walk as far. That's a huge open space between the two.

If this demo was truly fully autonomous for eight hours, with the robots handling their own errors, that’s one of the more impressive humanoid demos we’ve seen. Xiaomi deserves credit for putting its robots on a real production line and publishing some real numbers along the way. But we’ve been burned by “autonomous” robot demos before, so we’ll wait for more details before getting too excited.

The wheeled robot is our favorite part of the video, though. It’s a great example of why you shouldn’t bet everything on the humanoid form factor. Wheels are far more efficient than legs on most terrain, and a flat factory floor is the best-case scenario for wheels. A wheeled base doesn’t burn energy just to stay upright, it’s more stable when carrying parts, it has room for a much bigger battery (which would solve that 8-hour power question), and it needs fewer expensive actuators.

The value in these robots is in the hands, arms, and the AI driving them. Legs make sense in homes with stairs, and maybe that’s where Xiaomi wants to take them eventually. But factories were designed around carts and forklifts, not people walking around carrying parts.

As robots and AI put more strain on the grid and electricity rates climb, solar and batteries are becoming essential home infrastructure, not a luxury. #4808. Walking, Yoga, or Zumba? Which Exercises Help Relieve Menopause Mood Symptoms

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Physical activity may help support mood and improve quality of life during the menopause transition, according to a study published on October 13 in Menopause, the journal of the Menopause Society.

This review of 12 clinical trials involving over 1,000 people ages 40 to 65 found that both aerobic and mind-body exercises may be associated with reduced depressive symptoms and improved well-being.

It is common for individuals to experience psychological symptoms during menopause, including depression, anxiety, low mood, stress, and difficulty with thinking and memory. These symptoms can have a significant effect on quality of life, with an estimated 1 in 10 women leaving work due to menopause symptoms.

This study found that regular physical activity may help improve certain psychological symptoms and overall quality of life during menopause.

The types of physical activity in the review were broken into two categories: aerobic-based exercise (ABIs) and mind-body activities (MBAs).

The researchers found that ABIs showed the strongest effects on reducing depressive symptoms and improving overall quality of life. This may be particularly relevant during perimenopause when women may be more vulnerable to mood instability due to estrogen fluctuations.

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, pointed out that there are various ways that ABIs can have positive effects.

Ross explained that aerobic exercise may help improve menopausal symptoms through several mechanisms, including the release of endorphins, reduced inflammation, improved cholesterol levels, and better regulation of body temperature and sleep cycles.

MBAs were shown to have a greater effect on improving psychological quality of life. These exercises involve mindfulness, body awareness, and breathwork. They may help improve emotional regulation.

ABIs tended to require fewer sessions. This may mean these types of exercises are easier to fit in, especially if you are facing time or energy constraints. For example, in one trial, participants were instructed to walk for 30 minutes, three times per week. In another trial, participants attended three hour-long Zumba classes per week.

In contrast, one trial using MBAs instructed participants to attend two 75-minute yoga classes each week, and another had participants attend daily one-hour yoga classes.

Stephanie Faubion, MD, Director for the Mayo Clinic Center for Women’s Health and Medical Director for The Menopause Society, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that these findings are encouraging.

However, she also cautioned that this was a relatively small body of evidence. “While physical activity is an important component of a healthy lifestyle during midlife, I would be cautious about interpreting these findings to mean that exercise is a treatment for depression related specifically to menopause,” she said.

Research has shown that physical activity can positively affect mental health. Studies have also found that regular exercise can help improve symptoms of depression and anxiety.

Faubion told Healthline that, broadly speaking, regular physical activity may support mood through multiple pathways, including better sleep, improved physical function, and overall well-being.

However, she also noted that this study cannot tell us precisely why the improvements occurred or whether aerobic exercise is superior to other forms of activity.

Faubion said that recommending physical activity during and after menopause extends well beyond mood.

“Physical activity is important for cardiovascular and metabolic health, maintaining muscle mass and physical function, and supporting bone health,” she said.

“These become particularly important at midlife as women experience age- and menopause-related changes in body composition, bone, and cardiometabolic health,” Faubion added.

Ross agreed that physical activity is important for more than just mood and mental health improvement. She also noted that there are other types of exercise beyond ABIs and MBAs that can benefit people during menopause:

“These types of activities help with weight loss or allow women to maintain a healthy weight. If you are not ready for aerobic exercises, stretching and simple exercises help with balance and stability,” Ross said.

Faubion told Healthline that physical activity is an important part of healthy aging and should be encouraged during the menopause transition, but it should not be suggested that women can simply exercise their way out of significant depression or other bothersome menopause symptoms.

“Women experiencing persistent depression, anxiety, or symptoms that interfere with their quality of life should seek evaluation and discuss the full range of evidence-based treatment options with their healthcare clinician,” Faubion said.

#4807. Are You Hanging On to a Negative Story That Keeps You Stuck?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

This morning I was in the dog park with Bucket, my Cocker Spaniel. Bucket’s favorite thing to do is chase sticks; he’d rather chase sticks than eat, sleep, or do almost anything else. Each stick seems to bring him unabashed and unwavering delight, as is evident in his dog smile. That’s the story I’ve written for him anyway… and I’m “sticking” with it.

During this morning’s “sticking” session, Bucket discovered a gigantic, thick, and heavy, unwieldy branch, which he managed to get between his jaws and then proudly carried around in his mouth, snubbing my attempts to offer him smaller, more navigable options. After some time, I noticed that he was starting to notice the squirrels running up a tree, and then another couple of dogs arrived, and I saw him staring at them seemingly with longing, as if he wanted to say hello and play.

Several times Bucket attempted to approach the dogs or run at the squirrels, but each time he tried, he was stymied by his big stick, which kept getting caught on everything he encountered. So too, the other dogs moved away when they saw the big stick coming. Bucket continued to grip onto it because he didn’t yet realize that he had the power to open his jaws and release it. He didn’t understand, yet, that the choice was his; he was the one grasping the very thing that was causing his limitation.

We sometimes do the same thing as humans: we hold onto our self-constructed stories long after they serve us, and long after they’re true in reality. Bucket eventually dropped his stick, but we humans often don’t, and continue living out a frustrating life, unknowingly depriving ourselves of what we really want—gripping onto and remaining loyal to our story, but not to ourselves. We stick with our stories even when our stories keep us stuck and cause us suffering.

If someone’s narrative, for example, is that they are unlovable, even in the face of someone loving them, they reject that truth, dismiss what’s happening in reality, the love they’re receiving, and continue insisting on their unlovable “me” story—the very story, paradoxically, that makes them suffer. Reality be damned, they are loyal to their story, not the facts, and not even to their own happiness.

We may stick with our story, even a negative story, simply because we don’t realize that it’s a story. We’re not aware of the fact that much of what we call “me” is a story, something we construct, and can in fact deconstruct. But we think our assumptions about ourselves, our “me” story, are fundamental truth—just who we are. The problem is that our “me” story is inside the lens through which we see everything in our life. The “me” whose identity story we’ve created is the one interacting with and experiencing reality, and reporting back to us on it. And what we experience is inherently interpreted and corrupted through the beliefs of this self-made “me” lens. In order to free ourselves and take off our “me” lenses, we need to see the seer, to get behind the lens through which we’re looking. We need to ask ourselves again and again what we believe about ourselves before anything else happens; what we are coming into the situation already assuming. We have to get behind the eyes through which we’re looking at and experiencing our life. When you can look at the looker, and question the story about the “me” you’ve constructed, then things change.

Our stories are often built around wounds that we’ve suffered. If, for example. our self-story is that we have to do everything ourselves, no one can help us, it’s not because it’s fun to believe that or feel that way. At some point, we probably needed help and didn’t receive it. Our early caretakers may have been unable to give us what we needed. Maybe they didn’t care or were limited and unable or unwilling to help us. The point is we felt alone. And so we created this story of being fundamentally un-helpable. If we’re somebody who has to do everything for ourselves, and no one can help; if we can know that for sure. If it’s set in stone, then we don’t have to mourn the fact that we experienced it; it’s just what is—our karma, if you will. Knowing that this is who we are, we feel more in control; we don’t have to hope for help or be sad or angry about the fact that we didn’t receive it. We don’t have to question why this happened to us, or continues to happen now, because we reject and dismiss the help that’s offered and any evidence that we’re not “all alone.” Furthermore, we don’t ever have to grieve how much we wanted and needed help, and suffered without it. We hold onto the past, counter-intuitively, as a way to not have to mourn and accept the past.

With this as our self-story, locked in place, we can know the kinds of things that happen to us. We don’t have to face the uncertainty of each moment, how it will go, and whether or not we will receive what we need. We don’t have to be vulnerable and ask for help when we need it, because we know we’re not going to get it. Even though our story is not what we want, even though it impedes our connection with others and prevents the opportunity to feel gratitude, even though it gets in the way of our satisfaction and happiness, nonetheless, it becomes our identity and a way to protect ourselves, avoid grief, shame, uncertainty, and the hope that things really can change. Our self-story, as awful as it is, becomes a kind of security blanket. We are secure in the knowing that we won’t get what we need.

The story we’re holding onto about who we are might not be true, that is, if we stopped proving it were true. It might be something we could drop, like Bucket and his stick. But to let it go can feel even more painful than sticking with our negative story. To let it go can feel like we’re abandoning our suffering, moving from it as if it’s no longer part of us. Paradoxically, it feels too painful to move on from our story of pain.

But getting free is not about dropping your story as if that experience never happened. It is about honoring the wound that the story triggers, grieving and acknowledging what you have experienced—but—without the assumption that this present moment is going to be the same, and without the story that this is who you are, a person whose life is always “like this.” The wound matters, the story can be dropped.

Are you carrying a stick that you're ready to drop?

#4805. BYD’s new luxury electric SUV has one big problem: It weighs over 8,200 lbs

BYD’s new full-size electric SUV, the Denza N9 EV, was revealed in a regulatory filing, weighing in at over 8,200 lbs (3,760 kg). That creates one issue.

The Denza N9 first went on sale last March, a flagship plug-in hybrid (PHEV) SUV with a combined CLTC range of about 807 miles (1,300 km) on a full charge and full tank of gas.

BYD introduced the updated Flash Charging version earlier this year, which uses its second-generation Blade Batteries and delivers a pure electric CLTC range of 260 miles (420 km). Combined, it gets up to 944 miles (1,520 km) under CLTC conditions.

In the latest batch of new energy vehicles (NEVs) approved for sale by China’s MIIT (Ministry of Information Technology) this week, a new all-electric Denza N9 appeared.

The filing revealed the Denza N9 EV will be available with three electric motors, delivering a combined output of 1,193 horsepower (890 kW).

A badge on the back reveals it will use BYD’s tri-motor e3 platform. The setup features a single 362 horsepower (270 kW) front motor and two 415 horsepower (310 kW) rear electric motors.

The electric version measures 5,270 mm long, 2,030 mm wide, and 1,830 mm tall, with a 3,125 mm wheelbase, which is the same as the current Denza N9 PHEV Flash Charging model. It will be available in five- and six-seat configurations.

While the filing reveals it will use a lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery with cells from BYD’s battery unit, FinDreams, it does not provide specifics like capacity or driving range.

One relatively big thing to note: the Denza N9 EV has a gross weight of 3,760 kg, or about 8,289 lbs.

Obscene weight , and that applies to other SUVs as well. Vehicle registration taxes should be based on vehicle weight as it wears out roads and endangers other road users and infrastructure and wastes resources.

As AutoNext points out, this makes it too heavy for an EU category B driving license, which covers up to 3,500 kg (7,716 lbs).

Last year, the EU updated the rules, allowing drivers who have held a category B license for two years to drive alternative-fuelled vehicles with a maximum weight of up to 4,250 kg (9,369 lbs).

While the Denza N9 EV would qualify as listed, newer drivers would still need a commercial EU category C1 driving license to drive it.

BYD launched its luxury sub-brand, Denza, in Europe and the UK in March, starting with the Z9 GT and the D9 van. More recently, it introduced the Bao 5 PHEV SUV and the Denza Z electric supercar.

#4803. Germany’s EV share hits record 37% as BEVs beat gas and diesel combined

Battery-electric cars took a record 37% of Germany’s new car market in October, according to data released today by the Kraftfahrt-Bundesamt (KBA), the country’s federal motor vehicle authority. That’s 94,086 BEVs, up 79.5% from 52,425 a year earlier.

For the second month in a row, German buyers registered more fully electric cars than gasoline and diesel cars combined. And with two months left in the year, Germany has already blown past the auto industry’s EV forecast for all of 2026.

The overall market barely grew in October. Total new car registrations rose 1.7% to 254,492 units, which means the EV growth came straight out of combustion cars.

Gasoline car registrations crashed 40.5% to 38,516 units, a 15.1% share. Diesel fell 21.6% to 23,882 units, or 9.4% of the market. Add them up and pure combustion cars made up just 24.5% of new registrations, 12.5 points less than BEVs alone.

Plug-in hybrids slipped 7.9% to 28,499 units (11.2%). So plug-ins accounted for about 48% of the German market last month, and the average CO2 emissions of new cars dropped 21.1% year-over-year to 79.4 g/km.

As we reported this summer, BEVs became the most popular powertrain in Germany for the first time in June with a 28.4% share. They’ve kept climbing every month since: 32.4% in August, 34.5% in September, and now 37% in October.

Through the first ten months of the year, Germany has registered 698,230 BEVs, up 60.7% year-over-year. That’s about 28.2% of the 2,476,290 new cars registered in 2026 so far, up from roughly 19% for the full year 2025.

And the industry didn’t see it coming. In December, the VDA, Germany’s auto industry association, forecast about 693,000 BEV registrations for all of 2026. Germany passed that in October. At the current pace, it’s on track for somewhere around 875,000 by the end of the year.

The biggest driver is Germany’s new EV incentive, which applies retroactively to cars registered since January 1. It pays €3,000 for a new BEV, bought or leased, and up to €6,000 for lower-income households with children. Households with taxable income above €80,000 (€90,000 with kids) don’t qualify.

The program targets private buyers, and that’s exactly where the KBA data shows the jump. Private registrations rose 20.6% in October, while commercial registrations fell 8.2%. Private buyers now make up 40.7% of the market.

Germany has been here before. The government killed its previous EV incentive overnight in December 2023, and BEV sales fell off a cliff in 2024. This time, the money is going to the buyers who need it most, and it’s moving the needle fast.

Tesla’s German numbers swing wildly with its quarterly delivery cycle. In September, the end of the quarter, it registered 12,552 cars (+268.7%) and the Model Y was the best-selling car in Germany. In October, it registered only 2,932, which is still up 290.9% but from a tiny base, for a 1.2% market share.

Year-to-date, Tesla is up 206.1% to 47,742 registrations. That’s a big rebound from a terrible 2025, but BYD is now slightly ahead with 47,961 (+216.1%). BYD registered 5,173 cars in October (+54.3%), though the majority of its German sales are plug-in hybrids, not BEVs.

The other Chinese brands are growing even faster. Leapmotor registered 3,661 cars in October, up 288.6%, and is at 19,705 for the year. Xpeng was up 284.4% to 1,380 units, and SAIC’s MG was up 48.5% to 4,140.

Volkswagen Group still owns the top of the EV charts, though. Through September, the Skoda Elroq was Germany’s best-selling EV with 30,845 units, ahead of the Tesla Model Y (29,464) and the VW ID.3 (24,352).

The EV boom is happening during one of the worst years in the history of Germany’s auto industry.

In October, the VW brand’s German registrations fell 4%, Audi dropped 8.4%, Porsche lost 5.7%, and Opel crashed 24.1%. BMW was flat (+0.1%), and Mercedes was the exception, up 8.5%. VW is still by far the biggest brand in Germany with an 18.2% share, but it’s down 5.6% for the year.

Last week, Volkswagen Group reported a €5.9 billion operating loss for the third quarter, its biggest quarterly loss since Dieselgate, driven by a writedown of its Porsche stake, weak sales in China, and thin EV margins. The group has already approved a plan to cut 100,000 jobs and close several German plants. Mercedes cut its 2026 car margin guidance to 2-3% last week, mainly because of China.

It’s not a product problem. VW’s new ID. Polo is sold out with over 30,000 orders, BMW has over 50,000 orders for the Neue Klasse iX3, and the Elroq leads the German EV market.

Germany is proving what we’ve been saying for years. Give buyers EVs they can actually afford, and they switch fast. Fully electric cars went from about 19% of the market last year to 37% in October, and the country beat the industry’s full-year forecast with two months to spare. That’s not a slow transition.

One thing to consider when talking about EVs not being Germany-made is that Germany car makers can't make them fast enough. A lot of the models are sold out for months. It's not an excuse but something to keep in mind.

Compare that with North America, where EV sales dropped 44% after the US killed its federal EV tax credit. Same technology, sometimes the same cars. The difference is policy.

Germany’s problem is who’s capturing the demand. Hint: it’s not German automakers.

But whose fault is that? You can’t spend years dragging your feet on electrification and lobby government back and forth on EV incentives and then complain when those who are all-in on EVs are able to take better advantage of the customer shift amid another oil crisis.
